===== ICLE-US-IND-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong personal engagement and a clear moral position. You care deeply about environmental damage and corporate responsibility, and that passion gives your writing energy. You also use a well-known historical example, the Exxon Valdez spill, which is a good choice for an argumentative essay because it gives your claims a concrete case. For revision, focus on making your argument more precise, more formal, and better organized.

Main strengths

Strong opinion and clear concern  
Your viewpoint is easy to understand: you believe large corporations must be held accountable for environmental harm.

Effective use of example  
The Exxon Valdez disaster is a strong case study. It helps the reader see the real-world consequences of negligence.

Emotional impact  
Phrases such as “needless suffering, death and devastation” communicate urgency and show why the issue matters.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis clearer and more arguable
Your opening begins with “Money has been said to be the root of all evil,” but the essay itself is really about corporate negligence and environmental responsibility, not money in general. Try to state your main argument directly.

Possible revised thesis:  
*The Exxon Valdez disaster shows that large corporations must be held legally and financially accountable for environmental damage caused by negligence.*

This is clearer, more focused, and easier to support.

B. Improve organization
Right now, some ideas repeat, and the essay moves between personal reaction, background information, and argument without clear structure. A stronger structure could be:

Introduction: introduce the issue and thesis  
Background: explain what happened in 1989  
Argument: show how Exxon’s negligence and poor response increased the damage  
Solutions: explain what corporations and governments should do  
Conclusion: restate the main point

Each paragraph should have one main idea.

C. Use a more academic tone
Some wording is too emotional or informal for an academic essay. Strong feelings are fine, but they should be expressed in a formal way.

For example:
“mind boggling” → difficult for ordinary citizens to fully grasp
“I loathed the corporation” → I became deeply critical of the corporation
“pollute and rape our land” → seriously damage and exploit the environment

Also, avoid overusing “I feel” and “I personally.” In argumentative writing, confident statements are often stronger than personal reactions.

D. Support claims with evidence
You make serious claims, but some need more evidence or explanation. For example:
Why was Exxon’s response “slow, disorganized and unacceptable”?
What specific failures in ship maintenance were revealed?
How were local communities and wildlife affected?

Adding a few factual details would make your argument more persuasive. Even one or two specific examples per paragraph would help.

E. Fix unclear or incomplete sentences
There is one serious problem here:

> “Each time Exxon was confronted with this question, spokespersons said the same thing. .”

The symbol suggests missing text. You need to complete or remove this part.

Also, some sentences are awkward or grammatically incorrect.

Language and grammar corrections

Here are some examples:

“I personally witnessed first hand”  
  → *I witnessed firsthand*  
  (“personally” is unnecessary)

“Prince William sound”  
  → *Prince William Sound*  
  (capitalize proper nouns)

“What Exxon did not plan for was the negligence and gross human error of the Captain in charge the vessel that night.”  
  → *What Exxon did not plan for was the negligence and gross human error of the captain in charge of the vessel that night.*

“After the super tanker ran aground on Bligh Reef, the Exxon Valdez Tanker, poured over 11 million gallons…”  
  → *After the supertanker ran aground on Bligh Reef, the Exxon Valdez poured over 11 million gallons…*  
  (remove unnecessary comma; “supertanker” is one word)

“The publishing of Exxon's notes and court transcriptions, prove…”  
  → *The publication of Exxon’s notes and court transcripts proves…*

“more monies”  
  → *more money* or *additional funds*  
  (“money” is usually uncountable)

Style suggestions

Try to vary sentence openings and avoid repetition of “Exxon” at the start of many sentences. You can also combine short ideas into smoother sentences.

For example:

Original:  
*The effects of the raw crude oil in the waters were and still are devastating. The carnage was and still is unbelievable.*

Revision:  
*The effects of the crude oil were devastating and, in many areas, continued for years after the spill.*

This sounds more formal and avoids repetition.

Final advice for revision

When revising, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis about corporate accountability.  
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one central point.  
Replace emotional or informal language with academic vocabulary.  
Add specific evidence to support your claims.  
Correct grammar, punctuation, and capitalization errors.

You have a strong foundation and a meaningful topic. With clearer structure and a more formal style, this essay can become much more persuasive and effective.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0002.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear moral position: you argue that “crime does not pay,” and you support this claim with a real-life example. This is a strong starting point because argumentative essays need a clear opinion, and your viewpoint is easy to understand. The personal example also makes the essay more vivid and emotionally effective. To revise successfully, focus on organization, clarity, grammar, and formal academic style.

Main strengths
Clear argument: Your central claim is consistent throughout the essay.
Relevant example: The story about the man involved in real estate fraud supports your point well.
Strong conclusion: The final sentence clearly restates your message.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your first paragraph introduces the topic, but the thesis is not fully developed. A stronger thesis should clearly state your argument and possibly mention your main reason.

For example:
Current idea: Crime does not pay.
Stronger thesis: Although some people turn to crime to become rich quickly, illegal wealth ultimately destroys lives, families, and futures.

This would prepare the reader for the example in the next paragraphs.

B. Improve paragraph structure
Your essay has three paragraphs, but the first one is too broad and includes several ideas. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce the topic of people seeking quick wealth through crime.
   - End with a strong thesis.

Body paragraph
   - Present the example of the man who became rich through fraud.
   - Explain what happened to him and his family.

Analysis paragraph
   - Explain why his choice was unnecessary and harmful.
   - Connect the example back to your main argument.

Conclusion
   - Restate your thesis in a fresh way.

Right now, your ending is quite brief. A separate conclusion would make the essay feel more complete.

C. Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases sound conversational rather than academic. For example:
“get rich quick attitude”
“the bottom line is”
“I know several people”
“fit in this category”

In academic writing, use more formal phrasing:
“a desire for quick wealth”
“ultimately”
“some individuals”
“belong to this category”

Also, avoid saying “I know several people” unless personal experience is required. In argumentative essays, it is often better to focus on the example itself rather than on yourself as the writer.

Grammar and language corrections

Here are some sentence-level issues to revise:

“societies rules” → “society’s rules”
“find it expectable” → probably “find it acceptable”
“see and easier way” → “see an easier way”
“catigory” → “category”
“not being will” → “not being willing”
“Crime does not pay, is a very true statement.”  
  Better: “The statement ‘Crime does not pay’ is very true.”
“His family way very proud” → “His family was very proud”
“The one day” → “Then one day”
“beleive” → “believe”
“old couples money” → “old couples’ money”
“families money” → “family’s money”
“fortune 500 company” → “Fortune 500 company”

Also check verb tense consistency. For example, you move between present and past:
“He decided to get rich quick and he did.”
“The family did not want to believe it…”

Since this is a past example, mostly use the past tense when telling the story.

Develop your analysis more
The middle paragraph tells the story well, but the essay would be stronger if you added more explanation of why this example proves your point.

For example, after describing the arrest, you could explain:
crime may bring temporary wealth, but it creates long-term suffering;
family members also suffer, even if they were innocent;
illegal success is unstable because it depends on deception.

This kind of analysis will make the essay more argumentative, not just narrative.

Suggested revision of your opening
Here is one possible improved version of the beginning:

> Many people want wealth and success, but some choose illegal ways to achieve them. Instead of working patiently, they steal, sell drugs, or commit fraud in order to become rich quickly. Although crime may seem like an easy path to money, it eventually causes serious harm to the criminal and to innocent family members. For this reason, crime does not truly pay.

Notice that this version is more concise, formal, and focused.

Final advice
When you revise, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Organize the essay into introduction, body, analysis, and conclusion.
Use more formal vocabulary.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors.
Add more explanation of how your example supports your argument.

You have a strong message and a useful example. With clearer structure and more careful language, this can become a much more effective argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0003.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay presents a thoughtful argument about how society undervalues child care work. You move beyond the simple idea of “equal work deserves equal pay” and focus on a deeper issue: how cultural values shape pay and status. This is a strong and interesting argument. Below are suggestions to help you revise.

Main strengths

Strong central idea
Your main point is clear: child care work is essential to society, but it is not rewarded financially or socially because society does not truly recognize its value. This is a sophisticated argument.

Good use of example
Using child care work as your main example is effective. It helps you make an abstract discussion about “social value” more concrete.

Academic tone
Your vocabulary and sentence style are appropriate for argumentative writing. The essay sounds serious and analytical.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more direct
The first paragraph is intelligent, but the main claim is slightly hard to locate. Try to state your thesis more clearly at the end of the introduction.

For example, you could write something like:
“Although Americans claim to believe in equality, the low pay and low status of child care workers show that society does not reward all socially necessary work equally.”

This gives readers a clear roadmap.

B. Improve organization and paragraph focus
Your essay has only three long paragraphs. The ideas would be easier to follow if you separate them more clearly.

A possible structure:
Introduction: explain the general issue of equality and social value.
Body paragraph 1: explain why child care is socially important.
Body paragraph 2: explain why society still undervalues it economically and socially.
Conclusion: restate the argument and discuss implications.

This structure will make your reasoning easier to follow.

C. Clarify some sentences
Several sentences are interesting but too long or complex, which makes the meaning less clear. Try to shorten some sentences and express one main idea at a time.

For example:

Original:
“If we recognize that a work force filled with such diversity of workers will inevitably include women - of all ages - as well as man with family responsibilities, then we see the undeniable economic connection…”

Possible revision:
“A diverse workforce includes women of all ages as well as men with family responsibilities. For this reason, child care is directly connected to the nation’s economic strength.”

This version is easier to read and more grammatically correct.

Language corrections

Here are some specific issues to revise:

Word choice
“profess to the belief” → better: “profess belief in” or “claim to believe”
“pereniel” → “perennial”
“makes a good example for this point” → “is a good example of this point”
“man with family responsibilities” → “men with family responsibilities”
“contribution to the society they live in” → better: “contribution to the society in which they live”

Grammar
“equality of work as defined by specific task” → “equality of work as defined by specific tasks”
“Americans profess to value children, spending time, money and thought…”
  This is grammatically acceptable, but “attention” may sound more natural than “thought.”
“without involving employers or the government”
  Remove the extra space before “involving.”

Articles and phrasing
“the child care worker may reasonably be judged as not providing…”
  Better: “child care workers may reasonably be judged as not providing…”
“valuable labor - valuable contributions”
  Try to choose one term and use it consistently.

Development of argument

Your essay would be stronger if you added a little more evidence or explanation. Right now, the argument is persuasive, but somewhat general.

You could strengthen it by adding:
an example of low wages in child care,
a comparison with other socially respected professions,
a brief explanation of why work associated with women is often undervalued,
or a specific policy issue, such as public funding or employer-supported child care.

Even one or two concrete details would make the essay more convincing.

Conclusion
Your final sentence has an interesting idea, but it is slightly too complicated. Try to end with a clearer and stronger statement.

For example:
“Until child care workers receive better pay, society will continue to treat their work as less valuable, despite its essential role in supporting families and the economy.”

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Write a clearer thesis.
Break the essay into more focused paragraphs.
Shorten and simplify complex sentences.
Correct grammar and word-choice problems.
Add one or two concrete supporting details.

Overall, this is an intelligent essay with a strong social argument. Your ideas are thoughtful and mature. With clearer organization and more precise language, the essay will become much more powerful.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0004.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important and thoughtful argument: society often values paid work more highly than unpaid domestic labor, even though homemaking and childrearing are socially essential. You show clear engagement with the topic and use examples effectively to provoke reflection. The paper has a strong central concern, but it would become much more persuasive with a clearer thesis, stronger organization, and more careful language choices.

Main strengths

Strong topic and clear social relevance  
You address a meaningful issue about labor, value, gender, and family. The reader can easily see why the topic matters.

Good use of contrast  
You effectively contrast marketplace jobs with unpaid home labor. This comparison helps frame the debate.

Thought-provoking examples  
The comparison between a neurosurgeon and someone raising four children is memorable and helps illustrate the difficulty of measuring social value.

Thesis and argument

Your essay’s main claim is somewhat unclear because it moves between two possible arguments:

homemakers should receive financial compensation, or  
society should redefine what it considers valuable.

These are related, but they are not exactly the same. At the end, you present both again without choosing one firmly.

How to revise:  
State one precise thesis in the introduction. For example:

*Unpaid domestic labor should be formally recognized through financial compensation because it provides essential social and economic value.*  
or  
*Rather than paying homemakers directly, society should broaden its definition of valuable work to include unpaid care labor.*

Then make sure each paragraph supports that one main position.

Organization

The essay has a logical general movement, but some paragraphs are too broad and contain multiple ideas. This weakens the structure.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction
Explain the problem
Present a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1
Show how society links money with worth

Body Paragraph 2
Explain why unpaid domestic labor is socially valuable

Body Paragraph 3
Discuss consequences of failing to value homemaking

Body Paragraph 4
Present and defend your proposed solution

Conclusion
Restate thesis and emphasize significance

The final paragraph currently introduces a possible solution very late. You should prepare for this solution earlier in the essay.

Development and evidence

Many of your claims are interesting, but some need stronger support. For example:

“many of the ills of society in the U.S. today have been traced…”
“the media makes her feel behind the times”
“our market-driven culture”

These statements are plausible, but in an academic argumentative essay, they need evidence, examples, or citations.

How to revise:  
Add specific support such as:
studies about the economic value of unpaid household labor
research on childcare and family well-being
examples of government policies in countries that support caregivers

This would make your argument more credible and less dependent on generalization.

Style and tone

Your style is sophisticated, but sometimes it becomes too informal, outdated, or imprecise.

Examples:
“doctors, lawyers and Indian chiefs”  
This phrase sounds humorous and culturally inappropriate in an academic essay. Replace it with a neutral list.
“career with a capital C”  
This is expressive, but slightly informal. Academic writing usually prefers direct phrasing.
“worth its salt”  
This idiom is too conversational for formal argumentative writing.

Also, terms like “first-world” and references to “the 90s” make the essay sound dated. You could use “wealthier societies,” “industrialized societies,” or “contemporary society,” depending on your intended meaning.

Grammar and sentence-level issues

You write in advanced English, but there are several errors in articles, word choice, and sentence construction.

Some important corrections:

“equated with worth as a person”  
Better: *equated with a person’s worth*

“handled by people of both sexes”  
Better: *performed by people of all genders*

“for he or she who works outside the home”  
Better: *for those who work outside the home*

“an neurosurgeon”  
Correct: *a neurosurgeon*

“this contribution to society in so immense”  
Correct: *this contribution to society is so immense*

“if, of course, the worker is married”  
You wrote *it* instead of *if*

“near-dependence”  
This phrase is unclear. Perhaps you mean *adulthood* or *independence*.

Also watch for long sentences. Some are grammatically correct but difficult to follow. Shorter sentences would improve clarity.

Suggestions for revision

Write a clear one-sentence thesis.
Decide whether your essay argues for financial payment, social recognition, or both.
Reorganize paragraphs so each has one main idea.
Add evidence rather than relying mainly on assertion.
Replace informal or outdated expressions with more academic language.
Proofread carefully for article errors, typing mistakes, and unclear phrases.

Final comment

This essay has a strong intellectual foundation and an important argument. Your biggest task in revision is to make the paper more focused, more academic in tone, and better supported with evidence. With those changes, it could become a persuasive and mature argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0005.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong intellectual ambition. You are not just agreeing or disagreeing with the saying; you are trying to test its logic, define terms like *desire*, *virtue*, and *happiness*, and show why the claim is difficult to prove. This is an impressive strength. The next step is to make your argument clearer, more organized, and easier for readers to follow.

Main strengths
Thoughtful argument: You challenge a familiar saying in an original way.
Abstract thinking: You engage with difficult concepts such as evil, virtue, happiness, desire, and wealth.
Confident voice: The essay has a serious, philosophical tone that suits the topic.

Main areas to improve

A. Clarify your thesis
Your central claim appears to be that the saying “money is the root of all evil” is too vague or impractical because concepts like evil and desire cannot be measured objectively. This is a good argument, but it needs to be stated more directly and earlier.

For example, in the introduction, you could write something like:
> The saying “money is the root of all evil” is misleading because it oversimplifies the relationship between wealth, desire, and morality. Since desire and evil cannot be measured objectively, the phrase fails as a serious explanation of human corruption.

This gives the reader a clear map.

B. Improve organization
At the moment, some paragraphs move quickly from one idea to another. Try to give each paragraph one main purpose:

Paragraph 1: Introduce the saying and explain why its logic is flawed.
Paragraph 2: Discuss happiness, contentment, and the wealthy/poor comparison.
Paragraph 3: Explain the contradiction between pursuing money and pursuing virtue.
Conclusion: State your final position and connect it to history/power.

Also, the symbol “” should be removed. It interrupts the flow and looks like an unfinished note.

C. Define key terms more carefully
A major issue is that your essay uses important abstract words in shifting ways. For example:
Is virtue the same as happiness?
Is happiness the same as lack of desire for money?
Is the essay about money itself, the desire for money, or power that comes from money?

You raise these distinctions, which is good, but you should make your definitions more consistent. If you define *virtue* as moral goodness, do not later replace it with *happiness* unless you explain why.

D. Use clearer logic and transitions
Some sentences are intellectually rich but difficult to follow because too many ideas are packed into one sentence. Try to break long sentences into shorter ones and use transitions such as:
However
Therefore
For example
In contrast
As a result

This will help your reader see how one idea leads to the next.

Language and style issues

A. Grammar and word choice
There are several errors in spelling and phrasing. Here are some examples:

“If money was the root of all evil”  
  Better: “If money were the root of all evil”  
  (“Were” is more standard in hypothetical statements.)

“privelaged” → “privileged”
“acruement” → possibly “accrual” or “accumulation”
“wheighed” → “weighed”
“serrendipitously” → “serendipitously”
“where in which” → choose one: “where” or “in which”

B. Awkward phrasing
Some phrases sound unnatural in English:
“virtue obtained” → “virtue displayed” or “moral character”
“financial aptitude” probably does not fit here. You may mean “financial success” or “wealth”
“can be appropriated objectively” may be better as “cannot be assessed objectively”

C. Punctuation
Be careful with hyphens and dashes. For example:
“meaningless-or a least impractical -”  
  Better: “meaningless—or at least impractical—”
“would-must- coincide”  
  Better: “would, or must, coincide” or “would have to coincide”

Example of revision
Here is one possible revision of part of your first paragraph:

Original:  
> I contend that the saying is meaningless-or a least impractical - for summing up the woes and corruption of humanity since desire-much less evil- cannot be appropriated objectively and wheighed against financial aptitude.

Revised:  
> I contend that the saying is at least impractical as an explanation of human corruption, because neither desire nor evil can be measured objectively and compared with wealth in any reliable way.

This version keeps your idea but makes it easier to understand.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Make each paragraph support one main idea.
Define money, desire, virtue, happiness, and evil consistently.
Simplify long sentences.
Correct spelling, grammar, and punctuation.

You already have the basis of a strong philosophical essay. With clearer structure and more precise language, your argument will become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0006.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay raises an interesting question about whether Marx’s principle can work in modern Western society. You show thoughtful engagement with family, religion, individual freedom, and moral relativism. The paper has a clear intellectual ambition, but it needs revision in thesis clarity, organization, evidence, and language accuracy to make your argument more persuasive.

Main strengths
Ambitious topic: You connect Marxism, Christianity, family life, and individualism.
Critical thinking: You do not simply accept the quotation; you question how it works in practice.
Broad perspective: You move from small social units (family) to larger society.

Main issues to revise

A. Clarify the thesis
At present, the essay discusses several ideas, but the main argument is not sharply stated. By the end, you seem to argue that Marx’s egalitarian principle is admirable but unrealistic in Western society because individual freedom, disagreement about value, and moral relativism make it impossible to apply consistently.

State this clearly in the introduction.

Possible revised thesis:
> Although Marx’s principle of contributing according to ability and receiving according to need is morally appealing, it is unlikely to succeed in modern Western society because people disagree about what counts as contribution, individual freedom limits external control, and moral relativism weakens shared ethical standards.

This gives the essay a clear direction.

B. Improve organization
Your body paragraphs contain good ideas, but the logic between them is sometimes unclear. A stronger structure would be:

Introduction: introduce Marx’s idea and present your thesis.
Paragraph 1: family as a small-scale example of need-based support.
Paragraph 2: religion/Christianity as a source of charitable values in Western society.
Paragraph 3: problem 1—difficulty measuring contribution and reward.
Paragraph 4: problem 2—individual freedom versus social obligation.
Paragraph 5: problem 3—moral relativism and lack of shared standards.
Conclusion: restate that the ideal is admirable but difficult to implement.

Use clear topic sentences so each paragraph supports the thesis.

Development and evidence
Some claims are too general and need support. For example:
“In Western society, the primary guide is Christianity.” This may be too broad today. Many Western societies are secular and religiously diverse.
“The modern person lives in a world where relativism reigns supreme.” This is a strong claim and should be explained or softened.

Try to provide:
specific examples,
brief references to philosophers, history, or social realities,
more careful wording such as “in many cases,” “to some extent,” or “often.”

Also, when you ask rhetorical questions (“Must I go instead into the streets…?”), make sure to answer them directly, so the paragraph advances your argument.

Style and tone
Your tone is formal and appropriate, but some sentences are too abstract. Try to be more precise. For example:
Instead of “the concept provided,” say “Marx’s principle.”
Instead of “forces that bind us,” explain exactly what you mean: family, religion, and social norms.

Also avoid overgeneralization. Academic writing is stronger when it sounds careful rather than absolute.

Language corrections
There are several grammar, vocabulary, and punctuation problems. Here are important examples:

“on e would understand” → one would understand
“social institutions & practices” → social institutions and practices  
  (avoid & in formal academic writing)
“who it to define” → who is to define
“form the family” → from the family
“will unlikely be achieved” → is unlikely to be achieved
“The  religious” → The religious
“intrinsic, values” → intrinsic values

Sentence-level suggestions
Original:
> These are the words of Karl Marx, the father of modern communism.

This is fine, but you should include the quotation itself clearly and explain its meaning.

Original:
> A mother nurtures her helpless infant.

Good example, but connect it more directly to your argument:
> A family often functions according to need: parents give care and resources to children not because children have earned them, but because they need them.

Original:
> Ultimately, it is for the individual to determine what his/her contribution is to society, if any.

This is a strong point, but “his/her” is awkward. Better:
> Ultimately, individuals decide for themselves what, if anything, they owe to society.

About the missing quotations
You use “” in several places. In revision, make sure to include the exact quotations. Without them, the reader cannot fully understand your references. If you quote Marx and the New Testament, introduce and cite them properly.

Final advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Make each paragraph support that thesis.
Add specific explanation and evidence.
Reduce broad claims or qualify them.
Edit carefully for grammar and formal academic style.

You have the basis for a strong argumentative essay. Your ideas are thoughtful; now the goal is to present them in a clearer, more structured, and more precise way.

===== ICLE-US-IND-0007.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: whether crime “pays” depends on how we define “pay,” and we should consider not only money earned but also costs, risks, and losses. This is a strong approach for an argumentative essay because it goes beyond a simple yes/no answer. You also organize your body paragraphs around different types of loss, which gives the essay a logical structure.

Strengths

Clear controlling idea
   - You successfully redefine “pay” in a broader way. This gives your essay depth and makes your argument more interesting.

Logical organization
   - The essay moves from direct financial costs to time costs, then to physical/mental health and legal consequences. This progression is effective.

Use of examples
   - The opening jewelry store example helps readers understand your point immediately.

Consistent focus
   - Most paragraphs connect back to the main question, so the essay stays on topic.

Main Areas to Improve

Make the thesis more direct
Your argument is present, but it could be stated more clearly in one sentence near the beginning. Right now, the essay discusses the issue well, but the exact position is a little indirect.

For example, you could write:
“Crime rarely pays when all financial, physical, mental, and legal costs are considered.”

This would help readers understand your position immediately.

Improve paragraph development
Some body paragraphs introduce good ideas but do not fully explain them. For example, in the paragraph about time investment, you compare criminals with legal workers, which is interesting, but the explanation could be clearer and more detailed.

For instance:
Why does education change whether crime “pays”?
How should time be measured against legal employment?
What makes crime less efficient than legal work?

Try to add 1–2 more sentences of explanation after key points.

Use a more formal academic tone
At times, the essay becomes creative or conversational, especially when comparing crime to a “small business” and “larger criminal corporations.” This is engaging, but some phrases sound informal or overly humorous for an academic argument.

Examples:
“small businessman criminal”
“drive him out of business”
“no taxes”

These ideas are interesting, but the conclusion should sound more serious and precise. You could revise the final idea like this:
“Although some crimes may produce short-term financial gains, these are often outweighed by long-term legal, physical, and psychological costs.”

Watch grammar and word choice
There are several language issues that affect clarity. Here are important examples:

“all manners of loss” → better: “all kinds of losses”
“equiptment” → “equipment”
“health and well being” → “health and well-being”
“comes about from” → better: “results from”
“A crime's income” → better: “The income from a crime”
“he does better the legitimate businessman” → “he does better than the legitimate businessman”

Also be careful with article use and parallel structure.

Avoid overgeneralization
Some statements are too broad, such as:
“Very few criminals have worker’s compensation.”
“Larger criminal corporations will always seek to drive him out of business.”

These sentences are vivid, but “very few” and especially “always” are difficult to prove. In academic writing, softer wording is often better:
“Criminals typically do not have access to the protections available in legal employment.”
“Organized criminal groups may also create additional risks and competition.”

Sentence-Level Suggestions

Here are a few revised versions of your sentences:

Original: “In terms of criminal work, pay must be weighed as gains made from crime versus all manners of loss that occur as a result.”
Revised: “In criminal activity, whether crime pays should be measured by comparing its gains with all the losses that result from it.”

Original: “If the criminal can only expect to earn minimum wage in a legal job, then crimes can earn less to justify time spent.”
Revised: “If a criminal could earn only minimum wage in a legal job, a smaller return from crime might seem worthwhile in terms of time spent.”

Original: “The stress that comes about from constant risk of injury and incarceration is a cost in itself...”
Revised: “The stress resulting from the constant risk of injury and imprisonment is itself a serious cost...”

Conclusion Advice

Your conclusion should more clearly restate your main argument. Right now, the ending is memorable, but it weakens the seriousness of your essay. Try to end by reinforcing your claim that crime may appear profitable in the short term, but usually does not pay when all consequences are included.

Final Revision Priorities

Write a clearer thesis in the introduction.
Develop each body paragraph with more explanation.
Replace informal or humorous phrases with more academic language.
Correct spelling, grammar, and word choice errors.
Strengthen the conclusion so it clearly supports your overall argument.

Overall, this is a thoughtful and well-structured essay with a strong central concept. With clearer academic wording and a more direct thesis, it could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a consistent argument: money itself is not evil, but the love of money can lead to harmful behavior. This is a strong central idea, and you support it with several relevant examples. The essay also shows good moral reflection and a serious tone. For revision, focus on organization, precision, grammar, and style so your argument becomes more academic and persuasive.

Main strengths
Clear thesis/stance: You directly reject the idea that money is the root of all evil.
Logical distinction: The difference between money and love of money is the most effective part of the essay.
Relevant examples: You mention families, companies, nations, and individuals, which broadens the discussion.
Purposeful conclusion: The last paragraph returns to the main argument well.

Improve the organization
Right now, the essay reads more like a series of connected reflections than a fully structured argumentative essay. Try using a clearer paragraph pattern:

Introduction
   - Introduce the issue.
   - Present your thesis clearly.

Body Paragraph 1: Why money is necessary
   - Explain practical uses of money.
   - Give one or two concrete examples.

Body Paragraph 2: Why love of money is dangerous
   - Show how greed affects individuals and families.

Body Paragraph 3: Wider social effects
   - Discuss companies, crime, or society.

Conclusion
   - Restate your thesis in a concise way.

A possible revised thesis:
> Money itself is not the root of all evil; rather, it is the excessive love of money that leads people to selfishness, corruption, and harm.

Make the tone more academic
Some expressions sound too emotional or conversational for an academic essay.

Less academic:
“This idea is completely erroneous.”
“How many times have we seen on the evening news…”
“It is a cross-cultural thing.”

More academic:
“This claim is mistaken.”
“Many examples in public life show that…”
“This problem exists across cultures.”

Also, avoid very absolute claims like “the root of all evil” unless you explain them carefully. In academic writing, it is often better to be slightly more precise:
“a major cause of harmful behavior”
“a source of many social and moral problems”

Develop examples more specifically
Your examples are interesting, but some are too general. For instance, “a family being broken apart” or “a nation crumbling” could be explained more clearly. Instead of listing many examples quickly, choose one or two and explain how greed causes the damage.

For example:
> Greed can damage families when individuals prioritize wealth over relationships, spending excessive time pursuing status or income while neglecting emotional responsibilities.

This makes your point stronger and more analytical.

Sentence-level grammar and style corrections
There are several language issues to fix.

Common problems in this essay:
a. Capitalization
“he begins” → He begins
“is money the root of all evil?” → Is money the root of all evil?

b. Word choice / spelling
“all together” → altogether
“capitivates” → captivates
“sued for good” → used for good
“it’s needs met” → its needs met
“love it” → love of it

c. Punctuation
Avoid overusing commas:
“Money perhaps, is a necessary evil…” → Money, perhaps, is a necessary necessity for daily life or better Money is necessary for daily life.
“However, it is true that…” is fine, but some short sentences could be combined more smoothly.

d. Informal abbreviations
“commerce & exchange” → commerce and exchange

e. Pronouns / inclusiveness
“what he owns” sounds old-fashioned and not fully inclusive.
  - Better: what they own or what a person owns

Clarity and concision
Some sentences are too long and repetitive. For example:

> “The love of whatever represents the financial exchange between parties wreaks havoc in a nation, a family and even an individual life.”

This idea is good, but it could be clearer:
> Across cultures, greed for wealth can harm nations, families, and individuals alike.

Shorter, clearer sentences will improve the essay greatly.

Suggested revision of the opening
Your opening could be stronger and more formal. For example:

> Some people claim that money is the root of all evil, but this argument is mistaken. Money is a necessary part of modern life because it allows people to trade, save, and meet their needs. The real danger lies not in money itself, but in the excessive love of money, which can lead to selfishness, corruption, and suffering.

This introduction is direct, clear, and academic.

Final advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Organize body paragraphs by idea.
Use more formal academic language.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors.
Explain examples more specifically instead of listing many.

You already have a strong core argument. With better structure and more precise language, this could become a very effective argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear focus and an interesting argument: you challenge the common saying that “crime does not pay” by showing that crime can sometimes benefit criminals and even institutions. This is a strong argumentative idea because it questions a familiar belief rather than simply repeating it.

What you do well

Clear central claim  
Your thesis is understandable: the phrase is not always true and may be more “wishful thinking than a truism.” This gives the essay direction.

Logical progression  
The essay moves in a sensible order:
common use of the phrase,
question of why crime persists,
examples of criminals who still benefited,
argument that the prison industry also profits.

This structure helps the reader follow your reasoning.

Strong critical thinking  
You do more than describe; you analyze. The point that behavior usually continues because it is reinforced is especially effective because it adds a psychological explanation.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more direct earlier
Your main position becomes fully clear only near the end. In an argumentative essay, state your claim earlier, ideally in the introduction.

For example, after introducing the phrase, you could write something like:  
“Although the phrase suggests that criminal behavior always leads to loss, crime can sometimes produce financial, social, or institutional rewards; therefore, the saying is misleading.”

This will help readers understand your argument from the beginning.

Strengthen paragraph unity
Some paragraphs contain several ideas at once. Try to give each paragraph one main point.

Possible organization:
Introduction: meaning of the phrase + thesis  
Why the phrase is socially useful  
Why crime persists if it supposedly does not pay  
Examples of individual criminals who benefited  
How institutions profit from crime  
Conclusion

This will make your argument feel more controlled.

Use evidence more carefully
Your examples are interesting, but some need more precise support.

“the average amount of time that a convicted murderer spends in prison is 15 years” — this is a striking claim, but it needs a source if used in formal writing.
The examples of Michael Milken and Richard Nixon are persuasive, but you should explain more clearly how these examples prove your point.
Be careful with broad claims such as “for most, it is an issue of costs.” This sounds too general unless you have evidence.

In revision, either add sources or use more cautious language such as:
“some reports suggest…”
“in certain cases…”
“this example implies…”

Clarify wording and improve precision
There are several places where wording is awkward or slightly inaccurate.

Examples:

“a university teacher”  
  Better: “a university lecturer” or “an educator”, depending on what you mean.

“stock broker scams”  
  Better: “stock market fraud” or “securities fraud.”

“cost effective”  
  Better in this context: “worth the cost” or “a rational calculation for some criminals.”  
  “Cost-effective” is usually used for business decisions, so here it may sound too casual or imprecise.

“after, not only saves money…”  
  This is a grammar problem. Better:  
  “which not only saves money on placement but also generates local jobs and stimulates the economy.”

Watch grammar and sentence form
Your writing is generally advanced, but some sentences are too long or contain small errors.

Examples:

“The phrase plays an important role in reassuring people who play by the rules or admonishing potential criminals.”  
  This is good, but parallel structure could be smoother:  
  “The phrase plays an important role in reassuring law-abiding people and in admonishing potential criminals.”

“Behavior that is not reinforced in some way will not persist.”  
  Good sentence—clear and strong.

“For whom is there a payoff?”  
  Also effective.

“Some of them go on to argue for a local detention center, which, after, not only saves money…”  
  “after” should be removed.

Read your draft aloud during revision. This often helps you notice where sentences are too long or unnatural.

Consider tone and fairness
Your argument is strongest when it sounds analytical rather than sarcastic. Phrases like “country club prison” are vivid, but they may seem emotionally loaded. If your goal is academic argument, a more neutral tone may be stronger.

For example:
Instead of “country club prison,” use “a relatively lenient prison environment.”

This makes your writing sound more formal and credible.

Suggested revision goal
Try revising around this stronger line of argument:

The saying “crime does not pay” is socially useful as a moral warning, but it is not always factually true because some offenders gain financial or social benefits, and some institutions also profit from crime.

This version keeps your original idea but sharpens it.

Final comment
This is a thoughtful and mature essay with a strong argumentative core. Your biggest task in revision is not to change your main idea, but to present it more directly, support it more carefully, and polish the language for clarity and formality. You already have an interesting position; now make the reasoning tighter and the evidence more precise.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear position: you believe people should be financially rewarded for individual effort. The piece has a logical overall direction, and you use comparison between past and present to support your argument. Your voice is confident, and the conclusion returns well to the main idea of reward and motivation. To revise effectively, focus on clarity of argument, paragraph development, grammar, and word choice.

Strengths
Clear opinion: Your position is easy to identify from the introduction.
Relevant topic development: You connect survival in earlier times with reward for labor today.
Logical structure: The essay moves from past society, to modern society, to a hypothetical equal-reward society, and then to a conclusion.
Engaged tone: The writing shows conviction, which is good for argumentative essays.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis is currently:
> “I feel it is vital that in today's society men and women should be financially reward for their individual efforts.”

This contains a grammar mistake (“reward” should be “rewarded”), but more importantly, the idea could be sharper. Are you arguing that:
equal pay for unequal effort is unfair,
financial reward motivates progress,
society benefits when effort is rewarded?

Try combining these clearly.

Possible revision:
> In modern society, people should be financially rewarded according to their individual effort because this system encourages productivity, innovation, and personal responsibility.

B. Develop each body paragraph with one clear reason
Some paragraphs contain interesting ideas, but they are too general or move too quickly from one point to another.

For example, paragraph 4 imagines a society where everyone is treated equally regardless of effort. This is a useful counterexample, but the claims become extreme very quickly (“the world would become divided or even destroyed”). That weakens your argument because it sounds exaggerated.

Instead, explain step by step:
If all people receive the same rewards regardless of effort,
some people may feel less motivated to work hard,
productivity may decrease,
innovation may slow down,
and society may have fewer improvements.

That is more convincing than predicting destruction.

C. Use more specific examples
Your essay relies mostly on broad statements. To make your argument stronger, include concrete examples.

For instance:
workers who study longer or train more should earn more,
inventors or doctors contribute specialized skills,
businesses often reward employees who perform better.

Specific examples make your reasoning more persuasive.

Language and grammar issues

A. Sentence-level corrections
Here are some important corrections:

“financially reward” → “financially rewarded”
“the quest for fulfillment of these needs took up of majority” → “the quest to fulfill these needs took up the majority”
“To have clothing, you had to sew.” → correct, but could be smoother: “To obtain clothing, you had to sew.”
“machines to held us meet” → “machines to help us meet”
“treated equal” → “treated equally”
“as advanced of a society” → “as advanced a society”
“jealously” → “jealousy”
“selfish and hoarding” → “selfish and hoarding resources” or “selfish and possessive”
“lazy and stagnate people” → “lazy and stagnant people”

B. Avoid run-on or awkward sentences
This sentence is too long and awkward:
> “We have many machines to held us meet our food, clothing and shelter needs with little or no effort on our part, it would appear.”

You can split it:
> We now have many machines to help us meet our needs for food, clothing, and shelter. As a result, daily survival requires less physical effort than it did in the past.

C. Word choice
Some phrases are understandable but not natural in academic English:
“modern times” → “the modern era”
“paying someone to do their labor” → “paying others to perform physical labor”
“reward equaling effort” → “reward corresponding to effort”

Organization suggestions
A stronger structure might look like this:

Introduction
background on work and reward
clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1
In the past, survival depended directly on effort

Body Paragraph 2
In modern society, effort still matters, even if work is different

Body Paragraph 3
Rewarding effort encourages motivation, innovation, and responsibility

Body Paragraph 4
Refute the idea that equal reward regardless of effort is better

Conclusion
restate thesis
emphasize that financial reward supports both individual and social progress

Final advice
Your essay has a solid foundation, but it needs more precision, evidence, and careful language control. When revising:
write a clearer thesis,
make each paragraph focus on one main reason,
replace exaggerated claims with logical explanation,
add specific examples,
correct grammar and awkward phrasing.

You have good ideas here. With revision, this could become a much stronger and more persuasive argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear engagement with a difficult social question: whether people can or should be paid according to their “contribution to society.” You present a strong overall position—that equal compensation is impossible because “contribution” is subjective—and you support this with examples about inherited wealth, workers, and doctors. The writing also has a thoughtful, reflective tone. For revision, focus on making your argument more precise, your organization clearer, and your language more accurate.

Main strengths

Clear central idea:  
Your essay consistently argues that contribution to society cannot be measured objectively, so equal compensation is not realistic.

Interesting examples:  
The discussion of 1920s America, inherited wealth, migrant workers, and doctors gives the essay range and makes it more engaging.

Academic tone:  
You generally write in a formal style, which suits argumentative writing.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more direct and specific
Your first paragraph introduces the issue well, but the exact argument could be stated more clearly. At the moment, the essay moves between several ideas:
salary depends on employers’ opinions,
contribution to society is subjective,
wealthy people may contribute little,
democracy/capitalism allows unequal outcomes.

Try to combine these into one focused thesis.

Possible revised thesis:  
*Although many people believe workers should be paid according to their contribution to society, this is impossible in practice because social contribution is subjective, economic systems reward market value rather than fairness, and people do not begin life with equal opportunities.*

This would give the essay a clearer direction.

B. Improve paragraph unity and logical development
Some paragraphs contain good ideas but do not fully connect them to the main claim.

For example:
The paragraph about the 1920s is interesting, but you need to explain more explicitly how it proves that compensation cannot be equal to social contribution.
The paragraph about wealthy people asks moral questions (“do they have the right…?”), but your essay’s main argument is about whether equal compensation is possible. Stay focused on that question.

A stronger structure might be:
Introduction + thesis
Why “contribution to society” is subjective
Why market systems reward wealth/investment, not social value
Why unequal opportunity makes “fair pay” difficult
Conclusion

C. Use stronger transitions
At times, the essay feels like a series of related reflections rather than a tightly connected argument.

Add phrases such as:
*First,*  
*Moreover,*  
*For example,*  
*However,*  
*As a result,*  
*Therefore,*  

These will help the reader follow your reasoning.

Language and grammar issues

There are several grammar and word-choice errors. Below are some important corrections:

“In the 1920s in American” → “In the 1920s in America”

  “live off the interest of investors” should be “live off the interest from investments”

  This is a sentence fragment/punctuation problem. Better:  
  “What about people who work very hard, yet seem to be paid very poorly?”
“A migrant work” → “A migrant worker”
“The answer this question” → “The answer to this question”
“Democracry” → “democracy”
“in  life” → remove extra space: “in life”
“responsibly” → “responsibility”
“make much too money” → “make far too much money”
“we cannot give pay equal to contribution to society” → more natural:
  “we cannot ensure pay that is equal to each person’s contribution to society.”

Also be careful with quotation marks:
“equally'” should be “equally.” or simply remove quotation marks.

Some claims are too broad, for example:
*“everyone seems to think that doctors make much too money…”*

This is not really true and weakens your credibility. Instead write:
*“Many people believe that doctors are overpaid…”*

Choose more precise vocabulary
A few phrases sound dramatic but vague, such as:
*“live as parasites”*
*“search of a new thrill”*

These can work, but in an academic essay you should explain them carefully or use more neutral language.

Without definitions, readers may interpret these ideas differently.

Final advice for revision

Organize each body paragraph around one main point connected directly to the thesis.
Edit carefully for grammar and word choice, especially singular/plural forms, spelling, and sentence structure.

Overall, this is an intelligent and thoughtful essay with strong potential. Your ideas are interesting and mature; now the main task is to present them in a more organized and precise way.

Your essay has a clear argumentative position: you challenge the common saying “crime does not pay” and argue that, in America, crime can bring profit, fame, or other benefits. Your essay is also well organized at the paragraph level: introduction, three body paragraphs, and conclusion. That structure helps the reader follow your ideas.

What is working well

Good use of examples

Confident argumentative voice

Some examples do not fully support your claim, or they are historically questionable.

Boston Tea Party: This was illegal, but it was also a political protest. It does not clearly prove that crime is a “profitable profession.”
Benedict Arnold: His case is confusing in this essay because readers may not understand how it supports your argument.

For instance, in the history paragraph, after mentioning Prohibition and Las Vegas, explain:
who profited,
how they profited,
and why this shows that crime “pays.”

A strong body paragraph should follow this pattern:
topic sentence -> example -> explanation -> link back to thesis.

Some sentences sound too casual or exaggerated for an academic essay.

Examples:
“National Enquirer brand of fame”
“if one has the right agent”

Grammar and word choice
“There are many persons” -> There are many people
“In fact one needs only” -> In fact, one needs only
“the vents shaping America's early history” -> the events shaping America’s early history
“accused -- Divine Brown” -> accused—Divine Brown
“speak-eases” -> speakeasies
“Los Vegas” -> Las Vegas
“monetary rewards gained” -> financial rewards gained

“Finally, is the issue” -> Finally, there is the issue
“should they become arrested again” -> if they are arrested again
“ever present laws” -> ever-present laws
“tes.” -> delete this typo

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear opinion about feminism, gender roles, and modernization. You also try to discuss both past and present views of women’s roles, which gives your essay scope. To improve your work, focus on three main areas: argument quality, organization, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear position: The reader can understand that you think women should have freedom of choice, but you are critical of some feminist ideas.
Relevant topic sentences: Most paragraphs introduce a new idea, such as workplace issues, combat roles, harassment, or traditional roles.
Willingness to engage with a controversial issue: This is good for argumentative writing, because it shows you are trying to form a position.

Main problems with the argument
Your essay has a strong opinion, but the argument needs to be more balanced, logical, and supported by evidence.

a) Avoid generalizations
Some claims are too broad, for example:
“Women just can't fight in a war very well.”
“Many employers are the victims.”
“Traditional roles were a good thing.”

These statements sound absolute, but you do not provide proof. In academic writing, avoid saying that all women or all men are the same. Instead, use cautious language:
“Some people argue that…”
“In certain cases…”
“Research suggests that…”

b) Support claims with evidence
Many points need facts, examples, or sources. For instance:
pay inequality
sexual harassment cases
divorce rates
women’s stress and heart attacks
women in combat roles

Without evidence, the essay sounds based only on personal belief. In revision, add statistics, studies, or specific examples from reliable sources.

c) Separate opinion from prejudice
Some sentences may offend readers because they sound biased rather than reasoned, especially the paragraph about combat. Academic argument should explain *why* you think something, using evidence, not assumptions about women’s weakness or rape risk alone. If you want to discuss physical differences, do so carefully and objectively.

d) Clarify your thesis
Your main argument is not fully clear. Are you arguing that:
feminism helped women but has gone too far, or
women should be free to choose traditional or modern roles, or
men and women are equal in value but different in ability?

These ideas all appear in the essay. Choose one main thesis and make sure each paragraph supports it.

Organization and structure
Your essay would be stronger with a more formal structure.

Suggested structure:
Introduction
Introduce the topic.
Give brief background.
End with a clear thesis.

Body Paragraph 1
Historical problems women faced.
Give evidence.

Body Paragraph 2
Positive effects of feminism.
Give examples.

Body Paragraph 3
Your criticism: pressure on women to reject traditional roles.
Explain carefully and support with evidence.

Body Paragraph 4
Workplace issues and fairness today.
Discuss both women’s and employers’ concerns.

Conclusion
Restate your main point.
End with a balanced final thought.

Also, some paragraphs jump from one idea to another. For example, the fourth paragraph moves from equality to religion, nature, jobs, divorce, stress, and children. Try to keep one main idea per paragraph.

Language issues to correct
There are frequent grammar, vocabulary, and word-choice errors. Here are some important examples:

“societies role” → “society’s role”
“work place” → “workplace”
“verses” → “versus”
“their piers” → “their peers”
“The are not strong enough” → “They are not strong enough”
“the woman” → “women”
“being more stressful” → “experiencing more stress”
“they to do” → “them to do”

Also, avoid informal phrases such as:
“gone is the age”
“these people were crazy”
“a lot of”

Use more academic alternatives:
“In the past…”
“I disagree with this position because…”
“many,” “numerous,” or “a significant number of”

Tone and academic style
Your tone is often too conversational or emotional for an academic essay. For example:

Make each paragraph support that thesis.
Remove sweeping generalizations.
Add evidence for important claims.
Use a more formal, respectful tone.
Edit carefully for grammar and word choice.

You have a good starting point and a clear interest in the topic. With better organization, stronger evidence, and more careful language, your essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear opinion: crime may seem rewarding at first, but it leads to worse behavior and more serious consequences. This is a strong main idea, and your example of the store robbery helps show how a small crime can become much more dangerous. You also write with energy and conviction, which gives your essay a strong voice.

To improve the essay, focus on organization, formal tone, grammar, and clarity.

Strengths
Clear position: You strongly argue that crime does not pay.
Relevant example: The robbery story supports your point that crime can escalate.
Engaging voice: Your writing feels personal and passionate.

Main areas to revise

A. Make the thesis more direct
Your introduction is interesting, but the main argument should be stated more clearly and formally.

Current idea:
crime may bring short-term rewards
criminals may become bolder
eventually crime leads to punishment and harm

A stronger thesis could be:
Although crime may offer temporary benefits, it often encourages more dangerous behavior, harms innocent people, and results in serious punishment.

This gives your essay a clear roadmap.

B. Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs are very long and contain too many ideas. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce topic
   - State thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Crime gives only short-term rewards
   - Explain how early success may tempt people to continue

Body Paragraph 2: Crime escalates
   - Show how small crimes can lead to bigger and more dangerous ones

Body Paragraph 3: Serious consequences
   - Use your robbery example here

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis
   - Emphasize that crime harms both criminals and innocent people

C. Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too conversational for an argumentative essay. Examples:

“a cool million”
“run like hell”
“guess what happens”
“Sounds like trouble to me”

These expressions are vivid, but they sound informal. In academic writing, use more neutral language.

For example:
“a large amount of money” instead of “a cool million”
“run away quickly” instead of “run like hell”
“As a result” instead of “guess what happens”

D. Shorten and clarify sentences
Many sentences are extremely long and difficult to follow. This makes your argument less effective. Break long sentences into two or three shorter ones.

For example, this sentence:
> “For an individual that starts out doing small crimes... the cycle goes on and on.”

could become:
A person who begins with minor crimes may feel encouraged each time they are not caught. As a result, they may take greater risks and commit more serious offenses. This pattern can become a cycle.

Shorter sentences improve clarity and grammar.

Grammar and language points

A. Word choice and spelling
Check these errors:
committs → commits
Its's → It’s
indivudual → individual
deterent → deterrent
whrn → when
convenient store → convenience store
strats → starts
hurridly → hurriedly
ths → this
persistant → persistent
lets say → let’s say
just happen → just happened
simply robbery → simple robbery

B. Relative pronouns
You often write “a person that” or “someone that.” For people, “who” is usually better:
A person who commits a crime...
Someone who needs punishment...

C. Subject-verb agreement
Example:
someone could definitely headed for destruction  
  → someone could definitely be headed for destruction

D. Possessives and contractions
Be careful with:
Its's → It’s
Lets → Let’s
someone's life is correct

E. Consistency in point of view
You move between I, you, someone, and they. In formal essays, try to stay consistent. It is fine to use people, criminals, or an individual throughout.

Development of ideas
Your robbery example is vivid, but it is too long and repetitive. The reader understands the point before the story ends. Try to summarize it more efficiently.

For instance:
If two men rob a convenience store and one of them fires a gun while escaping, the crime becomes far more serious. Even if the child is not killed, the offenders could face charges such as armed robbery, assault with a deadly weapon, and attempted murder. This shows how quickly a “simple” crime can escalate and harm innocent people.

This version is shorter, clearer, and more persuasive.

Final advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis.
Divide the essay into logical paragraphs.
Replace informal expressions with formal language.
Shorten long sentences.
Proofread carefully for spelling and grammar.

You have a strong central message and good supporting material. With better organization and more formal language, this essay can become much more powerful and convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: money itself is neutral, but people’s attitudes toward it can lead to harmful behavior. This is a strong argument because it moves beyond the simple saying in the title and adds nuance. You also use thoughtful ideas such as money representing power, status, and survival. These are good foundations for an argumentative essay.

Strengths
Clear topic: You focus consistently on the relationship between money, power, greed, and evil.
Thoughtful position: You do not simply repeat the saying “money is the root of all evil”; instead, you argue that human desire and greed are the real problem.
Logical progression: The essay moves from defining money, to discussing its influence, to explaining greed.

Main areas to improve

Make your thesis more precise
Your essay suggests two slightly different claims:
money is the root of evil
money itself is neutral, but people’s attitudes make it harmful

These ideas conflict a little. Since your body paragraphs support the second idea more strongly, your thesis should clearly state that money is not evil by itself; greed and obsession with what money can buy cause evil actions.

A possible revised thesis:
Although money provides security and social power, it is not inherently evil; rather, greed and unhealthy desire for what money can buy can lead people to act immorally.

Improve paragraph development
Your ideas are interesting, but some paragraphs are too general. Try to develop each paragraph around one main point with explanation or examples.

For example:
Paragraph 1: Explain money as power and why people value it.
Paragraph 2: Show how obsession with money can become destructive.
Paragraph 3: Explain greed and how it affects moral choices.

Add one or two specific examples in each body paragraph. For instance, instead of only listing “drug use, gambling and prostitution,” explain how the desire for money may push people toward exploitation, corruption, or dishonesty.

Strengthen the conclusion
The last sentence feels incomplete and too brief for a conclusion. A conclusion should restate your argument clearly and leave the reader with a final thought.

For example:
In conclusion, money should not be blamed for evil actions because it is only a tool. What turns money into a destructive force is human greed, addiction, and the willingness to sacrifice values in order to obtain more of it.

Language and grammar corrections

Here are some important sentence-level issues to revise:

Opening sentence
Current:
Money is the root of all evil is an ancient saying-- but its truth applies to all generations.

Better:
“Money is the root of all evil” is an ancient saying, but its message still applies to every generation.

Why:
Put the saying in quotation marks.
Use a comma instead of “--” in formal writing.
“Truth” is possible, but “message” sounds smoother here.

Article and word form errors
Current:
The key is in the definition of term money
Better:
The key lies in the definition of the term money.

Current:
Ones's attitude about money
Better:
One’s attitude toward money

Current:
The list of addictions people develop are endless
Better:
The list of addictions people develop is endless

Current:
when the need for money becomes the driving force
Better:
When the need for money becomes the driving force

Sentence structure and punctuation
Some sentences are long and contain comma splices. For example:

Current:
Enough money gives you the power to get whatever you need to survive, lack of money may mean you can't survive.

Better:
Enough money gives people the power to obtain what they need to survive. By contrast, a lack of money may threaten survival.

This revision is clearer and more formal.

Possessive and plural forms
Current:
a greedy persons life
Better:
a greedy person’s life

Current:
Values and ethics play very little importance in structure of a greedy persons life
Better:
Values and ethics play very little role in the structure of a greedy person’s life.

Final sentence
Current:
Money does good or evil--depending on who has the money
Better:
Money can be used for good or evil, depending on the character of the person who possesses it.

Style suggestions
Avoid repeating “the need for” too often in one paragraph.
Use more formal vocabulary where possible: for example, “obtain” instead of “get,” “immoral” instead of “evil or not” in some places.
Be careful with capitalization: When should begin a sentence with a capital letter.
Use quotation marks correctly for sayings or well-known expressions.

Revision advice
When you rewrite, try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce the saying
   - Explain that money represents power
   - End with a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1
   - Money helps people survive and gain status
   - Explain why it is valuable

Body Paragraph 2
   - Money is neutral, but obsession with it creates problems
   - Give examples

Body Paragraph 3
   - Greed causes people to ignore ethics
   - Explain consequences

Conclusion
   - Restate the thesis
   - Emphasize that people, not money itself, are responsible for evil actions

Overall, your essay shows strong thinking and a solid argumentative direction. The next step is to make the thesis fully consistent, develop your examples more clearly, and revise grammar and sentence structure for accuracy and formality.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear personal voice, and your essay shows strong engagement with the topic. Your main position is understandable: you support some goals of feminism, but you believe traditional dating manners and chivalry have declined. This gives your essay a recognizable argument and makes it feel sincere. For revision, the main areas to improve are focus, organization, tone, and support for claims.

Strengths
Clear opinion: Readers can easily identify your stance.
Personal experience: Referring to dating in the 1960s and 1990s gives your essay an authentic perspective.
Acknowledgment of complexity: You do not reject all feminist ideas; this makes the argument more balanced.

Main revision priorities

A. Strengthen your thesis
Right now, your central claim appears gradually, but it should be stated more directly in the introduction. Try to express your argument in one sentence.

For example:
“Although feminism has helped women gain important rights and opportunities, it has also contributed to confusion about traditional dating roles and the decline of chivalry.”

This gives the essay a clearer direction from the start.

B. Improve organization
Your essay has many long sentences and several ideas in one paragraph. A stronger structure would help the reader follow your argument.

You could organize it like this:
Introduction: Present the topic and thesis.
Paragraph 1: Explain the positive achievements of feminism.
Paragraph 2: Describe how dating norms have changed.
Paragraph 3: Explain why you value traditional chivalry.
Conclusion: Restate your argument clearly.

This structure will make your reasoning easier to follow.

C. Be careful with generalizations
Some claims are too broad, such as:
“Men were gentlemen in the 60’s”
“women in general are offended”
“because of the feminists in this country”

These statements may weaken your essay because they suggest that all men or all women behave the same way. Academic argument is usually stronger when it uses more precise language.

Instead of saying:
“Women in general are offended if you open doors for them”

You might say:
“Some women may interpret traditional gestures such as opening doors as unnecessary or outdated.”

This sounds more fair and persuasive.

D. Use a more academic tone
Your essay currently sounds very conversational, which gives it personality, but an argumentative essay usually needs a more formal style. Informal phrases such as:
“Believe me”
“this is bunk”
“you men out there”

can be replaced with more academic wording.

For example:
“Believe me” → “In my experience”
“this is bunk” → “I find this view unconvincing”

Also, phrases like “opposite-sexed dinner date” sound awkward. A simpler phrase like “date” or “romantic partner” is more natural.

E. Support your claims with evidence
At the moment, most of your support comes from personal experience and anecdotes from friends. Personal experience can be useful, but argumentative essays are stronger when they also include broader evidence, such as:
social research on changing gender roles
surveys about dating expectations
statistics about women’s employment and household labor

If this is for an academic class, adding even one or two outside sources would make your argument much stronger.

Sentence-level issues

Grammar and word choice
A few word choices are unnatural or incorrect:
“in the realm of date, courting” → “in the realm of dating, courtship”
“stigmatism” → “stigma”
“traditional techniques” → “traditional expectations” or “traditional manners”
“different now (1990’s)” → “different in the 1990s”

Punctuation and sentence length
Many sentences are very long and contain multiple ideas. This can confuse the reader. Try breaking long sentences into two or three shorter ones.

For example, this sentence:
“While I think that there are always exceptions to this rule, whether you are in the 60's or the 90's, however, the overall general attitude...”

could be revised as:
“Of course, there were exceptions in both the 1960s and the 1990s. However, I believe the general attitude of men toward women in the 1960s was more respectful and marriage-oriented.”

This version is clearer and easier to read.

Content suggestion
One important issue to consider is that your essay sometimes assumes feminism is the main cause of changing dating behavior. To make the argument stronger, you might acknowledge other possible causes too, such as:
changing social values
economic pressure
media influence
increased awareness of consent and harassment

Even if you still argue that feminism played a role, this acknowledgment would make your essay seem more thoughtful and balanced.

Final advice
For your revision, focus on:
Writing a clear thesis
Dividing ideas into well-structured paragraphs
Using a more formal tone
Avoiding overgeneralizations
Adding evidence beyond personal experience

You have a strong starting point because your position is clear and personally meaningful. With a more organized and academic presentation, your argument will become much more effective and convincing.
