Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: crime has serious costs, not only for victims but also for offenders, families, taxpayers, and communities. You also try to look at the issue from several angles, which is a strength. To revise successfully, focus on three main areas: clarity of thesis, organization, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths
Relevant topic: You discuss social, financial, and moral consequences of crime.
Multiple perspectives: You consider criminals, victims, taxpayers, and property owners.
Serious tone: Your essay sounds formal and appropriate for an argumentative piece.

Main Issue: Unclear thesis
Your introduction begins with “The phrase ,” but the phrase itself is missing. Because of that, the reader does not know exactly what argument you are responding to. I assume the phrase is something like “crime does not pay.” If so, state it clearly and directly.

Also, your thesis should present your main claim in one sentence. At the moment, the introduction is general and somewhat repetitive.

Suggested revision of the thesis:
> The saying “crime does not pay” is true because crime brings financial, emotional, and social costs to offenders, victims, and the wider community.

This gives your essay a clear direction.

Organization and development
Your body paragraphs each introduce a different consequence of crime, which is a good start. However, the logic can be improved.

Current paragraph ideas:
Cost of incarceration
Moral/emotional consequences for criminals
Effects on law-abiding citizens and property values
Long-term stigma after conviction

These ideas are useful, but you should make the structure more explicit. Start each paragraph with a topic sentence that links back to your thesis.

Example:
Financial costs to society: imprisonment and taxes
Emotional and moral costs to offenders: guilt and remorse
Costs to communities: falling property values and reduced opportunities
Long-term personal consequences: criminal record, jobs, housing

Also, some claims need more support. For example, “for the most part” criminals feel remorse is too broad. Not all offenders feel this way. Be careful not to make universal statements unless you can prove them.

Instead, write:
> Many offenders may experience guilt or regret after recognizing the harm they caused.

This is more accurate and academic.

Style and argumentation
Your essay is closer to an explanatory essay than a strongly argumentative one. To make it more argumentative:
Clearly state your position in the introduction.
Use each paragraph to prove that position.
End with a conclusion that restates the main argument.

You currently do not have a concluding paragraph. Add one to unify the essay.

Example conclusion:
> In conclusion, crime does not pay because its consequences extend far beyond the act itself. Offenders may lose freedom, future opportunities, and peace of mind, while victims and communities suffer financial and emotional harm. For these reasons, crime creates losses for everyone involved.

Grammar, word choice, and mechanics
There are several language errors that affect readability. Below are common patterns to fix.

A. Word choice / spelling
to often overlooked → too often overlooked
freinds → friends
acquantances → acquaintances
so to will → so too will
oppurtunity → opportunity
their is no escape → there is no escape

B. Sentence punctuation
Many sentences are too long or missing commas. For example:

Original:
> The individual who commits a crime, if and when they are caught will in most cases be faced with the loss of his or her freedom.

Improved:
> An individual who commits a crime, if caught, will in most cases face the loss of freedom.

This version is shorter and clearer.

C. Capitalization
so even though should begin with So if it starts a sentence.

D. Agreement and pronouns
Be careful with singular/plural consistency:
The phrase has been repeated many times but have you ever → The phrase has been repeated many times, but have you ever
the one's own freedom → one’s own freedom

You also switch between they and his or her. Choose one system and use it consistently. In modern academic English, singular they is usually acceptable:
> If individuals are caught, they may lose their freedom.

Revision priorities
When you rewrite, do these steps in order:
Insert the missing phrase and write a clear thesis.
Add a conclusion.
Improve topic sentences for each body paragraph.
Shorten long sentences and correct punctuation.
Fix spelling and grammar errors.

Final comment
You have a solid idea and several relevant points. The next step is to make your argument more precise and polished. If you clarify your thesis, organize the essay more clearly, and correct the language errors, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear voice and strong personal engagement with the topic. You sound confident, and the contrast between “organized” and “unorganized” crime gives the essay a recognizable structure. However, to make this a stronger academic argumentative essay, you need to improve three main areas: thesis and focus, evidence and logic, and academic style.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your main claim is not fully clear at the beginning. You seem to argue that organized crime is more profitable and more protected than unorganized crime, but this idea appears gradually rather than in a direct thesis statement.

Try stating your argument clearly in the introduction. For example:

> This essay argues that organized crime is more profitable and harder to control than unorganized crime because it depends on long-term cooperation, financial power, and corruption.

This will help the reader understand your purpose from the start.

Strengthen the logic of your argument
Some of your claims are interesting, but they need more careful reasoning.

a) Definition problems
You describe “unorganized crime” as crimes such as robbery, assault, rape, and theft, and later call them “petty offenses.” This is problematic. Assault and rape are serious violent crimes, not petty offenses. Be careful with classification.

Also, your definition of organized crime is too broad:
> “two or more individuals working together for the same cause”

That could describe many kinds of crime, even a small robbery. In academic writing, organized crime usually refers to structured, ongoing criminal activity aimed at profit and often supported by corruption or violence. Use a more precise definition.

b) Unsupported generalizations
Several statements are too absolute, such as:
“The police and other government officials are willing to overlook the illegal acts…”
“This activity will continue until the end of time…”
“with money, a person can have ANYTHING they want.”

These claims are exaggerated and need qualification. In academic writing, avoid saying “all,” “always,” or “definitely” unless you can prove it. Use softer, more accurate language such as:
“in some cases”
“often”
“may”
“can contribute to”

Use stronger evidence
You mention your class as a source of knowledge, but the essay needs specific evidence, not only personal opinion. For example:
statistics from reliable sources
definitions from criminology
examples of organized crime operations
evidence about corruption or illegal gambling

You include one statistic about illegal gambling, but it is not cited and may be outdated or unclear. In revision, support each major point with evidence.

A useful structure could be:
Define unorganized crime
Define organized crime
Compare profitability
Explain the role of corruption and networks
Conclude with your main argument

Adopt a more academic tone
Your current tone is very personal and conversational. That works for informal writing, but in academic argument it can weaken credibility.

Examples:
“save the best for last”
“MONEY!”
“BIG sum of money”
“Now that you have read this and may be trying to figure me out…”

These phrases sound dramatic rather than analytical. Also, the final self-introduction does not belong in the essay. It shifts attention away from your argument and should be removed.

Try using a more formal tone:
replace “To me” with “In this essay” or simply state the point directly
avoid capital letters for emphasis
avoid direct conversation with the reader

Improve organization and paragraph unity
Your essay has three body sections, which is good, but some paragraphs contain too many ideas. For example, the organized crime paragraph includes:
definition
examples
corruption
gambling
police salaries
social values

These could be separated into clearer paragraphs. Each paragraph should have one main idea with supporting details.

Language and sentence-level revision
Your English is generally understandable, but some sentences need correction for grammar and word choice.

Examples:
“both sides of the track” → probably both sides of the tracks
“must be very good” → unclear; perhaps must plan carefully
“less educated” → better: may have less formal education
“which takes time. The persons must be patient and will to deal with others.”  
  → which takes time. Those involved must be patient and willing to work with others.
“is not exclusive to” → is not limited to
“21 year old” → 21-year-old

Also watch punctuation. You overuse dashes and informal emphasis. Simpler sentence structure will make your ideas clearer.

What to do next
For revision, I suggest this plan:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Remove the personal biography at the end.
Use more precise definitions of both types of crime.
Replace emotional or exaggerated claims with evidence-based analysis.
Separate large paragraphs into smaller, focused ones.
Edit for formal academic style and grammar.

Final comment
You have a strong topic and a confident writing voice. The next step is to shift from personal opinion to academic argument. If you support your claims with evidence, define your terms more carefully, and use a more formal tone, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay tells a vivid personal story and clearly supports your main idea: crime does not pay. The narrative is emotionally strong, and your reader can understand how deeply the burglary affected you. You also do a good job showing a change in your thinking—from fear and doubt to renewed trust in the legal system. That gives the essay a clear argumentative purpose.

Strengths

Strong personal evidence  
   Your experience makes the argument convincing because it is specific and real.

Clear message  
   The repeated idea from your father—“crime doesn’t pay”—creates unity across the essay.

Emotional impact  
   Details such as returning home from the doctor, finding everything gone, and feeling afraid to be alone make the essay powerful.

Main Areas to Revise

Make the thesis clearer in the introduction
Your first paragraph introduces the idea, but the sentence structure is confusing:
“I always believed them and they saying that .”

This part needs rewriting. End the introduction with a clear thesis statement, for example:

My parents taught me that crime does not pay, and after my home was robbed, I learned that justice may be slow, but criminals can still be held responsible.

This gives the reader a clear argument from the beginning.

Improve organization
The essay is mostly chronological, which works well, but some paragraphs are too long and contain several ideas at once. Try this structure:

Paragraph 1: Introduction and thesis  
Paragraph 2: The robbery and immediate shock  
Paragraph 3: Recovery of some property and hope for justice  
Paragraph 4: Court process and emotional difficulty  
Paragraph 5: Plea bargain issue and your response  
Paragraph 6: Conclusion and lesson learned  

This will make your argument easier to follow.

Strengthen the argumentative focus
Right now, much of the essay is narrative. Since this is an argumentative essay, connect each event more directly to your claim. After important moments, explain how they support your point.

For example:
After describing the robbery, explain that criminals often cause long-term harm not only by stealing property but also by destroying a victim’s sense of safety.
After the sentencing, explain why punishment proves that crime ultimately brings consequences.

Work on grammar and sentence structure
There are several grammar errors that make some sentences hard to understand. Focus especially on:

a. Verb forms
“they saying” → they said
“they stolen property came from” → the stolen property came from
“told be” → told me
“one women” → one woman

b. Articles and possessives
“my husbands firearms” → my husband’s firearms
“call for police help” → call the police or call for help from the police
“prosecutors office” → the prosecutor’s office

c. Repetition and wordiness
Some sentences repeat the same idea too much. For example:
“everything, I mean everything” is emotionally effective, but later the long list plus “everything” again is a bit too much. You can shorten it for stronger style.

Correct punctuation
You need more commas, apostrophes, and sometimes sentence separation.

Examples:
“My stereo, CD's, telephone, jewelry, my husbands firearms, clothes, furniture...”  
  → My stereo, CDs, telephone, jewelry, my husband’s firearms, clothes, and furniture...

“This upset me, because I felt...”  
  Usually no comma is needed before because here:
  → This upset me because I felt...

Improve word choice
A few expressions sound unnatural in English:
“made it out of the house” is okay in speech, but in formal writing you might say escaped before I could call the police
“for police help” → for help from the police or the police
“these awful people” is understandable, but in academic writing the burglars or the defendants may sound stronger and more formal

Example of a Revised Sentence
Original:
“We would have to go to court and testify that it was our home they stolen property came from and that these people did not have permission to be in our house.”

Revised:
We had to go to court and testify that the stolen property came from our home and that these people did not have permission to enter our house.

Conclusion Advice
Your conclusion is effective because it returns to the father’s lesson. To make it stronger, restate the broader argument:
crime may bring temporary gain,
but it leads to punishment, prison, and lasting consequences.

For example:
Although the burglars may have profited briefly from what they stole, they ultimately lost their freedom. My experience taught me that crime may seem rewarding at first, but in the end, it does not pay.

Final Comment
This essay has a strong foundation: a clear central idea, real emotional power, and a meaningful conclusion. Your main revision goals should be clarity, grammar, organization, and stronger argumentation. With careful editing, this can become a very effective essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that feminism originally aimed at equality, but that some extreme uses of the term have damaged its public image. This gives your essay a strong central idea, and you also show personal engagement with the topic, which makes the writing sound committed and sincere.

Main strengths

Clear thesis and purpose  
You consistently argue that feminism’s original goal was equality rather than female dominance.

Awareness of complexity  
You do not simply praise or attack feminism; instead, you argue that it has done much good while also being misrepresented or misused. This balanced approach is a good feature of argumentative writing.

Use of historical perspective  
Referring to the origins of the movement helps give your essay a broader context.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the argument more academic
At the moment, the essay relies heavily on generalizations and emotionally loaded language, which weakens credibility.

For example:
“dirty words”
“big chunky lesbian who hates all men”
“a bunch of whining women”
“all men are evil”

These phrases sound informal, stereotypical, and judgmental. In academic argument, it is better to describe ideas more neutrally, even when criticizing them.

Better approach:  
Instead of repeating stereotypes, you could write:
“Feminism is sometimes unfairly associated with anti-male attitudes.”
“Some critics view certain radical forms of feminism as hostile rather than egalitarian.”

This keeps your meaning but sounds more mature and persuasive.

B. Support claims with evidence
Many of your points are stated as facts, but no evidence is provided. For example:
“both ‘feminist’ and ‘feminism’ are dirty words”
“men are not taking the feminist cause seriously anymore”
“some feminist groups will take cases to an extreme”

Ask yourself:
Who says this?
Is this true in all places?
Can I give an example, statistic, or source?

To strengthen the essay, add:
historical evidence
examples of feminist achievements
specific examples of “radical” positions if you want to criticize them
perhaps a distinction between mainstream feminism and extreme fringe views

Without evidence, the argument can sound too sweeping.

C. Avoid overgeneralization
Your essay sometimes treats large groups as if they all think the same way:
“outsiders now see the feminist movement as…”
“feminists are becoming anarchists”
“many women… have separated themselves from modern day feminism”

These are very broad claims. Use cautious academic language such as:
“some people perceive…”
“certain critics argue…”
“some women feel that…”

This makes your argument more accurate and more persuasive.

Organization and development

Your essay has a recognizable structure, but the paragraphs could be more focused.

Suggested structure:
Introduction  
   Introduce the debate and present your thesis clearly.
Origins of feminism  
   Explain its historical goal of equality.
Achievements of feminism  
   Voting rights, equal pay awareness, recognition of violence and discrimination.
Problems with public perception / radical misuse  
   Explain how some extreme voices may affect the movement’s image.
Conclusion  
   Restate that feminism should return to its core principle of equality.

This would help the essay feel more logically developed.

Language issues to revise

Grammar and word choice
A few examples:

“by this I do not mean” → “By this, I do not mean”
“A women is” → “A woman is”
“The equality of woman” → “The equality of women”
“to an extremes” → “to extremes”
“what wonders the feminist movement has done” → “what the feminist movement has achieved”
“lay the foundation” → better here: “form the foundation”

Sentence control
Some sentences are too long and contain several ideas. Break them into shorter sentences for clarity.

For example:
> “Consequently, the feminist movement has become a shelter for the radical women to get angry under, to shout from and to feel safe within.”

This could become:
> “Consequently, some people see the feminist movement as providing a platform for more radical voices. As a result, these voices can overshadow the movement’s original goal of equality.”

Capitalization and punctuation
Start sentences with capital letters: “By this…”
Be careful with commas after introductory phrases.
Avoid unnecessary quotation marks around common terms unless you are discussing the words themselves.

Most important revision advice

When revising, focus on these three priorities:

Use neutral, academic language instead of stereotypes or insults.
Support major claims with examples or evidence.
Clarify that you are criticizing some forms or perceptions of feminism, not all feminists.

Final comment

You have a strong opinion and a clear argument, which is an excellent starting point. The next step is to make the essay more precise, balanced, and evidence-based. If you revise the tone and strengthen the support for your claims, your essay will become much more convincing in an academic context.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear central idea: the Women’s Liberation Movement was damaged less by its radical members than by how outsiders perceived and stereotyped the movement. This is a strong argumentative position, and your conclusion returns to that claim effectively. You also show awareness of the difference between public image and actual goals, which gives the essay an interesting focus.

To revise, I recommend working on thesis clarity, historical accuracy/support, organization, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengthen the thesis
Your main argument appears in pieces across the essay, especially in these sentences:

“the fault does not lie with the radical members of the movement, but rather, in the perception of those observing the movement.”
“so-called feminists have not in and of themselves harmed the Women’s movement. However, the popular perception of them has.”

This is a good claim, but it should appear once, clearly, in the introduction. Right now the first paragraph is broad and general. Try ending the introduction with a direct thesis, for example:

Suggested revision:  
*Although radical figures often become the public face of social movements, the Women’s Liberation Movement in America was harmed less by its activists themselves than by outsiders who reduced the movement to stereotypes and ignored its ordinary supporters and practical goals.*

This gives the reader a clear roadmap.

Improve organization and paragraph development
The essay has a logical direction, but some paragraphs move too quickly from one idea to another. A stronger structure would be:

Introduction: explain stereotypes in social movements and state thesis.
Historical background: explain how early feminists were ridiculed or ignored.
1960s–70s revival: show how media/public attention focused on radicals.
Continuing effects: explain how these stereotypes still affect views of feminism.
Conclusion: restate the main point.

Also, each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that supports the thesis. For example:

*From its earliest years, the women’s movement was judged more by its most controversial figures than by its broader aims.*
*The same pattern reappeared in the late 1960s, when media attention emphasized radical images over ordinary women’s experiences.*

Use stronger evidence and be careful with examples
Your essay makes broad claims, but it needs more evidence. At the moment, some examples are either unclear or historically questionable.

Examples to reconsider:
Emily Dickinson is not usually identified as a leader in the feminist movement. If you want an example of a less radical or less publicly controversial women’s rights figure, you might choose someone more directly connected to women’s activism.
George Sand was a significant writer and unconventional public figure, but because she was French, her connection to the American Women’s Liberation Movement is weak.
“Stein” is unclear. If you mean Gloria Steinem, use her full name.

Try to use specific and accurate evidence, such as:
media portrayals of “bra-burning” feminists,
Betty Friedan and *The Feminine Mystique*,
women returning to school or work,
campaigns for equal pay and legal equality.

Even one or two well-explained examples would make your argument much more convincing.

Clarify your reasoning
Sometimes your claims are interesting but not fully explained. For example:

“thus defeating their cause, because of their aggressive action”
“it was again destined for failure”

These statements are too absolute. Did radicals really “defeat” the cause, or did critics use their behavior to discredit the movement? Since your thesis says the real problem was public perception, be careful not to contradict yourself.

A better phrasing would be:
*Their outspoken actions gave critics an opportunity to portray the movement as extreme.*

That wording supports your argument more precisely.

Sentence-level corrections
There are several grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors. Here are some important ones:

“Whenever a movement is began” → *Whenever a movement begins* / *Whenever a movement is begun*
“People normally associated” → *People associated*
“oppostion” → *opposition*
“therfore” → *therefore*
“docile and backward in modern standards” → *docile and old-fashioned by modern standards*
“Women’s Lib really took off again” → acceptable in informal writing, but in an academic essay use *gained momentum* or *reemerged*
“and and” → delete repetition
“have promote” → *have promoted*
“in lieu of” is incorrect here; it means “instead of.” You probably mean *in favor of*, *because of*, or *overshadowed by*
“readiliy” → *readily*
“if the public it was being aimed was not so jaded” → *if the public at which it was aimed were not so jaded* or more naturally *if the public were less cynical*

Also watch comma use. For example:
“This ignorance of other less aggressive feminists, made it seem…”  
  The comma after *feminists* should be removed.

Style and tone
Your tone is thoughtful, but phrases like “So what am I getting at?” sound conversational. In academic writing, use a more formal transition such as:

*The central point, then, is that…*
*This pattern suggests that…*

Final advice
Your essay has a strong core argument and a clear sense of purpose. For revision, focus on:
writing a precise thesis in the introduction,
organizing paragraphs around clear topic sentences,
replacing weak or inaccurate examples with stronger evidence,
making your reasoning more precise,
correcting grammar and spelling errors.

With these changes, your essay will become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a confident argumentative voice. You focus on the impact of feminism in the United States and use historical examples to support your position. The piece is readable and organized, but to make it a stronger academic argument, you should improve the thesis, evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear focus: You consistently discuss feminism’s impact on American society.
Logical progression: The essay moves from women’s suffrage to second-wave feminism and then to present-day opportunities.
Engaged voice: Your writing shows conviction and interest in the topic.

Main areas to revise

A. Strengthen the thesis
At the moment, your main claim is not fully precise. The final sentence, “America continues to set an example for women around the world,” appears to be the central argument, but it comes only at the end and is quite broad.

What to do:  
State your thesis clearly in the introduction. For example:

> *Feminism has significantly transformed American society by expanding women’s political rights, educational access, and professional opportunities, although the struggle for full equality continues.*

This thesis is stronger because it:
makes a specific claim,
identifies the main areas of discussion,
allows for a more balanced argument.

B. Use more specific evidence
Your essay refers to important developments, but many points remain general. Academic argument becomes more persuasive when you include concrete evidence.

For example:
women’s suffrage: mention the 19th Amendment (1920);
second-wave feminism: refer to key changes such as Title IX, increased access to higher education, or workplace protections;
equal pay: mention that this issue is still debated and supported by statistics if available.

Instead of saying:
> *Women’s access to law schools, medical schools, college campuses, and corporate board rooms have expanded greatly...*

You could say:
> *Since the rise of modern feminism, women have entered universities and professions in far greater numbers, including law, medicine, business, and the military.*

If possible, add one or two facts rather than many broad claims.

C. Improve balance and critical thinking
Your essay mostly praises American feminism, but strong argumentative writing also acknowledges limits or counterarguments.

For example, after discussing progress, you could add:
women still face wage inequality,
leadership positions remain male-dominated,
progress has not been equal for all women.

This would make your conclusion more convincing because it would show that you recognize complexity.

Organization
Your essay has a basic structure, but paragraphing can be improved.

Suggested structure:
Introduction  
   Introduce feminism globally, then narrow to the U.S., and present your thesis.
Paragraph 1: Political rights  
   Discuss suffrage and its significance.
Paragraph 2: Social and professional change  
   Explain the impact of feminists such as Betty Friedan and Gloria Steinem.
Paragraph 3: Ongoing challenges  
   Show that the “battle” is not over.
Conclusion  
   Restate the main argument in a more thoughtful way.

Also, there is a missing section here:
> *They have always preached the same sermon, from the same pulpit: .*

You need to replace this with an actual idea or quotation, or remove it.

Style and tone
Some parts sound more emotional or rhetorical than academic. Phrases like:
*battle cries*
*same sermon, from the same pulpit*
*slammed in the faces*

are vivid, but they may sound dramatic rather than analytical.

Try using a more formal academic tone. For example:
“advocated for equality”
“argued consistently for women’s rights”
“faced barriers that limited access”

Also be careful with very absolute statements such as:
> *No one today would say that women are not equal to men in the voting booths of America.*

This is too sweeping. A more academic version:
> *In legal terms, women now possess the same voting rights as men.*

Language corrections
Here are some important sentence-level issues:

“World Conference Women” → *World Conference on Women*
“what impact the cause has had” → *the impact feminism has had*
“the women of America” → often simply *American women*
“suffragates” → *suffragists* or *suffragettes*
“Betty Friedan and Gloria Steinam” → *Betty Friedan and Gloria Steinem*
“board rooms” → *boardrooms*
“Women’s access … have expanded” → *Women’s access … has expanded*  
  (“access” is singular)
“resulted in the women of America being recognized as full citizens”  
  This is historically overstated; women were citizens before suffrage, but not full political participants. A better wording:
  > *resulted in women gaining the constitutional right to vote*

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Replace general statements with specific examples.
Add one paragraph about continuing inequality.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Correct grammar, spelling, and historical wording.

You have a strong foundation and a meaningful topic. With clearer evidence and a more academic style, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you disagree with the statement “Money is the root of all evil” and argue that individual choice is the real cause of evil. This is a strong central idea, and your conclusion clearly restates it. You also use relevant examples, such as honest ways of earning money and charitable donations. These points help support your argument.

Strengths

Clear thesis
   - Your essay consistently argues that money itself is not evil.
   - The final sentence gives a strong alternative claim: evil comes from human choice.

Logical organization
   - The essay moves from defining the issue, to giving reasons, to considering exceptions, and then to concluding.
   - This structure makes your argument easy to follow.

Balanced thinking
   - You acknowledge that money can be connected to evil actions, but you distinguish between money itself and the misuse of money.
   - This makes your argument more thoughtful.

Main Areas to Revise

Make the introduction more direct
Your first paragraph is a little confusing because you begin by discussing a “corrected statement” and then mention the “original subject.” A reader may not immediately understand your thesis.

Instead, introduce the debate clearly and state your opinion in one sentence.

Current idea:
The original phrase is incorrect.
The corrected phrase is true.
Therefore, you disagree with the original statement.

Stronger approach:
“I disagree with the statement that money is the root of all evil. Money itself is neutral; evil comes from the choices people make in pursuing or using it.”

This is clearer and more academic.

Strengthen your reasoning
Some points are good, but they need deeper explanation.

For example:
“We all have money, so we all would have to be evil” is understandable, but it may sound too simplified.
A stronger version would explain that money is a tool, and tools are not moral or immoral by themselves.

You could develop this idea:
Money can be used for food, housing, education, charity, or crime.
Therefore, the moral issue is not money itself, but the intentions and actions of the person using it.

Improve paragraph development
Some paragraphs are very short and could be expanded with examples or explanation.

For instance, the paragraph beginning “It is true to say that money is the root of some evil” is useful, but you can improve it by explaining:
why people commit harmful acts for money,
how greed influences behavior,
why this still does not prove that money itself is evil.

A possible revision:
“It is true that the desire for money can lead people to commit evil acts such as lying, stealing, or even murder. However, these actions result from greed and moral failure, not from money itself.”

Use a more formal academic tone
Some expressions are slightly informal or imprecise:
“worth-while causes” → worthwhile causes
“I am sure that” → better to avoid personal certainty unless supported by evidence
“will he choose” → this sounds outdated because it uses only “he” for a general person

Use more formal alternatives:
“an individual may choose”
“people choose”
“many donated funds are intended to support reputable organizations”

Correct grammar and word choice
There are several language issues to revise.

Spelling
wold → would
honestly in “how can honestly be associated with evil?” → should be honesty
worth-while → worthwhile

Verb tense / form
“If money was the root of all evil” → If money were the root of all evil
  - Use the subjunctive form were for hypothetical situations.

Punctuation and wording
“Other things as well can be the root of evil: attitudes, morals (lack of), greed;”  
  This is awkward. Try:
  - “Many other factors can also lead to evil, including greed, poor morals, and destructive attitudes.”

Sentence clarity
“When money is accumulated by these means, it seems incorrect to say that money is the root of all evil.”
  - This is good, but you could make it more concise:
  - “When money is earned honestly, it is difficult to argue that money itself is evil.”

Make the conclusion more powerful
Your conclusion is good, but it can connect more directly to your argument.

Current:
“the root of all evil is the choice of the individual”

Improved:
“In conclusion, money is not the root of all evil. Rather, evil arises from human greed, dishonesty, and the choices individuals make.”

This restates your thesis more clearly.

Suggested Revision Focus
When revising, try to do these three things:
Rewrite the introduction so your thesis is immediately clear.
Expand your body paragraphs with more explanation and examples.
Edit grammar and word choice for a more formal academic style.

Example of a stronger thesis
“I disagree with the claim that money is the root of all evil. Although the desire for wealth can motivate harmful behavior, money itself is morally neutral; evil comes from greed and from the choices people make.”

Overall, your essay has a solid argument and a clear message. With clearer phrasing, stronger development, and some grammar correction, it can become a much more persuasive academic essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear opinion: money is necessary, but people should control it rather than let it control them. This is a strong central idea, and your final sentence gives the essay a memorable conclusion. You also show independent thinking by challenging the original saying instead of simply agreeing with it. That is a good strength in argumentative writing.

What is working well

Clear position  
   You argue that money is essential in modern society, but obsession with money and power can be dangerous. This is a thoughtful and balanced argument.

Relevant examples and contrasts  
   You compare people who work hard for money with those who always have plenty, and people who “use money to live” versus those who “live to use it.” These contrasts help develop your ideas.

Effective ending  
   The last paragraph clearly states your message: money should serve human needs, not become our master.

Main areas to improve

Make the thesis more precise

Although money is necessary for survival in modern society, it becomes harmful when people treat it as a source of power and personal worth.

This would help unify all your paragraphs.

Improve organization
The essay has good ideas, but some paragraphs feel loosely connected. Try to give each paragraph one main purpose:

Paragraph 2: Explain the connection between money and power.

Spelling

“This begats the will for more power”  
  Better: “This creates a desire for even more power.”

  ones basic needs → one’s basic needs

  Make sure singular subjects match singular verbs.

  You often use one, they, someone, and person in the same section. Try to stay consistent.
Parallel structure:  
  Example: “to eat, have somewhere to sleep, transportation, and clothing” should be in a balanced form.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a strong personal position: money itself is not evil, but a person’s attitude toward money can lead to harmful behavior. This is a good basis for an argumentative essay. You also use rhetorical questions effectively at the beginning to engage the reader.

Strengths
Clear opinion: Your main argument is understandable throughout the essay.
Logical contrast: You distinguish between money as an object and human choices about money.
Relevant examples: You show both negative and positive uses of money, which strengthens your argument.

Main areas to improve

Make the thesis more precise
Your final claim is strong, but it appears most clearly at the end. In an argumentative essay, your thesis should appear early, ideally in the introduction.

For example, after the opening questions, you could write:
“Money itself is not evil; rather, the desire for money and the misuse of it can lead people to act in evil ways.”

This gives the reader a clear roadmap for the essay.

Improve organization
Your ideas are relevant, but the essay would be stronger if each paragraph had one clear purpose.

A possible structure:
Introduction: explain the saying and present your thesis
Body paragraph 1: explain why people connect money with evil
Body paragraph 2: argue that money itself is neutral
Body paragraph 3: explain how money can be used for good
Conclusion: restate your argument clearly

Right now, the paragraph about the Christian life feels somewhat separate from the main argument. If you keep it, connect it more directly to your thesis. Also, consider whether your audience shares a specifically Christian viewpoint. If not, you may want to broaden the argument to include general moral values.

Develop your reasoning
Some points are asserted too quickly. For example:
“the more you have the more you want”
“This leads you away from the christian way of life and into a life of evil”
“You can be lead into gambling”

These are interesting ideas, but they need explanation. Why does desire for money become dangerous? Under what conditions? Not everyone with money becomes greedy. A stronger argument would acknowledge this and focus on greed, obsession, or misuse, rather than money alone.

For example:
“When people become obsessed with wealth, they may begin to value money more than relationships, honesty, or compassion.”

That is more precise and persuasive.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some expressions are conversational, such as:
“You hear people say…”
“I think not”
“almost anything for it”

An argumentative essay usually sounds stronger with a more formal tone.

For example:
“People often say that money is the root of all evil.”
“However, this statement is misleading.”
“In my view, money is a neutral tool that can be used either well or badly.”

Grammar, word choice, and spelling
There are several language errors, but many are easy to fix. Here are important ones:

you always want more that you have → you always want more than you have
christian / A christian → Christian / A Christian
subservant → subservient
can be lead into → can be led into
mear → mere
can not → cannot
educated the uneducated → educate the uneducated

Also check subject-verb and sentence clarity:
“It is the person whose judgements, decisions, and actions that can be evil.”
  Better:
  “It is a person’s judgments, decisions, and actions that can be evil.”

Punctuation and sentence structure
Some sentences are too long or joined incorrectly. For example:
“I think not, it is all in how it is used and how you allow it to use you!”

This is a comma splice. Better versions:
“I think not. It all depends on how money is used and how much power people allow it to have over them.”
Or: “I do not believe so, because it all depends on how money is used.”

Try to avoid overusing “you” in formal writing. Using people, individuals, or someone often sounds more academic.

A revised version of your main idea
Here is one possible improved thesis and conclusion:

Thesis:  
*Money is not inherently evil. However, when people become greedy or allow money to control their decisions, it can contribute to harmful and immoral actions.*

Conclusion:  
*Therefore, money should not be blamed for evil in itself. It is human desire, greed, and misuse of wealth that cause harm, while money can also be used to improve lives and reduce suffering.*

Final advice
For revision, focus on:
writing a clear thesis in the introduction,
organizing each paragraph around one main idea,
using more formal language,
correcting grammar and spelling errors,
explaining your points more fully.

You have a solid central argument. With clearer structure and more precise language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have written a clear argumentative essay with a strong position: you argue that “crime does pay.” Your main strengths are that your opinion is direct, your essay has an introduction, body paragraphs, and a conclusion, and you try to support your claim with comparison and numerical examples. This gives your essay a recognizable argumentative structure.

However, to revise successfully, you need to improve logic, tone, grammar, and use of evidence.

Strengthen the argument

Your essay clearly states a claim, but the argument is not fully convincing because it only focuses on the short-term financial benefits of crime. It ignores major counterarguments, such as:

the risk of arrest and imprisonment
fines and legal costs
violence and danger
damage to victims and society
lack of long-term security

In an argumentative essay, you should show that you understand the other side. Right now, the essay sounds one-sided and oversimplified. For example, when you say, “A criminal will always be employed,” this is too absolute and unrealistic. Also, the statement “There is never a lack of work” is not supported with evidence.

Revision advice:
You have two possible directions:
Keep your current position, but acknowledge risks and explain why some people still think crime is worth it.
Refine the thesis so it is more nuanced, for example:  
   *“Although some criminals may gain money quickly, crime rarely pays in the long term because of legal, financial, and personal consequences.”*

A nuanced thesis will make your essay stronger and more academically persuasive.

Use more reliable evidence

You use numbers to compare workers and criminals, which is a good strategy, but the figures seem invented and are not supported by sources. In academic writing, unsupported statistics weaken credibility.

For example:
“The average worker works 8 hours per day.”
“They make about $7.50 per hour.”
“The average criminal can work one hour a day and make $300.00.”

These are broad claims and need evidence. Also, “average criminal” is too vague. Different crimes produce very different outcomes.

Revision advice:
Use real sources if possible.
Avoid exact numbers unless you can support them.
If you do not have sources, use more cautious language such as:
  - *some criminals may earn large amounts quickly*
  - *many legal workers earn steady but lower wages*
  - *crime can appear profitable in the short term*

Improve organization and paragraph development

Your essay follows a basic structure, but each body paragraph could be developed more clearly.

A stronger pattern would be:
Topic sentence
Explanation
Example or evidence
Link back to thesis

For instance, your second paragraph discusses job security, but it moves quickly and includes several grammar problems. Try making one main point per paragraph and explaining it fully.

Tone and academic style

Your essay uses an informal and sometimes conversational tone, such as:
“Let’s take time to compare…”
“I’d definitely say…”

For university writing, use a more formal style.

Better alternatives:
*A comparison between criminal activity and legal employment suggests…*
*Based on these points, one might argue that…*

Also, avoid language that seems to promote crime without critical distance. You can analyze an idea without sounding as if you support it personally.

Grammar and language corrections

There are several language errors that need revision. Here are important ones:

“it’s advantages” → “its advantages”  
  (“it’s” = “it is”; “its” shows possession)

“hard working person” → “hard-working person”

“rather or not” → “whether or not”

“laid off work” → “laid off”

“There is never a lack of work are a means” →  
  “There is never a lack of work or a means of continuing employment.”

“will costs them” → “will cost them”

“a specific numbers of hours” → “a specific number of hours”

“If he/she invest” → “If he or she invests”

Pronoun consistency: you shift between “they,” “he/she,” and “the criminal.” Choose one consistent form. In modern academic English, singular “they” is often best.

Sentence style

Some sentences are too long or awkward. Shorter, clearer sentences will improve readability. For example:

Original:  
“Further the criminal does not have to be at work at a specific time for a specific numbers of hours each day.”

Revised:  
“Furthermore, criminals are not required to follow fixed schedules or standard working hours.”

Suggested thesis revision

Here is one possible revised thesis:

“Although crime may seem financially attractive because it can provide quick income and personal freedom, it is not a reliable or sustainable alternative to legal employment.”

This keeps your original topic but makes the essay more balanced and mature.

Final advice

Before submitting a new draft, focus on:
making your thesis more nuanced  
adding counterarguments  
using realistic, supported evidence  
correcting grammar and word choice  
using a more formal academic tone

You have a solid starting point and a clear central idea. With revision, your essay can become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear argument and uses vivid examples to make your point. The writing has energy, and the reader can easily understand your main claim: that some poor urban youth may see crime as financially rewarding because of limited alternatives and weak punishment. This is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on improving your logic, supporting your claims with evidence, and using a more balanced academic tone.

Strengths
Clear thesis/topic: The essay consistently argues that crime appears profitable in some urban communities.
Strong voice: Your language is confident and direct.
Effective paragraph focus: Each paragraph develops one part of the argument, such as poverty, policing, jail, or low-paying jobs.

Main Revision Priorities

A. Use evidence, not assumptions
Right now, many claims are presented as facts, but you do not provide statistics, studies, or examples from reliable sources. In academic argument, this is essential.

For example:
“In many cases the answer is simple: crime.”
“The consequences are few.”
“Police are no threat as they are far outnumbered.”
“violent crime rising and financial support for more police falling”

These may or may not be true, but the essay needs proof. Add:
crime statistics
incarceration rates
research on poverty and employment
studies about recidivism, policing, or sentencing

Without evidence, the essay sounds more like opinion than argument.

B. Avoid overgeneralization
The essay often makes broad claims about “inner-city youth,” “criminals,” and “urban America.” This can make your argument seem unfair or stereotypical.

For example:
“many youths are well aware of the fact that crime pays”
“The only danger that exists for a criminal come from other criminals”
“these criminals are merely working a job”

These statements suggest all or most poor urban youth think the same way. A stronger academic approach would use more careful wording:
“some youth may perceive crime as financially rewarding”
“in certain communities…”
“for a portion of offenders…”

This makes your argument more credible and nuanced.

C. Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should consider opposing views. Your essay argues that crime pays, but it does not seriously discuss the costs of crime:
risk of arrest
violence
addiction
trauma
criminal records
difficulty finding later employment
harm to victims and communities

If you include and answer these counterarguments, your essay will be much stronger. For example, you could argue: although crime may offer quick money in the short term, it often creates long-term personal and social damage.

D. Clarify your purpose
At times, the essay seems to explain why some people commit crime, but at other times it seems to justify crime. Be careful. If your goal is analysis, make that clear. You can say that understanding the economic appeal of crime does not mean approving of it.

Tone and Style
Your current tone is vivid but sometimes too informal or emotionally charged for academic writing.

Examples:
“go get it--at any cost”
“like a dog chasing its tail”
“thug bent on destroying society”
“stuck in a service job”
“crime pays”

These phrases create impact, but some sound biased or exaggerated. In revision, aim for a more formal tone:
replace slang and dramatic phrasing with precise language
avoid rhetorical questions unless used sparingly
reduce repetition

For instance, instead of beginning with several questions, you could open with a direct thesis about poverty, consumption, and criminal incentives.

Organization
The essay has a logical sequence, but your argument would improve with a clearer structure:
Introduction: Present the issue and thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Economic inequality and limited legal opportunities.
Body paragraph 2: Social influence and visible rewards of crime.
Body paragraph 3: Weaknesses in policing or sentencing, supported by evidence.
Body paragraph 4: Counterarguments and long-term costs of crime.
Conclusion: Restate argument and suggest solutions.

Right now, the conclusion mostly repeats earlier claims. Try to end with a broader insight or policy recommendation, such as improving education, wages, youth programs, or employment opportunities.

Language Corrections
There are several grammar and word-choice issues to fix:

“The only danger that exists for a criminal come from other criminals”  
  → “comes”
“as to wether one gets caught”  
  → “whether”
“The public calls for more police but refuse”  
  → “refuses”
“The few police on the street are thus often left chasing criminals like a dog chasing its tail. .”  
  → remove extra period
“with no en in sight”  
  → probably “end”
“If a criminal is apprehended, and in an odd circumstance is placed in jail for non-violent crime”  
  → sentence is awkward; simplify it
Be consistent with punctuation around quotation marks and dashes.
“Guess? clothing” appears incorrect; check punctuation.

Final Advice
For revision, focus on this question: Are you proving your argument, or only asserting it?  
To make this a strong academic essay:
add reliable evidence
avoid stereotypes
use a more formal tone
acknowledge counterarguments
clarify that explanation is not justification

You have a strong central idea and clear argumentative energy. With better support and a more careful tone, this essay could become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a definite position: computers have made life easier physically but harder mentally. This gives your argument a strong starting point. You also organize the essay into an introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion, which helps readability. Below are suggestions to help you revise it into a more focused and persuasive academic argument.

Strengthen the thesis
Your main idea is interesting, but the thesis is still somewhat broad and unclear. The phrase “a catch-22” is not quite accurate here, because it usually means a situation where solving a problem is impossible because of contradictory rules. What you seem to mean is that computers bring both benefits and drawbacks.

A clearer thesis could be:

“Although computers have greatly improved efficiency in workplaces and daily life, they have also increased people’s dependence on technology, reduced some practical skills, and contributed to job displacement.”

This version is more precise and easier to support in the body paragraphs.

Improve paragraph focus and development
Each body paragraph should focus on one clear point and explain it with examples.

Paragraph 2
You discuss physical effects, but the paragraph mixes several ideas:
office efficiency
factory automation
calculators
worldwide computer access

Try to focus the paragraph more narrowly on how computers reduce physical labor and increase efficiency. Then give 2–3 specific examples.

Also, the sentence about “2nd & 3rd world countries” is inappropriate and outdated in academic writing. Avoid broad or insensitive generalizations. If you want to discuss unequal access to technology, say something like:

“Although access to computers is widespread in many societies, it remains uneven across regions and income levels.”

Paragraphs 3 and 4
These paragraphs discuss “mental effects,” but the idea needs clearer explanation. Right now, several claims are too general:
people do not understand how computers work
this creates needless computations
models are riddled with errors
computers are more reliable than people

These are interesting points, but you need to explain how they connect to your thesis. For example:
People may rely on software without understanding the underlying mathematics.
This can lead to mistakes when data or assumptions are wrong.
Overreliance on computers may weaken critical thinking or practical skills.

That would create a stronger academic argument than saying computers cause “needless computations.”

Use evidence and avoid overgeneralization
Many claims are stated very strongly without support, for example:
“computers make errors all the time”
“mathematical models… are absolutely riddled with errors”
“This supposed reliability has left many workers out of work”

In argumentative writing, such claims should be supported with examples, facts, or cautious wording. If you do not provide evidence, use more precise language:

“Computers can produce errors when users enter incorrect data or rely on flawed assumptions.”
“In some industries, automation has reduced demand for certain types of labor.”

This makes your writing sound more credible and academic.

Improve tone and word choice
Some expressions are too informal, vague, or emotionally loaded for an academic essay:
“catch-22”
“needless” computations
“absolutely riddled with errors”
“for whatever reason”

Try to replace these with more formal alternatives:
paradox / mixed consequence
unnecessary or excessive calculations
prone to error
because of limited knowledge

Also be careful with phrases like “everyone already knows.” In academic writing, avoid assuming that all readers agree.

Revise grammar and sentence structure
There are several grammar and style issues that make some sentences difficult to follow.

Common issues:
a. Apostrophes
“1940's” → “1940s”
“A computer's circuits don't get tired” is correct, but make sure apostrophes are only for possession or contractions.

b. Parallel structure
“The computer has eliminated many jobs through automation of factories; accounting, engineering, and other scientific programs, etc”
This sentence is not parallel and is confusing.

You might write:
“Computers have eliminated some jobs through factory automation and have transformed fields such as accounting, engineering, and scientific research.”

c. Word forms
“Due to this errors frequently exist” → “Because of this, errors frequently occur”
“other type functions” → “other types of functions”

d. Awkward phrasing
“if that person knew what he should do” is unclear.
Try:
“If users understood the software and the mathematical process better, they would be less likely to accept incorrect results.”

Make the conclusion more specific
Your conclusion suggests education as a solution, which is a good direction. However, “curtailing some theoretical programming” is vague. What exactly should be changed?

A stronger conclusion would:
restate the argument,
summarize the main harms and benefits,
offer a practical recommendation.

For example:
“Schools and workplaces should teach not only how to use computers, but also how to evaluate digital information critically. In this way, society can benefit from computer technology without becoming overly dependent on it.”

Revision priorities
When revising, focus on these first:
Write a clearer thesis.
Make each body paragraph focus on one main idea.
Replace vague or extreme claims with precise, supported ones.
Use more formal academic language.
Edit grammar and sentence clarity.

Your essay has a strong central concern and good potential. With clearer reasoning, more precise wording, and better paragraph development, it can become much more persuasive.

Logical progression: You move from general importance, to workplace example, to banking, and then to a concluding warning.

Main areas to improve

This version gives your essay a clearer roadmap.

Develop each body paragraph more fully

Some ideas appear suddenly without smooth connection. For instance, the shift from workplace skills to banking is understandable, but a transition sentence would help.

This makes the essay easier to follow.

internet working → probably networking or internet systems of

auto-pasts → auto-parts
put all of their inventory on computer → put all of its inventory on a computer system
defecit → deficit
Millions of dollars transfer → Millions of dollars are transferred
see no errors have been made → make sure no errors have been made

Also pay attention to article use and plural forms. For example:

“the work” should be the workplace.

Some phrases are a little awkward or too informal. For an academic essay, aim for precise and formal wording.

For example:

“keep abreast of” is acceptable, but make sure the sentence is complete and developed.

A more formal opening could be:

Body Paragraph 1: Employment

   - Explain how lack of computer skills can reduce job performance and job security

Body Paragraph 2: Banking and finance

   - Show why computer literacy protects people financially

Body Paragraph 3: Daily life / future changes

   - Emphasize the need for continuous learning

Conclusion
   - Restate the thesis
   - Summarize the main reasons
   - End with a strong final thought

Final advice

With more development and language correction, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

What you do well

Good organization overall

These examples help make your argument more convincing.

Main areas to improve

Current idea:

Stronger version:

Use a more academic tone
Some phrases sound too conversational for an academic essay. For example:
“I feel”
“Not to mention”
“EVERYTHING”
“a bunch of wires”

These expressions make the essay sound informal. In academic writing, try to use a calm and precise style.

Examples:

“Not to mention” → “Furthermore”
“EVERYTHING” → “many aspects of modern life”
“a bunch of wires” → “complex technological systems”

Improve grammar and word choice

“people’s life styles” → “people’s lifestyles”
“made live transpire much quicker” → “made life move much more quickly” or “made daily activities much faster”

“are able to proceed with the same transactions” → “are able to perform the same transactions”

“obstical” → “obstacle”
“an on-coming obstical is approaching” → repetitive; better: “when an obstacle is approaching”
“in some instances to function” → “to function effectively in some situations”

Also check subject-verb agreement and article use. For example:

This makes your writing more credible.

In conclusion

These will help your essay flow more naturally.

Explain your examples more fully.  
Reduce exaggerated statements.

Final comment

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and compassionate message: you argue that greater cultural awareness and the effort to unlearn prejudice and discrimination are among the most significant developments of the 20th century. This is a thoughtful topic, and your moral commitment to equality gives the essay sincerity and purpose. To improve the essay, focus on clearer argument structure, more precise language, and stronger evidence.

Strengths

Strong personal voice
Your essay shows genuine conviction. For example, the sentence *“I greatly value all of humanity and I believe that all people should learn to live in peace”* communicates your position clearly and emotionally.

Important and meaningful topic
You chose a topic that goes beyond technology or science and instead looks at social progress. That is interesting and original.

Clear overall direction
Your central idea is understandable: society has started to recognize racism and discrimination, and this growing awareness is an important development for humanity.

Main areas to revise

A. Make your thesis more specific
Right now, your introduction says that the most significant discovery or invention depends on the individual, and then you choose your own topic. However, your essay would be stronger if you made a more direct argumentative claim.

For example, instead of:
*“I chose cultural awareness and understanding prejudice & discrimination as my topic…”*

You could write:
“One of the most significant developments of the 20th century was the growing awareness of prejudice and discrimination, because it challenged long-accepted injustice and encouraged more equal treatment of different racial, ethnic, and social groups.”

This gives the reader a clear argument from the beginning.

B. Clarify the idea of “discovery”
Your essay calls this a “discovery,” but the topic is really more of a social realization, social movement, or change in public understanding. You should explain this clearly. For example:
“Although unlearning prejudice is not a scientific invention, it can be seen as a major social discovery: humanity’s recognition that racism and discrimination are destructive and must be challenged.”

This will make your essay more logically consistent.

C. Improve paragraph organization
Your second paragraph contains many ideas: racism in media, historical discrimination, courageous people who resisted, the value of diversity, and modern organizations. These are all relevant, but they need better organization.

A good structure could be:
Past problem: discrimination was widely accepted.
Change: courageous individuals and movements challenged it.
Present significance: society now values diversity more.
Why it matters: this helps peaceful coexistence.

Use topic sentences to guide the reader.

D. Add examples
Your essay would be much more persuasive with specific examples. For instance, you could mention:
the Civil Rights Movement
Martin Luther King Jr.
the struggle for women’s rights
opposition to segregation or discriminatory laws

Even one or two examples would make your argument stronger and more concrete.

Language corrections

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are several grammar, spelling, and word choice problems. Here are some important corrections:

world wide → worldwide
peoples lives → people’s lives
discriminitive → discriminatory
more then → more than
excepted → accepted
enought → enough
breed sterotypes → deeply rooted stereotypes
burried → buried
forms → probably forums
avalibility → availability
existance → existence

Also, be careful with punctuation. For example, this sentence is a run-on:
*“The opportunity to learn about the vast world around us is opening up, the avalibility to study cultural difference and reflect the knowledge on ourselves is improving.”*

You could revise it as:
“The opportunity to learn about the vast world around us is increasing, and the availability of opportunities to study cultural differences and reflect on that knowledge is improving.”

Style suggestions

Use formal academic vocabulary
Some phrases can be made more academic:
“all of humanity” is good, but avoid repeating emotional phrases too often.
Instead of “people have come to realize,” try “society has increasingly recognized.”
Instead of “learn to live in peace,” try “promote more peaceful coexistence.”

Avoid overgeneralization
For example:
*“racism is almost inherent in today’s society”* is a strong statement.
You could soften it to:
“racism remains deeply embedded in many social structures and attitudes.”

This sounds more precise and academic.

Suggested revision strategy

When revising, try this order:
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs around 2–3 main points.
Add at least one specific historical example.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation.
Rewrite long sentences to make them clearer.

Final comment

This essay has a strong ethical perspective and an important argument. Your main challenge is not the idea itself, but how clearly and formally you express it. With better organization, more precise vocabulary, and specific examples, this could become a very effective argumentative essay. You have a meaningful message—now focus on presenting it in a more structured and polished academic form.

Feedback Report

Your paragraph raises an important argument about American society: media promotes narrow ideas of success, and fast-paced modern life can make inequality easier to ignore. You have a strong critical perspective, and your writing shows that you are trying to connect success, media, technology, individualism, and inequality into one argument. That is a good foundation. For revision, focus on making your main claim clearer, organizing ideas more logically, and improving sentence control.

Main strength
Your essay has a clear critical attitude toward the idea that “everyone has an equal opportunity to succeed.” You also make an interesting connection between popular media and social values. These are strong ideas for an argumentative essay.

Main problems to revise

A. Clarify your thesis
Right now, your main argument is present, but it is difficult to identify because the first sentence is very long and contains several ideas at once.

For example, this sentence:
> “A current ideology in the United States today is for one to be successful, one must follow all of the written & unwritten rules of society, everyone has an equal opportunity to succeed and when one does not do this one fails.”

This should be divided and clarified. Ask yourself:
What exactly is the ideology?
Are you arguing that it is false, harmful, or promoted by media?
What are your main reasons?

A possible clearer thesis could be:
In modern America, popular media promotes the belief that success is available to everyone, but this idea hides social inequality and causes people to blame individuals for failure rather than examine larger social problems.

You do not need to use this exact sentence, but your introduction should make the argument direct and specific.

B. Improve organization
Your paragraph contains several important points, but they are not arranged in a clear order. Try this structure:

Introduction + thesis
Media promotes models of success
Technology and fast-paced life increase individualism
As a result, inequality becomes less visible
Conclusion

Each paragraph should focus on one main idea. Right now, many ideas are combined into one block of writing, which makes the essay harder to follow.

C. Develop your argument with examples
You make broad claims such as:
TV commercials project models of how one should be
society blames unsuccessful people for lack of effort
media replaces the voices of struggling people

These are promising claims, but they need specific support. For example:
What kinds of TV programs or commercials?
What “written and unwritten rules” are you referring to?
Who is being ignored in society?
How does technology contribute to this problem?

Adding even one concrete example in each paragraph would make your argument much stronger.

Sentence-level issues

A. Avoid run-on sentences
Several sentences are too long and connect ideas without correct punctuation. This makes the meaning unclear. For example:

> “In the 20th cen, the values of/that the United States was founded on have changed in meaning and sense when applied to modern day America, nothing was ever equal.”

This could become:
In the twentieth century, the values on which the United States was founded changed in meaning when applied to modern America. In reality, equality was never fully achieved.

B. Use formal academic style
Avoid:
“&” → use and
“T.V.” repeatedly → write television if possible
abbreviations like cen
informal phrasing like one nolonger sees

C. Word choice and grammar
There are several spelling and word form errors that affect clarity:
comercials → commercials
substitued → substituted
americans → Americans
nolonger → no longer
realitive → relative
unsuccessfull → unsuccessful
thier → their
achieve → achievement / succeed
need oiled → need to be oiled

Also, some phrases are awkward or unclear:
“the realitive humanness between people”  
  Better: shared humanity among people
“attribute thier inadequacy to achieve to a lack of effort”  
  Better: attribute their inability to succeed to a lack of effort
“The parts of the new machine that need oiled and regulated.”  
  This is an interesting metaphor, but it is incomplete as a sentence. You need to explain it more clearly.

Suggested revision strategy
When revising, try these steps:

Write a one-sentence thesis.
Break the essay into separate paragraphs.
Give each paragraph one controlling idea.
Add specific examples.
Shorten long sentences.
Proofread for spelling, punctuation, and grammar.

Example of a clearer revised opening

In the United States, many people believe that anyone can succeed if they follow society’s rules and work hard enough. Popular media, especially television, reinforces this belief by presenting ideal models of success and normal behavior. However, this message ignores the reality of social inequality and encourages people to blame individuals for failure instead of questioning the system itself.

This version keeps your main idea but presents it more clearly and academically.

Final advice
You have strong ideas and a meaningful critique of American culture. Your next step is to make those ideas easier for the reader to follow. Focus especially on clarity, paragraph structure, and evidence. If you revise carefully, this could become a thoughtful and persuasive argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a strong personal voice and an ambitious central idea: technology is both beneficial and harmful depending on how people use it. You also use personal experience effectively to make the topic concrete. The examples about education, geography, and language learning help the reader see why you are concerned. For revision, focus on making your main argument clearer, improving organization, and correcting some sentence-level problems.

Main argument and focus

At the moment, your introduction presents a broad claim: technology is both “the closest friend” and “the greatest Nemesis” of the world. This is interesting, but the essay later shifts to a more specific argument about technology contributing to narrow-mindedness and poor education in the United States. Because of this shift, the essay feels somewhat unfocused.

What to revise
State your thesis more clearly and make it specific. For example:

Possible thesis: “Although technology has improved health, travel, and communication, in the United States it can also encourage isolation and weaken young people’s knowledge of other cultures and languages.”

This thesis matches the points you actually discuss later.

Organization and paragraph development

Your essay has four paragraphs, but the connection between them could be stronger.

Current structure
Paragraph 1: broad introduction to technology
Paragraph 2: benefits of technology and warning about misuse
Paragraph 3: technology as a detriment to youth, especially in the U.S.
Paragraph 4: education, foreign languages, and national attitudes

The strongest ideas are in paragraphs 3 and 4, but paragraph 2 is very broad and does not fully prepare the reader for the focus on education.

What to revise
Try this structure:

Introduction: present the topic and your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: explain the benefits of technology (travel, medicine, work).
Body paragraph 2: explain the negative effect—technology can encourage isolation and ignorance.
Body paragraph 3: give examples from U.S. education (geography, language study, children’s lack of world knowledge).
Conclusion: restate your argument and suggest what should change.

Also, make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence that clearly states its main point.

Use of evidence and reasoning

Your personal examples are vivid, but sometimes the connection between example and argument is not fully explained. For instance, the story about children asking whether Spain is in Michigan is memorable, but you need to explain more directly how technology contributes to this ignorance. Is your point that technology makes people feel connected while actually reducing real global knowledge? If so, say that clearly.

Also, some claims are too general, such as:
“Americans live in their own personal closets.”
“Our awareness of other people… is very low.”
“That will slowly destroy us.”

These statements are strong, but they need more support or more careful wording.

What to revise
Use more precise language:
Instead of “destroy us,” say “limit our understanding of the world” or “encourage cultural isolation.”
Instead of “Americans live in their own personal closets,” say “many people remain socially and culturally isolated despite modern communication technologies.”

Style and tone

Your writing is expressive, but sometimes it becomes too dramatic for an academic argumentative essay. Phrases like “greatest Nemesis” and “slowly destroy us” sound literary rather than analytical. Academic writing is usually stronger when it is clear and precise rather than highly emotional.

What to revise
Aim for a balance between personal voice and formal argument. You do not need to remove all passion, but choose words that sound more academic.

Grammar and sentence clarity

Several sentences are either run-on sentences or contain awkward phrasing. This makes some ideas difficult to follow.

Examples
“Science and technology have allowed me to travel to this part of the world, they have provided a living for my family…”
  - This is a run-on sentence. Split it into two sentences or use semicolons.
“This thing, technology has been…”
  - “This thing” is informal and awkward.
“The use of the means humans have developed is wherein the greatest problems lie.”
  - Grammatically unclear. Simpler is better.
“The effort of the United States has caused our minds to be heard…”
  - This sentence is unclear and may not express what you mean.

Suggested corrections
“Technology has allowed me to travel to this part of the world. It has provided a living for my family and helped cure my grandmother of cancer.”
“The greatest problems lie not in technology itself, but in how people use it.”
“Because of the U.S. education system, many students have limited opportunities to learn foreign languages and engage with other cultures.”

Final advice

Your essay has good potential because it has a clear concern, strong personal engagement, and memorable examples. For the next draft, focus on:
Writing a specific thesis
Improving organization
Explaining the link between technology and isolation
Using a more academic tone
Correcting run-on sentences and unclear phrasing

If you revise with these points in mind, your argument will become much more convincing and easier to follow.
