Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear topic, a strong sense of purpose, and an organized attempt to evaluate arguments about teaching evolution and creation together. You also use academic features such as examples, references to studies, and counterarguments. These are good strengths. For revision, the main areas to improve are: clarity of thesis, accuracy of evidence, paragraph focus, and language control.

Main strengths
You address an important controversy and show that there are two sides.
You try to build an academic argument by using examples, authorities, and a study.
You include counterarguments, which is important in argumentative writing.
Your conclusion returns to the main issue, which gives the essay a sense of completion.

Most important revision issue: thesis and position
Your essay sometimes sounds like an analysis of the two-model advocates’ argument, but at other times it sounds like you personally support the two-model approach. This makes the essay’s purpose unclear.

For example, your introduction asks whether the issue should be taught in the classroom, but your thesis becomes:  
> “Those who support a two-model approach… present a strong, effective argument...”

This is not exactly the same as arguing that the approach should be adopted.

How to revise
Decide on one clear purpose:
Argument essay: “Schools should/should not teach both creation and evolution.”
Rhetorical analysis: “Advocates of the two-model approach use several persuasive strategies.”

At the moment, your body paragraphs mostly fit rhetorical analysis, not a direct argument about education policy.

Organization and paragraph development
Many paragraphs begin with a useful point, but then become too long or repetitive. Some ideas are also not fully explained.

Examples
Paragraphs about “effective reasoning” repeatedly say the advocates are “strong” or “effective,” but do not always explain why the evidence proves that.
The paragraph about Newton and Einstein is interesting, but it needs a clearer connection to your main point. Questioning accepted ideas is valuable, but this does not automatically prove that creationism belongs in a science classroom.

How to revise
Use this structure in each body paragraph:
Topic sentence: state one clear claim.
Evidence: give one example, quotation, or study.
Analysis: explain how the evidence supports the claim.
Link: connect back to your thesis.

Use of evidence
A major problem is that several quotations are missing and replaced by “”. This makes the essay incomplete and weakens credibility. Also, some evidence needs more context or verification.

Important concerns
The “anonymous quote” is not strong evidence in academic writing.
The study by Richard Bliss may be useful, but you need proper citation and explanation of its reliability.
The Newton/Einstein example is oversimplified scientifically. Be careful: if historical or scientific details are inaccurate, readers may distrust your argument.

How to revise
Replace all “” with actual quotations or paraphrases.
Identify your sources clearly.
After each source, explain why it matters.
Use stronger academic sources if possible.

Counterarguments
You do include opposing views, which is good. However, the essay often presents the opposition too briefly and the response too generally.

For instance:
> “Some people feel .”

This is too vague. A strong academic essay should represent the opposing side fairly and specifically.

How to revise
State counterarguments clearly:
Why do some people think creationism should not be taught in science?
Is the concern legal, scientific, religious, or educational?
Then answer each one with evidence and analysis.

Style and tone
Your tone is sometimes too emotional or informal for academic writing. Examples include:
“incredible claims”
“surprise”
“isn’t the classroom the most effective place...?”
“the latest debate”

These choices make the essay sound persuasive in a conversational way, but less objective in an academic way.

Better approach
Use a more formal tone:
Instead of “incredible claims,” say “central claims” or “key arguments.”
Instead of rhetorical questions, use direct statements.

Grammar and sentence-level issues
Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and style problems.

Common issues
Word choice: “pondered at some point in their lives this” → awkward
Agreement: “many of the world’s population are students” → incorrect
Spelling/typing: “fro ma,” “where as”
Capitalization/punctuation: “the opposition asks” should not follow a question with a lowercase sentence unless punctuated correctly
Pronouns: “the student… she/he” → better as “students” or “they”
Sentence length: several sentences are too long and hard to follow

Example revision
Original:  
> “Since many of the world's population are students who are in the prime of their education...”

Better:  
> “Because students are in an important stage of intellectual development, schools are often seen as appropriate places to examine major questions.”

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:

Choose one clear thesis: argument or rhetorical analysis.
Replace missing quotations and cite sources properly.
Improve paragraph focus with clearer topic sentences and analysis.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Edit carefully for grammar, wording, and punctuation.

You have a strong foundation and a serious topic. With clearer purpose, stronger evidence, and more precise language, this essay can become much more convincing and academically effective.

Biggest problem: unclear thesis

This sounds like you support euthanasia as a personal choice.

But later you also say:
“to allow mercy killing to be a phenomenon would be outrageous”
“euthanasia should be a restricted process”

These statements create confusion. Do you support euthanasia, oppose it, or support it only in limited cases?

How to revise
Write one clear thesis at the end of the introduction. For example:

Possible thesis:  
“Although euthanasia conflicts with some religious and moral beliefs, it should be allowed in limited cases when a competent, terminally ill patient freely chooses it in order to end unbearable suffering.”

This thesis is clear, arguable, and consistent with many parts of your essay.

Organization and development
Right now, several paragraphs repeat similar ideas about “values” and “beliefs,” but the essay does not build a clear argument step by step. Some examples are also incomplete because of the missing sections marked .

Introduction
   - Define euthanasia briefly
   - Give background on the debate
   - State your thesis clearly

Body Paragraph 1: Argument for personal choice
   - Explain why patient autonomy matters
   - Discuss competent consent

Body Paragraph 2: Suffering and quality of life
   - Explain why unbearable pain may justify the choice
   - Be careful not to say a person is “useless to society,” which sounds harsh and unethical

Body Paragraph 3: Counterargument
   - Present religious or moral objections
   - Then respond to them respectfully

Body Paragraph 4: Legal example
   - Use the Kevorkian case clearly
   - Explain exactly how it supports your argument

Conclusion
   - Restate your position
   - Emphasize the conditions under which euthanasia should or should not be allowed

Use of evidence
You mention articles such as “The Last Goodbye” and “Prolifers Say Cruzan Death a Signal of Things to Come,” but you do not explain them enough. Also, some references are incomplete. Your reader needs:
the main point of each source
how it supports or challenges your argument
your analysis, not just summary

For example, instead of saying an article is “melancholy,” explain what argument it makes and why that matters.

Some phrases are too emotional or imprecise for academic writing. For example:
“fiery debate”
“spark up the issue”
“useless to society”
“People will start making this a habit”

a. Articles
“an complex issue” → a complex issue
“the patients personal decision” → the patient’s personal decision

b. Word form
“ethic and human rights” → ethics and human rights
“Opposers” → Opponents

c. Subject-verb and pronoun agreement
“someone else life” → someone else’s life

  or use plural consistently: Patients... their pain

d. Sentence clarity

Fill in all missing sections .

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that the case against capital punishment is stronger than the case in favor of it. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay because your reader can understand your main claim early. You also show awareness of both sides of the debate, which is important in academic argument. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths

Clear thesis and purpose: In the introduction, you state that the anti-death-penalty argument is “stronger and more sensible.” This gives your essay direction.
Balanced structure: You discuss arguments for capital punishment before presenting the opposing case. This shows fairness.
Strong engagement with the topic: Your writing is serious and persuasive, and you clearly care about the issue.

Main revision priorities

A. Add real evidence and complete citations
The biggest weakness is that many quotations are missing: “” appears several times. Because of this, your essay currently makes claims without support. In academic writing, this is a major problem.

What to do:
Replace every “” with an actual quotation or paraphrase.
Introduce each source clearly: who is the author, what is the source, and why is it credible?
Cite your sources in the style your teacher requires.

For example, instead of:
“Richard Nygaard supports this concept by saying, .”

Write:
“Judge Richard Nygaard argues that the state weakens respect for life when it deliberately takes life as punishment.”

B. Make your argument more analytical, less emotional
Your essay sometimes uses strong emotional language such as:
“heinous action”
“barbaric”
“society simply craves violence”

These phrases are powerful, but in academic argument they can sound exaggerated unless supported by evidence. Try to explain why a point is true rather than only expressing moral judgment.

For example:
Instead of “society simply craves violence,” say:  
  “Public support for capital punishment may partly reflect a desire for retribution rather than evidence of social benefit.”

This sounds more formal and analytical.

C. Strengthen paragraph unity
Some paragraphs begin with one point and then move into several others. Each body paragraph should focus on one main idea.

A possible organization:
Introduction and thesis  
Retribution argument for capital punishment  
Deterrence argument for capital punishment  
Moral/value-of-life argument against capital punishment  
Premeditation and state violence  
Refutation of deterrence with evidence  
Conclusion

This would make your essay easier to follow.

Logic and development

A. Avoid overgeneralizations
Some claims are too absolute, for example:
“Anyone who is placed in their shoes would wish for the death penalty…”
“for many violent criminals there simply is no fear”
“society and the government have no right to take the life of another man”

These statements may express your opinion, but they need qualification.

Use phrases such as:
“many family members may feel…”
“some violent offenders may not be deterred by…”
“critics argue that the state should not…”

This makes your argument more precise and credible.

B. Explain counterarguments more fairly
You present the pro-death-penalty side, but sometimes too briefly. A stronger essay shows the best version of the opposing view before challenging it. For example, when discussing deterrence, use data or studies from both sides, then explain why one side is more convincing.

Language and style

Your English is generally understandable, but some sentences are wordy or slightly awkward. Aim for simpler and more direct academic style.

Examples of sentences to revise

Original: “The fact still remains that in our current society, the majority of Americans support capital punishment.”  
  Better: “Currently, a majority of Americans support capital punishment.”

Original: “When forced to compare these arguments, it is clear that the argument opposing the death penalty is both stronger and more sensible.”  
  Better: “A comparison of these arguments suggests that the case against the death penalty is stronger.”

Original: “Opponents to capital punishment center their beliefs around…”  
  Better: “Opponents of capital punishment base their argument on…”

Grammar points
Use opponents of, not opponents to.
Be careful with they/society/government agreement. “Society” is singular in form, though “they” is common in informal use.
Watch for spelling/formatting issues, such as “indivi dual” split incorrectly.
Avoid repetition of phrases like “value of human life” too many times without adding new explanation.

Conclusion
Your conclusion is passionate, but it would be stronger if it summarized your main reasons more clearly:
capital punishment reduces respect for human life,
deterrence is unproven,
life imprisonment can achieve similar social protection without execution.

That kind of ending would sound more academic and persuasive.

Final advice
For revision, focus first on:
adding source evidence,  
improving organization,  
reducing emotional or absolute language, and  
making your claims more precise.

You already have a clear argument and a good sense of the debate. With stronger evidence and a more formal academic tone, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position: affirmative action is still necessary, and advocates should argue for it more effectively. You also show awareness of both sides of the debate, which is a good foundation for an argumentative essay. Below are the main areas to revise.

Clarify and strengthen your thesis

Your introduction summarizes the issue well, but the thesis is not direct enough. You say what you “plan” to do rather than making a strong claim.

Current approach:  
“I plan first to define affirmative action…”

Better approach:  
“This essay argues that affirmative action remains necessary because racial discrimination continues to limit opportunities for Black Americans, and opponents’ criticisms often confirm the need for stronger legal protections.”

This makes your argument more confident and academic.

Focus the essay more tightly

At times, the paper shifts between:
campus protests,
affirmative action in general,
quotas,
legal issues,
and how advocates should write better arguments.

These are related, but too many directions make the essay feel unfocused. Choose one main line of argument. For example:

Define affirmative action and distinguish it from quotas.  
Explain why it is legal when properly implemented.  
Argue why it is still necessary.  
Respond to major objections.

The sections about how “advocates could strengthen their argument” are interesting, but they partly shift away from your own argument. If you keep them, make sure they clearly support your thesis.

Define key terms carefully

A major issue in the essay is that affirmative action and quotas are sometimes treated as the same thing. They are not always identical. Since your paper discusses both, you need to define them clearly at the beginning.

For example:
Affirmative action: policies intended to increase equal opportunity for historically disadvantaged groups.
Quotas: fixed numerical targets or requirements, which are more controversial and legally restricted.

This distinction will make your discussion more accurate and persuasive.

Develop your evidence

You refer to authors and statistics, but many quotations are missing (“”), so the argument feels incomplete. In revision, make sure every body paragraph includes:
a clear topic sentence,
evidence from a source,
and your explanation of how the evidence supports your claim.

For example, instead of saying “Another author states that…,” explain:
who the author is,
what exactly they argue,
and why that point matters.

Also, avoid unsupported statements such as “the only way” unless you can prove them.

Improve logic and balance

Some points are strong, especially when you argue that continuing racial inequality shows affirmative action is still needed. However, some reasoning is too broad.

For instance, this sentence is too absolute:  
“The only way to guarantee they get into the system is through affirmative action programs.”

This may be your opinion, but in academic argument you should avoid overstatement unless you have strong evidence. You could revise to:  
“One important way to increase access to systems that have historically excluded Black Americans is through affirmative action programs.”

Also, when discussing opponents, represent their views fairly. This makes your essay stronger. Sometimes your wording sounds too general or emotional, especially in the paragraph beginning “Opponents argue that blacks will never experience true equality…” Try to separate what some critics say from what all opponents say.

Work on organization and paragraph unity

Several paragraphs contain multiple ideas. Try to make each paragraph do one main job.

For example:
One paragraph on legality,
one on the “reverse discrimination” argument,
one on the claim that affirmative action harms confidence,
one on why structural discrimination still requires policy intervention.

Use transitions to connect ideas more smoothly.

Language corrections

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are frequent grammar and word-choice problems. Here are some important examples:

“1960's” → “1960s”
“tents of affirmative action” → “tenets of affirmative action”
“achie ved” → “achieved”
“in regards to” → “with regard to” or “regarding”
“stope using” → “stop using”
“Beside the fact” → “Besides the fact”
“insure the rights” → “ensure the rights”
“writer's need” → “writers need”
“opponents statement” → “opponents’ statement”

Some sentences are awkward or ungrammatical. For example:
“Opponents state that it is up to blacks to raise their morals…”  
  This wording is problematic and sounds imprecise. Consider:  
  “Some opponents argue that Black communities must improve educational outcomes and social conditions without relying on affirmative action.”

Also be careful with capitalization. In modern academic writing, Black is often capitalized when referring to race/ethnicity.

Tone and academic style

Try to make your style more formal and precise. Avoid conversational phrases such as:
“Now that we have established…”
“How should we do it then?”

Instead, use academic transitions:
“Having considered the legal issue, the next question is whether affirmative action remains necessary.”

Revision priorities

Write a direct thesis statement.  
Clearly distinguish affirmative action from quotas.  
Reorganize body paragraphs around 3–4 main claims.  
Add complete evidence and explanation.  
Revise grammar, word choice, and tone for academic style.

You have a strong central concern and clear engagement with an important issue. With a sharper thesis, clearer organization, and more precise language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong sense of purpose: you argue that the case against teaching New Age beliefs is stronger than the case for it. Your essay also shows effort in researching sources and comparing two sides of a debate. Those are good foundations. To improve the essay, focus on thesis, organization, evidence, and academic tone.

Main strength
Your essay does not simply describe New Age beliefs; it tries to evaluate arguments. This is a good strategy for an argumentative essay. You also attempt to use concepts such as questions of value and questions of consequence, which shows analytical thinking.

Biggest problem: unclear focus
At times, the essay seems to argue different things:
what New Age means,
whether yoga belongs to New Age,
whether New Age should be taught,
whether Christian sources are more trustworthy,
whether the Bible is true.

Because of this, the central argument becomes difficult to follow.

What to do
Choose one precise claim and build the whole essay around it. For example:

> “The argument against teaching New Age beliefs in public schools is stronger than the argument for it because supporters rely too heavily on personal opinion, while opponents provide clearer consequences and more consistent moral reasoning.”

This thesis is narrower and easier to defend.

Introduction needs revision
The opening joke about “Yoda/yoga” is creative, but it does not fit the serious academic style of the essay. The introduction also spends too much time on a hypothetical conversation and on general comments about society.

What to do
Start with background directly related to your topic:
What are “New Age beliefs”?
In what setting are they being “taught”?
Why is this controversial?

Then end the introduction with your thesis.

Organization and paragraph structure
Many body paragraphs are long and contain several ideas at once. Some points are repeated, especially the claim that pro-New-Age authors rely on opinion.

What to do
Use a clearer structure. For example:

Define the debate  
Weaknesses in arguments supporting New Age teaching  
Strengths in arguments opposing it  
Evaluation of sources  
Conclusion

Also, give each paragraph:
a topic sentence,
evidence,
analysis,
a link back to your thesis.

Use of evidence
This is the area that needs the most work. You mention sources, but often you do not explain them fully or provide enough context. Some quotations are missing, shown as , so the reader cannot evaluate your evidence. In addition, several claims are too broad, such as:
“none of the authors had any books or hard facts”
“the case against… reaches more people, [so] the argument is more powerful”

These statements need proof.

Important issue
You often treat popularity as evidence of truth or strength. For example, saying that *Christianity Today* has many readers does not automatically make its argument stronger. A strong argument depends on quality of reasoning and evidence, not only audience size.

What to do
For each source, explain:
who wrote it,
what the main claim is,
what evidence it uses,
why that evidence is convincing or weak.

Also, avoid personal reactions such as “I ran out of my room…” in formal academic writing. Instead, write:
> “This claim reduced the author’s credibility because it appeared unsupported and exaggerated.”

Bias and credibility
Your essay strongly favors Christian sources and the Bible. That is acceptable if your assignment allows a faith-based argument, but in a university argumentative essay, you need to recognize that not all readers will accept the Bible as objective proof. When you say the Bible is “very real and true,” that expresses personal belief rather than academic analysis.

What to do
If you use the Bible, present it more carefully:
> “For Christian critics of New Age teaching, the Bible functions as an authoritative moral text.”

This is more analytical and less personal.

Also, be careful about relying mainly on Christian magazines to evaluate New Age beliefs. These sources may be useful, but they also have their own perspective. You should acknowledge possible bias on both sides.

Style and language
Your writing is understandable, but several sentences are wordy, informal, or grammatically awkward.

Examples
“the te aching” → typo
“that one authors opinion” → “that one author’s opinion”
“furthermore” should be capitalized at the start of a sentence
“can not” is usually written as “cannot”
“many time” → “many times”
“attaching it legally” probably means “challenging it legally”

Style advice
Avoid phrases like:
“stupendous job”
“for all I know”
“that little green guy”
“buy into the argument”

Use more formal alternatives:
“effectively supports”
“it is unclear whether”
“the author persuades the audience”

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier points but does not reflect critically on the limits of your argument.

What to do
In your final paragraph, briefly restate:
your thesis,
the strongest reasons,
why this matters in the context of education or public discourse.

Revision priorities
Write a clearer, narrower thesis.
Remove informal material and improve academic tone.
Reorganize paragraphs around one main point each.
Replace personal reactions with source analysis.
Use fuller, more balanced evidence and explain why it is credible.
Edit grammar, punctuation, and word choice.

You have the basis for an argument, but it will become much stronger if you focus less on personal belief and more on careful evaluation of evidence and sources.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure. You argue that supporters of nuclear power are more persuasive because they use facts and figures, while opponents rely more on fear and public values. This is a strong central idea. To improve the essay, focus on clarity, logic, evidence, and sentence-level accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear topic: The essay consistently stays focused on nuclear power.
Organized body paragraphs: Each paragraph discusses one main supporting point: cost, safety, cleanliness, and waste.
Awareness of rhetoric: You are not only arguing about nuclear power; you are also analyzing how each side persuades the public. This is an interesting approach.

Thesis and focus
Your introduction presents the topic well, but the thesis is somewhat wordy and unclear. Try to make it more direct.

Current idea:
opponents appeal to values and fears
proponents use facts and figures
proponents are more convincing

A stronger thesis could be:
“Although opponents of nuclear power appeal effectively to public fears about safety, proponents make the stronger argument because they use economic, environmental, and safety evidence to show that nuclear energy is a practical source of electricity.”

This version is more precise and easier to support.

Organization and development
The overall structure works, but some paragraphs become repetitive or drift away from the main point.

Suggestions:
In each body paragraph, begin with a clear topic sentence.
After presenting a claim, explain how it supports your thesis.
Avoid repeating the idea that “people are afraid” in too many places.
The conclusion should summarize your argument, not introduce dramatic patriotic language.

For example, the final sentence (“our revolutionary country of life, liberty, property…”) sounds emotional rather than analytical. A stronger conclusion would restate why the proponents’ argument is more effective.

Use of evidence
This is the area that needs the most revision.

You mention many statistics and claims:
nuclear is 50% cheaper in New England
20% cheaper in the Midwest
coal plants cause many deaths
nuclear saves 20 to 100 lives per 1,000 megawatts
coal plants emit more radiation than nuclear plants

These points could be powerful, but you do not cite any sources. Without evidence, readers may question the accuracy. In academic writing, you need to show where facts come from.

What to revise:
Add credible sources for all statistics.
Be careful with broad claims such as “there is nothing to worry about.” This sounds too absolute.
Acknowledge complexity. For example, nuclear waste may be small in volume, but long-term storage remains controversial.

This will make your argument more balanced and more persuasive.

Tone and fairness
At times, your essay sounds biased against opponents. For example:
“closed minded”
“uninformed American”
“beat the nuclear waste issue to death”

These phrases sound informal and dismissive. In academic argument, it is better to describe opposing views respectfully.

Instead of “closed minded,” you could say:
“many members of the public associate nuclear energy with war and radiation accidents.”

Instead of “uninformed American,” you could say:
“audiences unfamiliar with nuclear technology.”

A respectful tone will strengthen your credibility.

Grammar and word choice
There are many language errors, but most can be fixed through careful editing. Common problems include:

Word choice / form
“do to” → due to
“analyzation” → analysis
“then” → than
“counter act” → counteract
“the fact around” → the facts about
“precaution took” → precautions taken
“radio active” → radioactive

Sentence structure
Some sentences are run-ons or comma splices. Example:
“Thus, a large group of opponents to nuclear power are formed, they fear this new entity…”
This should be divided or revised:
“Thus, a large group of opponents to nuclear power is formed because many people fear it as a dangerous threat to their lifestyles.”

Subject-verb agreement
“a large group… are formed” → is formed
“the proponents… was” → were
“nuclear power plants are more cost-efficient then conventional power plants” → than

Spelling
“meggawatts” → megawatts
“rail road” → railroad
“today’ world” → today’s world

Style
Try to use simpler and more precise language. Some expressions are too dramatic:
“visions of mushroom clouds”
“shot down from the start”
“fear this new entity”

These phrases sound persuasive, but they reduce academic seriousness. Aim for a more formal style.

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis so it is concise and specific.
Add sources for all major factual claims.
Remove biased or emotional wording.
Fix grammar and sentence errors, especially run-on sentences and word choice.
Strengthen the conclusion by summarizing your analytical point.

Final comment
You have a strong foundation: a clear argument, useful paragraph structure, and an interesting comparison of emotional appeals versus factual appeals. If you revise for accuracy, fairness, and language control, this essay can become much more convincing and academic.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important and controversial topic, and it shows clear effort to consider both benefits and drawbacks of school integration. A major strength is that you do not treat the issue as simple; instead, you discuss academic outcomes, self-esteem, race relations, and community attitudes. Your personal experience with Chapter 220 also gives the essay a strong sense of voice and makes the argument more concrete.

What is working well

Balanced argument
   You present both positive and negative views, which is essential in an argumentative essay.

Clear overall focus
   The essay stays on the topic of school integration and consistently asks whether it helps or harms students and communities.

Use of evidence and experience
   You include statistics, references to research, and personal observation. This combination can be persuasive if it is organized carefully.

Main areas to revise

Make your thesis clearer and more specific
Your introduction gives historical background, but the main argument is still somewhat vague. The last sentence says the debate involves “value and consequence,” but it does not clearly state your own position.

Try revising the thesis so it directly answers the question. For example:
*Although school integration can improve educational opportunity and reduce racial isolation, its success depends on early implementation, supportive school environments, and community cooperation.*

This kind of thesis tells the reader exactly what your essay will argue.

Improve organization
Right now, some body paragraphs have strong ideas, but the essay would be easier to follow if each paragraph had one clear claim connected to the thesis.

A possible structure:
Introduction: background + thesis
Paragraph 1: integration can improve academic opportunity
Paragraph 2: emotional and identity challenges can reduce those benefits
Paragraph 3: personal experience showing mixed effects on race relations
Paragraph 4: community attitudes determine success or failure
Conclusion: restate argument and explain what is needed for successful integration

Also, some paragraphs are very long. Breaking them into smaller paragraphs will improve readability.

Use evidence more carefully
Several places contain placeholders like “”, which makes the argument feel incomplete. You should either add the missing quotation or remove the reference. Every source you mention should be fully explained:
Who is the author?
What does the source claim?
How does that support your point?

Also, avoid relying too heavily on personal experience in place of analysis. Your Chapter 220 example is useful, but after describing it, explain how it supports your larger argument.

Be careful with generalizations
Some claims about black students and white students sound too broad and may weaken your credibility. For example:
“many of them were rebellious”
“Many black students took advantage...”

These statements risk sounding biased because they describe large groups in a negative way without enough evidence. In academic writing, it is better to use more precise and cautious language.

For example:
*Some students who entered the program later appeared less willing to adapt to the new environment.*
*In some cases, disciplinary tensions increased, partly because school officials were uncertain how to respond to conflict.*

This keeps your point while sounding more objective and analytical.

Strengthen academic tone and word choice
Some phrases are too informal or imprecise for an academic essay. Examples:
“infested with crime and drugs” → too emotional
“220 kids” → too informal
“in a frenzy” → too conversational

Better alternatives:
*high-crime neighborhoods*
*students in the Chapter 220 program*
*strongly opposed* or *highly anxious*

Fix sentence-level grammar and style
There are several grammar and phrasing issues that should be revised. Here are some important examples:

“The ethical dispute over school integration has based itself...”  
  Better: *The ethical debate over school integration is based on questions of values and consequences.*

“more suitable and conductive to learning”  
  Better: *more suitable and conducive to learning*

“a hinderance”  
  Correct spelling: *a hindrance*

“impose any negative attitudes on to the children”  
  Better: *impose negative attitudes on the children*

“As can seen”  
  Correct: *As can be seen*

“more students were implemented into the program”  
  Better: *more students were added to the program*

Also, many of your sentences are very long. Try shortening some of them to avoid confusion and comma errors.

Add a stronger conclusion
Your final paragraph begins an important idea: school integration needs support beyond schools themselves. This could become a powerful conclusion. Instead of only repeating that there are “values and consequences,” state your final judgment clearly.

For example:
*Overall, school integration can create important educational and social benefits, but these benefits do not happen automatically. Without community support, sensitive school leadership, and attention to students’ emotional adjustment, integration may deepen rather than reduce division.*

Final advice
For revision, focus first on:
writing a clear thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one main point,
removing or rephrasing broad generalizations,
completing and explaining your evidence,
editing grammar and sentence clarity.

You have a strong foundation and an interesting perspective. With clearer structure and more precise language, this essay could become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

You have chosen an important and complex topic: racism on predominantly white campuses and whether separate Black student organizations help or harm racial progress. Your essay shows strong engagement with the issue and a clear opinion. The central argument is understandable: racism is real, but separation is not the best solution; integration is better. That is a solid argumentative foundation.

Strengths

Clear position: Your thesis is visible early and remains consistent.
Serious engagement with the topic: You are trying to address history, campus life, stereotypes, and possible counterarguments.
Argumentative structure: You attempt to move from problem → causes → effects → counterargument → conclusion, which is good for an academic essay.

Main areas to revise

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction is interesting, but the thesis is too broad and emotionally worded (“race war,” “politics of difference”). Try to state your claim more directly and calmly.

Possible revision:
> Although Black students at predominantly white universities face real racism and exclusion, creating separate organizations can reinforce division; universities should instead encourage integration, equal participation, and cross-racial dialogue.

This version is clearer and sounds more academic.

B. Use evidence fully and clearly
Several places contain placeholders like “”, and some examples are mentioned without explanation. This is the biggest weakness right now. In an argumentative essay, each body paragraph needs:
a clear topic sentence,
specific evidence,
explanation of how the evidence supports your claim.

For example, you mention:
Alberta at Marquette University
a study at Pennsylvania State University
Isaiah Thomas
Steele’s essay

But these references are incomplete or unclear. You need to explain:
Who are these people?
What exactly did they say or experience?
Where is the study from?
What does Steele argue?
How does each example support your point?

Without this, the essay sounds general rather than persuasive.

C. Be careful with overgeneralizations
Some claims are too absolute, for example:
“the white majority in this country have held themselves in higher respect”
“the white population is not going to be the one to step forward”
“it is the actions of the white community that have forced the black community...”

These statements may weaken your argument because they sound too sweeping. Academic writing usually avoids blaming an entire group in total terms.

Instead, qualify your claims:
“many white Americans”
“some university environments”
“historical and structural racism has contributed to...”

This makes your argument more credible.

D. Improve organization within paragraphs
Some paragraphs contain many ideas at once. Try to keep one main idea per paragraph. For example:

Paragraph 2: transition to college + fear + equality + history of segregation
Paragraph 5: inferiority + legal rights + activism + student organizations

These should be separated more clearly. A possible structure:

Introduction + thesis  
Racism and stereotyping on white campuses  
Psychological effects on Black students  
Why separate organizations emerge  
Why separation may also reinforce division  
Counterargument: value of cultural identity  
Response: integration with support is better  
Conclusion

Language and grammar issues

Your writing has good energy, but there are frequent grammar and style problems. Focus especially on these:

A. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or ungrammatical.

Original:  
“Responding to the question of the day: what is would be like to be of another color…”

Better:  
“Responding to the question of what it would be like to be a different race…”

B. Word choice
Some phrases sound unnatural:
“being of color” → “as a woman of color”
“freedoms abound” → “new freedoms”
“felling of inferiority” → “feeling of inferiority”
“ones life” → “one’s life”
“a University” → usually “a university”

C. Repetition
You repeat words like “problem,” “difference,” “segregation,” and “white students/black students” many times. Try varying sentence patterns and vocabulary.

D. Pronouns and agreement
Example:
“The black student faces their discrimination…”  
Better:
“Black students face discrimination…”  
or  
“The Black student faces discrimination…”

E. Punctuation
Avoid unnecessary commas:
“Leaving home for that first time to attend a University, is for many, a major step…”  
Better:

Tone and academic style

“race war”
“out of sight, out of mind”
“a horrible reminder of the past”

Most important revision priorities

Fill in all missing evidence and citations.
Rewrite the thesis clearly and academically.
Reduce overgeneralizations about racial groups.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has one main idea.
Edit for grammar, word choice, and punctuation.

Final comment

This essay has a strong core argument and addresses a meaningful issue. Your next step is to turn strong opinions into a more careful academic argument supported by specific evidence. If you revise for clarity, evidence, and tone, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear position: you argue that surrogate motherhood is unethical because it causes emotional harm and creates conflicts about parenthood. That clear stance is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. You also try to consider opposing views, which is important in this genre. For revision, the main areas to improve are organization, completeness of evidence, grammar/style, and precision of argument.

Biggest problem: missing content
Your essay contains many “” markers. These make the argument incomplete and sometimes hard to follow. Before revising grammar, you should first fill in all missing parts. In particular, the reader needs:
a clear definition of surrogate motherhood in the introduction
the missing details of the Baby M case
the names and ideas of the sources you mention
the exact counterarguments and your responses to them
a complete final claim in the conclusion

Without these parts, the essay feels unfinished.

Strengthen your thesis
Your thesis appears at the end of the first paragraph:

> “Surrogate mother hood is not an ethical way for infertile couples to have a child because of the emotional strains and ties present among the surrogate mother and the other two parents.”

This is a workable thesis, but it should be more precise and grammatically correct. For example:

Revised thesis:  
*Surrogate motherhood is ethically problematic because it creates emotional conflict, blurs parental roles, and can reduce the surrogate mother to a reproductive function rather than recognizing her full humanity.*

This version gives you three clear points to organize the body paragraphs.

Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs repeat similar ideas: emotional pain, the surrogate’s feelings being ignored, and questions about biology. Try a clearer structure:

Paragraph 1: Introduction
Define surrogacy
Mention Baby M as context
Present thesis

Paragraph 2: Counterargument
Explain why some people support surrogacy (freedom, helping infertile couples)

Paragraph 3: Emotional harm to the surrogate
Pregnancy creates attachment
Giving up the baby may cause distress

Paragraph 4: Problems of parenthood and identity
Tension among biological father, surrogate mother, and adoptive/social mother
Questions about rights and relationships

Paragraph 5: Rebuttal
Explain why regulation cannot solve the emotional and ethical problems

Paragraph 6: Conclusion
Restate your position clearly

This will make the essay easier to follow.

Use evidence more carefully
You refer to “the author,” “Health services,” and “Annas,” but the reader does not know exactly what they argued because the quotations or summaries are incomplete. In an argumentative essay, each body paragraph should include:
a clear claim
evidence from a source or example
explanation of how that evidence supports your claim

For example, instead of saying:

> “Opposing this view Annas argues .”

write something like:

*In contrast, Annas argues that the desire for a child does not justify arrangements that may exploit a surrogate mother emotionally. This supports the view that surrogacy may treat women as means to an end rather than as persons with independent interests.*

Even if you paraphrase, be specific.

Make the argument more balanced and academic
Sometimes your language becomes too emotional or too absolute. For example:

“just a walking womb”
“No human being should be looked upon as a reproductive structure”

These phrases show strong feeling, but they can sound informal or exaggerated in academic writing. Try using a more formal tone:

*The surrogate mother may be treated primarily for her reproductive capacity rather than as a person with emotional interests.*
*This view risks reducing women to biological functions.*

Also, avoid overgeneralizations such as “They do not see the surrogate mother as a person.” Not all intended parents think this way. Use phrases like:
*in some cases*
*may*
*can lead to*

This makes your argument more credible.

Grammar and language corrections
You have good ideas, but grammar errors sometimes distract the reader. Focus on these patterns:

Word form / spelling
mother hood → motherhood
bore → borne
whose / who’s: use whose for possession  
  Example: “people whose lives…”
substancial → substantial

Subject-verb agreement
“Health services, however, argues” → “Health services, however, argue”
“Others feels” → “Others feel”

Possessives
“the infertile couples lives” → “the infertile couple’s lives”
“the fathers” → “the father’s”
“the surrogate mothers” → “the surrogate mother’s”

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are awkward or repetitive. Example:

> “The longing that the parents have to fill the biological and emotional need is not significant enough to put the surrogate mother through suffering.”

Possible revision:
*The intended parents’ desire to satisfy biological and emotional needs is not sufficient justification for the suffering that surrogacy may cause the surrogate mother.*

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier points, which is good, but it should end more strongly. Instead of listing ideas again, give a final judgment:

*Although surrogacy may seem to offer hope to infertile couples, its emotional consequences and conflicts over motherhood make it ethically troubling. For this reason, surrogacy should not be viewed as a simple or acceptable solution to infertility.*

Final advice
When you revise, do these steps in order:
Fill in every missing section.
Rewrite the thesis clearly.
Reorganize the body paragraphs by main point.
Add specific evidence and explanation.
Edit for grammar and formal tone.

You already have a strong central opinion. With clearer structure and more precise language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position and a logical overall structure. You introduce the issue, present your thesis, organize the body into three main reasons, and end with a conclusion that restates your claim. This is a strong foundation. Your topic is also appropriate for an argumentative essay because it allows you to compare opposing views and defend your own. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths

Clear thesis: You clearly argue that the death penalty should be abolished.
Logical organization: The essay is divided into three main arguments: ineffectiveness, constitutional problems, and ethical concerns.
Formal tone: Your language is mostly academic and serious.
Use of counterargument: You mention the opposing side before arguing against it, which strengthens your essay.

Most important revision issue: missing evidence

The biggest problem is that many sentences contain incomplete citations or missing information, marked by . Because of this, some paragraphs feel unfinished and your argument loses credibility.

For example:
“A study conducted by Dane Archer, Rosemary Gartner, and Marc Beittel showed that the murder rates of fourteen nations, .”
“Time magazine reported that, .”
“Social Theory and Practice wrote, .”

What to do:
Complete every quotation, paraphrase, or statistic.
Explain the evidence after presenting it. Do not just insert a study; show how it supports your point.
If you cannot find the original source, remove the reference and replace it with evidence you can verify.

Development and analysis

Sometimes your paragraphs list ideas without fully developing them. A strong body paragraph should follow this pattern:

topic sentence → evidence → explanation → link to thesis

For example, in the first body paragraph, you mention several studies, but some points are not clearly connected. Instead of listing one study after another, explain:
what each study found,
why the finding matters,
and how it proves that the death penalty is not an effective deterrent.

Also be careful with broad claims such as:
“courts are so eager to find a scapegoat”
“criminals seem to model their behavior after the state”

These statements may be too general unless you support them with reliable evidence.

Organization and paragraph unity

Your essay is generally well organized, but some paragraphs are too long and include too many different ideas. The first body paragraph especially could be clearer if you grouped the material more carefully.

Suggestions:
In the deterrence paragraph, separate statistical evidence from the brutalization effect if needed.
In the ethics section, you do a good job presenting the opposing view first. However, the transition between “supporting” and “opposing” ethical arguments could be smoother.

You can use clearer transition phrases such as:
“However,”
“In contrast,”
“More importantly,”
“This evidence suggests that…”

Style and academic tone

Your style is mostly appropriate, but a few expressions are too informal or conversational for academic writing.

Examples:
“fair is fair” → better to write: supporters argue that capital punishment is a just form of retribution
“Simply putting the murderer to death” → consider a more formal phrasing
“How can the government protect innocent people…” → rhetorical questions can be effective, but too many may weaken the formal tone

Try to replace rhetorical questions with direct argumentative statements.

For example:
Instead of “If one life is taken, is it right to take another?”
Write: Opponents argue that taking another life does not morally justify the original crime.

Grammar and language corrections

You show strong control of English overall, but there are several language issues to revise:

Capitalization
“both sides cite statistics…” → Both sides cite statistics…

Word choice
“put a stop to it all together” → put a stop to it altogether
“lack of ethics” → ethical problems or moral injustice
“known murderer” may sound too absolute; consider convicted murderer

Spelling / formatting
“nu merous” → numerous

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and may confuse the reader. Break them into two sentences when necessary.

For example:
“However, the opposing side offers more accurate statistics as well as more valid reasons…”
This is clear, but some later sentences become overloaded with clauses.

Thesis and conclusion

Your thesis is clear, but it could be made more precise. Instead of saying the opposing side is stronger simply because it has “more accurate statistics,” state your own argument more directly.

For example:
Because it fails to deter crime, risks unconstitutional miscarriages of justice, and raises serious moral concerns, capital punishment should be abolished in the United States.

Your conclusion is effective, but it mostly repeats earlier points. Try to end with a stronger final sentence that highlights the broader significance of the issue.

Final revision priorities

Complete or replace all missing evidence and citations.
Develop analysis after each piece of evidence.
Improve paragraph unity and transitions.
Use more formal academic language and fewer rhetorical questions.
Edit grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity.

You already have a solid argumentative structure. With stronger evidence, fuller explanation, and careful editing, this essay could become much more persuasive and academically convincing.

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that Prozac should continue to be prescribed because it helps people with depression and has had positive medical and social effects. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. You also try to address opposing views, which is important in persuasion. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths

Logical organization: The essay generally moves from medical benefits, to social benefits, to critics’ concerns, and then to a conclusion.
Use of counterargument: You recognize criticism about suicide, side effects, and personality change. This makes the essay more balanced.
Relevant topic sentences: Many paragraphs begin with a clear main idea.

Most important revision issue: evidence

The biggest weakness is that many quotations and references are incomplete. Several places contain “”, which makes your evidence unusable. In an academic argument, readers must be able to see exactly what the source says.

What to do
Replace every “” with the actual quotation or remove it.
Introduce the source clearly: who is speaking, what their expertise is, and why their opinion matters.
After each quotation, explain how it supports your argument.

Example
Instead of:
> One doctor commented that Prozac .

Write:
> One physician argues that Prozac helps many patients regain emotional stability. This supports the claim that the drug can reduce symptoms without causing the extreme highs and lows associated with other substances.

Even better, include the exact quotation and the source.

Improve the introduction

Your introduction is broad and spends too much time on general comments about medicine. Try to get to the topic faster.

Current problem
The opening about “home remedies” and “advanced chemistry” is interesting, but it feels distant from the main issue of Prozac.

Revision advice
Begin with depression or controversy over antidepressants.
State the debate more directly.
End with a precise thesis.

Possible model
> Prozac has been controversial since it became widely used as an antidepressant. Although some critics argue that it can cause harmful side effects or personality changes, Prozac should continue to be prescribed because it has been effective for many patients, has been judged safe by medical authorities, and often improves patients’ ability to function socially.

Develop analysis, not just examples

You include examples such as Sam and Tess, but sometimes the essay simply tells their stories without fully explaining the argument.

Ask after each example:
What does this case prove?
Is this one person’s experience enough?
How does it connect to the thesis?

For instance, Tess’s dating life may not by itself prove medical value. You need to explain that her improved relationships suggest better emotional functioning after treatment.

Be careful with claims and tone

Some statements sound too absolute or too informal.

Examples
“This cannot be cast aside.” → too dramatic
“what is wrong with making yourself better?” → rhetorical, but too casual for academic writing
“should be easily tolerable” → too dismissive of side effects

Better approach
Use more cautious academic language:
“These concerns should be taken seriously.”
“However, available evidence suggests that severe adverse outcomes are rare.”
“For some patients, side effects may be manageable compared with the effects of untreated depression.”

This makes your argument sound more credible and fair.

Grammar and word choice

There are several sentence-level issues. Here are common patterns to revise:

“banish ment” → “banishment”
“an architect name Sam” → “an architect named Sam”
“prescribed with Prozac” → “prescribed Prozac”
“pas social lives” → “past social lives” or better, “normal social lives”
“Some patients com plain” → “Some patients complain”
“non-addicting effects” → better: “non-addictive nature”
“The drug causes the serotonin to slow in spaces” → awkward/inaccurate. Better: “The drug increases the availability of serotonin between nerve cells.”

Also check article use, verb tense, and awkward phrasing throughout.

Paragraph-level suggestions

Paragraph 2: Explain Prozac’s function more accurately and clearly. Avoid scientific wording unless you are sure it is correct.
Paragraph 3: Good point about safety, but it needs stronger evidence and source support.
Paragraph 4: Social benefits are useful, but make sure they relate to treating depression, not just becoming more socially active.
Paragraphs 5–6: Good attempt at addressing opposition. Try to present critics fairly, then respond with evidence.
Conclusion: End by summarizing your strongest reasons, not by asking a rhetorical question.

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these priorities:

Fix all missing quotations and citations.
Make the introduction more direct and focused.
Strengthen analysis after each example.
Use a more formal and balanced academic tone.
Edit grammar and word choice carefully.

You already have a solid argumentative structure. With better evidence, clearer language, and more precise analysis, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position, a recognizable introduction, and a paragraph structure that helps the reader follow your ideas. You also attempt to compare two articles and explain why one is more convincing than the other, which is a strong approach for an argumentative response. Below are the main areas to revise.

Strengths

Clear thesis/position: You clearly argue that Pattullo’s article is more persuasive than Wall’s.
Organized structure: Your essay moves from introduction to body paragraphs to conclusion in a logical way.
Comparative focus: You do not only summarize; you compare the two authors’ arguments.
Authorial voice: Your writing shows confidence and engagement with the topic.

Main Problems to Revise

A. Missing evidence and incomplete quotations
Several places in the essay include , which means key textual evidence is missing. This is the biggest problem in the paper. Because your argument depends on comparing two authors, you need to include actual quotations or accurate paraphrases from both texts.

For example:
“He states that, .”
“He feels that, .”
“Pattullo also points out that the lack of, .”

Without evidence, the reader cannot judge whether your interpretation is fair or convincing.

What to do:  
Add short quotations and then explain them. Use this pattern:
Introduce the source  
Quote or paraphrase  
Explain how it supports your point

B. Reasoning needs stronger support
At times, your essay repeats Pattullo’s claims without questioning them or proving them. For example, you argue that allowing gay people in the military would “destroy” unity and camaraderie, but you do not provide evidence beyond Pattullo’s opinion. This makes the essay sound more emotional than analytical.

Also, some claims are very broad:
“This would offend many heterosexuals”
“It would be difficult to control what goes on in the barracks”
“homosexuality will be virtually accepted in the military”

These statements need support and careful wording. Ask yourself:
Is this a fact, an opinion, or an assumption?
Can I prove it from the text?
Is there another possible viewpoint?

A stronger essay would explain why Pattullo’s logic is persuasive, not simply agree with it.

C. Tone and fairness
In academic argument, it is important to represent the opposing side fairly. Sometimes your discussion of Wall is dismissive rather than analytical. For example, “Wall attacks the issue… with a lighthearted attitude” may be too strong unless you prove it clearly with quotation and explanation.

Try to describe his argument more neutrally first, then explain why you think it is weaker.

Language and Grammar Issues

Below are some sentence-level corrections.

“the right for homosexuals to enlist”  
  Better: “the right of homosexuals to enlist”

“there should be a ban gays serving in the military”  
  Better: “there should be a ban on gays serving in the military”

“will make on change their mind”  
  Better: “will make one change one’s mind” or “will change the reader’s mind”

“James M. Wall’s articles does not give”  
  Better: “James M. Wall’s article does not give”

“share a barrack”  
  Better: “share a barracks room” or simply “share barracks”

“for whomever”  
  Better: “for whoever” or “for anyone involved”

“there should be homosexuals in the military solely to retain the basic civil rights”  
  Better: “homosexuals should be allowed in the military in order to protect basic civil rights”

“there was not reason why”  
  Better: “there was no reason why”

“claims has had an affect”  
  Better: “claims have had an effect”

Also watch for:
subject-verb agreement (“article does,” not “article do”)
word choice (“effect” vs. “affect”)
article use (“the military,” “a ban,” “the company”)
awkward phrasing

Introduction and Conclusion

Introduction
Your introduction gives historical context, which is useful, but it is a little long and contains some awkward sentences. Try to make the thesis more precise. For example:

Possible revision:

This is clearer and more direct.

Your conclusion restates your view, but the sentence “Before reading Pattullo’s article…” is confusing and seems to contradict your earlier position. If you mean your opinion changed after reading, say so clearly.

Revision Priorities

Insert the missing quotations/evidence
Clarify your thesis
Explain your reasoning more fully
Use a more formal and balanced academic tone
Edit grammar and word choice carefully

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that mandatory drug testing for athletes is ethical and necessary. This is a strong topic, and you show good awareness of fairness, health, and role-model responsibility. Your introduction also gives useful background and presents a clear thesis. These are good foundations for revision.

Strengths
Clear thesis
   - Your main argument is easy to identify: drug testing should be mandatory.
Relevant reasons
   - You give three strong reasons: fair competition, athlete health, and influence on young people.
Good use of examples
   - The Ben Johnson example is effective because it shows real consequences of steroid use.
Logical overall structure
   - The essay moves from ethics, to health effects, to examples, and then to possible solutions.

Main Areas to Improve

Complete and support your evidence
The biggest problem is that many quotations are missing and replaced with “”. This makes your essay feel unfinished and weakens your argument.

Add the exact quotations, or
Paraphrase the source clearly instead of quoting.

Also, explain each source after introducing it. Don’t only insert a quotation—tell the reader why it matters.

Example:
Instead of:
> Dr Gary Wadler agrees, .

Write:
> Dr. Gary Wadler argues that steroid use gives athletes an unfair advantage and damages the integrity of sports. This supports the idea that testing is necessary to protect honest competitors.

Improve paragraph focus and development
Some paragraphs contain several ideas mixed together. Try to make one main point per paragraph.

A possible structure:
Paragraph 1: Ethics and fairness
Paragraph 2: Health risks
Paragraph 3: Athletes as role models
Paragraph 4: Examples of testing programs and how they work
Conclusion

This will make your essay more persuasive and easier to follow.

Strengthen your counterargument
You mention privacy concerns, which is good, but the counterargument is not fully developed. A strong argumentative essay should show the other side fairly before responding to it.

For example:
Opponents say random testing violates privacy.
You can respond that athletes already accept many regulations to ensure safety and fairness in competition.

This will make your argument sound more balanced and mature.

Language and Grammar Suggestions

Word form and vocabulary
There are several small errors with word choice or form.

Examples:
athletes rights → athletes’ rights
long term → long-term
unan nounced → unannounced
lesson → lessen
meathod → method
strickter → stricter
On example → One example
Lial Alsado → likely Lyle Alzado

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long or awkward. Try to make them more direct.

Example:

Better:
> Mandatory drug testing would reduce and eventually help prevent the use of performance-enhancing drugs.

Punctuation and hyphenation
You sometimes need commas or hyphens:
performance enhancing drugs → performance-enhancing drugs
100- meter → 100-meter
Add commas after introductory phrases when needed.

Avoid informal or unclear questions
Rhetorical questions can be effective, but you use several in a way that sounds informal.

Example:

This should either be a proper question mark or rewritten more academically:

Style and Academic Tone
Your tone is generally serious, which is good. To sound more academic:
Avoid emotional phrasing like “Worst of all”
Use more formal transitions such as:
  - Furthermore
  - In addition
  - However
  - Therefore
  - For example

Suggested Revision Priorities
Replace all “” with real evidence.
Revise each paragraph so it has one clear topic sentence.
Improve your counterargument about privacy.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors.
Make your conclusion slightly stronger by restating your three main reasons in a concise way.

Final Comment
This essay has a strong central argument and a relevant topic, and you already have the basis of a persuasive paper. The most important next step is to make it feel complete, organized, and well supported. If you revise the evidence, improve paragraph unity, and correct language errors, your essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you compare different views on prayer in public schools and argue that a moment of silence is the best solution. This is a strong argumentative goal, and your position is clear from the introduction. You also show effort in discussing multiple sources rather than only giving personal opinion. That is a good foundation for revision.

Strengths
Clear thesis  
   Your introduction presents your main claim: the Civil Liberties side is better supported than the Conservative Religious side, but the best solution is the one in “Jefferson Still Lives.”

Organization by viewpoint  
   The essay is divided into sections: first the Conservative Religious Group, then the Civil Liberties Group, then your preferred solution. This helps the reader follow your argument.

Attempt to evaluate evidence  
   You do more than summarize. You judge whether reasons are strong, weak, biased, or unsupported. That is an important argumentative skill.

Main Areas to Revise

Add the missing evidence
The biggest problem is that many quotations are replaced by “”. Because of this, the reader cannot evaluate your analysis. Your essay often says “this is a good statement” or “this is weak reasoning,” but we cannot see the actual evidence.

What to do:
Insert the real quotations or paraphrases from the articles.
After each quote, explain exactly how it supports your point.
Avoid repeating “this is a good statement.” Instead, say *why* it is effective or ineffective.

Example revision:
Instead of:  
“This is a good statement but the fact that the court ruled…”  
Try:  
“Although the Supreme Court protected student speech in 1969, this example does not fully address the issue of state-sponsored prayer. Freedom of speech protects individual expression, but school-led prayer raises a separate constitutional question about government endorsement of religion.”

Strengthen paragraph structure
Many body paragraphs list points, but they do not always develop them fully. A strong paragraph should have:
a topic sentence,
evidence,
analysis,
a link back to your thesis.

Suggested structure:
Topic sentence: state the argument you are evaluating.
Evidence: quote or paraphrase the article.
Analysis: explain why the reasoning is strong or weak.
Conclusion sentence: connect to your overall position.

Improve fairness and precision
You often call one side “biased” or “weak,” but sometimes the explanation is too general. In academic argument, try to be specific and balanced.

For example, instead of saying:
“uses a biased account”
say:
“relies more on emotional appeal than on constitutional evidence.”

Instead of:
“less factual article”
say:
“less convincing because it does not provide evidence linking school prayer to lower crime or divorce rates.”

This will make your tone more academic.

Clarify your own argument
Your thesis is promising, but it is slightly too long and hard to follow. Simplify it so the reader immediately understands your position.

Possible revised thesis:
“Although the Civil Liberties argument against school prayer is better supported than the Conservative Religious argument for it, a moment of silence offers the most practical and respectful solution for public schools.”

Language and Style

Word choice and grammar
There are frequent spelling and word-form errors. Some examples:
arrise → *arise*
know as → *known as*
torwads / torwards / torward → *toward*
conatin → *contain*
reaons → *reasons*
stengthen → *strengthen*
mionorities → *minorities*
Libery → *Liberty*
affacts → *effects* or *affects* depending on meaning

Please proofread carefully for spelling and verb forms.

Sentence style
Some sentences are awkward or too long. For example:
“Each of these groups has different ethical values and beliefs to cause them to back up their own side.”
A clearer version:
“Each group supports its position because of different ethical values and beliefs.”

Try to write shorter, more direct sentences.

Formal academic tone
Avoid phrases like:
“o.k.”
“bring out the idea”
“This is good use by the author”

Use more formal alternatives:
“acceptable”
“introduces the idea”
“The author effectively uses this point”

Conclusion
You have a solid topic, a clear position, and a good basic structure. Your revision should focus on three priorities:

Insert the missing quotations/paraphrases
Develop analysis more specifically
Edit grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity

If you make those changes, your essay will become much more convincing and academic. Your central idea—that a moment of silence respects both religious freedom and religious diversity—is thoughtful and worth developing.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You present a clear position: you oppose the death penalty because it does not reduce crime, it is immoral, and it harms society. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. Your paper also shows good awareness of counterarguments and attempts to use sources. Below are the main areas to revise.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: Your opinion is easy to understand.
Logical structure: The essay has an introduction, body paragraphs with different reasons, and a conclusion.
Good argumentative purpose: You do more than express feelings; you try to persuade readers.
Awareness of complexity: You mention morality, deterrence, fairness, and politics, which gives the essay breadth.

Most important revision priorities

A. Complete and support your evidence
Several places contain “”, which suggests missing quotations or evidence. These gaps weaken your argument significantly. You should either:
insert the exact quotation or statistic, or
paraphrase the source clearly and cite it properly.

For example:
“In 1983 a scientist named Forst did one such study, and he concluded that, .”
“The writer Jim Gittings agrees with me in his statement .”

If you keep references to sources, make sure readers know:
who the source is,
what exactly the source argues,
why that evidence supports your point.

B. Use a more formal academic tone
Some expressions are too conversational for an academic essay:
“Well, I believe…”
“Well it has been proven…”
“something to talk about”
“huge mistake”

Replace these with formal alternatives:
“I argue that…”
“Research indicates that…”
“public controversy”
“serious crime” or “grave offense”

Also avoid emotional or imprecise wording like “murdering someone because they made a huge mistake.” In an academic essay, precise language is stronger than dramatic language.

C. Improve paragraph unity
Each body paragraph should focus on one main claim.

For example, your third paragraph discusses:
morality,
justice for victims,
social debate,
the purpose of the judicial system.

These ideas are related, but the paragraph becomes too broad. Consider splitting it:
one paragraph on the moral argument,
another on whether execution brings justice or closure.

Organization and argumentation

Introduction
Your introduction gives useful background, but it is too long and repetitive. Move more quickly to the thesis. Also, some background claims are too broad, such as “the crime rate has continued to increase.” If you make a factual claim like this, you need evidence.

Your thesis is effective, but you can make it more concise:
> “The death penalty should be abolished because it does not deter serious crime, it is morally unjustifiable, and it is applied unfairly.”

Body paragraphs
Try this pattern for each paragraph:
topic sentence,
evidence,
explanation,
link back to thesis.

At present, you often move from one idea to another without enough explanation. For example, in the fairness paragraph, you mention rich criminals, appeals, crowded courts, and wrongful convictions. These are all useful, but they need clearer connections.

Conclusion
Your conclusion restates the main idea, which is good. However, it becomes informal and emotional at the end. Try to finish with a stronger academic statement about justice, public policy, or human rights.

Language issues to correct

Here are some common errors from the essay:

“increases crime” → “increased crime” or “the increase in crime”
“debating over” → “debating”
“it necessity” → “its necessity”
“the rate of homicides” → acceptable, but “the homicide rate” is more natural
“full-proof” → “foolproof”
“a death penalty” → usually “the death penalty”
“so ciety” → “society”
“If somebody makes a horrible mistake of murdering someone else”  
  Better: “If someone commits murder”
“then is it moral” → “is it moral, then,” or rewrite for clarity
“many politicians, especially Liberal Democrats”  
  Be careful with political labels unless you can support them accurately.

Also review:
comma use after introductory phrases,
subject-verb agreement,
article use (“a,” “an,” “the”),
sentence clarity.

Suggestions for stronger revision

replace generalizations with facts
define key terms like “immoral,” “justice,” and “deterrence”
use specific evidence rather than personal opinion alone
acknowledge counterarguments fairly before refuting them

For instance, instead of “it has been proven,” write:

That sounds more precise and academic.

Final advice
Your essay has a solid central argument and good potential. The biggest improvements will come from:
filling in the missing evidence,
using a more formal tone,
strengthening paragraph focus,
correcting grammar and wording.
