Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that prayer should not return to public school classrooms, and you support this view by responding to arguments from the other side. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. You also show awareness of counterarguments, which is an important academic skill. To revise effectively, focus on evidence, organization, style, and language accuracy.

Strengths

Clear thesis: Your main claim is consistent throughout the essay.
Use of counterargument and rebuttal: You do not simply state your opinion; you engage with supporters’ claims and challenge them.
Engaging introduction: The opening classroom scene is vivid and helps readers understand why the issue matters.
Reasonable conclusion: Your final paragraph returns to the main constitutional and practical issues.

Most important revision issue: missing and incomplete evidence

The biggest problem is that many quotations and references are incomplete, marked with “”. In its current form, this seriously weakens the essay because readers cannot evaluate your evidence.

What to do:
Replace every “” with the exact quotation, paraphrase, or information from the source.
Identify each source clearly: author, title, and publication if relevant.
If you mention statistics, explain where they come from.
Make sure each body paragraph includes:
  1. a claim,
  2. evidence,
  3. your explanation of why that evidence supports your claim.

For example, instead of writing that a source “states ,” introduce the idea and analyze it:
*Ralph Reed argues that school prayer would create better citizens, but he does not explain the mechanism by which reciting a prayer would improve moral behavior.*

Organization and argument development

Your essay generally follows a logical pattern, but some paragraphs need stronger topic sentences and clearer connections.

Suggestions:
Each paragraph should focus on one main point.
Avoid repeating the phrase “a second claim” twice. That confuses the structure.
Group similar ideas together. For example:
  - Paragraphs on majority opinion/polling
  - Paragraphs on morality and social decline
  - Paragraphs on non-sectarian prayer as a solution
  - Paragraphs on your proposed alternative: voluntary groups

Thesis revision
Your thesis is understandable, but it is too long. Try a more direct version:
*Although supporters of school prayer argue that it reflects public opinion and would improve students’ morals, prayer should not be reintroduced into public school classrooms because it violates religious freedom, excludes minorities, and lacks evidence as a solution to social problems.*

Analysis: move beyond summary

Sometimes you summarize what supporters or opponents say, but your own analysis needs to be stronger. Academic argument improves when you explain why the evidence matters.

For example:
When discussing polls, do not only say the statistic may be biased. Explain why majority opinion does not decide constitutional rights.
When discussing moral decline, explain that prayer itself is not the same as ethical education or social reform.
When discussing bland, non-sectarian prayer, explain how a state-written prayer can satisfy neither sincere believers nor non-believers.

Style and academic tone

Your tone is mostly appropriate, but some wording can be made more precise.

Examples:
“it is obvious” → This sounds too absolute. Try: *the evidence suggests* or *the opposing arguments demonstrate*.
“social ills” is acceptable, but it becomes repetitive. Vary with *social problems*, *violence*, *moral concerns*, etc
“destroy the separation of church and state” could be softened to *undermine the principle of separation between church and state*.

Also, avoid rhetorical questions too often. One or two can be effective, but in academic writing, direct analysis is usually stronger.

Language corrections

Here are some common errors to revise:

first Amendment → First Amendment
wide-spread → widespread
many writes → many writers
intrest → interest
at the sake of → for the sake of
oppotunity → opportunity
parishoners → parishioners
daily prayer before lunch must be said → acceptable, but *is recited* sounds smoother

Sentence-level examples:
“These numbers, according to the supporters, are to reflect the desire…”  
  → *Supporters argue that these numbers reflect public desire for prayer in schools.*
“This prayer according to opponents is anything but the perfect solution.”  
  → *According to opponents, however, this prayer is far from a perfect solution.*
“Students may be turned off to religion for good.”  
  → *Students may become alienated from religion altogether.*

Final advice for revision

When you revise, prioritize in this order:

Complete all missing quotations and citations
Strengthen paragraph structure and topic sentences
Add more of your own analysis after each source
Edit grammar, spelling, and word choice

You have a solid argumentative base and a meaningful topic. With stronger evidence and cleaner organization, this essay could become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: government-sponsored prayer should not be instituted in public schools. This is a strong, arguable thesis, and you stay focused on it throughout the essay. You also attempt to consider opposing views, which is an important feature of argumentative writing. Below are suggestions to help you revise more effectively.

Strengths

Clear central argument: Your main claim is easy to identify.
Consistent focus: Most paragraphs relate to the main issue of prayer in public schools.
Use of counterargument: You include an opposing view and respond to it.
Serious tone: Your essay sounds formal and appropriate for an academic argument.

Main revision priorities

A. Make your thesis more precise
Your thesis appears at the end of the first paragraph, but it is too long and slightly unclear.

Current idea:
> “Time for prayer in public schools should not be instituted... because religion is imposed on the children...”

A clearer version could be:
> Government-sponsored prayer should not be instituted in public schools because it imposes religion on students, undermines genuine religious expression, and distracts schools from their educational purpose.

This version gives you 3 clear reasons that can organize your body paragraphs.

B. Improve paragraph organization
Some paragraphs contain several ideas at once. Try to use this structure:

Topic sentence: state one main reason.
Explanation: explain the reason.
Evidence/example: include support.
Analysis: explain how the evidence supports your point.
Link back to thesis.

For example, your second paragraph discusses:
parents teaching morals,
religious communities,
school curriculum,
whether students will pray seriously.

These ideas are related, but they should be more tightly connected around one paragraph focus.

C. Replace vague or missing evidence
You refer to many sources, but several quotations are shown as , so the reader cannot evaluate your evidence. In revision:
insert the actual quotations if required,
or summarize the source clearly,
and explain why each source matters.

Also, do not rely too heavily on listing publications. Instead of repeatedly naming dates and newspapers, focus on the idea from the source and your analysis of it.

Argumentation issues

A. Avoid unsupported generalizations
Some claims are too broad, for example:
“The majority of communities would, indeed, find agreement...”
“Students will not pray since there will be no reason...”
“The majority would simply receive a prayer that would pertain to them only.”

These statements may or may not be true, but they need evidence. If you cannot prove them, soften them:
“Many communities might agree...”
“Some students may not take the prayer seriously...”

B. Clarify your position on after-school religious activities
You argue against prayer during school hours, but later you support school-based religious activities after class. This is interesting, but you need to explain the distinction more clearly:
voluntary vs. required,
student-led vs. government-sponsored,
after school vs. during instructional time.

Without this explanation, the reader may think your argument is inconsistent.

C. Strengthen the counterargument paragraph
You do include the opposing side, but your response could be more direct. State the opponent’s point clearly, then answer it with logic:
Yes, some children lack moral guidance at home.
However, government-written prayer is not the best solution.
Community support, counseling, and voluntary religious groups are better alternatives.

Language and grammar

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are several grammar and wording problems. Here are common patterns to revise:

A. Word choice
“moral values i children” → moral values in children
“if they are of a religion” → if they belong to a religion
“become angered” → become angry
“degrade the already decadent public school system” → further weaken the already struggling public school system

B. Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long and awkward. Break them into shorter units.

Example:
> “Prayer is a time to reflect, praise, or forgive, and a classroom is not the proper place or time to seriously take into consideration the healing of the soul.”

Possible revision:
> Prayer is a time for reflection, praise, and forgiveness. A classroom, however, is not an appropriate place for such deeply personal spiritual practice.

C. Grammar corrections
“Does this means” → Does this mean
“Even is there is” → Even if there is
“students could utilize time” → students could use time
“those students that do not believe in God” → those students who do not believe in God

D. Avoid repetition
You repeat “public schools,” “moral values,” and “government” very often. Try synonyms where appropriate:
schools, classrooms, the state, official prayer, religious instruction

Style improvements

Use a more academic tone by avoiding rhetorical questions too often.
Be careful with emotionally charged words like “decadent” unless you can justify them.
Prefer precise wording over dramatic wording.

Suggested revision plan

Rewrite the thesis into 2–3 clear reasons.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each has one main point.
Add or complete the missing quotations/evidence.
Explain more clearly why voluntary after-school religion is different from state-sponsored prayer.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear engagement with an important ethical issue, and your position is easy to identify: you argue that animal testing should continue because it protects human life. You also try to consider the opposing view, which is a strong feature of argumentative writing. To revise effectively, focus on four areas: thesis and organization, use of evidence, academic tone, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear topic and stance: You clearly argue in favor of animal testing in at least some circumstances.
Relevant ethical focus: You connect the issue to morality, religion, and human benefit.
Attempt to include counterarguments: You mention animal rights activists and describe their objections.
Concrete examples: The Timmy/epinephrine case gives your essay a human-centered example.

Main problems to revise

A. Clarify your thesis
Your argument changes slightly across the essay. Sometimes you argue that all animal testing is necessary; other times you say some experiments should be avoided. These are not the same claim.

Try to state a more precise thesis, for example:
“Animal testing should remain legal for essential medical research that saves human lives, but non-essential and excessively painful experiments should be restricted or banned.”

This would match your later point that some experiments are “evil and inhumane.”

B. Improve organization
Right now, the essay repeats ideas and moves back and forth between points. A clearer structure would help.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: explain the issue and present your thesis.
Background: define animal testing and why it is controversial.
Argument 1: medical benefits for humans.
Argument 2: lack of adequate alternatives.
Counterargument: animal suffering and activists’ concerns.
Response/qualification: some testing is unjustified and should be limited.
Conclusion: restate your balanced position.

Also, some paragraphs begin strongly but then drift into new ideas. Make sure each paragraph has one main point.

C. Use stronger evidence
Many parts of the essay rely on emotional examples or unsupported statements. For an academic argument, you need evidence that is:
specific,
accurate,
cited,
and clearly explained.

Examples:
“Seventeen to twenty-two million animals are killed...” — this needs a source.
Biblical claims such as humans having authority over animals need an exact quotation and should be presented carefully, since not all readers share the same religious beliefs.
The thalidomide example is confusing and may be historically inaccurate. Be careful: thalidomide is associated with serious birth defects, so this paragraph needs fact-checking.
Several places contain , suggesting missing quotations or sources. These must be completed or removed.

A good revision strategy is to use 2–4 well-researched sources and explain them fully rather than giving many unsupported examples.

Academic tone and argumentation
Some sentences sound emotional or biased, which weakens your credibility in an academic essay.

Examples:
“Thank God for little Timmy...”
“Animals were put on earth for humans to do with as they please...”
“if human's want to continue as the superior species”

These phrases sound informal, absolute, or ideological. Try replacing them with more neutral academic language:
“This case suggests that animal research may contribute to life-saving treatments.”
“Some religious perspectives place humans above animals in moral priority.”
“Supporters argue that human welfare should take precedence in medical research.”

Also avoid rhetorical questions too often. One or two can be effective, but too many make the essay sound less analytical.

Language issues to correct

A. Grammar and word choice
There are many sentence-level errors. A few examples:

“Some of them easily justified” → “Some of them are easily justified”
“which friends side to take” → “which friend’s side to take”
“base this argument of experience” → “base this argument on experience”
“Pain less” → “painless”
“animals my not be conscious” → “animals may not be conscious”
“medicine aides in the healing off birth defects” → “medicine that aids in treating…”  
“to insure” → “to ensure”

B. Agreement and pronouns
“human's want” → “humans want”
“Animal researches also argue” → “Animal researchers also argue”
“the researcher and their centers” → “researchers and their centers”

C. Punctuation and mechanics
Avoid comma errors such as:
“An example of this, is...” → “An example of this is...”
Also correct spelling:
“contro versy” → “controversy”
“reconciliation oath” may not be the right term; perhaps “oath of confidentiality” or “seal of confession.”

Most important revision priorities
Write a precise thesis that matches your actual position.
Reorganize the essay so each paragraph supports one clear point.
Remove or complete all missing quotations/sources ().
Fact-check your examples, especially thalidomide.
Adopt a more formal academic tone.
Edit grammar carefully for sentence structure, possessives, and word choice.

Final advice
You have the basis for a thoughtful argument, especially because you recognize both human benefit and animal suffering. Your revision will be much stronger if you present a balanced, carefully supported claim rather than a purely emotional defense. Aim to show not only what you believe, but why a critical reader should accept your reasoning.

you should include:

examples from specific schools,

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that distributing condoms in high schools is necessary to reduce the spread of AIDS. This is a strong, focused claim, and your essay is organized around responding to objections. That argumentative structure works well. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths

Clear thesis: In the introduction, you clearly state your opinion.
Logical organization: Each body paragraph addresses a different opposing argument, which makes your essay easy to follow.
Relevant topic sentences: Most paragraphs begin with a claim that connects to your overall argument.
Awareness of counterarguments: This is one of the strongest features of the essay. You do not ignore the other side.

Main revision priorities

A. Replace missing evidence
The biggest problem is the many places marked with . These gaps make the essay feel unfinished and weaken your credibility. Since your argument depends heavily on facts, statistics, and quotations, you must either:

insert the exact evidence and cite it properly, or
rewrite those sentences more generally if you do not have the original source.

Without this evidence, several claims sound unsupported.

B. Make your argument more precise
Sometimes your language is too absolute. For example:

“This shows that the idea of abstinence is a mere fantasy.”
“Condoms are the only means to prevent AIDS from spreading among sexually active people.”

These statements are too broad. A stronger academic style would be more careful:

“This suggests that abstinence-only messages are often ineffective for many teenagers.”
“Condoms are one of the most important methods of reducing the sexual transmission of HIV.”

Avoid overgeneralizing, especially on sensitive public health topics.

C. Update terminology and accuracy
“AIDS” is used throughout, but in many sentences you actually mean HIV infection or HIV transmission, not AIDS itself. AIDS is a later stage of HIV infection. For accuracy, consider revising some sentences to say:

“prevent HIV transmission”
“reduce the spread of HIV”
“protect students from HIV infection”

This will make your argument more medically precise.

Organization and development

Your structure is solid, but some paragraphs need stronger analysis. Often you present evidence and then move on quickly. After each piece of evidence, explain:

What does this evidence show?
Why does it matter?
How does it refute the opposing argument?

For example, in the paragraph about abstinence, you include statistics about sexually active teens. After that, explain more directly that public policy should address actual student behavior, not only ideal behavior.

Also, your conclusion repeats earlier points. Try ending by reinforcing the broader principle: schools have a responsibility to protect student health while still providing education about abstinence and responsibility.

Style and tone

Your tone is persuasive, but at times it becomes emotional or informal. Phrases like:

“this horrible epidemic”
“should be reason enough”
“mere fantasy”

can sound too dramatic. Academic argument is usually more effective when it sounds calm and evidence-based. Try replacing emotional wording with precise language.

Sentence-level language corrections

Here are some important grammar and word-choice issues:

“a step which needs to be taken” → “a step that needs to be taken”
“compared to anything proposed previously” → “compared with anything previously proposed”
“grounds to pre-marital sex” → “grounds related to premarital sex” or “because they oppose premarital sex”
“steer away form” → “steer away from”
“John Hopkins Medical School” → likely “Johns Hopkins Medical School”
“more apt to practice safer sex” → “more likely to practice safer sex”
“According to an analysis of a 1988 national survey of adolescent male” → “adolescent males”
“than anyone who engages” → “then anyone who engages” is wrong here; actually the sentence should be “If condoms are our only means of protection…, then anyone…”
“the rate in which AIDS is spreading” → “the rate at which HIV/AIDS is spreading”
“the refutals of arguments” → “the refutations of these arguments”

Also watch article use (“a,” “the”), plural forms, and prepositions.

Suggestions for revision

When you rewrite, try this plan:

Complete all missing quotations/statistics or remove them.
Revise the thesis to be more precise: condom distribution should be part of a broader health education strategy.
Strengthen analysis after each example.
Use more cautious academic language and avoid sweeping claims.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and word choice.

Possible revised thesis

You might revise your thesis to something like:

“Although some parents and religious leaders argue that school condom distribution encourages sexual activity, providing condoms together with accurate sex education is a practical public health measure that can reduce HIV risk among sexually active teenagers.”

Overall, this essay has a strong foundation. Your argument is clear and your paragraphing is effective. The next step is to make the essay more complete, precise, and academically polished.

Logical overall structure: Many body paragraphs focus on one main reason.

Conviction: The essay has energy and purpose, which is valuable in argumentative writing.

Most important revision needs

examples,
facts,
brief references to court decisions,
or expert opinions.

For this topic, you could mention the constitutional principle of separation of church and state, or relevant Supreme Court cases such as *Engel v. Vitale*.

C. Avoid overstatement
Some claims are too absolute, which weakens your credibility. Examples:

“A prayer alone cannot subdue major societal problems.”

These may be partly true, but the wording sounds too extreme. Academic writing is stronger when it is careful and qualified:
“This argument is unconvincing because…”

This needs revision for clarity.

“determent” → “obstruction” or “interference”

A. Subject-verb agreement
“prayer lead” → “prayer leads”

B. Articles and prepositions
“in public school” → “in public schools”
“allow for its inclusion into” → “allow its inclusion in”

C. Word form
“discourage” → “discouraged”
“acceptable” is fine, but sometimes “persuasive” is better in argumentative writing.

Edit grammar, word choice, and awkward sentences.

Your essay has a clear position and a strong sense of moral purpose. You argue consistently that discrimination against people with HIV/AIDS is wrong, and you use examples to support that claim. This gives your essay passion and direction, which are important strengths in argumentative writing.

Some claims are too broad or factually inaccurate. For example:

“when someone becomes infected with HIV, it is going to develop into AIDS, and they are going to die” is medically outdated.
“AIDS as a disease is actually relatively young, and therefore is not fully understood” may have been more reasonable in older sources, but today it sounds inaccurate unless placed in historical context.

Because this topic involves health and law, accuracy is essential. Revise your claims so they are precise and supported.

Your essay is passionate, but at times the tone becomes too dramatic for academic writing:
“martyr to the AIDS community”
“ousted by corporate America”
“bury someone with AIDS alive”
“walking dead”

These phrases are powerful emotionally, but they can weaken your credibility. Try to persuade through evidence, logic, and careful wording, not only through strong emotion.

C. Improve organization
The essay has a thesis, but the body paragraphs need clearer structure. Try this pattern:
Topic sentence stating the paragraph’s main point  
Evidence/example
Explanation of how the evidence supports your argument
Link back to thesis

For example, your paragraphs on workplace cases would be stronger if each began with a sentence like:  
“Several documented employment cases show that HIV-positive workers were excluded not because of actual risk, but because of fear and stigma.”

D. Integrate sources properly
You use placeholders like “,” which means your evidence is incomplete. You must include:
the exact quotation or paraphrase
the source name
the relevance of the source

Also, avoid dropping quotations into the essay without explanation. After each quotation, explain what it shows and why it matters.

“work place”  
  → “workplace”
“hospice car”  
  → “hospice care”
“their final fays”  
  → “their final days”
“We societies of the world”  
  → “Societies around the world”

Punctuation

  - a statistic,
  - or a specific example.

“just out for themselves” → too informal
“they are just greedy” → too emotional

Common issues
A. Punctuation
Add commas after introductory phrases and between independent clauses when needed.
Example:
  - Original: “When someone thinks of business what do they think of?”
  - Correct: “When someone thinks of business, what do they think of?”

B. Word form / agreement
“Ethics are the goals…” → better: “Ethics is the study of…” or “Ethical behavior involves…”
“businesses are suppose to follow” → “businesses are supposed to follow”

C. Repetition
You repeat words like “relationship,” “profit,” and “business” too often. Use synonyms when possible:
relationship → trust, connection, partnership
profit → financial gain, earnings
business → company, corporation, firm, commercial activity

D. Informal or unclear expressions
“worked great” → “worked well”
“get you places in life” → “help people succeed”

Conclusion
Your conclusion has a good message, but it should sound more formal. Also correct language errors:
“Money can not buy you friends, health of happiness” should be “Money cannot buy friendship, health, or happiness.”

A stronger conclusion would connect your argument back to business success:
> In conclusion, profit alone cannot sustain a company. Ethical conduct and strong relationships create trust, loyalty, and long-term success in the business world.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these three priorities:
Complete the missing evidence
Make your thesis and paragraph structure clearer
Edit for grammar, formal tone, and repetition

You have a solid central idea. With stronger evidence and more careful organization, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

You have a clear overall position: Shriver’s argument is stronger than Methvin’s. Your essay also shows a good understanding of key argumentative concepts such as claim, support, opposition, and fallacies. The paper is organized in a logical way, moving from introduction to analysis of Methvin, then Shriver, and finally a conclusion. This gives your essay a solid base.  

To revise effectively, focus on (1) precision of argument, (2) use of evidence, and (3) sentence-level correctness/style.

Strengthen your thesis and analysis
Your thesis is clear, but it can be more precise. Right now, you argue that Shriver is stronger because he uses facts and avoids fallacies. That is a good start, but your body paragraphs sometimes repeat general statements such as “this is a strong argument” or “this is effective” without fully explaining why.

Revision advice:
Make your thesis more specific:
  - Current idea: Shriver is stronger because he uses facts and no fallacies.
  - Stronger version: Shriver presents a clearer claim, organizes his reasons more effectively, addresses opposing views, and supports his position with evidence that is better explained.
In each paragraph, explain not just what the author does, but how it persuades the reader.

For example, instead of:
> “This is a very strong aspect to his argument because it is hard to argue against.”

You could write:
> “This point strengthens Shriver’s argument because it appeals to a broad principle that many readers accept, making his conclusion seem reasonable even to those who may disagree with him politically or religiously.”

Use quotations and evidence more effectively
The biggest weakness in the current draft is the missing quotations marked by . Since your essay evaluates how each author argues, you must include the actual words from the texts. Without them, your analysis is incomplete and sometimes hard to trust.

Revision advice:
Insert the exact quotations from Methvin and Shriver.
Introduce each quotation clearly.
After each quotation, explain its significance in detail.

A useful pattern is:
Introduce quote → present quote → analyze quote

Example:
> Methvin argues that the First Amendment has been misunderstood when he states, “...”. However, this point is underdeveloped because he does not explain how this historical interpretation applies to modern public schools.

Also, be careful when calling something a fallacy. If you say Methvin uses an “appeal to unqualified authority,” you need to prove that the authority is actually unqualified. Otherwise, your criticism may seem unfair. You could weaken the claim slightly if needed:
> “Methvin relies heavily on quoted opinions, but he does not fully establish why these sources should persuade the reader.”

Develop a more balanced academic tone
Sometimes your language is too absolute:
“This is hard to disagree with.”
“It is hard to argue against.”
“The reader is lead to believe that he is hiding something.”

These claims may sound too strong unless you support them carefully.

Revision advice:
Use more cautious academic language:
“This may be persuasive because…”
“This is likely to appeal to readers who…”
“This omission may weaken his credibility because…”

This makes your writing sound more analytical and less emotional.

Improve paragraph focus and transitions
Most paragraphs have a clear purpose, but some are too long and contain several ideas at once. For example, your paragraph on Shriver’s support includes organization, theology, opposition, and Biblical quotation all together. Breaking it into two paragraphs would improve clarity.

Revision advice:
Give each paragraph one main focus:
one paragraph on clear claim
one on quality of evidence
one on treatment of opposition
one on organization and effectiveness

Also improve transitions. For example:
“In contrast to Methvin…”
“More importantly…”
“Another reason Shriver is more persuasive is…”

Sentence-level grammar and style
There are many grammar and wording errors that make the essay less polished. Here are some important patterns to fix:

Articles and word forms
“influential factor” → an influential factor
“ha become” → has become
“A research” → As research for his argument / In his research
“praying in the public schools” → usually prayer in public schools

Sentence structure
“people and learned through war and consequence” → people learned through war and its consequences
“Religion essentially was the foundation of society of the past” → Religion was essentially the foundation of past societies
“The founders of the Constitution first drafted…” → awkward; simplify.

Agreement and phrasing
“facts that support his position rather than just the opinions of people” → fine idea, but smoother as:
  evidence that more effectively supports his position rather than relying mainly on personal opinion
“The reader is lead” → The reader is led
“any where else” → anywhere else
“momen tum” → momentum

Capitalization/punctuation
“although” at the start of the final paragraph should be Although
Add commas after introductory phrases where needed.

Final revision priority list
Add the missing quotations.
Explain your evidence more fully.
Use more precise, less absolute analytical language.
Fix grammar and awkward phrasing.
Separate large paragraphs into more focused ones.

Overall, your essay has a strong structure and a clear argumentative direction. With better integration of textual evidence and careful editing for grammar and academic tone, this could become a convincing and well-developed rhetorical analysis.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: the media should be allowed to witness and report on executions, but filming should be restricted for safety reasons. This is a strong argumentative claim because it is not extreme; instead, it balances public access with security concerns. You also use a specific case (Robert Alton Harris) to support your argument, which gives the essay focus and credibility.

What is working well

Clear thesis
Your main argument appears in the introduction and stays consistent throughout the essay. The reader understands your position from the beginning.

Use of a real example
The Harris case helps ground the argument in a concrete legal and historical situation. This is much more effective than discussing the issue only in general terms.

Acknowledgement of the opposing view
You mention the argument that media coverage is “grisly” and driven by ratings. Including opposing views strengthens argumentative writing.

Logical distinction
Your essay makes an important distinction between witnessing/reporting and filming. That distinction is one of the strongest parts of the paper.

---

Main areas to revise

Complete missing quotations and evidence
Several places contain “”, which makes the argument feel unfinished. These missing parts are important because they seem to include legal reasoning or quotations from sources. Before revising style, make sure all these missing parts are filled in and properly introduced.

For example, when you write:
“Vasquez reasoned that ”
“on the grounds that it would ”
“the First Amendment … states ”

the reader needs the exact content to evaluate your claim.

Improve paragraph unity and organization
Some paragraphs mix background, legal details, and opinion. Try to make each paragraph do one job:

Introduction: thesis and scope
Background paragraph: Harris case and execution history
Legal conflict paragraph: media lawsuit and court decision
Counterargument paragraph: objections about ratings/gruesomeness
Rebuttal paragraph: why witnessing is justified
Limits paragraph: why filming may still be restricted
Conclusion: restate argument clearly

This structure will make your reasoning easier to follow.

Strengthen analysis
At times the essay summarizes events but does not fully explain why they support your argument. For example, after describing Judge Schnacke’s ruling, explain how this ruling supports the broader principle of press access. Don’t assume the reader will make that connection alone.

Use a more academic tone
Some sentences sound too informal or overly absolute, especially the ending:  
“If you find the event grisly and gruesome, turn it off; it is that simple.”  
This sounds emotional rather than academic. A stronger conclusion would emphasize public interest, constitutional principles, and reasonable restrictions.

---

Language and grammar corrections

Here are some sentence-level issues to revise:

“there access should be restricted” → “their access should be restricted”
“One such case which receive national attention” → “One such case that received national attention”
“The defendant, and person to be executed” → “The defendant, the person to be executed,”
“had made to effort” → “had made no effort”
“inmate are more tense” → “inmates are more tense”
“they are not reason to exclude journalists” → “they are not reasons to exclude journalists”

Also watch for word choice:
“persons undergoing the death penalty” sounds unnatural. Better: “people being executed” or “those facing execution.”
“put into effect” could be simpler: “carried out.”

Article use and prepositions
As a non-native writer, pay special attention to articles:
“the media should have access to report on executions” is fine
but “allow for reporters to be in the witness execution room” would be better as  
  “allowed reporters to be in the execution witness room.”

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long and confusing. For example:

Original:  
“The reason Schnacke made the decision that he did was because the warden, Vasquez, had made to effort to justify his ban on all reporters that no evidence was presented to suggest that reports using notebooks and pencils posed any security risks.”

Possible revision:  
“Schnacke ruled this way because Vasquez had made no effort to justify the total ban on reporters. No evidence showed that reporters using notebooks and pencils posed a security risk.”

This revision is clearer and easier to read.

---

Suggestions for revision

Fill in all missing quotations and citations.
Revise the introduction so the thesis is concise and formal.
Separate summary from argument: after each factual paragraph, add analysis.
Use more precise legal language when referring to the First Amendment and court rulings.
Edit grammar carefully, especially articles, verb tense, and plural forms.
Rewrite the conclusion in a more formal tone.

---

Final comment

You have the basis for a persuasive essay with a thoughtful central claim. Your strongest idea is that press access and public information are important, but safety-based limits on filming can still be justified. With better organization, fuller evidence, and sentence-level editing, this essay can become much more convincing and polished.

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0028.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear personal voice and a strong sense of conviction. The opening story is engaging, and your experience gives the topic emotional force. You also stay focused on one main issue: young people need guidance about sex and relationships. That is a strong foundation. For revision, the main goals are to clarify your argument, improve organization, and make the language more academic and precise.

Main strength: strong personal engagement
Your introduction is memorable because it begins with a real experience. This helps readers understand why the issue matters to you. Your conclusion also returns to your own life, which creates a sense of closure.

However, in an argumentative essay, personal experience alone is not enough. You need to combine it with clear claims, logical reasoning, and reliable evidence.

Clarify your thesis
Right now, your thesis is not fully clear. At different points, the essay seems to argue that schools should teach:

sex education,
sexual morals and values,
abstinence until marriage,
safe sex,
the value of marriage.

These are related, but they are not exactly the same. Choose one central claim and express it clearly.

For example, a stronger thesis might be:

“Because many parents avoid discussing sex, schools should provide comprehensive sex education that includes information about health, relationships, responsibility, and decision-making.”

This is stronger because it is specific and easier to support.

Improve organization
Several body paragraphs repeat similar ideas: parents do not teach enough, media influences youth, churches are not helping, and schools should step in. Try organizing the essay into clear sections.

A possible structure:

Introduction – personal story + thesis  
Problem 1: Parents often do not discuss sex openly  
Problem 2: Lack of guidance creates risks for teens  
Why schools can help – education about health, consent, relationships, and responsibility  
Counterargument – some say morals should not be taught in school  
Response – schools can teach respect and responsibility without replacing families  
Conclusion – return to personal experience

This structure would make your argument easier to follow.

Use evidence carefully
You mention statistics and studies, but some places have missing information: “”. These gaps seriously weaken your essay. If you use research, include:

the source,
the year,
the exact finding,
and why it supports your point.

Also, be careful not to overstate conclusions. For example, if a study shows that sex education reduces teen pregnancy, that does not automatically prove it teaches “the sacredness of marriage.” Keep your claims connected directly to your evidence.

Make the tone more academic
Some word choices sound emotional or judgmental, which can weaken an academic argument. For example:

“casual sex is unacceptable, its degrading, animalistic”
“Parents are hiding behind their bedroom doors”
“the church turning their backs on our youth”

These phrases are vivid, but they sound accusatory. In academic writing, it is better to sound balanced and reasoned. Instead, you could say:

“Casual sex can expose young people to emotional and physical risks.”
“Many parents feel uncomfortable discussing sexual topics.”
“Religious institutions may no longer play the same educational role they once did.”

This makes your argument stronger because it sounds fair and credible.

Address complexity
You often present your view as the only correct one. A stronger argument acknowledges complexity. For example, not all parents avoid sex education, and not all school programs are equally effective. Also, “morals and values” differ across families and cultures. You could argue that schools should teach health, consent, respect, and responsibility, while families and religious communities can add their own moral beliefs.

This would make your essay more persuasive to a wider audience.

Language and grammar issues to revise
There are many sentence-level errors. Focus especially on:

Capitalization: “father” and “him” should not usually be capitalized in the middle of a sentence.
Possessives/plurals: “the bird’s and the bee’s” should be “the birds and the bees.”
Word choice: “class mate” → classmate; “its degrading” → it’s degrading; “some one” → someone.
Sentence boundaries: some sentences are run-ons or comma splices, such as:  
  “We were wrong, after a few difficult years of marriage we divorced.”  
  Better: “We were wrong. After a few difficult years of marriage, we divorced.”
Agreement and formality:  
  “Educations of sex in school” → “Sex education in schools”  
  “youths’ emphasizes” → “youth emphasizes” or “young people emphasizes” (better: “The lack of parental guidance regarding sexual relationships for young people emphasizes…”)

Final advice
As you revise, focus on these priorities:

Write one clear thesis.
Organize body paragraphs around distinct reasons.
Replace emotional claims with balanced academic language.
Add complete and reliable evidence.
Edit carefully for grammar, punctuation, and word choice.

You have an important topic and a sincere perspective. With clearer structure and more academic support, this essay could become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important ethical question and clearly states a position: the right to refuse life support should belong to the individual. You also show strong personal engagement with the topic, especially in the final paragraphs. The paper has thoughtful ideas, but to become a stronger argumentative essay, it needs a clearer structure, more precise reasoning, and careful editing.

Main strength: clear topic and sincere voice
Your introduction establishes the issue well: medical advances create difficult decisions about life and death. The essay also keeps returning to one main claim—people should decide for themselves whether to accept life support. This gives the paper unity. Your personal paragraph is especially effective because it shows what your position means in real human terms.

Stronger thesis and argument
At the moment, the essay is partly reflective and partly argumentative. To strengthen it, make the thesis more direct and specific at the end of the introduction.

For example:
“Because definitions of life and death vary from person to person, competent individuals should have the legal and moral right to refuse life-support treatment.”

This thesis is stronger because it tells the reader:
your position,
the reason for it,
and the focus of the essay.

Then make sure each body paragraph supports that claim.

Improve organization
Some paragraphs contain interesting ideas, but the progression is not always clear. A possible structure:

Introduction – medical advances create ethical dilemmas; thesis.
Different views of life – values differ, so choices should differ.
Changing definitions of death – medical criteria are not simple.
Reasons some people want life support – hope, survival, future treatment.
Reasons some people refuse life support – pain, loss of dignity, no meaningful recovery.
Role of doctors – they should advise, not decide for patients.
Personal position / conclusion – reaffirm individual choice.

This would help the essay feel more logical and persuasive.

Develop ideas with examples
Several sentences make broad claims but stop before explaining them. For instance:
“Everyone has their own attitude toward life.”
“The cost here is more than money.”
“This is a point worth considering.”

After each claim, add explanation or an example. Ask yourself:
Why is this true?
How does it support my thesis?
Can I give a real or hypothetical example?

For example, instead of only saying the emotional cost matters, you could explain how prolonged life support may affect family members emotionally and financially, while still respecting the patient’s wishes.

Use a more formal academic tone
The essay often uses rhetorical questions (“Where do you draw the line…?” “How do they face it?”). A few are acceptable, but too many make the essay sound informal. In academic writing, it is usually better to turn questions into statements.

Example:
Less effective: “Who can say that a person in a coma does not feel pain?”
More academic: “Because the experience of pain in unconscious patients is uncertain, some individuals prefer not to remain on life support.”

Also, phrases like “I’ve heard of people” are weak unless you provide evidence. If possible, refer to examples more formally.

Clarify logic
Some parts are hard to follow because the connection between ideas is not fully explained. For example, you move from defining life to defining death, which is a good idea, but you need clearer transitions showing why these definitions matter for life-support decisions.

You can use transition phrases such as:
Therefore
In contrast
As a result
For this reason
Similarly

These will help the reader follow your argument.

Grammar and language issues
There are frequent sentence-level problems. Focus especially on these:

a. Word choice
“need re-defining” → need to be redefined
“what makes our life important to use” → probably important to us
“prolonging imminent death” → better prolonging the process of dying
“our memories, our personality, other events…” → needs parallel structure

b. Sentence structure
Some sentences are incomplete or awkward:
“These are to be considered…” → These factors should be considered…
“They do not want life to be…” → incomplete idea; finish the sentence clearly.

c. Agreement and pronouns
“Everyone has their own attitude” is common in modern English, but in formal writing you might use Each person has a unique attitude toward life.
“our life, our death” may be better as our own life and death.

d. Punctuation
Watch comma use and sentence endings:
“Would we want someone else to make this decision without regarding our wish(es).”  
  → should end with ?
Avoid unnecessary commas after But or before short dependent phrases.

About the missing parts
Your draft includes markers like , which suggest missing sentences or examples. These gaps interrupt the flow. Be sure to complete them in revision, especially where the argument needs support.

Final revision advice
When revising, try this checklist:
Is my thesis precise and arguable?
Does each paragraph clearly support the thesis?
Have I explained my points with examples?
Have I reduced rhetorical questions?
Have I corrected unclear or incomplete sentences?

Overall
This essay has a thoughtful foundation and a clear ethical concern. Your strongest idea is that decisions about life support depend on personal values, so the choice should remain with the individual. With a clearer argumentative structure, fuller development, and careful language revision, this could become a convincing and mature academic essay.

This gives the paper a strong sense of purpose, and your personal example makes your concern feel genuine and engaging. You also organize the essay in a recognizable argumentative structure: introduction, background, criticism of current proposals, your own solution, and conclusion. These are good foundations.

Strengths

Main areas to revise

Your main claim appears gradually, but it should be stated more directly at the end of the introduction. For example:

This is clearer and more precise than the broader idea of restoring “American family values.”

Some claims are too broad and may weaken your argument because readers may see them as unfair or unsupported. For example:
“families… have forgotten all about pride and self worth”
“have become indolent and/or chemical abusers”

These statements judge large groups of people without evidence. In academic writing, try to be more careful and specific. You can say that some long-term dependency may reduce motivation or create intergenerational problems, but you need support and nuanced wording.

Use stronger evidence
The essay relies mostly on opinion and personal experience. Personal experience is useful, but academic argument also needs outside evidence:
statistics about welfare dependency
research on childcare and job training
evidence about effects of family instability
quotations or policy details from Republican welfare proposals

Also, there are two places marked with , which suggest missing quotations or information. These must be completed or removed.

Paragraph 2: background/history

Paragraph 5: your proposed reforms
Conclusion: restate argument and implications

This will make your logic easier to follow.

Language and style corrections

Grammar/word choice
Some common errors appear throughout the essay:
its / it’s:  
  - *it’s* = it is  
  - *its* = possessive  
  Example: “the family and its values”
Avoid unnecessary capitalization:  
  - “welfare system,” “country,” “society” usually do not need capitals
“self worth” → self-worth
“low paying jobs” → low-paying jobs

“Mr Gingrich” → Mr. Gingrich
“It’s purpose” → Its purpose

Who is Erik Erikson, and what exactly did he argue?
What does the *Newsweek* article say about Baby Jessica?
What did Mark Hansen write in the *ABA Journal*?

Also, some claims need better support. For example, the statement that a two-and-a-half-year-old “cannot yet comprehend abstract concepts such as love” is too absolute and may be inaccurate. Be careful with developmental psychology; avoid oversimplifying. Instead, say something like:
“At four years old, Richard may have formed deeper and more stable attachments than he had as an infant.”

C. Make the tone more academic
Your argument is persuasive, but some language is too emotional for formal academic writing:
“ripped away”
“Nobody likes to be hurt”
“All I see is a little boy”
“if he knew what was best for his son”

These phrases show feeling, but they weaken academic credibility. Try using more balanced language:
“removed from the only family he has known”
“this decision may cause serious emotional harm”
“the ruling appears to prioritize biological rights over the child’s established attachments”

D. Address the opposing side fairly
You mention the biological father, but you do not fully engage with the strongest counterargument: that a biological parent who was deceived has legal and moral rights. A stronger essay would acknowledge this and then explain why, despite those rights, the child’s best interests should come first after several years have passed. This will make your argument more convincing.

Paragraph-level comments
Introduction: Good opening, but it is too long and includes too much background before the thesis. Condense the facts and end with a precise thesis.
Baby Jessica comparison: This is a useful comparison, but explain more clearly why the cases are similar and different.
Psychological paragraph: Good idea, but it depends too much on prediction without enough evidence. Add a source on child attachment if possible.
Adoption paragraphs: You raise an important social issue, but some claims are too broad, such as “Adoption is the alternative.” Make them more qualified.

Language and grammar issues to correct
Here are some common problems:

Capitalization:  
  “when Daniela Janikova…” → “When Daniela Janikova…”

Word choice:  
  “whom are referred to as Jane and John Doe” → “who are referred to as Jane and John Doe”

Preposition:  
  “away form the only family” → “away from the only family”

Agreement/spelling:  
  “laws will no longer very from state to state” → “laws will no longer vary from state to state”

Awkward phrasing:  
  “It is in the parents where the emotional bonds are the most strong” → “The emotional bonds may be strongest for the adoptive parents”

Comma use:  
  Avoid too many long sentences joined loosely. Break them into shorter, clearer sentences.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong foundation: a clear opinion, an interesting case, and several meaningful arguments. For the next draft, focus on:
replacing incomplete citations,
organizing each paragraph around one clear claim,
using more precise and academic language,
supporting psychological claims with reliable evidence,
acknowledging the biological father’s rights before refuting that position.

With these revisions, your essay can become much more persuasive and academically strong.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that euthanasia should be more restricted in the United States. You also organize the paper around three main reasons, which gives the essay a visible structure. This is a good foundation. To revise successfully, focus on clarity of argument, accuracy of evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Main strengths
Clear thesis and stance: The reader can understand your opinion.
Organized body paragraphs: You try to develop three separate arguments.
Engaged tone: Your essay shows commitment to the topic, which is important in argumentative writing.

Most important revision issue: strengthen the logic
Right now, some reasons do not directly prove your main claim.

For example, your first body paragraph argues that because some Americans protested euthanasia of animals, America is not ready for human euthanasia. This is not a strong comparison. Public protest does not necessarily show that euthanasia is morally wrong or should be illegal. It only shows disagreement.

Similarly, the abortion comparison in the second body paragraph is weak because the two issues are legally and ethically different. If your claim is that euthanasia should be restricted, you need evidence about euthanasia itself: patient consent, mental competence, medical mistakes, legal safeguards, or abuse.

How to improve
Revise your three reasons so they connect more directly to your thesis. For example:
Patients requesting euthanasia may be depressed or under emotional pressure.
Doctors and families may have difficulty judging competence and informed consent.
Laws may not contain enough safeguards to prevent abuse or error.

These points are more focused and persuasive than “America is not ready.”

Use stronger evidence
A major problem is that some quotations are missing: you have “” in the paper. This makes the essay incomplete and weakens your credibility. Every quotation must be included, introduced, and explained.

Also, several claims need support:
“Euthanasia is practiced throughout the U.S.”
“Currently, euthanasia has little restrictions on its practice.”
“The ruling on euthanasia took place in 1988.”

These statements may be inaccurate or too general. Be careful with legal facts. In academic argument, factual accuracy is essential.

How to improve
Replace missing quotations with real source material.
Verify legal and historical claims.
After each source, explain how it supports your argument.
Avoid depending mainly on emotional examples.

Improve paragraph development
Many paragraphs begin with a topic sentence, but the development is sometimes repetitive or unclear. Try this pattern:

Topic sentence → evidence → explanation → link back to thesis

For example, in paragraph 3, after describing the law, spend more time explaining exactly which parts are vague and why that vagueness is dangerous.

Style and tone
Your essay often uses rhetorical questions:
“Who is to decide whether a life is worth living?”
“Who can project whether a patient in a coma will come to consciousness or not?”

One or two rhetorical questions can be effective, but too many make the essay sound emotional rather than analytical. In academic writing, it is usually better to turn questions into statements.

Example:
Instead of: “Who is to decide whether a life is worth living?”
Write: “It is extremely difficult to determine objectively whether a life is worth living.”

Also, avoid absolute statements such as “there is no black or white answers.” Try more precise academic language: “The issue involves significant ethical ambiguity.”

Grammar and language corrections
Here are some recurring errors to fix:

Word form / grammar
“all American possess” → all Americans possess
“there own lives” → their own lives
“there is no black or white answers” → there are no black-and-white answers
“too much gray areas” → too many gray areas
“can not” → cannot
“a live is worth living” → a life is worth living
“an animals life” → an animal’s life
“accepted my America” → accepted by America
“supreme court” → Supreme Court
“full of wholes” → full of holes
“has little restrictions” → has few restrictions

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are awkward or too long. Example:

“Simply because that there are few people who are thinking of taking there own lives and are also of sane mind.”

This sentence is hard to understand. A clearer version:
“Few people who consider taking their own lives are likely to be in a fully stable mental state.”

Introduction and conclusion
Your introduction is long and somewhat repetitive. Try to state the issue briefly, define euthanasia if necessary, and present your thesis clearly.

Your conclusion repeats earlier points but could be stronger if it briefly summarizes your best arguments and ends with a firm final statement rather than only a question.

Revision priorities
Correct factual inaccuracies and missing citations.
Replace weak comparisons (animals, abortion) with stronger euthanasia-specific evidence.
Clarify your thesis and body paragraph claims.
Edit grammar, word choice, and sentence structure.
Use a more academic tone and fewer rhetorical questions.

You have a strong starting position, but the next draft needs more precise reasoning and more reliable support. If you revise the argument around competence, consent, and legal safeguards, your essay will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that KQED’s case for televising executions is weaker than the opponents’ case. You also try to analyze the issue using types of arguments such as value and consequence, which is a strong academic approach. The paper shows effort in organization and in considering both sides. For revision, focus on thesis clarity, use of evidence, paragraph development, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear overall stance: the reader can tell that you disagree with televising executions.
Awareness of counterargument: you discuss both supporters and opponents.
Interesting introduction: the story of Amy creates emotional impact and draws the reader in.
Analytical intention: you try to evaluate the *quality* of the arguments, not only give your opinion.

Most important revision priorities

A. Strengthen the thesis
Your thesis is too long and somewhat unclear:

> “The argument that executions should be televised… is not a strong argument because the support for the claim is not as concrete…”

This can be made more direct. For example:

Possible revision:  
*Although KQED claims that televising executions is protected by the First Amendment, this argument is weaker than the opposition’s because it relies mainly on an abstract claim of rights, while opponents provide clearer evidence about harmful social consequences.*

This version tells the reader:
what KQED argues,
what you think,
why you think it.

B. Replace missing evidence
The biggest problem is the many places marked . These missing quotations make your argument incomplete. Because your essay depends on comparing evidence, you must include the exact quotations or paraphrased ideas from your sources.

For each source, do three things:
Introduce the source  
   “Jonathan Sherman argues that…”
Give the quotation or paraphrase
Explain how it supports your point

Right now, you often mention that the opposition is “concrete,” but you do not always show the concrete evidence.

C. Develop body paragraphs more clearly
Some paragraphs repeat the same point: that KQED’s case is weak. Instead, give each paragraph one clear purpose.

A possible structure:
Introduction + thesis
Supporters’ argument: explain KQED’s First Amendment claim
Why that argument is weak: limited evidence, focus on rights only
Opponents’ argument: social harm, violence, effect on viewers
Why the opponents’ case is stronger: examples, historical evidence
Conclusion

Use stronger topic sentences. Example:

*KQED’s main argument depends almost entirely on freedom of the press.*
*In contrast, opponents provide historical and social evidence about the dangers of publicizing executions.*

D. Be careful with logic
Sometimes you make claims that seem unsupported. For example:

> “the only concern that they have at this time is how televising this execution will effect their ratings.”

This is a strong accusation. Unless your source directly says this, avoid it. Academic writing should not assume motives without proof.

Also, the essay sometimes says the issue is “not a matter of consequence” and then later discusses consequences. Be consistent and explain these categories more carefully.

Style and language corrections

Grammar and word choice
There are frequent small errors. Some examples:

“Six-year old” → *Six-year-old*
“She followed usual routing” → *She followed her usual routine*
“effect their ratings” → *affect their ratings*
“their first amendment right” → *their First Amendment rights*
“more thoroughly defined, presented, and clarified” → a bit repetitive; simplify

Capitalization
Use capitals for proper nouns and titles:
*First Amendment*
*Pat Clark*
*Jonathan Sherman*

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long. Break them into shorter ones.

Example:
> “The television station itself felt that it had the right to televise this execution with the justification and reasoning that not allowing them to do so infringed on their first amendment right.”

Could become:

Tone
The introduction is very emotional. That is not necessarily wrong, but in an academic argument, avoid relying too much on emotional appeal. After the story, move quickly to analysis and evidence.

Conclusion advice
Your conclusion mostly repeats earlier ideas. Try to do more than restate. You might briefly explain why this debate matters—for example, because it concerns the balance between freedom of the press, public interest, and social responsibility.

Final recommendation
When revising, focus on these three goals:
Add the missing evidence and explain it
Make your thesis and paragraph structure more precise
Edit for grammar, word choice, and concision

You have a solid central idea, but the essay will become much stronger when each claim is supported with specific evidence and clearer reasoning.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a serious argumentative purpose. Use of sources/authorities: You refer to Daniel Callahan, Germain Grisez, Dr. Liley, and the Hippocratic Oath. This shows an attempt to support claims rather than relying only on opinion.
Engaged voice: The rhetorical questions create emphasis and show conviction.

Most important revision issue: incomplete quotations and missing evidence

For example, when you introduce evidence from Callahan, Grisez, Louisiana law, or the Hippocratic Oath, the reader cannot evaluate it because the quotation is absent.

What to do
Replace every “” with the full quotation or paraphrase.

After each quotation, explain how it supports your argument.

A useful pattern is:
claim → evidence → explanation.

For example:
Claim: The fetus is biologically alive.
Evidence: Quote or paraphrase a scientific source.
Explanation: Show why being biologically alive matters to your moral conclusion.

Right now, some paragraphs jump from evidence directly to “therefore murder” without enough explanation.

Your essay is persuasive in tone, but some claims need more careful reasoning.

A. Avoid assuming the conclusion
At times, you use words like “child,” “murder,” and “innocent human life” before fully proving those points. bodily autonomy,

You do not need to agree, but you should show that you understand the strongest counterarguments.

Question form:  
  “Or ‘endure’ the right word?” → “Or is ‘endure’ the right word?”

Feedback Report

Your essay takes on a very ambitious topic: whether modern medicine’s focus on preserving individual life may harm society in the long run. You clearly want to challenge common assumptions, and that gives the paper energy and originality. The essay also uses concrete examples (PKD, antibiotics, AIDS) to support a broad social argument. These are strengths. For revision, however, you need to improve thesis clarity, logic, evidence, organization, and tone.

Clarify your main claim
Right now, the essay raises many related ideas:
medicine preserves life at too high a cost,
symptom treatment is less valuable than prevention,
genetic counseling should discourage some births,
antibiotics create resistant bacteria,
keeping people alive weakens the gene pool.

These ideas do not yet form one clear argument. Try to write a thesis that states exactly what you want to prove. For example:

> Modern medicine should place greater emphasis on prevention, causes of disease, and responsible use of resources rather than focusing mainly on prolonging life through expensive short-term treatments.

This is more precise and easier to defend than a broader claim about the “value of life.”

Strengthen logic and avoid overgeneralization
Some of your claims are too absolute or depend on weak reasoning. For example:
“The medical community derives its existence solely on the preservation of life.”  
  This is too strong. Medicine also aims to reduce suffering, prevent disease, improve quality of life, and support patient choice.
The argument that people with genetic diseases should die earlier so they cannot reproduce is ethically and logically problematic. It assumes natural selection should guide human medical policy, which many readers will reject.
The discussion of antibiotics is one of your strongest sections, but the example is inaccurate: antibiotics do not treat viral infections. A cut on a finger may lead to a bacterial infection, not a viral one.

When revising, ask:
Is this always true?
Can I prove this?
Will readers see a missing step in my reasoning?

Use evidence more carefully
Several places contain placeholders (“”), so key evidence is missing. Without those quotations or facts, readers cannot evaluate your argument. You must either:
insert the missing quotations and explain them, or
remove those sections and replace them with complete evidence.

Also, your essay makes major scientific and social claims without sources. For example:
PKD statistics,
Darwinian claims about gene frequency,
statements about immune systems becoming weaker,
costs of health care,
claims about doctors prescribing antibiotics irresponsibly.

These need reliable academic sources. Be careful especially with scientific claims; if they are oversimplified, they damage your credibility.

Improve organization
The paper currently moves from constitutional rights, to PKD, to genetic counseling, to antibiotics, to AIDS, to healthcare costs. The connections are not always clear.

A better structure might be:

Introduction: present the debate and thesis  
Body paragraph 1: medicine focuses too much on prolonging life rather than prevention  
Body paragraph 2: example of antibiotic misuse and resistance  
Body paragraph 3: costs and limits of symptom-based treatment  
Body paragraph 4: role of genetic counseling and ethical limits  
Conclusion: restate argument in a more balanced way

Notice that this structure moves from your strongest practical argument (prevention vs. treatment) to more controversial issues.

Adopt a more balanced and academic tone
Some sentences sound emotional or judgmental:
“This is disgraceful”
“selfish act”
“by all means adopt a child…”

Academic argument is usually stronger when it sounds measured rather than angry. Instead of judging people, analyze the issue. For example:

Instead of:  
> I see no honor to life in this parent’s selfish act.

You could write:  
> This decision raises ethical questions about parental responsibility, suffering, and the use of genetic information.

That sounds more persuasive to an academic audience.

Sentence-level language corrections
You have many strong sentences, but there are grammar and word-choice problems. A few examples:

“a prescription to cure a common infection” → “prescription drugs used to treat common infections”
“fallibility to disease” → “vulnerability to disease”
“runs the gamut” is acceptable, but may sound informal here
“t decide” → “to decide”
“uniformed people” → “uninformed people”
“highly advance defense mechanism” → “highly advanced defense mechanism”
“over thousands years” → “over thousands of years”
“Doctors” should not be capitalized in the middle of a sentence
“Aids” → “AIDS”

Also watch pronoun agreement:
“If the parent is prepared… care for their own child”  
  Better: “If parents are prepared… care for their own child” or “If a parent is prepared… care for his or her child.”

Final revision advice
Your best path is to narrow the essay. The current draft tries to argue too many things at once, and some ideas are ethically extreme in ways that distract from your stronger point. The most convincing argument here is that medicine and society should invest more in prevention and long-term solutions than in expensive short-term treatment. If you focus on that, use accurate evidence, and adopt a calmer tone, your essay will become much stronger.

You have an ambitious and thought-provoking draft. Now work on making it precise, supported, and logically organized.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses a difficult ethical issue and shows clear effort to consider both supporters’ and opponents’ arguments. This is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. You also try to evaluate the strengths and weaknesses of each side, which is more advanced than simply listing opinions.

What you do well

Strong engagement with the topic
Your introduction raises important questions and shows why the issue matters. The topic is clearly controversial, and you capture that well.

Awareness of multiple perspectives
You do not treat the issue as simple. You discuss pain, religion, morality, medical ethics, and social consequences. This gives your essay depth.

Clear paragraphing
Most body paragraphs focus on one main argument, which helps the reader follow your discussion.

---

Main areas to revise

Make your thesis clearer
At the end of the introduction, your thesis says that both sides have strengths and weaknesses. This is true, but it is too general. A stronger thesis should state your main evaluative claim more directly.

For example:
*Although supporters of assisted suicide make a compelling argument about relieving suffering, opponents present stronger reasoning because they consider broader medical and social consequences.*
Or, if you want a more balanced essay: *The debate over assisted suicide is persuasive on both sides, but the strongest arguments are those that address both individual suffering and the long-term effects on medical ethics.*

This will help unify the essay.

Use evidence more effectively
Several quotations appear as “”, so the reader cannot see your actual evidence. In revision, include the real quotations or paraphrase the source clearly. Then explain how the evidence supports your point.

A good pattern is:
Introduce the source  
Give the quotation or paraphrase  
Analyze it

For example:
*Herbert Hendin argues that legalizing euthanasia may gradually expand its use beyond terminal illness. This supports the “slippery slope” concern because it suggests that limits may weaken over time.*

Right now, some paragraphs rely on summary or opinion without enough concrete support.

Strengthen analysis and avoid overstatement
Sometimes your reasoning becomes too extreme, and you notice this yourself. For instance, the idea that suicide could become “as popular as fast food” weakens the argument because it sounds exaggerated. Try to use more careful academic language:
instead of *“It could easily become…”* → *“Critics argue that it could eventually become…”*
instead of *“the only solutions”* → *“one of the most accepted solutions”*

Academic writing is stronger when it sounds precise and measured.

Improve organization within paragraphs
Some paragraphs repeat the same idea several times. Try to begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence, then develop one argument fully.

For example, your paragraph about hospice care could be organized like this:
Topic sentence: Opponents argue that better palliative care is a more ethical response than assisted suicide.
Evidence/source
Explanation of hospice and pain management
Why this matters to the debate

This will make your essay more focused.

---

Language and style corrections

You have many good ideas, but grammar and word choice sometimes make them unclear.

Common issues to fix

Capitalization and punctuation
*when his case was first released* → *When his case was first brought to public attention*
Avoid sentence fragments and comma splices.

Word choice
*neglection* → *neglect* or *failure*
*reliance of the belief* → *reliance on the belief*
*sufferings* → usually *suffering*
*af raid* → *afraid*
*a doctors stands* → *a doctor stands* or *doctors stand*

Agreement and article use
*a doctors stands for* → *what a doctor stands for*
*the acceptance of these practices might cause a breakdown of the faith i doctors* → *the acceptance of these practices might weaken the public’s trust in doctors*

Awkward phrasing
*a seemingly peaceful death at the hand of their doctor* → *a seemingly peaceful death assisted by a doctor*
*not giving them a chance for recovery* → *without giving them a chance to recover*

Tone

*mercy killings* is strong and controversial; if it is your own wording, consider *physician-assisted death* or *assisted suicide*, depending on your meaning.

Rewrite the thesis so your position is more specific.
Insert real evidence where the placeholders appear.
Clarify your distinction between euthanasia and assisted suicide if you use both terms.
