Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that opponents of corporal punishment make an ineffective argument because they use unclear definitions and do not maintain a consistent stance. This is a strong central claim, and your paper shows good awareness of argumentative strategy rather than only the topic itself. The opening anecdote is vivid and engaging, and it helps draw the reader into the issue.

Strengths

Clear thesis
   - Your main argument appears early and is easy to identify: the opponents’ case is weak because of ambiguous terminology and wavering position.

Logical organization
   - The essay is structured around two main reasons:
     - unclear definition of corporal punishment
     - inconsistency in the opponents’ argument  
   This gives the paper a recognizable argumentative shape.

Engaging introduction
   - The Tommy story creates emotional interest and gives the topic a human dimension.

Appropriate academic aim
   - You are not simply expressing an opinion; you are evaluating the strength of other people’s arguments. That is a sophisticated academic task.

Main Areas to Revise

Complete and develop your evidence
The biggest problem is that some quotations are missing: “” appears several times. Without these quotations, the essay cannot fully support its claims. Since your paper depends on analyzing what these authors say, you must include the exact evidence.

Also, after each quotation, explain it more fully:
What definition does the author give?
How is it different from another author’s definition?
Why does that difference matter?
How exactly does it weaken the anti-corporal-punishment position?

Right now, the essay often makes a claim and moves on too quickly.

Clarify your argument
Some of your claims are too general. For example, you say the opponents “vacillate” or “contradict themselves,” but the reader needs more precise explanation. Are they inconsistent about:
whether corporal punishment works?
whether it causes harm?
whether it should be eliminated immediately?
whether alternatives are always better?

Be specific. Precision will make your argument much stronger.

Improve paragraph unity
Several paragraphs begin with a strong point but then drift. For instance, the paragraph starting “How is it, then...” begins with a question about why readers might believe the opponents, but then it shifts to readers’ personal experiences and possible revisions for the opponents’ argument. Consider separating ideas more clearly:
one paragraph on why the current argument is weak
another on how it could be strengthened

Use a more formal academic tone
The anecdote is effective, but some later phrases are too conversational:
“Were you a tommy?”
“maybe you were a tommy”
“bad”
“in the next breath”

These create voice, but in an academic essay you should sound more precise and formal. For example:
“Perhaps the reader experienced such punishment personally”
“Many authors simply characterize corporal punishment negatively”

Also, avoid directly addressing the reader too often (“you probably know”). That style is persuasive, but less academic.

Language and Style Corrections

Here are some sentence-level issues to revise:

“Tommy, with his head hung low responded”  
  → “Tommy, with his head hanging low, responded”

“took he seat”  
  → “took his seat”

“The bell to begin the third period loudly sounded its warning”  
  Grammatically acceptable, but awkward.  
  → “The bell signaling the beginning of third period rang loudly.”

“Even if definition of the focal term had been clearly achieved”  
  → “Even if the central term had been clearly defined”

“unteeteringly”  
  This is not the best word choice here.  
  → “consistently” or “steadfastly”

“The opponents to corporal punishment”  
  → “The opponents of corporal punishment”

“subjected the paddle”  
  → “subjected to the paddle”

Be careful with capitalization and names:
  - “tommy” should not be lowercase if you mean the character Tommy.
  - If used as a general type, consider another phrase, such as “a child in Tommy’s position.”

Suggestions for Revision

When you rewrite, try this structure:

Introduction
   - Brief anecdote or historical context
   - Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Definition problem
   - Present one author’s definition
   - Present another author’s different definition
   - Explain why inconsistency matters

Body Paragraph 2: Inconsistent claims
   - Give specific examples of contradiction or hesitation
   - Analyze them carefully

Body Paragraph 3: Why this weakens persuasion
   - Explain how unclear terms and inconsistent reasoning reduce credibility

Conclusion
   - Restate your thesis more forcefully
   - Avoid ending only with rhetorical questions

Final Comment

This essay has a strong argumentative foundation and an interesting angle. Your best ideas are already present: definition matters, consistency matters, and persuasive arguments need both. The next step is to make the essay more academic by adding complete evidence, improving precision, and revising for clarity and grammar. With those changes, this could become a convincing and well-focused argumentative analysis.

Clear stance: Your opinion is consistent throughout the essay.

Use of evidence: You include studies, TV programs, a Senate example, and public opinion. This gives your essay argumentative potential.
Counterargument included: The paragraph about parental responsibility helps make the essay more balanced.

Biggest issue: the essay talks *about advocates* instead of making *your own argument*
A major pattern is that you repeatedly write phrases like:
“the advocates for censorship realize…”
“the proponents prove…”
“the supporters of censorship show…”

This creates distance between you and your claims. Instead of describing what advocates think, state the argument directly yourself.

Example revision
Original:
> “The advocates for censorship realize that television is a popular and very impressionable part of society.”

Better:
> “Television is a powerful influence on children’s behavior, so stronger censorship is necessary.”

This change will make your writing more confident and direct.

Organization and thesis
Your essay is very long and repetitive. Several paragraphs repeat the same idea: children are impressionable, networks want ratings, society is becoming numb. Try a simpler structure:

Suggested structure
Introduction
   - Define censorship clearly
   - Give background
   - End with a strong thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Children imitate what they watch
   - Use the Huston study
   - Explain why this matters

Body Paragraph 2: Specific TV examples
   - Choose 1–2 strong examples only
   - Explain how they normalize violence or sex

Body Paragraph 3: Counterargument and rebuttal
   - “Parents should monitor children”
   - Rebut: not all parents supervise; TV still shapes culture

Body Paragraph 4: Social consequences
   - Use your “numbing” argument
   - Keep examples focused and clearly explained

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis
   - Summarize main reasons
   - End with a strong final statement

Thesis revision
Your introduction needs a more precise thesis. Right now, the opening is dramatic but not fully controlled.

Possible revised thesis
> “Because television strongly influences children’s behavior, normalizes violence and sexual content, and contributes to society’s growing tolerance of offensive material, stronger censorship standards are necessary.”

This is clear, specific, and argumentative.

Use evidence more carefully
You include many references, but sometimes they are unclear, incomplete, or not fully explained. For example:
Some quotations are missing: 
Some program references are vague
Some claims are extreme and need stronger support

What to improve
Introduce each source clearly
Explain what the evidence proves
Avoid overclaiming

Example
Original:
> “After each show the group that viewed the violent programs were prone to hit and throw objects.”

Better:
> “In Huston’s study, children who watched violent programs behaved more aggressively afterward, suggesting that television violence can affect young viewers’ behavior.”

Notice how the revised version not only reports the evidence but also explains its significance.

Tone and logic
Your argument sometimes becomes emotional or exaggerated, which weakens academic credibility.

Examples:
“destroy the censorship that is on public television now”
“children will be punished for life”
“society will not be civilized”

These claims may sound too absolute. Academic writing is stronger when it is measured.

Better approach
Instead of:
> “children will be punished for life”

Write:
> “repeated exposure to violent or sexual content may have lasting effects on children’s attitudes and behavior.”

Grammar and language issues
Your essay has many grammar errors that make sentences difficult to follow. Focus especially on:

a. Word choice
effect / affect  
  - *affect* = verb  
  - *effect* = noun  
  Example: “what they view affects their behavior”

censorships → usually just censorship
suppose → supposed
peak interest → pique interest
caniving → probably conniving
effecting America’s youth → affecting America’s youth

b. Sentence structure
You often write very long run-on sentences. Break them into shorter sentences.

Original:
> “Thus posing the thought: if some shows in the seventy’s could produce such an effect on children imagine how the shows today are effecting the lives of today's children.”

Better:
> “This study raises an important question. If television programs in the 1970s could affect children so strongly, today’s less restricted programs may have an even greater influence.”

c. Apostrophes and plurals
seventy's → seventies
mind's of children → minds of children
photograph's → photographs

d. Formal phrasing
Avoid conversational phrases like:
“back in the day”
“birds and the bees”
“baby-sitter” (unless necessary and clearly explained)

Use more formal alternatives.

Final revision priorities
Write a direct thesis
Replace “advocates/proponents/supporters” with your own voice
Cut repetition
Use fewer but stronger examples
Fix major grammar and word-choice errors
Make tone more academic and less exaggerated

Final comment
You have the basis of a persuasive essay, especially because you clearly care about the issue and attempt to support your claims with evidence. Your next step is to make the paper more focused, direct, and academic. If you revise for structure and clarity, your argument will become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear effort and strong engagement with a difficult topic. You understand that genetic research raises ethical questions, and you try to build a structured argument by discussing history, counterarguments, and consequences. That is a good foundation. To revise successfully, focus on clarity of thesis, use of evidence, organization, and sentence-level accuracy.

Main strength
A major strength is that you recognize this is an argumentative essay, not just a summary. You attempt to:
introduce the issue,
mention opposing views,
refute them,
and present reasons against genetic research.

This shows good awareness of persuasive writing.

Main problem: unclear purpose
Right now, the essay does not directly argue against genetic research. Instead, it often explains how someone could write such an argument. For example, you repeatedly write phrases like:
“one should present…”
“one could then refute…”
“this would be effective in an argument…”

This makes the paper sound like an essay about argument strategy, not an argument itself.

How to fix it
State your own claim clearly and defend it directly. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
“Although genetic research offers medical benefits, it should be strictly limited because it can encourage eugenics, threaten privacy, and lead to unethical manipulation of human traits.”

Then each body paragraph should support that thesis directly.

Missing and incomplete evidence
Several quotations are marked with , which makes the paper incomplete. Since your argument depends heavily on sources, this is a serious problem. Without the quotations or proper paraphrases, the reader cannot evaluate your evidence.

How to fix it
Insert the full quotations or paraphrase them accurately.
Introduce each source clearly.
Explain how the evidence supports your point.
Cite sources consistently in the required style.

Also, some claims are too broad, such as comparing modern genetics to Nazi eugenics. This comparison can be powerful, but it must be used carefully and supported with precise evidence. Otherwise, it may seem exaggerated.

Organization
Your essay has a general structure, but some paragraphs are repetitive and do not develop one clear point.

Suggested structure
Introduction
   - background on genetics
   - ethical issue
   - clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Historical warning
   - eugenics and misuse of genetics
   - explain why this history matters now

Body Paragraph 2: Limits of “medical benefits”
   - screening for disease
   - unintended consequences, such as loss of beneficial traits

Body Paragraph 3: Privacy and discrimination
   - employers, insurers, military
   - genetic data misuse

Body Paragraph 4: Designer babies / manipulation
   - distinction between treatment and enhancement
   - ethical consequences for children and society

Body Paragraph 5: Weak regulation
   - governments are not prepared to control the technology

Conclusion
   - restate thesis
   - explain why caution is necessary

Development and analysis
Sometimes you mention an idea but do not fully explain it. For instance, the privacy paragraph raises good questions, but it needs deeper analysis:
Who would have access to genetic information?
What kind of discrimination could occur?
Why are current protections insufficient?

Try to move beyond “this could be harmful” and explain exactly how and why.

Style and academic tone
Some word choices are awkward or informal.

Examples
“Mendal” → Mendel
“Gregorian monk” → likely Augustinian monk
“layman” → general public or non-experts
“do three doings” → awkward; use three things
“malevolent” genes → better harmful genes
“than it should not be done” → then it should not be done

Also avoid phrases like “obviously,” because in academic writing they can sound too absolute.

Grammar and sentence issues
There are many sentence-level errors, especially with:
article use,
verb forms,
word choice,
pronouns,
sentence clarity.

Examples of revision

Revised:  
“One consequence involves privacy.”

Original:

Revised:

Original:

Revised:

Also watch for run-on sentences and very long sentences in the introduction.

Final advice for revision
When revising, do these four things first:
Rewrite the thesis so it clearly states your position.
Change the essay from “how to argue” to “the argument itself.”
Complete and integrate all source material.
Edit for grammar and clarity sentence by sentence.

Overall, you have a thoughtful topic and several strong ethical concerns. With a clearer thesis, fuller evidence, and more direct argumentation, this essay could become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you compare how *Newsweek* and *JAMA* present the same issue differently because of audience and purpose. This is a strong topic for an argumentative/rhetorical analysis essay, and you show good awareness that audience, context, and tone shape a text. The paper also has a clear overall structure, with an introduction, several body paragraphs, and a conclusion.

Strengths
Clear central idea  
   Your main claim is understandable: the two publications interpret binge drinking differently because they write for different audiences.

Good comparative focus  
   You do not simply summarize the articles; you try to compare them. This is important in analytical writing.

Attention to rhetoric  
   You discuss audience, physical context, social context, and attitude/tone. These are useful analytical categories.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis is interesting, but it is a little too broad and repetitive. It also becomes biased when you say popular magazines “exploit” a topic to increase readership. That is a strong accusation, and your essay does not fully prove it.

Current idea:  
“Newsweek and JAMA utilize context and attitude... because of differences in audience.”

Possible revision:  
“Although both *Newsweek* and *JAMA* address college binge drinking, they present the issue differently because they target different audiences: *JAMA* emphasizes balanced data and scientific method, while *Newsweek* uses emotionally engaging examples and selective statistics to attract general readers.”

This version is more specific and easier to support.

Use stronger evidence from the articles
A major weakness is that some claims are too general. For example, you often say *Newsweek* is emotional or *JAMA* is scientific, but you need more direct proof.

Include brief quotations or precise examples from each text.
Explain how a word choice, statistic, chart, anecdote, or structure affects the audience.
Avoid placeholders like “”; these make the analysis incomplete.

For example, instead of saying “The Newsweek piece is highly emotional,” show one emotionally charged phrase and explain its effect.

Avoid overgeneralizations
Some sentences sound too absolute or unsupported:
“People are for some reason drawn to look at the dark side of issues.”
“Making those assumptions is what makes a good writer.”
“The problems... are really not of concern to them.”

These statements are too broad. Academic writing should sound careful and precise.

Try using phrases like:
“The article appears to assume...”
“This strategy may appeal to readers who...”
“The magazine seems to emphasize...”

Improve paragraph focus
Some body paragraphs contain several ideas at once. Try to make each paragraph focus on one main point.

A possible organization:
Paragraph 1: audience differences
Paragraph 2: use of evidence/statistics
Paragraph 3: visual/physical presentation
Paragraph 4: tone and emotional appeal

Then, in each paragraph:
Topic sentence
Evidence from both articles
Analysis of why the difference matters

Strengthen the analysis
Sometimes the essay describes differences, but does not fully analyze them. Go one step further and answer:
Why does this choice fit the audience?
How does it influence credibility?
What effect does it have on the reader’s understanding of binge drinking?

For example, if *JAMA* includes non-drinkers, explain that this creates a more comprehensive and research-based picture, which increases scientific credibility.

Language and style corrections

Grammar and word choice
There are several language errors. Here are some important ones:

“any give time” → any given time
“todays college campuses” → today’s college campuses
“56% or men” → 56% of men
“has an affect on” → has an effect on
“take in to account” → take into account
“the writers assumptions” → the writers’ assumptions
“this make for differences” → this makes for differences
“hook it audience” → hook its audience
“articles initial appeal” → article’s initial appeal
“premade assumptions” → preconceived assumptions or assumptions
“those out of the surveyed” → those surveyed
“would serve a negative effect” → would have a negative effect
“rhetorical site” → probably rhetorical situation

Style
Try to avoid informal expressions such as:
“flip side”
“dark side”
“as any college student can tell you”

These make the tone less academic. Use more formal language.

Introduction and conclusion
Your introduction is engaging, but the opening scene is quite long. You can shorten it so you reach your main argument faster.

Your conclusion restates the main point, but it could be stronger if you explain why this comparison matters. For example: understanding rhetorical choices helps readers evaluate how media and academic sources shape public opinion.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Rewrite the thesis so it is specific and arguable.
Add direct textual evidence from both articles.
Remove unsupported generalizations and biased judgments.
Make each paragraph center on one analytical point.
Edit grammar and punctuation carefully.

You have a solid foundation and a clear comparative idea. With more precise evidence and a more formal academic style, this essay can become much stronger.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that comprehensive sex education is more effective and credible than abstinence-only education. This is a strong topic for an argumentative essay, and you do a good job showing that the debate involves evidence, credibility, and the needs of teenagers. You also try to use examples and outside sources, which is an important academic skill.

What is working well

Clear overall stance
   Your conclusion makes your argument understandable: students need accurate and comprehensive information.

Relevant topic sentences
   Most body paragraphs begin with a claim about the weaknesses of abstinence-only arguments. This helps organize your ideas.

Attempt to engage with opposing views
   You do not simply state your opinion; you respond to the other side. That is essential in argumentative writing.

Main areas to revise

Make your thesis more precise
Right now, your introduction is broad and repetitive. The reader needs one clear thesis statement.

For example, you could write:

> Although abstinence can be an important part of sex education, abstinence-only programs are less effective than comprehensive sex education because they often rely on misleading information, weak evidence, and fear-based reasoning.

This thesis is specific and gives the essay a structure.

Improve organization
Your body paragraphs contain useful ideas, but sometimes the argument becomes hard to follow. Try this structure:

Paragraph 1: Misleading claims about contraception and pregnancy prevention  
Paragraph 2: Weak or unreliable studies used by abstinence-only supporters  
Paragraph 3: Fear-based teaching and its negative effects on teenagers  

At the end of each paragraph, explain clearly how that evidence supports your thesis.

Replace vague wording with precise language
Some sentences are difficult to understand because they are too general or awkwardly phrased.

Examples:
“This controversy between ‘comprehensive sex education’ and ‘abstinence-only education’ is more than recent”  
  → Better: “The debate between comprehensive sex education and abstinence-only education has existed for many years.”

“supporters … can be easily refuted with common sense”  
  → Better: “Several claims made by abstinence-only supporters can be challenged by scientific evidence and logic.”

Avoid phrases like “common sense” in academic writing unless you explain what you mean.

Use a more academic tone
Some word choices sound emotional or informal:
“become enraged”
“cut down their opponents”
“made up”
“a joke”

Use neutral language instead:
“strongly criticized”
“challenged”
“inaccurate”
“unconvincing”

Academic argument is stronger when it sounds calm and precise.

Clarify source use and quotations
Your essay includes many placeholders (), so some quotations and evidence are missing. In revision, make sure every source is:
clearly introduced,
accurately quoted or paraphrased,
followed by your explanation.

Do not let quotations speak for themselves. After each quotation, explain:
What does this example show?
Why is it misleading, weak, or important?
How does it support your argument?

Strengthen grammar and sentence structure
There are several grammar issues that affect clarity. Common patterns to fix:

Subject-verb agreement  
  “the rates … would decrease” is fine, but be careful throughout.

Verb form  
  “teens will not being informed”  
  → “teens will not be informed”

Possessive/plural forms  
  “their opponents misinformation”  
  → “their opponents’ misinformation”

Word form  
  “prevelant”  
  → “prevalent”

Article use and countable nouns  
  “birthcontrol pills”  
  → “birth control pills”

Awkward phrasing  
  “have beared this scrutiny”  
  → “have withstood such scrutiny”

Try reading each sentence aloud. If it sounds too long or confusing, divide it into two shorter sentences.

Example of sentence revision

Original:
> Opponents of "abstinence-only" programs continuously refute others by focusing in on others' mistakes.

Revised:
> Opponents of abstinence-only programs often challenge these programs by pointing out factual errors and unreliable evidence.

This version is clearer, more formal, and easier to read.

Final advice for revision

When you rewrite, focus on these priorities:

Write a clear thesis in the introduction.
Organize each body paragraph around one main reason.
Use specific evidence and explain it fully.
Keep your tone formal and objective.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity.

You have a solid argument and good critical ideas. The next step is to present them in a more controlled, academic way so your reader can follow your reasoning easily.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong critical thinking. Rather than simply choosing one side, you evaluate how both sides argue, which is a sophisticated approach. You also use specific cases (Baby Jessica, Baby Richard) and repeatedly return to the idea that the issue is oversimplified. This gives your essay a clear intellectual purpose. For revision, focus on making your thesis sharper, your organization cleaner, and your language more precise.

Main strength: thoughtful analysis
A major strength is that you are not arguing “biological parents are right” or “adoptive parents are right.” Instead, you argue that both sides use weak reasoning, especially through oversimplification, emotional examples, and limited evidence. That is an interesting and arguable claim.

Your conclusion also returns well to this main point. The essay has a serious academic tone and shows good awareness of argument strategies such as values, consequences, examples, and public opinion.

Thesis and focus
Your thesis is promising, but it needs clearer wording. Right now, this sentence is awkward:

> “While both sides present some good arguments, neither proponents nor opponents to biological parents used strong values or consequences, nor attempt to change public opinion.”

Problems:
“opponents to biological parents” is unclear.
“used” should match “attempted.”
The claim is too broad.

A clearer version could be:

“Although both biological and adoptive parents’ supporters present some persuasive points, both sides weaken their arguments by relying on oversimplified values, emotional examples, and unsupported consequences.”

This version tells the reader exactly what the essay will prove.

Organization
The essay has many good ideas, but some paragraphs feel repetitive or loosely connected. You often make a point, then partly reverse it, then introduce a new idea in the same paragraph. This can confuse the reader.

Suggestion:
Organize the body into clearer sections, for example:
Weaknesses in arguments for biological parents
Weaknesses in arguments for adoptive parents
Role of media and extreme cases
Why each case should be judged individually

Also, begin each paragraph with a clear topic sentence that directly supports your thesis.

For example:
“Supporters of biological parents rely too heavily on legal and moral claims.”
“Supporters of adoptive parents often weaken their case by using extreme examples and hypothetical harm.”

Use of evidence
You refer to many examples, which is good, but sometimes the evidence is incomplete or not fully explained. In several places there are placeholders such as , which interrupt the argument. These must be filled in or removed.

Also, some claims need more support. For example:
“young children are known for their resilience”
“this point is useless”
“the media portrayed the adoptive parents as saints”

These are strong statements, so they need evidence or more careful wording. In academic writing, avoid absolute judgments unless you can prove them.

Instead of “this point is useless,” write:
“This point remains unconvincing without long-term evidence.”

Analysis vs. summary
Sometimes you summarize examples well, but the analysis can go deeper. After each example, ask:
What exactly does this example prove?
Is it typical or exceptional?
How does it support my thesis that both sides use weak arguments?

You often do this already, but make the connection more explicit.

Style and clarity
Your language is generally clear, but some sentences are wordy or grammatically awkward. A few patterns to revise:

Word choice
“proponents for adoptive parents” → “supporters of adoptive parents”
“opponents to biological parents” → “opponents of biological parents’ rights” or “supporters of adoptive parents”
“could possible happen” → “could possibly happen”
“each cases” → “each case”
“on could argue” → “one could argue”

Sentence clarity
This sentence is confusing:
> “Because this is so, there is still no answer as to who should have rights over a child.”

Try:
“As a result, the debate remains unresolved.”

Tone
Avoid overly emotional or informal phrases like:
“saints”
“evildoers”
“instill great fear into the hearts”

Use a more formal tone:
“The media portrayed adoptive parents sympathetically and biological parents negatively.”

Grammar and mechanics
Watch for:
subject-verb agreement
article use (“the courts,” “a moral right”)
punctuation around quotations
consistency in singular/plural forms

Examples:
“abuse, neglect or abandonment can be proved” → acceptable, but “proven” may sound more natural in this context.
“his/her” is understandable, but in modern academic English, “their” is often smoother.

Final revision advice
When revising, try these priorities:
Rewrite the thesis more clearly.
Reorganize paragraphs by category of argument.
Replace placeholders and complete missing citations.
Use more precise academic language.
Explain more clearly how each example proves your point.

Overall, this is an intelligent essay with a strong analytical foundation. With clearer structure and more polished language, it could become a very effective academic argument.

  - “on a first hand basis” → “on a firsthand basis”
Agreement
  - “the range of punishments are” → “the range of punishments is”
Articles
  - “presented to the school’s honor code” → “introduced to the school’s honor code”
Infinitive/adjective confusion
  - “to be less reluctant to cheat” probably means “more willing to cheat”

“unsuccessful consequences” → “insufficient consequences”

===== ICLE-US-MRQ-0045.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay shows a clear understanding of the controversy surrounding the Fourth Amendment and the O.J. Simpson case. You do a good job identifying two opposing positions and explaining the different rhetorical strategies each side uses. Your paper is also organized in a generally logical way: introduction, background, analysis of the proposition, analysis of the opposition, and conclusion. This is a strong foundation.

Main strength: balanced comparison
A major strength of this essay is that you do not simply summarize the case. Instead, you compare how each side argues. This gives your essay an analytical purpose. For example, you explain that the prosecution appeals to emergency and sympathy for the detectives, while the defense attacks credibility and procedure. That is a useful insight.

Main problem: unclear thesis
Your thesis needs to be more precise. At the end of the introduction, you suggest that both sides recognize their strengths and weaknesses and shape their arguments accordingly, but this idea is broad and somewhat obvious. A stronger thesis should state exactly what you will prove about the arguments.

For example, you could revise your thesis to something like:

> Although the prosecution justifies the warrantless search by emphasizing exigent circumstances and judicial approval, the defense more effectively challenges the evidence by exposing contradictions in the detectives’ statements and weaknesses in the way the evidence was collected.

Or, if you want to stay neutral:

> The debate over the search of Simpson’s home shows how legal arguments rely not only on constitutional interpretation but also on public trust in police credibility.

This would give the essay a sharper focus.

Analysis vs. summary
Some body paragraphs summarize events rather than analyze the argument. For instance, the paragraph describing June 13, 1994 is useful as background, but after that you should spend more time explaining why particular evidence or quotations are persuasive.

Ask yourself in each paragraph:
What is the claim?
What strategy is being used?
Why would this strategy influence readers?
Is it effective or limited?

Try to make each paragraph more argumentative, not just descriptive.

Missing evidence / placeholders
You have several places marked with , which weakens the paper significantly. These missing quotations make it hard for the reader to evaluate your points. If you refer to an article or testimony, include the exact quotation or paraphrase it clearly and cite it properly.

Also, when you mention articles, be consistent in identifying them and explaining their relevance. Right now, some references feel incomplete.

Style and tone
At times, your language becomes informal or too emotional for an academic essay. Examples include:
“they did a little homework”
“backed into a corner”
“fair game”
“picking on the L.A.P.D.”

These phrases sound conversational. Try using a more formal tone:
“they investigated the detectives’ backgrounds”
“the prosecution’s position was weakened”
“admissible as evidence”
“criticizing the L.A.P.D.”

Also be careful with statements that sound biased, such as:
> “After all, the detectives have earned their badge...”

This sounds like your opinion rather than analysis. Instead, write:
> “This argument encourages readers to trust the detectives’ professional judgment.”

Sentence-level issues
There are several grammar, word choice, and usage errors. Some important examples:

“except for in” → “except in”
“illegal search and seizure is unlawful” → awkward/repetitive; better: “unreasonable searches and seizures are prohibited”
“causes much skepticism in the validity” → “causes skepticism about the validity”
“question the validity” → “questions the validity” if the subject is singular
“Beside that” → “Besides that”
“one way” → capitalize: “One way”
“has revolved from one attempting” → “has shifted from one attempting”
“methods in which” → “methods by which”

You also have some spelling errors:
Goldwin → likely Goldman
Los Angelas → Los Angeles

Paragraph unity and topic sentences
Some paragraphs are too long and contain multiple ideas. Try to make each paragraph center on one main point. Start with a clear topic sentence. For example:

Paragraph on the prosecution: focus only on appeals to emergency and sympathy
Another paragraph: focus only on reliance on the judge’s authority
Paragraph on the defense: focus only on Fuhrman’s credibility
Another paragraph: focus only on contradictions in police testimony

This will make the argument easier to follow.

Conclusion
Your conclusion currently becomes too general. Instead of saying that both sides argue effectively, remind the reader of your central insight. What does this case show about law, police conduct, or constitutional rights? End with a stronger final statement connected to your thesis.

Revision priorities
Write a more specific thesis.
Replace all  with real evidence.
Strengthen analysis and reduce plot summary.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Correct grammar, spelling, and word choice errors.
Improve paragraph focus.

You have strong ideas here, especially in your discussion of how each side builds credibility. With clearer thesis control and more polished language, this could become a convincing academic essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay shows strong engagement with a difficult topic and a clear attempt to evaluate arguments critically. You do more than summarize: you question whether sources provide sufficient evidence, and that is an important academic skill. Your main line of argument is also consistent: opponents of suicide often make emotionally powerful claims, but they do not always support them with evidence. That is a workable thesis.

Strengths

Clear critical stance  
   You do not simply repeat others’ opinions. You evaluate them and ask whether they are supported.

Good organizational pattern  
   The essay moves through several categories of anti-suicide arguments: selfishness, cry for help, family suffering, religion, and slippery slope. This gives the paper structure.

Analytical intention  
   You often explain what each author “intends to show,” which is useful rhetorical analysis.

Main Areas to Revise

Clarify and narrow the thesis
Your introduction is broad and sometimes contradictory. For example, “Suicide means the end of all problems” sounds like you are endorsing a pro-suicide claim, while the rest of the essay focuses on weaknesses in anti-suicide arguments. Also, “those problems are usually solved easily” is too absolute and insensitive for an academic essay.

Revise toward a more precise thesis, for example:
> This essay argues that common arguments against suicide—especially claims about selfishness, family harm, and religion—are often emotionally persuasive but are weakened when authors fail to provide sufficient evidence.

That thesis is clearer, more academic, and matches your body paragraphs.

Use evidence accurately and fully
The biggest problem is that several quotations are missing and replaced by “”. Without the actual quotations or paraphrases, your reader cannot judge your analysis. This is essential to fix.

Also, when you criticize sources for lacking evidence, make sure you provide evidence for that criticism. For example, instead of saying “there is no proof,” explain:
what kind of evidence is missing,
why it matters,
and what kind of evidence would strengthen the claim.

Improve paragraph focus
Some paragraphs begin with one claim but drift into other ideas. For example, the paragraph on selfishness moves from Rauscher’s claim to cancer, family pain, and whether the suicidal person thinks about others.

Try this structure for each body paragraph:
Topic sentence naming the argument you are evaluating  
Source/quotation  
Explanation of the source’s claim  
Your critique of its logic or evidence  
Concluding sentence linking back to thesis

Strengthen academic tone
Some sentences are too conversational, emotional, or speculative. Examples:
“Maybe Mr Rauscher does not think…”
“For all the reader’s know, they could be car salespeople or accountants.”
“I am sure that…”

These weaken your authority. Replace them with more formal phrasing:
“Rauscher does not consider the possibility that…”
“The article does not establish Parks and Weiss’s expertise.”
“It is likely that…”

Also avoid overgeneralizations such as:
“The topic of suicide will somehow affect every person on earth.”
“There is no escape from involvement with suicide.”

Address sensitive topic carefully
Because suicide is a serious issue, use precise and respectful wording. Some sentences sound dismissive or simplistic, such as “those problems are usually solved easily.” Be careful not to reduce psychological suffering.

Grammar and style
There are many sentence-level issues. Common ones include:

Word choice: “the person attempting suicide thinking” → “the person attempting suicide is thinking”
Agreement: “For all the reader’s know” → “For all readers know”
Sentence fragments/awkward structure: “Though, if the readers analyze…”  
Repetition: “good statements,” “good one,” “good arguments”

Try to vary vocabulary: instead of repeating “good,” use “persuasive,” “plausible,” “coherent,” or “unsupported.”

Specific Revision Suggestions

Introduction: Define “rational suicide” briefly and state your position clearly.
Source use: Insert all missing quotations and cite them correctly.
Analysis: Distinguish between lack of evidence, weak logic, and emotional appeal.
Counterargument: Consider one paragraph acknowledging that some anti-suicide arguments may rely on ethical principles rather than empirical data. Then explain whether that still limits their persuasiveness.
Conclusion: End by reinforcing your thesis, not by making universal claims.

Example of a stronger revised sentence
Original:
> The argument is well stated, but there is no evidence provided for the proof of this argument.

Revised:
> Although the argument is clearly presented, the authors do not provide concrete evidence—such as research findings, case studies, or expert testimony—to support it.

Final Comment
You have the basis for an interesting analytical essay. Your strongest idea is that moral certainty is not enough in academic argument; claims need support. Focus your revision on clarity of thesis, complete use of sources, paragraph unity, and academic tone. If you make those changes, your essay will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an important and difficult topic, and one clear strength is that you try to connect a social problem to sociological theory rather than only describing statistics. That gives your paper an academic direction. For revision, focus on three main areas: thesis and argument, organization and development, and grammar/style.

Thesis and argument

Your introduction presents adolescent suicide as a serious social problem, but your main argument is not yet clear enough. Right now, the paper seems to say both:
adolescent suicide is an overlooked social problem, and  
Durkheim and Marx help explain it.

Choose one central claim and state it directly. For example:

This essay argues that adolescent suicide can be better understood through Durkheim’s structural functionalism and Marx-inspired conflict theory, because both theories explain how social isolation, instability, and pressure shape young people’s mental distress.

This kind of thesis is more specific and tells the reader exactly what the essay will do.

Also, be careful with very broad claims such as “there are no theories…that argue that adolescent suicide isn’t a direct result of depression.” This is too absolute and may not be accurate. In academic writing, it is usually better to qualify claims:
“Many researchers identify depression as a major factor in adolescent suicide.”
“Depression is one of the most common factors associated with adolescent suicide.”

Organization and development

Your essay has a basic structure, but some paragraphs are underdeveloped or repetitive.

Introduction
The introduction includes many statistics, but it could be shorter and more focused. Use only 1–2 key facts, then move to your thesis.

Theory paragraphs
Your strongest idea is the application of theory, but you need to explain the connection more clearly.

Durkheim paragraph
This section is promising. You discuss egoistic and anomic suicide, which are very relevant. However:
explain each type clearly in simple language,
then connect it directly to adolescent experience,
use one specific example for each.

For example:
Egoistic suicide → adolescents who feel isolated, excluded, or disconnected from family/peers.
Anomic suicide → adolescents whose lives are disrupted by divorce, death, relocation, or sudden social change.

This will make your analysis stronger.

Marx/conflict theory paragraph
This paragraph is less convincing because the connection to adolescent suicide is more general. You mention change, pressure, and family patterns, but the analysis needs to be more precise. Ask yourself: How does conflict theory explain adolescent suicide better than simple individual psychology? Perhaps through:
inequality,
family stress,
social pressure,
lack of power among young people,
conflict between individuals and institutions.

Develop one or two of these clearly rather than mentioning many ideas briefly.

Conclusion
Your conclusion is too short. It should do more than repeat that adolescent suicide is a major problem. Summarize what the theories help us understand. For example:
Durkheim shows the importance of social integration and regulation.
Conflict theory shows how social pressure and instability affect young people.
Together, these perspectives suggest suicide is not only an individual issue but also a social one.

Use of evidence

You use statistics and references, which is good, but they are not always integrated smoothly. Instead of listing many numbers, explain what they mean. Also, some quotations or references are incomplete (“as stated by a young lady…”). Make sure every source is properly introduced and explained.

Try this pattern:
make a point,
give evidence,
explain the evidence.

For example:
“Durkheim’s concept of egoistic suicide helps explain why social isolation is dangerous for adolescents. Studies of suicidal teenagers often report feelings of alienation and disconnection from peers and family.”

Grammar and style

There are frequent language errors, but many can be fixed through careful editing. Common problems include:

Word choice / form
“the worst problem concerning are young people” → “the worst problem concerning young people”
“contraversal” → “controversial”
“publically” → “publicly”
“appropiatly” → “appropriately”
“there classifications” → “three classifications”
“programed” → “programmed”

Possessives and contractions
“its” often should be it’s (“it is”)
“there wasn't” is fine, but be consistent in formal style

Subject-verb agreement
“These works and studies has looked” → “have looked”
“adolescents who has moved” → “adolescents who have moved”
“the number of suicides… are phenomenal” → “is phenomenal”

Sentence structure
Some sentences are too long and difficult to follow. Break them into shorter sentences. For example:
“Thus, relating the social problem…” is not a complete sentence.
Avoid starting too many sentences with “And.”

Academic tone
Phrases like “goes right along with” are too informal. Replace with:
“relates to”
“supports”
“illustrates”
“can be applied to”

Revision priorities

When revising, do these steps in order:

Write a clearer thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each one explains one theory and applies it directly to adolescent suicide.
Reduce repetition and remove unsupported generalizations.
Strengthen the conclusion.
Proofread carefully for grammar, spelling, and sentence clarity.

Overall, your essay has a good topic and an interesting theoretical approach. With a clearer argument, more precise explanation of theory, and careful language editing, it can become a much stronger academic essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows clear concern about an important issue, and you make a good effort to connect water pollution to Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory. You also include examples and statistics, which helps make the topic feel real and urgent. For revision, focus on making your argument clearer, more organized, and more precise in language.

Main strengths
Strong topic: Water pollution is relevant and meaningful.
Good attempt to apply theory: You try to connect environmental behavior to sociological concepts, which gives the essay an academic purpose.
Use of examples and evidence: The references to beaches, illness, contaminated water, and laws help support your claims.
Clear concern for consequences: You explain that small actions can combine into serious environmental harm.

Most important revision need: clarify your thesis
Right now, the essay announces the topic, but the central argument is not sharp enough. You say the paper focuses on water pollution and its relationship to two theories, but you should state exactly what you are arguing.

For example, you could write a thesis like:
> Water pollution can be better understood through Rational Choice Theory and Exchange Theory because both theories explain how individual decisions, costs, rewards, and beliefs influence polluting behavior.

This would give the whole essay a stronger direction.

Organization and structure
The essay has useful ideas, but some paragraphs feel like lists of information rather than steps in an argument. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce water pollution
   - Explain why it matters
   - Present your thesis

Background on water pollution
   - Types of pollutants
   - Point-source vs. non-point-source pollution

Rational Choice Theory
   - Briefly define the theory
   - Explain how it applies to companies and individuals
   - Use evidence/examples

Exchange Theory
   - Briefly define the theory
   - Explain costs, rewards, and reinforcement in relation to pollution
   - Use evidence/examples

Conclusion
   - Restate the main argument
   - Emphasize responsibility and possible change

Also, some parts repeat similar ideas (for example, “people need awareness” appears several times). Try to say each point once, clearly and directly.

Development of theory
This is the area that needs the most academic improvement. You mention both theories, but the explanations are sometimes vague or incomplete.

Rational Choice Theory
You suggest that people or companies will make better decisions if they understand the consequences. That is a good start. However, Rational Choice Theory usually emphasizes that people weigh costs and benefits before acting. So instead of only saying people need awareness, explain:
Why might a company choose to pollute?
What benefits do they think they gain?
What legal, financial, or social costs might change their choice?

Exchange Theory
You correctly mention rewards and costs, but some examples are not fully convincing. For example, saying people will stop drinking bad-tasting water is more about reaction than about exchange. Instead, explain how behavior is reinforced:
If polluting is cheap and convenient, it may continue.
If penalties are severe or clean practices are rewarded, behavior may change.

Try to connect each theory to specific actions and clear mechanisms.

Use of evidence
You include many statistics, which is helpful, but they need:
clearer introduction,
smoother integration,
and citation.

Right now, some statistics appear suddenly. Explain what each number proves. For example:
> These statistics show that water pollution is not only an environmental issue but also a public health threat.

Also, the placeholders like “ <R>” must be removed or replaced. They interrupt the paper and make parts of the argument incomplete.

Style and clarity
Some sentences are too long or awkward, which makes your ideas harder to follow. Aim for shorter, clearer sentences.

For example:
“The focus of this paper is on water pollution and its' relationship...”
  - Better: “This paper examines the relationship between water pollution, Rational Choice Theory, and Exchange Theory.”

“There are two- non-point and point source water pollution.”
  - Better: “There are two main sources of water pollution: point-source and non-point-source pollution.”

Grammar and word choice
A few recurring language issues:

its / it’s / its’
  - Use its for possession: “its effects”
  - It’s = “it is”
  - Its’ is incorrect

one’s / ones’
  - Use one’s behavior, not “ones’ behavior”

Subject-verb agreement
  - “The main source of these agents is human excrement...”
  - “Human excrement ... enters the water cycle”

Word choice
  - “human race” → better: humans or human beings
  - “war pollution threat” seems to be a typo; probably water pollution threat
  - “insure” should likely be ensure

Punctuation
  - Avoid unnecessary hyphens and repeated words: “to to”
  - Watch comma placement

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on:
writing a clear thesis,
defining both theories more accurately,
organizing paragraphs around one main idea each,
removing repetition,
correcting grammar and placeholders.

You have a solid foundation and a strong topic. With clearer theory explanation and more careful editing, this essay can become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you strongly support the legalization of marijuana, especially for medical, industrial, and personal use. You also include historical background and try to apply sociological theories, which shows ambition and critical thinking. To revise successfully, focus on organization, academic tone, evidence, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear argumentative stance: Your opinion is consistent throughout the essay.
Wide range of supporting points: You discuss medicine, prisons, crime, industry, and tax revenue.
Use of theory: Bringing in structural functionalism, exchange theory, and conflict theory gives the essay an academic direction.
Strong conclusion: The ending clearly restates your main claim.

Most important revision priority: sharpen the thesis and structure
Right now, the essay feels like a long list of arguments. The introduction gives background, but the main thesis should be shorter and more precise.

Current problem
Your thesis paragraph says the paper will “focus on” many things. This sounds like a plan, not an argument.

What to do
Write one clear thesis sentence, for example:
“Marijuana should be legalized because it has important medical uses, criminalization creates social harm, and regulation would provide economic benefits.”

Then organize the essay into clear body sections:
Medical benefits
Problems caused by criminalization
Industrial/economic benefits
Counterargument and response
Conclusion

This will make your essay easier to follow.

Improve paragraph unity
Some paragraphs are too long and contain several ideas. Each paragraph should have:
one topic sentence
supporting evidence
explanation of how that evidence proves your point

For example, your paragraph on prisons also includes violent crime, class inequality, and sociological theory. Consider separating these into two paragraphs.

Use a more academic tone
Some words and phrases are too informal for an academic essay:
“pot”
“getting high”
“Mary Jane”
“crazed on pot”

Use “marijuana” or “cannabis” consistently unless you are directly quoting a source. Also avoid rhetorical questions like:
“Would it not make sense to reduce crime by simply legalizing marijuana?”
Instead write:
“Legalization could reduce some marijuana-related criminal activity by removing the product from the illegal market.”

Be careful with evidence and claims
Many claims are strong, but they need better support or more cautious wording. For example:
“Not a single death has ever been credibly attributed...”
“Rapists, murderers... are often released from prison to make room...”
“The black market would also very soon disappear.”

These may be debated, so in academic writing, avoid absolute language unless your evidence is very strong. Use phrases like:
“some researchers argue”
“evidence suggests”
“legalization may reduce”

Also, your source references like <R> are incomplete. You need proper citation style required by your class (MLA, APA, etc).

Integrate sociological theory more effectively
You mention three theories, but sometimes they feel added after the main argument instead of shaping the analysis.

Try this:
Structural functionalism: discuss how laws and institutions should promote social stability and public health.
Conflict theory: discuss unequal enforcement across social classes.
Exchange theory: compare social costs and benefits of criminalization vs. legalization.

Instead of repeating “the rewards outweigh the costs,” explain specifically whose rewards, whose costs, and how society is affected.

Language and grammar issues to correct
There are many sentence-level errors. Focus on these patterns:

a. Spelling/word choice
cannavis sativa → *Cannabis sativa*
heroine → *heroin*
foe → *for*
mar on drugs → *war on drugs*
POP may be incorrect; check the intended drug name
breakins → *break-ins*

b. Capitalization
Scientific names: *Cannabis sativa*
United States
DEA
Schedule I, Schedule II

c. Punctuation and sentence boundaries
Example:
“nicknames for an ancient drug called marijuana When…”
This needs punctuation:
“…called marijuana. When…”

d. Grammar
“Overcrowded prisons is another concern” → *Overcrowded prisons are another concern.*
“There has been no decrease in the use of marijuana” is fine, but be careful with subject-verb agreement elsewhere.
“doctors supervision” → *doctors’ supervision*

e. Awkward phrasing
“with marijuana being legalized” → *from legalizing marijuana*
“The sick people that could be helped…” → *Patients who could benefit…*

Avoid repetition
You repeat ideas such as:
marijuana has medical value
prisons are overcrowded
rewards outweigh costs

State each point clearly once, then develop it with evidence instead of repeating it.

Suggested revision strategy
Rewrite the introduction with a clear thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs by major argument.
Add proper citations.
Reduce informal language.
Revise each paragraph for one main idea.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and punctuation.

Final comment
You have strong material and a clear purpose, but the essay will become much more persuasive if you make it more focused, more academic, and more carefully edited. Your best next step is to restructure first, then polish the language.

Feedback Report on Your Essay

Your essay shows clear effort, wide knowledge of the topic, and strong concern for the issue of homelessness. You also attempt something ambitious: combining history, causes, and sociological theories. That is a strength. To improve the essay, focus on clarity of argument, organization, evidence, and sentence-level accuracy.

Main strengths
Important topic and strong engagement: Your concern about homelessness is sincere and persuasive.
Broad coverage: You discuss historical background, causes, public attitudes, and two theories.
Use of sociology: Bringing in conflict theory and symbolic interactionism gives the essay an academic direction.

Main revision priority: make your thesis clearer
At the moment, the introduction says homelessness is a serious problem, but your main argument is not precise enough. Ask yourself: what exactly are you trying to prove?

Possible stronger thesis:
> Homelessness in the United States should be understood not only as an economic problem but also as a social problem shaped by unequal power relations and negative public labeling.

This would help connect your whole essay. Right now, the essay sometimes feels like a report rather than an argument.

Improve organization
Your paper is very long and contains many ideas, but the structure can be clearer. Consider this organization:

Introduction
   - Introduce the issue
   - Give thesis

Historical background of homelessness
   - Brief, selective summary only

Major causes of homelessness
   - Housing costs
   - Unemployment / low-skill labor changes
   - Mental illness and deinstitutionalization
   - Discrimination

Conflict theory analysis
   - Explain theory briefly
   - Apply it directly to homelessness

Symbolic interactionist analysis
   - Explain theory briefly
   - Apply labeling and stereotypes to homelessness

Conclusion
   - Compare the theories
   - State which is more useful and why

A major problem now is that some sections are too long, especially the theory sections, while the historical part includes too many details that do not always support the thesis.

Use evidence more effectively
You mention a sourcebook, but one quotation is incomplete:  
> “...as seen in the book *Homelessness: A Sourcebook* ... which states that .”

This must be fixed. Every source should be properly quoted or paraphrased. Also, many claims need support, for example:
“There is an agreement between all opposing viewpoints in government...”
“Real estate agencies have also conspired against the poor...”
“In this decade the people in the government have tagged the homeless as lazy...”

These are strong claims. In academic writing, strong claims require specific evidence.

Make the analysis more precise
Sometimes your discussion becomes too general or absolute. For example:
“The powerful people in society are immensely greedy and take everything for themselves.”
“Any time one person has more than another problems will occur.”

These statements sound emotional, but not analytical. Try more academic phrasing:
> Conflict theorists argue that unequal access to resources allows powerful groups to shape institutions in ways that disadvantage the poor.

This sounds more formal and convincing.

Work on paragraph unity
Some paragraphs contain too many ideas. A good paragraph should have:
one main idea
explanation
example/evidence
connection to thesis

For example, the paragraph on causes of homelessness includes mental illness, housing costs, federal funding, real estate, and technology. This should probably be divided into two or three paragraphs.

Language issues to revise
Your meaning is often understandable, but grammar and word choice need attention.

Common grammar issues
Subject-verb agreement:  
  “People living without shelter has been a problem” → “People living without shelter have been...”  
  or better: “Homelessness has been a problem...”
Wrong word forms:  
  “effected” → “affected”  
  “mayor cause” → “major cause”
Article use / awkward phrases:  
  “A percentage of the population of the citizens of America” → “A portion of the U.S. population”
Typographical errors:  
  “lobs” → “jobs”  
  “Contact theorists” → “Conflict theorists”

Awkward or unclear sentences
“There is an agreement between all opposing viewpoints in government...”  
  Better: “Despite political disagreement, many policymakers acknowledge that homelessness requires action.”
“The necessary method to be used to understand homelessness is under careful scrutiny.”  
  Better: “However, there is disagreement about how homelessness should be understood and addressed.”

Tone and word choice
Avoid phrases that sound informal, exaggerated, or judgmental:
“mindlessly follow”
“atrocious things”
“get unruly or out of hand”

Use a more neutral academic tone. You can still criticize injustice, but do it carefully and precisely.

Conclusion
Your conclusion should do more than say the problem is serious. It should return to your thesis and explain what your analysis has shown. For example:
> While conflict theory explains the structural inequalities that produce homelessness, symbolic interactionism helps show how stereotypes and labeling deepen the problem. Together, these perspectives demonstrate that homelessness is both a material and social issue.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these steps in order:
Write a clear thesis
Reorganize the essay by sections
Cut repetition
Add or correct evidence
Revise grammar and style sentence by sentence

You have a strong foundation and an ambitious topic. With better structure and more precise academic language, this could become a much more effective argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong effort and serious engagement with a difficult social issue. You include historical background, theory, statistics, and examples, which gives the paper intellectual ambition. Your main position is also clear by the end: you reject abolishing welfare completely and support reform that expands opportunity while still recognizing abuse in the system. That is a solid argumentative direction.

Main strengths

Broad knowledge base: You use history of welfare policy, sociological theory, statistics, and case examples.
Balanced structure: You present both the conservative/Spencer view and the liberal/Merton view before giving your own conclusion.
Clear interest in argument: The essay is not just descriptive; it tries to evaluate ideas.

Most important revision priority: sharpen the thesis

The biggest problem is that your essay’s central claim arrives too late and is sometimes unclear. Early on, you ask many questions (“Why does welfare exist?” “What is wrong with society?”), but the reader needs a direct thesis.

For revision, state your argument near the end of the introduction. For example:

> Although the welfare system has encouraged some dependency and requires reform, abolishing it would ignore the structural disadvantages many recipients face; therefore, welfare should be redesigned to promote work, education, and child support rather than eliminated.

This would help unify the whole essay.

Improve organization and paragraph focus

The essay is long and rich, but some sections feel like a list of evidence rather than a sequence of connected claims.

Suggestions:
Divide the body into clearer sections:
  1. History and growth of welfare
  2. Conservative argument: Spencer and dependency
  3. Liberal argument: Merton and unequal opportunity
  4. Your position: reform with accountability
Begin each paragraph with a topic sentence that states the paragraph’s main point.
After presenting evidence, explain how it supports your argument.

For example, after statistics about low education levels, add analysis such as:
> These figures suggest that long-term welfare use is linked not only to personal choice but also to limited educational opportunity.

Be careful with tone and fairness

Some word choices sound judgmental and may weaken your credibility, especially in an academic essay. Phrases such as “laziness,” “illegitimate kids,” “unwillingness to work,” and “allow their extinction” are either too harsh or too emotionally loaded.

Even when you are summarizing the conservative view, make sure the language remains academic and precise. For example:
“children born outside marriage” instead of “illegitimate kids”
“dependency” instead of “laziness,” unless you are directly quoting a source
“face serious barriers to employment” rather than implying all recipients choose not to work

A more neutral tone will make your own argument more persuasive.

Use sources more critically

You include many statistics and examples, but sometimes they are presented as facts without enough discussion of reliability, date, or relevance. Also, some anecdotes about individual welfare recipients risk sounding like stereotypes if they are not balanced with broader evidence.

Revise by:
Explaining what each statistic proves.
Not relying too heavily on a few dramatic personal examples.
Acknowledging that older data may not reflect current conditions.
Distinguishing between individual abuse of the system and systemic causes of poverty.

Language and grammar issues

Your English is generally understandable, but there are recurring sentence-level problems.

Common issues:
Typos/spelling: “Me United States” → “the United States”; “deferent” → “different”; “exploded in to” → “exploded into”
Punctuation: comma splices, extra periods, and incorrect punctuation after transitions  
  Example: “Thus, to Spencer. if…” should be “Thus, to Spencer, if…”
Word choice: “abolishment” is less natural than abolition
Article use: check “the,” “a,” and “an”
Parallel structure: keep similar grammatical forms in lists

Example revision:
Original:
> As long as unwed mothers know that they can depend on the government for financial support, what incentive do they have to stop having illegitimate kids?

Improved:
> If the welfare system provides support without encouraging education, employment, or child-support enforcement, some critics argue that it may reduce incentives for long-term self-sufficiency.

This version is more formal and balanced.

Address missing or broken citations

Your draft contains placeholders such as <R> and , and some quotations are incomplete. These must be fixed before the essay is finished. Make sure every quotation has:
the full quoted words,
a clear source,
and proper citation formatting.

Without this, the essay feels unfinished and academically unreliable.

Strengthen the conclusion

Your conclusion has good moral seriousness, especially when discussing children. However, it should more clearly summarize your final position and proposed reforms.

You might end by naming 2–3 concrete reforms, such as:
childcare support,
realistic job training,
education/GED access,
stronger child-support enforcement,
limits that encourage work without harming children.

Final advice

When revising, focus first on argument and structure, then on tone, then on grammar and citation. You already have a lot of material; the next step is to make it more controlled, more precise, and more clearly centered on your own claim.

This essay has strong potential because it attempts a complex, two-sided argument. With a clearer thesis, more neutral language, tighter organization, and corrected citations, it could become a persuasive academic paper.

Feedback Report

Your essay shows strong effort and good knowledge of sociological theories. You clearly try to explain divorce from several perspectives, and your paper includes relevant concepts, examples, and concern for social consequences. The main revision goal is to make your argument clearer, more focused, and more polished in English.

Main strengths
Good use of theory: You discuss symbolic interactionism, functionalism, conflict theory, and exchange theory.
Serious topic development: You show that divorce is not only a personal issue but also a social issue.
Logical broad structure: The essay moves from background, to causes, to consequences, to possible solutions.

Biggest issue: unclear thesis and argument
Right now, the essay often feels more like an informative report than an argumentative essay. You describe many theories, but your own central claim is not strong enough.

What to revise
Create a clear thesis in the introduction. For example:

> Although divorce rates have stabilized, divorce remains a major social problem because changing expectations of marriage, weakened family functions, gender inequality, and cost-benefit decisions have made marital instability more likely.

This kind of thesis does two things:
It makes a clear claim.
It previews your body paragraphs.

You should also make sure each body section supports this thesis directly.

Organization and focus
The essay is very long and sometimes repetitive. Some ideas appear more than once, especially:
changing meanings of marriage/divorce
gender roles
family weakening
children as victims

Suggestions
Divide the body into clear sections, one for each theory.
At the start of each section, write a topic sentence that connects the theory to your thesis.
Avoid repeating the same point unless you add a new insight.
The paragraph about “what can be done” should be more selective and linked clearly to the theories.

A possible structure:
Introduction + thesis
Symbolic interactionism
Functionalism
Conflict theory
Exchange theory
Consequences for children
Solutions / conclusion

Use of evidence
You include statistics and references, which is good, but some claims feel outdated, too general, or unsupported. Since you use markers like <R>, I assume sources will be added. Make sure:
every statistic has a source
predictions are current and credible
sensitive claims are supported carefully

Be careful with statements like “less often, homosexuality.” This sounds outdated and may be inappropriate unless supported by strong academic evidence and clearly explained.

Also, when presenting theory, explain how the evidence supports the theory. Do not only list facts.

Style and academic tone
Your tone is mostly academic, but sometimes wording is too informal or imprecise. For example:
“okay” → “socially acceptable”
“get out of the marriage” → “leave the marriage”
“the problem of divorce will never be gone” → “divorce is unlikely to disappear entirely”

Also avoid moralizing language unless you support it academically. For example, instead of saying divorce is simply a “problem,” you can say it has “significant social and familial consequences.”

Grammar and language issues
There are many language errors, but most can be fixed in revision. Focus especially on:

a. Word choice
“coning” → “coming”
“then own job” → “their own job”
“fob” → “job”
“wilt” → “will”
“me changing sex roles” → “the changing sex roles”
“Piety needs to make” → probably “society needs to make”
“has involved through history” → “has evolved through history”
“possible lower” → “possibly lower”

b. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or incomplete:
“If they get divorced, what are the possibilities for remarriage, or will they live a satisfying single life.”
  - Better: “If they get divorced, they must consider the possibility of remarriage or the likelihood of living a satisfying single life.”
“As a couple moves forward divorce...”
  - Better: “As a couple moves toward divorce...”

c. Articles and prepositions
“in the medieval society” → “in medieval society”
“created difficulties in the marriage” → “created difficulties in marriage”
“attendance to public schools” → “attendance at public schools”
“take for granted of what to expect” → “take for granted what to expect”

d. Agreement and pronouns
Be careful with singular/plural consistency:
“A spouse must weigh his or her options. If they get divorced...”  
  Better to stay consistent: “A spouse must weigh his or her options. If he or she gets divorced...”
  Or better still, use plural: “Spouses must weigh their options.”

Paragraphing
Some paragraphs are too short, and others are too dense. Try this pattern:
Topic sentence
Explanation
Evidence/example
Link back to thesis

This will make the essay easier to follow.

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier points but can be stronger. Instead of restating everything, do this:
restate your thesis in new words
summarize the most important causes
end with a thoughtful final statement

Final advice
Your essay has strong content, but it needs sharper argument, better organization, and careful language editing. Start by revising the thesis and structure. Then shorten repetition. Finally, correct grammar and style sentence by sentence. This paper has a solid foundation and can become a much stronger academic argument with revision.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0001.1.txt =====
Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong sense of the topic. Your essay argues that coaches are often justified in “running the score up,” and you support this with several reasons: rankings, backups getting experience, the Heisman Trophy, money, and job security. The paper shows energy and confidence, which is good for argumentative writing. For revision, focus on making your argument more organized, precise, and formal.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: Your thesis is consistent: these coaches should not be blamed.
Relevant examples: The Florida/South Carolina example helps make the issue concrete.
Multiple supporting points: You do not rely on only one reason; this makes the essay more persuasive.

Main areas to revise

A. Strengthen the thesis and focus
Right now, the introduction begins with a dramatic football scene, which is interesting, but the main argument is not stated clearly enough at the end of the introduction. Add a direct thesis such as:

> Although many people criticize college football coaches for running up the score, they are often responding to the ranking system, player development, financial pressure, and fan expectations rather than simply trying to humiliate opponents.

This gives the reader a clear map of the essay.

B. Improve organization
Your essay has good ideas, but some paragraphs feel repetitive or loosely connected. Try this structure:

Introduction – explain the controversy and state your thesis.
Rankings pressure – discuss why coaches keep scoring to impress poll voters.
Backups and game strategy – explain that second- and third-string players still compete seriously.
Individual awards – discuss the Heisman Trophy and player statistics.
Financial/job pressure – explain bowls, revenue, and coaching security.
Conclusion – restate your main claim clearly.

Also, use transitions like First, In addition, Another reason, Finally, and Therefore.

C. Use a more formal academic tone
Some expressions are too informal for an academic essay. Examples:
“getting their butts beat bad”
“nice guy”
“jerk”
“bench warmer”

Replace them with more formal language:
“losing badly”
“trying to show restraint”
“unsportsmanlike”
“reserve player”

Also avoid rhetorical questions too often. Instead of:
> So is Steve Spurrier guilty?

you could write:
> For these reasons, Steve Spurrier and similar coaches should not automatically be considered guilty of poor sportsmanship.

D. Clarify logic and evidence
Some claims need more explanation or support. For example:
“No one knows” is too vague.
“Florida was clearly the better team” is an opinion that should be supported.
“If a player gets the award, the college he plays for will receive lots of money” may be partly true, but explain how or qualify the claim.

Try to make your points more precise:
> Poll rankings sometimes reward large margins of victory, which encourages coaches to keep scoring even when a game is already decided.

That sounds stronger and more objective.

Grammar and sentence issues

A. Run-on sentences and comma splices
You often join two complete sentences with only a comma. Example:

> Florida was clearly the better team, so why did the NCAA, who discourages running the score up, give the better ranking to a team that did just that.

This should be revised. Possible correction:
> Florida was clearly the better team, so it seems contradictory that the NCAA discouraged running up the score while ranking another team higher for doing exactly that.

B. Word choice and usage
A few words are incorrect:
unsurmountable → usually insurmountable
to → too in “smaller teams do it to”
it’s ranking → its ranking
all tables up → probably all adds up
“pressured to do the best that is capable” → pressured to do the best they can

C. Pronoun agreement
Watch agreement with singular/plural nouns:
“a player… he” is understandable, but you may vary with “the player” to avoid repetition.
“coaches… that are accused” is okay, but “who are accused” is better for people.

D. Punctuation
You need more control of commas and apostrophes. For example:
“Yes they are going for it.” → “Yes, they are going for it.”
Remove the extra apostrophe in “Bowden,’”
“what can a coach do , but pass?” → no space before comma

Content suggestion: include the other side
Your argument would be stronger if you briefly acknowledge the opposing view. For example:

> Critics argue that continuing to score against a much weaker team is disrespectful and damages sportsmanship. However, in many cases coaches are reacting to systems that reward large margins of victory.

This makes your essay more balanced and mature.

Final advice
When revising, focus on:
Writing a clear thesis.
Organizing each paragraph around one main reason.
Using more formal language.
Fixing sentence structure and grammar.
Adding a brief counterargument.

You have a solid base and strong ideas. With better organization and a more academic style, this essay can become much more persuasive.
