Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and shows personal engagement with the topic. You also try to acknowledge opposing views before presenting your own argument, which is a strong feature of argumentative writing. Below are the main areas to improve in revision.

Strengths
Clear thesis overall: You argue that the Confederate battle flag should not fly over the South Carolina State Capitol.
Personal voice: Your personal connection to Southern heritage makes the essay feel sincere.
Attempt at distinction: The difference you draw between the “battle flag” and the “real Confederate flag” gives your essay a specific line of argument.

Main problem: your argument shifts
Your introduction says the battle flag should not fly over the Capitol. However, later you argue that another Confederate flag should fly there instead. This creates a problem in logic and focus.

Ask yourself:  
Is your main claim “remove the battle flag”?  
Or is it “replace it with another Confederate flag”?

These are not the same argument. Many readers who agree with removing the battle flag may still disagree with raising another Confederate symbol. To strengthen the essay, choose one main claim and support it consistently.

Need for stronger evidence
Several claims are presented as facts, but they need evidence or more careful wording. For example:
“The battle flag stands for nothing but racism and hatred.”
“This clearly shows that the battle flag stands for nothing more than hatred.”
“The civil rights movement started” in 1964.

These are strong statements, but without sources they sound unsupported or historically inaccurate. In academic argument, you should:
give historical evidence
use specific dates carefully
avoid overgeneralizations unless you can prove them

For example, instead of saying “nothing but racism and hatred,” you might write:
> “Because the battle flag was raised during the civil rights era, many people interpret it as a symbol of resistance to racial equality.”

That is more precise and easier to defend.

Tone and audience
Some phrases may weaken your argument because they sound emotional or exclusionary:
“true southerners”
“all the wrong reasons”
“these people are wrong”

These expressions can make readers feel attacked. Academic writing is usually stronger when it is respectful and measured. Try replacing emotional judgments with analysis.

For example:
“true southerners” → “many people in the South”
“these people are wrong” → “this view is historically inaccurate”
“all the wrong reasons” → “to defend slavery and secession”

Organization
Your essay has paragraphs, but the structure could be clearer. A possible revision plan:

Introduction
Introduce the controversy
Present your thesis clearly

Body paragraph 1
Explain why some people connect the flag to heritage

Body paragraph 2
Explain the distinction between the battle flag and other Confederate flags

Body paragraph 3
Present historical evidence about why the battle flag was raised at the Capitol in the 1960s

Body paragraph 4
Explain why a state capitol should represent all citizens

Conclusion
Restate your position and explain what action should be taken

This structure will help readers follow your reasoning.

Development of ideas
Some points are repeated without enough explanation. For example, you say several times that the South has a “rich heritage” or a “way of life,” but you do not define what that means. Since this is central to your argument, explain it more specifically. Are you referring to family traditions, regional culture, language, food, music, or values? Clear definition will make your writing more persuasive.

Also, if you keep the argument that another flag should replace the battle flag, you must explain why a government building should display that symbol. That point needs much more justification.

Language and sentence-level issues
Your writing is understandable, but some grammar and word choice should be revised.

Examples:
“It holds many different beliefs for different groups of people.”  
  Better: “It carries different meanings for different groups of people.”

“reflects on my history”  
  Better: “reflects my history” or “is connected to my history”

“South Carolina succeeded from the union”  
  Better: “South Carolina seceded from the Union”

“capitol” / “Capitol”  
  Use “State Capitol” for the building.

“the civil rights movement started”  
  Better: “the flag was raised during the civil rights era”

Also watch capitalization: Civil War, Union, South, Southern.

Final advice for revision
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Choose one clear argument and keep it consistent.
Support historical claims with accurate evidence.
Use a more formal, balanced tone.
Explain key ideas like “heritage” and “way of life” more specifically.
Proofread for grammar, word choice, and capitalization.

You have the foundation of a thoughtful essay. With a clearer thesis and stronger evidence, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

You present a clear argumentative position and try to support it with causes and effects, statistics, and sources. Your essay shows ambition: you are not only giving an opinion, but also attempting to explain social, physical, and moral consequences. That is a strong starting point. For revision, the main areas to improve are focus, tone, organization, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear thesis: You clearly state that premarital sex is destructive and list three main reasons.
Use of evidence: You include quotations, statistics, and references, which helps the essay sound researched.
Strong sense of purpose: The reader can easily understand your position.

Main issue: argument and audience
Your essay uses very strong moral language from the first sentence (“the foundation… has crumbled greatly,” “headed down the toilet”). This creates an emotional tone, but in academic writing it can make readers feel you are judging rather than reasoning. Try to sound analytical instead of condemnatory.

For example:
Instead of “headed down the toilet”, write “contributed to social changes that the author views negatively.”
Instead of “immoral slaughter of babies”, write “a practice that many opponents consider morally wrong.”

Also, some claims are too broad or unsupported, such as:
premarital sex causes “loss of respect for women”
“illegitimate” families produce more crime and lower educational ability
the “only” solution is no sex before marriage

These are complex social issues. To make your argument stronger, qualify your claims:
use “may contribute to,” “can increase the risk of,” or “is associated with”
explain evidence carefully instead of presenting conclusions as absolute facts

Organization
Your overall structure is visible, but each body paragraph needs a clearer topic sentence and tighter focus.

A possible structure:
Introduction: define the issue and present thesis
Body paragraph 1: historical/social changes after the sexual revolution
Body paragraph 2: health risks and sexually transmitted diseases
Body paragraph 3: pregnancy, abortion, and family structure
Body paragraph 4: objectification and gender relations
Conclusion: restate argument in a measured way

Some paragraphs now are too long and mix different ideas. For example, the paragraph on pregnancy also discusses abortion, adoption, single parenthood, crime, education, and contraception. Break this into smaller, more focused paragraphs.

Use of evidence
You cite sources, which is good, but sometimes the quotations are inserted without enough explanation. After each quote or statistic, explain:
What does this evidence show?
How exactly does it support your thesis?
Are there limitations?

For example, if many people report multiple sexual partners, explain why that matters for your health argument. Also be careful with statistics that seem exaggerated or unclear, such as “99.91% ruled out.” If a number seems too precise, it may reduce credibility unless clearly supported.

Tone and word choice
Avoid insulting, sarcastic, or joking expressions in academic argument:
“piece of ass”
“free stuff from friends”
“.5 being the dog of course”

You may discuss offensive language if necessary, but do so formally:
“women are sometimes reduced to sexualized labels”

Also, the term “illegitimate communities/families” sounds outdated and judgmental. Better alternatives:
single-parent families
nonmarital births
children born outside marriage

Grammar and language corrections
There are many sentence-level errors. Here are common patterns to fix:

Word form / wrong word
One the most potent solvents → One of the most potent solvents
long ranging affects → long-ranging effects
lost of respect → loss of respect
have sex before marriage → having sex before marriage
there bad side affects → their bad side effects
person responsibility → personal responsibility
far to many → far too many
life long → lifelong
contacted through blood transfusion → contracted through blood transfusion
planed → planned
there children → their children
harder calculate → harder to calculate
bare the brunt → bear the brunt
lead to women becoming → led to women becoming or has led to

Sentence problems
Some sentences are fragments or awkwardly built:
“The major change, of course, coming thirty-five years ago…”  
  → “The major change, of course, came thirty-five years ago with the start of the sexual revolution.”

“What better way to depersonalize than to refer to only in physical feature…”  
  → “One way people are depersonalized is by being referred to only in terms of physical features…”

Punctuation/capitalization
Remove extra spaces.
Use commas more carefully.
Check quotation formatting and citation style.
Avoid random capitalization such as Christian values unless required by meaning.

Revision advice
When revising, focus on these priorities:
Reduce emotional and judgmental language
Narrow and clarify each body paragraph
Support claims with explanation, not only quotation
Correct grammar and word-choice errors
Use a more formal academic vocabulary

Final comment
You have a strong opinion and clear motivation to persuade. To make this essay more effective in an academic setting, aim for precision, fairness, and control of tone. If you revise with clearer structure and more careful language, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that women should have important roles in the military but not in combat. The paper also shows engagement with a difficult social issue, and your voice is confident and personal. These are strengths. For revision, the main areas to improve are argument quality, organization, tone, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengthen the argument with evidence
Right now, your essay relies mostly on personal belief: “I think,” “I believe,” “it seems.” In academic argument, readers expect reasons supported by evidence.

What to revise
Add reliable sources: military studies, physical standards data, injury rates, combat performance research, or policy reports.
Distinguish between physical requirements of specific roles and broad claims about all women.
Avoid unsupported statements such as:
  - “the standards… are sometimes lowered”
  - “the physical weakness of the weaker sex”
  - “the emotional makeup of a woman is quite different from a man’s”

These claims need evidence, and some sound like stereotypes rather than arguments.

Better approach
Instead of arguing that women as a group are unfit for combat, you could argue:
combat roles should have one rigorous standard for everyone
anyone who meets that standard should qualify
military effectiveness must be the priority

This would make your argument more precise and academically credible.

Improve organization and focus
Your essay repeats the same main idea several times: women and men are different physically and emotionally, and combat may ignore those differences. Repetition makes the essay feel less structured.

Suggested structure
Introduction
   - Present the issue clearly
   - State your thesis directly

Body Paragraph 1: Physical demands of combat
   - Explain why combat requires specific physical capacities
   - Support with evidence

Body Paragraph 2: Military standards
   - Discuss whether standards should be equal or role-specific
   - Explain the consequences of lowering standards, if you have evidence

Body Paragraph 3: Alternative roles
   - Show how women contribute significantly in non-combat or specialized military roles

Conclusion
   - Restate your argument without repeating emotional language

Thesis revision example
Current thesis is somewhat long and indirect. A clearer thesis might be:

“Although women can serve effectively in many military roles, combat positions should be assigned according to the same demanding physical standards for all personnel because military readiness must come before symbolic equality.”

This keeps your central concern but makes it more focused.

Use a more academic and respectful tone
Some phrases may alienate readers because they sound judgmental or biased.

Problematic examples
“the weaker sex”
“feminists… are so intent”
“a woman that wants to be treated like a man”

These expressions weaken your credibility because they sound emotional rather than analytical.

Better alternatives
“women may, on average, differ from men in some physical measures”
“some advocates of gender equality”
“women seeking access to combat roles”

Academic writing is stronger when it analyzes ideas rather than criticizing groups of people.

Avoid overgeneralization
You often make broad claims about all women and all men:
“A woman’s body… cannot function in every capacity as a man’s does”
“The emotional makeup of a woman is quite different”

These are too absolute. In argumentation, broad generalizations are easy to challenge.

Better language
Use qualifiers:
“on average”
“in some cases”
“many combat roles require”
“some researchers argue”

This shows careful thinking and makes your claims more defensible.

Fix sentence clarity and grammar
There are several grammar and style issues that make the essay hard to follow.

Main problems
Sentence repetition: One sentence in paragraph 2 is repeated.
Run-on sentences: Many sentences are too long and contain several ideas.
Word form/grammar:
  - “a males” → “a male’s”
  - “a women” → “a woman”
  - “women can be just as important a role” → “women can play roles just as important”
Capitalization and punctuation:
  - “sex. and goes…” should be corrected
  - Use commas more carefully
Agreement:
  - “the feminists that support this equality is” → “the feminists who support this equality are”

Example revision
Original:
> “The physical body of a female is so different from the male body, the physical demands of combat would not be feasible for a women without changing the way these demands are handled.”

Revised:
> “Because male and female bodies differ on average in strength and endurance, some combat tasks may be more difficult for many women unless standards or training methods are adjusted.”

This version is clearer, more precise, and less absolute.

Clarify your purpose
At times, the essay shifts from military policy to philosophical ideas about “uniqueness,” “creation,” and what women “were made” to be. If this is for an academic audience, that section may feel too personal or religious unless you clearly frame it.

Ask yourself:
Is my paper about military effectiveness?
Or about gender identity and social values?

Choose one main focus and develop it fully.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong sense of conviction, but to become a persuasive academic argument, it needs:
a clearer thesis
evidence instead of assumptions
less repetition
more neutral language
careful editing for grammar and sentence structure

If you revise with these goals, your paper will become much stronger and more convincing.

You have a clear topic and a strong sense of purpose: you are arguing that rigid gender stereotypes are harmful and that people should have more freedom to choose their roles. Main issue: your thesis is unclear and partly contradictory

This version keeps your balanced approach but makes your position clearer.

Organization and paragraph focus
Your essay has good ideas, but several paragraphs contain too many points at once. Try to make each paragraph focus on one main claim.

Paragraph on stereotypes of women: define the traditional female role and its limits.

You mention several sources, but some references are incomplete or unclear. For example:

For instance:

Example:
Claim: Traditional male roles pressure men to focus only on financial success.
Evidence: Warren Farrell’s example of Ralph.
Explanation: This shows that defining masculinity through work can damage family relationships.

Do this in each body paragraph. It will make your argument more academic and persuasive.

“shocking tale”
“damaging to the soul”

In academic writing, use more precise language:
“appealing”
“case”
“psychologically harmful”

Also avoid broad generalizations such as “women feel obliged to stay in bad marriages” unless you qualify them:
“Some women may feel pressured to remain in unhappy marriages…”

Language and grammar
There are frequent sentence-level errors, but your meaning is usually understandable. Focus especially on:

a) Spelling
Examples:

femine → feminine
famimlies → families
alot → a lot
enstranged → estranged
mascularity → masculinity
abnomal → abnormal
unfemine → unfeminine

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Rewrite the thesis so it clearly matches your argument.
Organize paragraphs around one idea each.
Complete and explain all source references.
Correct sentence structure and spelling errors.
Use more formal academic language.

You have a thoughtful argument and good material. With clearer structure and more careful editing, this could become a persuasive essay.

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that residence hall rules can prevent students from developing independence and that some policies are unfair. You also use a strong, personal voice, which makes your opinion easy to understand. For revision, focus on organization, tone, evidence, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths

Main revision goal: sharpen your thesis

Try a thesis like this:
Residence hall policies, especially visitation and quiet-hour rules, should be revised because they limit students’ independence, treat some students unfairly, and do not always reflect the realities of college life.

This gives the essay a clearer structure.

Improve organization

Visitation rules limit independence
Roommate agreements are a better way to manage shared space

Use a more academic tone
Your argument is strongest when it sounds reasoned rather than emotional. Phrases such as “mom and dad from downstairs,” “That’s ridiculous!” and “Heaven forbid” make the essay sound more conversational than academic.

Instead, use more formal wording:
“Residence staff may seem overly parental.”
“This policy appears unreasonable.”

Also, avoid addressing the reader directly with phrases like “I know you think” or “Think about it.” In academic writing, it is better to explain your point directly.

Strengthen evidence and fairness

Claims about rape, gender, and sexuality are sensitive and should be discussed carefully and respectfully.
The paragraph about LGBTQ+ students raises an important issue about unequal enforcement, but the wording may sound stereotypical or oversimplified.

Try to frame these points more carefully:
Explain that policies based only on male/female categories may not account for all students equally.
Focus on policy fairness, not assumptions about groups of people.

You could write:
Policies based solely on opposite-sex visitation may create unequal treatment because they do not account for same-sex relationships or for the diversity of students’ identities.

Work on sentence clarity and grammar
There are many grammar errors, especially with:
subject-verb agreement
word choice
plural/singular nouns
apostrophes
confusing sentence structure

“With all of the rules and regulations it has makes it hard…”

“guest of the opposite sex”

“Atleast”

rolls → roles
rapist → rapists
Men are rape also → Men can also be victims of rape

Conclusion
Your conclusion repeats your main point, which is good, but it can be more concise and powerful. End by clearly stating what should change:
colleges should involve students in policy decisions,
rules should balance safety and independence,
and policies should be fair to all residents.

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear, focused thesis.
Split long paragraphs into separate points.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Support claims with careful reasoning and fair language.
Edit closely for grammar and word choice.

You have a strong topic and a clear opinion. With better organization and more polished language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and strong enthusiasm for the topic. You argue consistently that college football should adopt a playoff system, and you use several historical seasons as evidence. This gives your essay energy and a sense of purpose. For revision, the main areas to improve are organization, conciseness, grammar, and use of evidence.

Strengths

Clear thesis: Your main claim is easy to identify: a playoff system would be fairer, more exciting, and more profitable.
Good use of examples: The 1992–93, 1993–94, and 1994 seasons help show that the ranking system created controversy.
Strong engagement: Your voice is passionate, which is useful in argumentative writing.

Main revision priorities

A. Improve organization
Right now, the essay is very long and sometimes repetitive. Many paragraphs retell football seasons in great detail, so the argument gets lost.

Try this structure:
Introduction
   - Present the problem
   - State your thesis clearly
Body Paragraph 1: Fairness
   - Explain why polls and bowl tie-ins fail
   - Use one or two key examples
Body Paragraph 2: Evidence from past seasons
   - Summarize the 1992–94 controversies more briefly
Body Paragraph 3: Benefits of a playoff
   - Revenue, excitement, dream match-ups
Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument
   - Some people worry about tradition and season length
   - Respond to those concerns
Conclusion
   - Restate why a playoff is the best solution

B. Be more concise
You include too many game details, rankings, and repeated statements such as “the only way to answer this question is with a playoff system.” After one or two examples, the reader already understands the point.

For example:
Instead of describing every team’s record in long sentences, summarize:
> “The 1993–94 season showed the weakness of the system because several one-loss or undefeated teams had legitimate claims to the title.”

C. Strengthen paragraph focus
Each paragraph should begin with one main idea and develop it. Some of your paragraphs mix summary, opinion, statistics, and quotation all at once.

Topic sentence example:
> “The biggest problem with the bowl system is that it often prevents the best teams from playing each other.”

Language and grammar issues

There are many sentence-level errors. Focus especially on these:

A. Word choice and form
“some debated” → some debate
“their was” → there was
“implied this rule” → applied this rule
“unjustly to Penn State” → unjust to Penn State
“would be skyrocket” → would skyrocket

B. Sentence grammar
Some sentences are incomplete or awkward.

Example:
> “So Alabama ended up being the nation's only unbeaten team and the national championship.”

Corrected:
> “So Alabama ended up as the nation’s only unbeaten team and won the national championship.”

Example:
> “Another reason the poll writers couldn't really Florida State ahead of Alabama...”

Corrected:
> “Another reason the poll writers could not rank Florida State ahead of Alabama...”

C. Articles, prepositions, and plurals
These are common issues for non-native writers:
“in a bowl game” not “in bowl game”
“go to the Rose Bowl”
“the Big Eight champion”
“a 16-team playoff system”

D. Punctuation and quotation format
Your quotations are not correctly introduced or punctuated.

For example:
> Sports Illustrated's Rick Reilly said, 11 How can you give the title...

Should be:
> Sports Illustrated’s Rick Reilly asked, “How can you give the title...?”

Also, check spacing and remove symbols like 11 and #l, which seem to be formatting errors.

Use evidence more effectively

You use examples, but often they are presented as long narratives instead of evidence supporting a claim. After each example, explain clearly how it proves your point.

For instance:
Example: Nebraska and Penn State both finished unbeaten.
Explanation: This shows that bowl tie-ins prevented a direct championship game, leaving voters to decide instead of results on the field.

That explanation is the heart of the argument.

Tone and academic style

Your opening questions are interesting, but some informal phrases reduce the academic tone:
“absolutely ridiculous”
“whipped”
“dream match-ups” is acceptable, but use sparingly

Try more formal alternatives:
“highly problematic”
“defeated decisively”
“anticipated matchups”

Final advice for revision

When revising, do these in order:
Cut repetition and shorten the season summaries.
Reorganize around 3–4 main argument points.
Fix grammar and sentence errors carefully.
Use formal citation/quotation style consistently.
Add analysis after evidence, not just description.

Overall

This essay has a strong foundation because your argument is clear and your examples are relevant. The next step is to make the essay more focused, more polished, and more academic. If you shorten the historical summaries and sharpen your analysis, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Feedback Report

You have chosen a clear and practical topic, and your essay has a strong sense of personal experience. Your job at the amusement park gives you credibility, so readers are likely to trust what you say. The essay’s main message is also easy to understand: people must follow go-cart rules to protect themselves, other riders, and workers.

What is working well

Strong real-world perspective  
   Your examples come from direct experience, which makes the essay convincing. For example, mentioning that you have seen injuries and that you were hit by a go-cart helps support your argument.

Clear purpose  
   The essay stays focused on safety rules. You do not go off-topic.

Good organizational idea  
   Each body paragraph discusses a different rule: seatbelts, brakes, bumping, accidents, and entering the pit. This is a solid structure for an argumentative essay.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction explains the topic, but the main argument could be stated more directly and formally.

Current idea:
> “There are many reasons why you should follow the rules...”

A stronger thesis could be:
“Riders at amusement parks should follow go-cart safety rules because these rules prevent injuries, protect workers and passengers, and reduce damage to the cars.”

This version is more academic and clearly previews your main points.

Use a more formal academic tone
Your essay sounds natural and sincere, but some phrases are too conversational for academic writing.

Examples:
“I don’t know how to express...”
“Believe me...”
“You got to remember...”
“it doesn’t feel good”

Try replacing informal phrases with more formal language:
“It is essential that riders follow safety rules...”
“My experience demonstrates...”
“Riders should remember...”
“This can cause serious injury...”

You can still use personal experience, but present it in a more controlled way.

Improve sentence grammar
There are many grammar errors, especially with:
verb forms
word choice
prepositions
run-on sentences
comma use

Examples:

“The seatbelts shouldn't be taking off during the race at anytime.”  
  → “Seatbelts should not be taken off during the race at any time.”

“I have seen it a hundred times, when people coming in the pit...”  
  → “I have seen many people entering the pit slam into other cars because they did not use their brakes.”

“The only reason the bumpers are on the cars in the first place are...”  
  → “The only reason the bumpers are on the cars in the first place is...”

“The bumpers are not their...”  
  → “The bumpers are not there...”

Be especially careful with subject-verb agreement:
“rules are” / “reason is” / “everyone understands”

Fix spelling and word form errors
A few spelling mistakes reduce the essay’s polish:
go-carts or go-karts (choose one and use it consistently)
announcements (not *annoucements*)
whenever (not *when ever*)
there (not *their* when referring to place)
taken off (not *taking off* in that sentence)

Strengthen paragraph development
Some paragraphs repeat the same point several times. Try to make each paragraph follow this pattern:

Topic sentence
Explanation
Example
Concluding sentence

For example, your paragraph about bumping is strong, but it is too long and includes many ideas at once. You could divide it into two paragraphs:
one about dangers to riders
one about damage to cars and workers

Improve punctuation
You often place commas where they are not needed:
“people, who don’t use their brakes”
“amount of people, who don’t use their brakes”

In English, restrictive clauses usually do not need commas:
“people who don’t use their brakes”

Also, some sentences are too long and should be divided into two shorter sentences.

Suggested revision plan

Rewrite the introduction with a clear thesis.
Keep one main idea per paragraph.
Use formal language instead of conversational expressions.
Check grammar carefully, especially verb forms and sentence structure.
Proofread for spelling and punctuation.

Example of a stronger revised opening

“Safety rules at go-cart tracks are essential and should always be followed. As an employee at Frankie’s Fun Park, I have seen many accidents caused by riders who ignored instructions. Riders should obey the rules because these rules prevent injuries, protect workers and passengers, and reduce damage to the cars.”

Final comment

This essay has a strong foundation because your argument is clear and supported by real experience. Your biggest challenge is not ideas—it is language control and academic style. If you revise the grammar, shorten some sentences, and make the tone more formal, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that Susan Smith should not receive the death penalty. You also try to support this position with background information about her life, mental health, and Christian beliefs about forgiveness. This gives your essay a strong sense of purpose. To improve the paper, focus on organization, use of evidence, tone, and sentence-level accuracy.

Main strength: clear opinion
Your thesis is understandable: the jury was right not to give Susan Smith the death penalty. This is good for an argumentative essay because the reader knows your stance early.

However, the essay needs a more precise thesis. Right now, your reasons are somewhat mixed: mental illness, guilt as punishment, public criticism, and God’s forgiveness. Try to state these reasons clearly in one sentence at the end of the introduction.

Example:
> Although Susan Smith committed a terrible crime, the jury was justified in sentencing her to life in prison rather than death because of her mental instability, her traumatic past, and the belief that lifelong punishment is more meaningful than execution.

Organization: improve paragraph structure
The essay currently moves from biography, to emotional description, to religion, but the ideas are not always connected smoothly. A stronger structure would help.

Suggested structure
Introduction
   - Briefly introduce the case
   - State your thesis clearly

Body Paragraph 1: traumatic background and mental health
   - Discuss her childhood, abuse, depression
   - Explain how these factors may have affected her judgment

Body Paragraph 2: life imprisonment vs. death penalty
   - Explain why life in prison is, in your view, a stronger punishment
   - Focus on guilt, long-term suffering, and accountability

Body Paragraph 3: moral/religious argument
   - Present your Christian perspective carefully
   - Explain forgiveness and the value of human life

Conclusion
   - Restate your main claim
   - Summarize why life imprisonment was more just

Argumentation: use evidence carefully
Some of your claims are persuasive, but others need stronger support or more careful wording.

For example:
“She is now living with a guilty conscience” — this may be true, but you cannot prove exactly what she feels.
“She will hear the two small voices” — this is powerful emotionally, but it is speculative and sounds dramatic rather than academic.

In argumentative writing, try to balance emotion with reason. Instead of imagining what she hears or sees, explain why life imprisonment can be considered a severe and continuing punishment.

Example revision:
> A life sentence requires Smith to live permanently with the consequences of her actions, which some people believe is a more meaningful punishment than a quick execution.

Tone: make it more academic
At times, the essay becomes very emotional or religious in a way that may not persuade all readers. Phrases like “God’s wrath,” “cold-hearted murderer,” and “spend eternity in hell” create a strong emotional tone, but an academic essay should sound more measured.

This does not mean you must remove your religious viewpoint. Instead, present it as your perspective and connect it to the argument respectfully.

For example:
> From a Christian perspective, forgiveness remains possible even after serious wrongdoing, and this belief supports the idea that society should not take her life.

Also, be careful with factual accuracy. You wrote:
> “These past behavior patterns are a few reasons why the Supreme Court did not impose the death penalty.”

This is incorrect. The jury, not the Supreme Court, made the sentencing decision in the trial.

Use of quotations
You include “The Bible says ” twice, but the quotations are missing. If you refer to the Bible, include the actual verse and explain its relevance. Also, make sure religious quotations support your argument instead of replacing it.

Language and grammar corrections
There are several language errors, but many can be fixed through careful proofreading.

Common issues
Spelling
  - “recieve” → receive
  - “prision” → prison
Sentence boundaries / comma splices
  - “The news media and many individuals have stated facts as well as opinions, I would like to express…”  
    → This should be divided into two sentences or joined correctly.
Word choice
  - “not endowed with logical mental coherence” sounds unnatural.  
    Better: was not mentally stable or did not think rationally.
Repetition
  - You repeat “Her boyfriend dumped her” twice very close together.
Articles and phrasing
  - “At the age of fifteen years old” → At the age of fifteen
  - “sleep with her” is understandable, but in formal writing had a sexual relationship with her may be better.

Final advice for revision
When you revise, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis with 2–3 main reasons.
Organize each paragraph around one central idea.
Replace highly dramatic descriptions with reasoned analysis.
Correct factual errors and incomplete quotations.
Proofread for grammar, spelling, and sentence structure.

You have a strong opinion and important ideas. With clearer organization and a more academic style, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a recognizable argumentative structure. You state your thesis early, and most body paragraphs connect back to your main claim that lowering the drinking age would reduce harm. This gives the essay focus. You also attempt to use sources, which is an important strength in argumentative writing.

Main revision priorities

A. Make your argument more logical and precise
Right now, many claims are repeated, but not fully proven. For example, you argue that lowering the drinking age would:
reduce crime,
make students more responsible,
reduce binge drinking,
improve national health.

These are strong claims, but often the essay assumes the result instead of explaining why it would happen with clear evidence. For example:

“Once these rights are given back, young adults will act responsibly.”
“The common problem of binge drinking would be cured…”

These statements are too absolute. A reader may ask: How do you know? What evidence proves this? Try using more careful language:
“might reduce”
“could help decrease”
“may encourage”

Also, some reasoning is weak or contradictory. For example, if alcohol is already involved in violence on campuses, lowering the age does not automatically reduce violence. You need to explain the mechanism more clearly.

B. Address counterarguments
A strong argumentative essay should acknowledge the other side. Many readers would argue that lowering the drinking age could:
increase drunk driving,
increase alcohol access for younger teens,
worsen health risks.

If you briefly present these concerns and then respond to them, your argument will sound more mature and persuasive.

Organization

Your essay follows a basic structure: introduction, body paragraphs, conclusion. However, some paragraphs mix several ideas together.

For revision, try this clearer structure:

Introduction
Introduce the issue
Give brief context
End with a precise thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Crime
Topic sentence
Evidence
Explanation of how legal access might reduce illegal behavior
Limit the claim

Body Paragraph 2: Responsibility and social attitudes
Explain how legal drinking at 18 might normalize alcohol
Use evidence from other countries carefully
Explain why normalization could reduce reckless behavior

Body Paragraph 3: Health / binge drinking
Present evidence about binge drinking
Explain how secrecy and illegality may encourage excessive drinking
Avoid unsupported claims like “binge drinking would be cured”

Counterargument Paragraph
Discuss safety concerns
Respond with evidence or a qualified rebuttal

Conclusion
Restate thesis in fresh language
Summarize strongest reasons

Use of evidence

You cite sources, but many citations are incomplete, informal, or not credible in academic writing. Examples:
email addresses
website links written directly in the sentence
“Netscape. Young Drinkers Homepage”
inconsistent source formatting

You need to use a consistent academic citation style (MLA, APA, etc) and rely on credible sources such as:
government reports,
academic articles,
public health research.

Also, do not simply place a source after a claim. Explain the evidence:
What does the study show?
Why is it relevant?
How does it support your argument?

Style and language

Your meaning is usually understandable, but there are grammar and word choice problems. Focus especially on:

A. Word form and spelling
“effect” → affect (verb)
“amoung” → among
“encourage” → encouraged
“year old's” → year-olds
“Is” → probably olds or adults

B. Articles and plurals
“in high school and college environment” → in high school and college environments
“improve the health of the nation” is acceptable, but sometimes too broad

C. Sentence correctness
“on most college campuses” → On most college campuses
“Lowering drinking age…” → Lowering the drinking age…

D. Avoid informal or exaggerated language
Examples:
“fight alcoholism”
“giving youths back their privileges”
“get a grip on alcohol”

Use more formal academic language:
“struggle with alcohol misuse”
“grant legal access”
“develop a healthier cultural attitude toward alcohol”

Thesis revision

Your current thesis is too broad and repetitive. A stronger version could be:

“Lowering the drinking age from twenty-one to eighteen could reduce illegal alcohol-related behavior, encourage a more open and responsible drinking culture, and help address binge drinking among young adults.”

This is clearer and more careful.

Final advice

When revising, focus on:
Stronger logic
Better evidence
A counterargument
More formal academic language
Grammar and citation corrections

You have a solid starting point because your position is clear and your essay has structure. The next step is to make your argument more credible, balanced, and academically polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you argue that swimming should be part of school Physical Education because it can prevent drowning and teach water safety. This is a strong, practical topic, and your opening creates urgency. You clearly care about the issue, which helps make the essay persuasive. For revision, focus on three main areas: organization, academic tone, and language accuracy.

Strengths

Engaging introduction: The opening scenario is vivid and grabs the reader’s attention.
Clear position: Your thesis is understandable: schools should require swimming instruction.
Relevant reasons: You explain several benefits: saving lives, teaching safety, using existing pools, and creating jobs.
Strong sense of purpose: The essay stays focused on the central idea.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the essay more academic
Right now, the essay sounds more like a speech or opinion piece than an academic argument. You often address the reader directly with “you,” which is effective emotionally but less formal.

For example:
“If you are like many people…”
“How often have you heard of dodgeball saving someone’s life?”

In a formal argumentative essay, try to use a more objective style:
“Many people are attracted to swimming pools during the summer.”
“Unlike many other physical education activities, swimming is a life-saving skill.”

B. Strengthen the thesis
Your main claim appears in the middle of the essay, but it could be sharper and more specific. A stronger thesis should state both your position and your main reasons.

Possible revision:
“Swimming instruction should be required in school physical education programs because it can reduce drowning deaths, teach essential water-safety skills, and make good use of community or school resources.”

C. Improve paragraph structure
Some paragraphs contain many ideas at once. Try using one main idea per paragraph:

Introduction + thesis
Why swimming instruction is necessary (drowning risk, lack of knowledge)
Why schools are the right place (all students can access it)
How the program could work (school pools, local pools, certified instructors)
Conclusion

Also, add clearer topic sentences. For example:
“Schools are the best place to provide swimming education because they can reach all students.”
“In many communities, the resources for swim instruction already exist.”

Development and evidence

Your argument would be much stronger if you included specific evidence. Right now, many claims are general:
“This is a common story…”
“Many school systems have pools that are under-used.”
“Swimming is a skill that is easy to learn…”

Ask yourself:
How common are drowning deaths among children?
Are there statistics about how many people cannot swim?
Do experts recommend swim instruction for safety?

Adding facts, statistics, or expert sources will make your argument more convincing.

Language and grammar issues

There are frequent errors in capitalization, spelling, punctuation, and sentence structure. These do not hide your meaning, but they weaken the essay’s credibility.

Common issues:
Capitalization
“your only problem now…” → Your
“one class that is common…” → One
“News” does not need to be capitalized unless it is part of a title.

Spelling
panicing → panicking
drownding → drowning
cericulum → curriculum
evryone → everyone
instuctor → instructor
wuold → would
instuctors → instructors
thaech → teach
employent → employment
simlpe → simple
remidied → remedied

Punctuation / run-on sentences
Example:
“This is not uncommon, most of your friends do not know how to swim.”
This is a run-on sentence. Correct it:
“This is not uncommon; most of your friends do not know how to swim.”
or
“This is not uncommon. Most of your friends do not know how to swim.”

Another example:
“It would give evryone the chance to learn how to swim, how to recognize when somebody is in trouble and how to handle that situation.The topic…”
Add a space and separate the ideas more clearly.

Article and word choice
“It is skill that could save their lives” → “It is a skill that could save their lives.”
“in the summer time” → “in the summertime” or “during the summer”

Rethink the introduction
The dramatic drowning scene is powerful, but it is also very long. In an academic essay, you might shorten it so that you reach your thesis sooner. Keep the emotional impact, but be concise.

For example:
“Every summer, news reports describe preventable drowning deaths, many involving people who never learned basic swimming and water-safety skills. Because these tragedies can often be prevented through education, swimming instruction should be required in school physical education programs.”

Conclusion
Your conclusion restates your opinion, but it could be stronger if it briefly summarizes the key reasons and ends with a firm final statement.

Possible model:
“In conclusion, requiring swimming in physical education would give students a life-saving skill, improve water safety awareness, and use existing community resources effectively. For these reasons, schools should treat swimming not as an extra activity, but as an essential part of education.”

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on:
Writing a clearer thesis
Organizing each paragraph around one main idea
Using a more formal academic tone
Correcting spelling, capitalization, and punctuation
Adding evidence to support your claims

You have a strong topic and a meaningful argument. With better structure and more careful editing, this could become a persuasive academic essay.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: society should move away from rigid gender identities because they can harm both women and men. This is a strong, arguable thesis, and your essay is organized around several reasons: social change, psychological effects, and the realities of single parenthood. These are good foundations for revision.

Strengths
Clear argument: Your main claim is consistent throughout the essay.
Logical paragraphing: Each body paragraph focuses on one main idea.
Use of sources: Referring to researchers and writers such as Betty Friedan and Warren Farrell shows an attempt to support your argument with authority.
Relevant examples: The discussion of single parents is practical and easy for readers to understand.

Main areas to improve

Make the thesis more precise
Your introduction repeats “distinct gender identities” many times, but the phrase is not fully clear. You should define it in simpler language. For example:

Current idea: “We should abolish distinct gender identities…”
Possible revision: “Society should reject rigid gender stereotypes because qualities such as strength, sensitivity, ambition, and care are human traits, not traits limited to one sex.”

This wording is clearer and more natural.

Replace placeholders and incomplete evidence
Several places contain symbols such as  and `<R>`. These make the essay feel unfinished. You need to either:
insert the exact quotation or evidence, or
remove the reference and paraphrase the source clearly.

Also, when you mention researchers, explain how their work supports your point. Right now, some references are too vague.

Strengthen topic sentences and analysis
Many paragraphs begin with a general statement, but the explanation could go deeper. Try this structure for each body paragraph:
topic sentence
evidence/example
explanation of why it supports your thesis
concluding sentence

For instance, in the paragraph about women’s unhappiness in traditional roles, explain more directly why rigid expectations caused psychological harm and how broader gender roles could prevent this.

Avoid overgeneralization
Some sentences sound too absolute. For example:
“these are strictly male gender identity characteristics”
“these are distinct female gender identity characteristics”

These statements may weaken your argument because they repeat the stereotypes you are criticizing. Instead, use more careful language:
“these traits have traditionally been labeled masculine”
“these qualities have often been associated with women”

Academic argument is stronger when it avoids extreme wording.

Improve style and concision
Some ideas are repeated too often, especially “distinct gender identity” and “male/female gender qualities.” Repetition can make the essay sound mechanical. Try varying your language:
rigid gender roles
traditional gender stereotypes
fixed expectations for men and women

Also, some sentences are longer than necessary. Shorter, clearer sentences will improve readability.

Grammar and language corrections

Here are some recurring issues to revise:

Word choice
“ever changing society” → ever-changing society
“women in the work place” → women in the workplace
“on order to succeed” → in order to succeed
“more healthy male gender” → healthier men / a healthier male identity

Grammar
“the male gender identity requires that a man be competitive and aggressive. if these traits…”  
  → capitalize If
“It was also thought to be abnormal if one gender possessed qualities…”  
  → better: It was also considered abnormal for one gender to display qualities associated with the other.
“There are women who are having to play…”  
  → more natural: Some women must play…
“a more well-adjusted female would exist”  
  → women might become better adjusted psychologically

Parallel structure
Be careful when listing qualities:
“strong, courageous, dependent, and self-reliant”  
“dependent” conflicts with “self-reliant.” You may mean independent.

Tone
Some phrases sound too informal or emotional for academic writing:
“it would be ridiculous to think…”  
  → It is unreasonable to argue…

Organization suggestions
A stronger structure might look like this:

Introduction  
   Define rigid gender stereotypes and state your thesis clearly.

Body Paragraph 1  
   Explain how traditional gender roles developed and why they are outdated.

Body Paragraph 2  
   Show psychological harm to women using Friedan or other evidence.

Body Paragraph 3  
   Show psychological pressure on men using Farrell or another example.

Body Paragraph 4  
   Use single-parent families as a real-world example that shows traits are not naturally limited by sex.

Conclusion  
   Restate the argument in a fresh way and emphasize why abandoning stereotypes benefits society.

Final advice
Your essay has a solid central idea and good potential. The most important next steps are:
clarify your thesis,
complete or replace missing source material,
reduce repetition,
use more precise academic language,
and revise grammar and sentence flow.

With these changes, your essay will become much more persuasive and polished.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear argumentative purpose: you argue that media and social beauty standards harm women physically and mentally. This is a strong and important topic, and your personal voice makes the essay passionate and engaging. You clearly care about the issue, and that helps the reader understand your position.

What you do well

Clear position
Your main claim is easy to identify: society and the media create unrealistic beauty standards, and these standards can lead to harmful consequences.

Strong emotional appeal
You use powerful language and rhetorical questions such as “Why should we put ourselves through this?” These choices make the essay persuasive and show your commitment to the topic.

Good use of examples
You include examples of models, eating disorders, plastic surgery, beauty products, and discrimination against larger women. These help show that the problem affects women in many ways.

Main areas to revise

Create a stronger thesis
Your introduction discusses beauty standards, but the thesis should be more precise and direct. Try to state exactly what your essay will prove.

Possible revised thesis:
*The media promotes unrealistic beauty standards for women, and these standards damage women’s self-esteem, encourage dangerous behaviors, and contribute to discrimination based on appearance.*

This is clearer and gives your essay a stronger structure.

Improve organization
Right now, many ideas are strong, but they sometimes feel crowded together. Try organizing the essay into clear body paragraphs, each with one main point.

A possible structure:
Introduction: beauty standards and thesis
Body paragraph 1: unrealistic images in media
Body paragraph 2: health effects, such as anorexia and bulimia
Body paragraph 3: beauty industry and cosmetic procedures
Body paragraph 4: social discrimination against women who do not fit the ideal
Conclusion: restate argument and call for healthier definitions of beauty

Use topic sentences at the start of each paragraph.

For example:
*Media images present a body type that is unrealistic for most women.*
*These impossible standards can lead to serious physical and psychological disorders.*

Use a more formal academic tone
Your essay is passionate, which is good, but some phrases sound too informal for an academic argument.

Examples:
“top notch models” → *highly successful models*
“yeah right” → remove this phrase or replace with a formal statement
“bogus beauty products” → *misleading beauty products*

Also, avoid speaking too generally from personal experience unless you connect it to the larger argument. Your personal paragraph is effective, but it should support the academic point rather than take over the essay.

Check facts and evidence carefully
You include many statistics and medical claims, which can strengthen your essay, but in academic writing they must be accurate and supported by sources. Some claims seem too absolute or possibly incorrect, such as:
“there is no cure” for anorexia
implants are “a leading cause of Cancer”
chemicals “break down skin, and bone”

Be careful with medical statements. If you cannot prove them with reliable sources, revise them to be more accurate and cautious.

For example:
*Eating disorders can be difficult to treat and may have serious long-term health effects.*
*Some cosmetic procedures carry significant medical risks.*

Work on grammar and word choice
There are frequent grammar and spelling errors. These do not hide your meaning, but they reduce the essay’s strength.

Some important corrections:
“The 1990’s base these standards” → *The 1990s established these standards*
“adolecense” → *adolescence*
“society place” → *society places*
“model’s fault” → *models’ fault* if plural
“there job” → *their job*
“perfect women” → *perfect woman* or *ideal woman*
“The average women is” → *The average woman is*
“how ever much” → *however much*
“self esteem” → *self-esteem*
“neither the media or society” → *neither the media nor society*

Also watch sentence boundaries. Some sentences are too long and contain too many ideas. Split them into shorter, clearer sentences.

Reduce overgeneralization
Sometimes the essay makes broad statements about “all women,” “everywhere,” or “we women.” Strong arguments are usually more convincing when they are precise.

For example:
Instead of *Women everywhere…*, write *Many women are affected by…*
Instead of *every woman is beautiful*, which is emotionally strong but broad, you might write *beauty should not be defined only by physical appearance.*

Final advice for revision

When you rewrite, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis
Organize each paragraph around one main idea
Use a more formal academic tone
Revise or verify statistics and medical claims
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, and sentence structure

Encouragement

This essay already has a strong foundation: a clear opinion, sincere voice, and an important social message. Your next step is to make the writing more structured, more precise, and more academically reliable. If you do that, your argument will become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay presents a clear and interesting topic: whether “trash TV” talk shows are harmful or useful. You show that the issue has two sides, and you try to consider both entertainment value and possible dangers. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay. To revise effectively, focus on clarity of thesis, organization, sentence control, and grammar/mechanics.

Main strength: strong engagement with the topic
You write with energy, and your language shows strong interest in the subject. For example, you describe how talk shows attract viewers through conflict, but you also note that some episodes offer help, reconciliation, and hope. This balanced perspective is valuable.

Thesis and argument
At the moment, your essay raises questions about talk shows, but your main argument is not fully clear. In an argumentative essay, the reader should know your position early.

Your introduction ends with:
> “These differences in opinions leads to numerous questions concerning the purpose and benefits, if any, of these talk shows.”

This sentence introduces the topic, but it does not clearly state your claim. By the end of the essay, your position seems to be:
talk shows can be sensational and risky,
but guests choose to participate,
so viewers should not simply condemn all talk shows.

If that is your position, make it direct in the introduction.

Possible revised thesis:
Although many daily talk shows encourage conflict for entertainment, they should not automatically be dismissed as worthless because they can also provide exposure to real social issues and offer help; ultimately, guests must take responsibility for choosing to appear.

This kind of thesis gives your essay a clear direction.

Organization
Your paragraphs contain many vivid ideas, but sometimes they are too long and include several different points. Try to give each paragraph one main purpose.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: introduce the debate and state your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: why some people support talk shows (entertainment, information, social issues).
Body Paragraph 2: why critics oppose them (conflict, exploitation, violence, vulgarity).
Body Paragraph 3: example of a dangerous consequence.
Conclusion: restate your position and explain who is responsible.

Also, use topic sentences more clearly. For example:
“Supporters argue that talk shows are not only entertaining but also informative.”
“However, critics claim that many programs exploit guests and encourage aggression.”

Development and evidence
You have good examples, especially the case of the unaired episode involving a secret crush. However, some claims are very broad and need support. For example:
“Many of these programs provide their viewers with helpful information...”
“This type of action is what keeps millions of viewers on the edge of their seats...”

If possible, explain how or why more specifically. Even if you do not use outside sources, you can give more concrete examples.

Also, be careful that some emotional wording weakens your academic tone. Phrases like “deep, dark secrets” or “dark skeletons taking over their daily lives” are vivid, but they sound more dramatic than analytical.

Style and tone
Your tone is passionate, which helps the essay feel lively. However, for university writing, aim for a more formal and controlled style.

For example:
“trash t.v..” → “so-called ‘trash TV’”
“lash out angrily and aggressively” is acceptable, but repeated dramatic phrases may sound exaggerated.

Try to balance strong language with clear analysis.

Grammar, spelling, and punctuation
This is the area that most needs revision. There are many errors that make the essay harder to read. Focus especially on:

a) Spelling
Examples:
audeinces → audiences
agressively → aggressively
thier → their
heath → health
fueding → feuding
dies deep → digs deep
thay → they
nieghbor → neighbor

b) Subject-verb agreement
“These differences in opinions leads...” → lead
“The guest are willing...” → guests are

c) Plurals and possessives
“television viewers lives” → television viewers’ lives
“guest panelist” → guest panelists
“each of the guest” → each of the guests

d) Sentence boundaries and punctuation
There are many misplaced periods and commas:
“trash t.v.. is vulgar. and inappropriate...”
“Many faithful viewers. feel...”
“neighbors. and lovers.”

Revise carefully for complete sentences. Read the essay aloud to hear where pauses should actually be commas, not periods.

e) Word choice
Some words do not fit the meaning:
“find solitude” probably should be comfort or solace
“one guest has no idea who the crush is” → better: one guest does not know that he is the object of the crush
“put their, character on the line” → put their character on the line

Conclusion
Your final paragraph makes an important point about personal responsibility, but it feels somewhat abrupt. Strengthen it by reconnecting to your thesis. Explain that while talk shows may be exploitative, participation is voluntary, and the genre should be judged with nuance rather than dismissed entirely.

Revision priorities
When you revise, do these in order:
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs around clear topic sentences.
Remove overly dramatic or unclear wording.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation carefully.

Overall, your essay has strong potential because you clearly care about the topic and recognize its complexity. With a clearer argument and more careful editing, it can become a much stronger academic essay.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear position: the legal drinking age in the United States should be lowered to 18. Your essay also shows strong opinion and energy, which is important in argumentative writing. You use several reasons to support your claim, especially the idea that if 18-year-olds can serve in the military, they should also be allowed to drink. This is a good starting point. To improve the essay, focus on organization, logic, tone, and grammar.

Main strengths
Clear thesis: You clearly state your opinion in the introduction.
Relevant topic: The issue is debatable and appropriate for an argumentative essay.
Passionate voice: Your writing shows commitment to your argument.

Biggest areas to revise

A. Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis is: “Eighteen should be the legal age to consume alcohol in the United States.”  
This is clear, but your introduction before it is weak because the history references are not accurate or necessary.

For example:
“Every since” should be Ever since
“Declaration of Independance” should be Declaration of Independence
The sentence about U.S. history does not help prove your claim.

Suggestion: Start with the issue directly and end the paragraph with a strong thesis.

Possible revision:
> In the United States, people can vote, join the military, and take on adult responsibilities at age eighteen. For this reason, the legal drinking age should be lowered from twenty-one to eighteen.

B. Improve paragraph structure
Each body paragraph should have:
a topic sentence
an explanation of the reason
evidence or examples
a concluding sentence

Right now, some paragraphs repeat ideas or jump suddenly to new points. For example, the paragraph about high school diplomas is confusing and does not connect strongly to your main argument.

Try organizing your essay around 3 strong reasons:
At 18, people already have adult legal responsibilities.
Lowering the drinking age may reduce secretive or unsafe drinking.
The current law punishes young adults for behavior that could be better regulated.

C. Use stronger logic and evidence
Some claims are too broad or unsupported. For example:
“Most crime among teenagers is drinking and driving.”  
This needs evidence and may not be true.
“Maybe a higher percentage of teenagers will graduate…”  
This is only a guess.
“It would just make the U.S. a better environment to live in.”  
This is too vague.

In academic argument, your ideas are stronger if you:
avoid exaggeration
use facts, statistics, or reliable examples
explain clearly how one idea leads to another

Also, some arguments need more careful thinking. For example, saying people should drink because they can “feel the affects of drinking alcohol” is not persuasive. A better approach is to discuss rights, responsibility, and regulation.

D. Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too informal:
“drink a beer”
“kids are dieing for our country”
“do anything an adult can do”

In academic writing, use words like:
young adults instead of kids
dying instead of dieing
effects instead of affects (in this case)

Also avoid focusing too much on yourself in the conclusion:
“I would feel more like an adult...”  
Instead, explain why the policy would benefit society more generally.

Language corrections

Here are some important sentence-level corrections:

Every since → Ever since
Independance → Independence
can not → cannot
dieing → dying
two young → too young
affects of drinking alcohol → effects of drinking alcohol
eighteen year old → eighteen-year-olds
containing alcohol in the car → probably possessing alcohol in a car
to to → to
convince the gonvernment → convince the government

Also watch sentence grammar. Example:
> “How can you think you are a man and fight in combat, when the government says that you are two young to do what all of the other adults.”

This sentence is awkward and incomplete. A clearer version:
> It is inconsistent for the government to allow eighteen-year-olds to fight in combat but not allow them to drink alcohol legally.

Conclusion advice
Your conclusion should restate the thesis and summarize your main reasons. Do not introduce new ideas. The military point belongs in the body, not at the end.

Possible conclusion:
> In conclusion, the legal drinking age should be lowered to eighteen because people at that age already accept adult responsibilities, including military service, and because the current law often encourages unsafe and secretive drinking. A lower drinking age would treat young adults more fairly and allow alcohol use to be handled more responsibly.

Revision goals
When you rewrite, try to:
create a more direct introduction
use one clear reason per body paragraph
remove weak or speculative ideas
support claims with logic or evidence
use more formal academic language
proofread carefully for spelling and grammar

You have a strong opinion and a clear argument direction. With better organization and more formal, precise language, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear purpose: you argue that children should develop gender identity in healthier ways and that society should challenge harmful stereotypes. You also use multiple sources, which shows ambition and engagement with the topic. The main area for revision is focus and control: your paper has interesting ideas, but the argument sometimes becomes unclear, contradictory, or too general. Below are the most important revision priorities.

Clarify your thesis
Your introduction raises several claims:
children should learn gender identity early,
there is a “core” of male/female behavior,
stereotypes cause harm,
men and women communicate differently,
gender roles should remain dynamic.

These ideas do not all fit together smoothly. For example, saying there is a fixed “core” of male/female behavior may conflict with your later argument that roles should be flexible and not restrictive.

Revise by writing one precise thesis that states exactly what you want to prove. For example:
> Society should teach children that gender expectations exist, but these expectations should not limit individual choices, because rigid stereotypes harm both boys and girls in adulthood.

A clearer thesis will help you decide what evidence belongs in the essay.

Improve organization
Right now, each paragraph introduces a source, but the essay often becomes a summary of readings rather than a developed argument.

A stronger structure might be:

Introduction + thesis
How children learn stereotypes (use Pollitt)
How stereotypes harm girls and boys (develop this with analysis)
Adult consequences of rigid roles (use Ferrell and Friedan)
Possible solution: more flexible models of gender (use Gray carefully)
Conclusion

At the start of each body paragraph, use a topic sentence that connects directly to your thesis. Example:
> Television teaches children narrow ideas about gender, and these messages can shape their expectations for adult life.

Analyze sources more deeply
You bring in Pollitt, Ferrell, Friedan, and Gray, but often you mention them briefly without fully explaining how they support your claim.

For example, when discussing Pollitt, explain:
What exact stereotype appears in children’s media?
Why is it harmful?
How does it affect girls differently from boys?

When discussing Ferrell and Friedan, explain the connection between childhood messages and adult unhappiness. Right now, that connection is implied but not fully argued.

Also, be careful with Dr. John Gray. His views may not be as scholarly as the other authors, and your essay treats his ideas as unquestioned fact. If you use him, explain specifically how his work supports flexibility rather than stereotype.

Avoid overgeneralization
Some statements are too broad or unsupported, such as:
“Parents are not interested in justice, they just want quiet.”
“Children need to know men and women walk, talk, drive, and grow in different ways.”

These claims sound absolute and may weaken your credibility. Academic argument usually works better with cautious language:
“Some parents may allow children to watch such programs for convenience.”
“Children should learn that individuals may express gender differently.”

Try to avoid presenting all men, all women, or all parents as the same.

Strengthen style and tone
At times your wording is vivid, but it can become informal or emotionally charged:
“life’s roller coaster ride”
“off to a wonderful start”
“scarry confession”

A more academic tone would improve the essay. Try to use precise language instead of sarcasm or exaggeration.

Also, some sentences are awkward or unclear. Example:
> “These twisted exogenic pressures deny women of any real choice in life.”

You could revise to:
> “These outside social pressures can limit women’s sense of choice and independence.”

Sentence-level grammar and mechanics
There are many grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors, and they sometimes distract from your ideas. Patterns to fix:

Spelling
Examples:
relazed → realized or relaxed (depending on meaning)
ammend → amend
pints → points
scarry → scary
sponce → spouse
metophor → metaphor
Califorian → Californian
Venue → Venus
rolds → roles

Apostrophes
“their’s” → theirs
“its easy” → it’s easy

Agreement
“types of behavior that is open” → are open
“society must ammend the method and the message we are giving our children”  
  Better: society must amend both the methods and messages we give children

Punctuation
You have several comma splices and punctuation problems:
“Pollit contends:that…” → remove the colon
“she cites. many painful examples” → period error
add commas carefully, but do not overuse them

Check names and citations carefully
Several authors’ names are misspelled:
Katha Pollitt
Betty Friedan
Warren Farrell
John Gray

This is important in academic writing because incorrect names reduce credibility. Also, make sure quotations and page numbers follow one citation style consistently.

Final advice

For revision, focus first on one clear thesis, then reorganize paragraphs around that thesis, and finally edit carefully for grammar and spelling. Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that legalizing some drugs, especially marijuana, could benefit society. This is a strong topic for an argumentative essay, and you show engagement with several issues such as public health, crime, jail overcrowding, government control, and personal freedom. You also try to consider counterarguments, which is an important academic skill.

What is working well

Clear overall opinion  
   From the introduction, the reader understands your main idea: drug use will continue, so legalization and regulation may be better than prohibition.

Range of arguments  
   You include several different supporting points:
   - regulation and purity
   - comparison with alcohol and cigarettes
   - crime reduction
   - prison overcrowding
   - tax money and jobs
   - disease prevention
   - personal liberty

Strong sense of voice  
   Your writing sounds engaged and persuasive. This can be a strength in argument writing.

Main areas to revise

Make the thesis more precise
Your thesis now is: “The legalization of some drugs like marijuana would improve many different aspects of society.”  
This is a good start, but it is too broad. Try to state exactly which drugs, how, and why.

For example:  
“The legalization and regulation of marijuana would benefit society by reducing drug-related crime, improving public health oversight, and decreasing pressure on the criminal justice system.”

This gives your essay a clearer direction.

Improve organization
Right now, some paragraphs mix several ideas together. This makes the argument harder to follow. Try this structure:

Introduction: background + thesis
Body 1: health and regulation
Body 2: age limits and youth access
Body 3: crime and prisons
Body 4: economic benefits
Body 5: personal freedom / counterargument
Conclusion: restate argument clearly

Each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that tells the reader the main point.

Example:  
“One important benefit of marijuana legalization is that the government could regulate its quality and reduce some health risks.”

Use more formal academic language
Some sentences sound too conversational or too absolute. Academic writing is stronger when it is careful and precise.

Examples:
“Drugs are here to stay whether anyone likes it or not.”  
  Better: “Drug use is unlikely to disappear completely.”
“If people want to harm their bodies, why stop them?”  
  Better: “Supporters of legalization argue that adults should have greater control over personal choices that mainly affect their own bodies.”

Also avoid phrases like “I do not know of many people…” unless the essay is specifically asking for personal experience.

Support claims with evidence
Many of your points are interesting, but they need evidence. For example:
“Most violent crimes stimulate from selling illicit drugs.”
“Hopefully, the number of drug users would decrease.”
“Intravenously used drugs would not likely lead to the spread of AIDS.”

These are strong claims, so you should support them with reliable sources, statistics, or studies. In academic argument, evidence is essential.

Be careful with logic
Some ideas need more explanation or qualification. For example:
You mainly argue for marijuana legalization, but sometimes you switch to “other drugs” without explaining which ones.
You say age limits may encourage children to get drugs. This may be true in some cases, but you need evidence and fuller reasoning.
The statement “If somebody does not want to live on this earth, why make them?” is too extreme and does not fit the argument well. It weakens your essay because it shifts away from the main issue.

Stay focused on the strongest and most defendable arguments.

Language corrections to notice

Here are a few common grammar and word-choice issues:

“whether anyone likes it or not” → acceptable, but informal
“lease harmful” → least harmful
“The side effects of marijuana is” → The side effects of marijuana are
“can not” → cannot
“get a hold of” → obtain
“Jails are over crowded” → Jails are overcrowded
“what anyone say” → what anyone says
“peoples body” → people’s bodies
“The government claim” → The government claims
“constitution liberty” → constitutional liberty
“injuries us all” → injures us all
“hann your body” → harm your body
“governments plan” → government’s plan

Also check punctuation, especially comma use and sentence fragments. For example:
“Issues such as permanent damage to the brain and other parts of the anatomy.”  
  This is a fragment. Make it a full sentence.

Final advice
For revision, focus on:
writing a more specific thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one main idea,
removing extreme or unclear statements,
adding evidence,
editing grammar carefully.
