Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a strong sense of purpose. You argue that professors at USC should be able to speak English well enough to teach effectively, and you support this claim with a concrete story, practical reasons, and a policy proposal. Your main strengths are:  
A clear thesis  
A relevant opening example  
A consistent focus on student needs and fairness

To revise successfully, focus on organization, tone, evidence, and sentence-level correctness.

Argument and organization

Your essay has a recognizable argumentative structure: anecdote → problem → reasons → solution. That is good. However, some paragraphs are repetitive, and the line of reasoning could be sharper.

What to revise
State your thesis more precisely and earlier.
Make each body paragraph do one clear job.
Avoid repeating the same point (“students pay tuition, so they deserve instruction”) too many times.

Suggested structure
Introduction: Briefly introduce the issue and state your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: Explain how limited English can harm classroom learning.
Body paragraph 2: Explain why this is unfair to students academically and financially.
Body paragraph 3: Address policy inconsistency (teaching assistants are tested, professors are not).
Body paragraph 4: Present your solution and explain why it is reasonable.
Conclusion: Reinforce the argument in a balanced way.

Your anecdote is effective, but it is too long. Try shortening it so that it supports the argument without taking over the introduction.

Tone and fairness

Your position is understandable, but some wording sounds too absolute or unfairly directed at “professors from other nations.” This may weaken your credibility. In academic argument, it is better to focus on teaching ability and communication, not nationality.

Examples
Instead of:
“professors who don’t speak English”
“Ph.D.s from other nations”

Try:
“professors whose English proficiency is not sufficient for classroom instruction”
“instructors, regardless of background, who are assigned to teach English-speaking students”

Also avoid claims like “the only solution.” Usually, academic arguments are stronger when they acknowledge complexity. For example, there may be multiple solutions: language screening, training, mentoring, co-teaching, or temporary teaching support.

Development and evidence

Right now, your main evidence is one personal story. It is a strong example, but one anecdote is not enough for a fully convincing argument.

To improve
Add broader support such as:
university policy comparisons,
examples of how communication affects learning in labs, office hours, and lectures,
possible consequences for graduation timelines,
acknowledgement that expertise alone is not enough without communication.

You should also consider a counterargument. For example:
Universities hire international scholars because they are leading researchers and experts.
Then respond:
Research excellence is valuable, but teaching assignments should still require sufficient communication skills or support systems.

Including a counterargument will make your essay sound more thoughtful and mature.

Clarity and style

Many sentences are wordy or difficult to follow. Aim for shorter, more direct sentences. You often say the same idea in several ways. Tightening your prose will make the essay more persuasive.

Example revision
Original:  
“The key is for the expert to be able to communicate his points clearly so that students can grasp the knowledge.”

Clearer:  
“Expert knowledge is useful only when professors can communicate it clearly to students.”

Original:  
“When professors can't speak English, they can't communicate with English speaking students; therefore they cannot teach them.”

Clearer:  
“When professors cannot communicate clearly in English, students may struggle to understand lectures, ask questions, and learn effectively.”

Notice that the revised version is more precise and less absolute.

Grammar and sentence-level issues

There are many editing problems that need attention before the final draft. These include:
sentence fragments,
run-on sentences,
punctuation errors,
spacing problems,
missing apostrophes,
awkward phrasing,
inconsistent pronouns.

Common issues in your draft
“It s” → It’s
“teacher's assistants” may need to be teaching assistants depending on meaning
“before a professor should be allowed domain over a classroom” → awkward phrasing
“speak a different language other than” → better: speak a different language from
pronouns such as he/his for all professors and students sound outdated; use they/their where possible

Example
Original:  
“She really needed the course for her major her only alternatives were…”

Corrected:  
“She really needed the course for her major. Her only alternatives were…”

Please proofread carefully, because some errors make sentences hard to understand.

Final advice for revision

When you revise, focus on these priorities:

Shorten the introduction anecdote
Write a more precise thesis
Organize body paragraphs by separate reasons
Use more neutral, academic language
Add at least one counterargument and response
Edit for grammar, punctuation, and conciseness

Possible revised thesis

You have a strong core argument. With better organization, more careful wording, and thorough editing, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that Art Modell’s decision to move the Cleveland Browns was driven by greed and betrayed the fans and the city. Strengthen your thesis and overall focus

For example, your thesis could be:
Art Modell’s decision to move the Cleveland Browns to Baltimore was unjustified because it ignored the loyalty of Cleveland fans, rejected the city’s financial support, and placed profit above community responsibility.

This gives the essay a clear direction and creates 3 main points for body paragraphs.

Improve organization

Body Paragraph 1: why the Browns mattered to Cleveland
Body Paragraph 2: Cleveland’s financial and civic support
Body Paragraph 3: Modell’s promises and contradictions
Body Paragraph 4: why the move was motivated by profit
Conclusion: restate argument and explain why it matters

Also, make sure each paragraph has one main idea. Some of your paragraphs mix background, emotion, and evidence all at once.

Use a more formal academic tone
Your argument is persuasive, but some wording is too emotional or informal for an academic essay. For example:

“dump city”
“suck it up and take it like a man”
“gave Cleveland the one finger salute”
“Arthur B. Modell is a liar”

These phrases sound forceful, but they weaken academic credibility. Instead of directly insulting Modell, explain his actions and let the evidence persuade the reader.

For example:
Instead of “Arthur B. Modell is a liar,”
  write “Modell contradicted his earlier public statements about keeping the team in Cleveland.”

This sounds stronger in formal writing.

Support claims more carefully
You include many facts, but some need clearer explanation or source support. In academic argument, facts are more convincing when introduced and interpreted carefully.

For instance, when you list what Baltimore offered Modell, explain why that matters:
How does this prove greed?
How does it compare with what Cleveland offered?
Why should public loyalty matter in a professional sports business?

Also, avoid overstatement unless you can prove it. For example:
“the only people who hurt are the fans of Cleveland” is probably too absolute.
Better:
“the move most directly harmed Cleveland fans, who had supported the team financially and emotionally for decades.”

Work on sentence clarity and grammar
There are many grammar and mechanics errors that make the essay harder to read. Focus especially on:

Apostrophes
cities most beloved team → city’s most beloved team
it's tax subsidized teams → its tax-subsidized teams

Word choice / spelling
Rains → Rams
rapid → probably rabid
march of 1983 → March of 1983
inwhich → in which
to fast → too fast
nation wide → nationwide

Punctuation
Some sentences are too long or joined incorrectly. Example:

Capitalization and formatting
St.Louis → St. Louis
54 year old → 54-year-old
10years → 10 years

Clarify meaning in several places

“The city of Cleveland will be consciously devastated”
  → Better: “The city of Cleveland will be deeply devastated”

  → Better: “The facts have become confused because events happened so quickly.”

Conclusion

Final advice
You have the basis for a compelling argumentative essay because you care strongly about the topic and already use concrete examples. In revision, try to control the emotion with structure and evidence. If you organize your ideas more clearly and use a more formal tone, your argument will become much more persuasive.

Strengths

Balanced scope: You discuss both women’s and men’s experiences, which makes the argument broader and more thoughtful.
Engaging style: Your rhetorical questions (“But what if it’s wrong?”) create reader interest.

Main areas to revise

For example:

This would give the essay a clearer direction.

Improve organization
Your essay has many good ideas, but some paragraphs are long and contain several different points. Try to organize the essay into clearer sections:

Body paragraph 1: pressure on women, using Friedan
Body paragraph 2: pressure on men, using Ralph
Body paragraph 3: consequences of following social expectations
Body paragraph 4: possible alternatives / middle path
Conclusion: restate the main argument clearly

*Similarly*
*In contrast*

*Therefore*
*However*

These will help the reader follow your logic.

Develop analysis more and summarize less
At times, the essay retells examples rather than analyzing them. For instance, the long description of the career woman is vivid, but after describing it, explain more directly how this supports your argument. Ask yourself:
What does this example prove?
Why does it matter?
How does it connect to my thesis?

The same applies to Ralph. Instead of mostly narrating his life, explain how his story demonstrates the danger of accepting other people’s definition of success.

Avoid overgeneralizations
Some claims sound too broad, such as:
“nothing has changed”
“today a woman is almost required to work outside the home”
“few men” or “few women”

These may be partly true, but in academic argument, sweeping statements can weaken credibility. Use more cautious language:
*In many cases*
*For some families*
*Often*
*Many people feel that…*

This makes your argument sound more balanced and persuasive.

Strengthen the conclusion
The last sentence points toward a solution, but the conclusion could do more to tie the whole essay together. Remind the reader of your key message: that social approval should not be the main basis for life decisions, because personal fulfillment and meaningful relationships matter more.

Language and style corrections

Your writing is expressive, but there are several grammar, word choice, and punctuation issues to correct.

Examples:
“being ,pressured” → being pressured
“what they want to do the rest of their lives” → what they want to do for the rest of their lives
“erected of them” → expected of them
“reouired” → required
“actuality prefer” → actually prefer
“tine-in” → tie-in or better connection
“a. corporate cog-in-the-wheel” → a corporate cog in the wheel

Sentence-level issues
Some sentences are too long and become hard to follow. Break them into shorter units. For example:

Original:  
“Many women followed this advice and at the same time did their best to have a successful marriage and be a perfect mother.”

This is clear, but when followed by several long sentences, the paragraph becomes heavy. Vary sentence length.

Word choice
Be careful with informal or slightly awkward phrases:
“What’s not to like about…” is conversational; in an academic essay, write:  
  Such a choice could bring both personal fulfillment and greater respect from one’s family.

Pronouns and consistency
You sometimes move between “man,” “woman,” “they,” and “he or she” in ways that feel inconsistent. Try to keep grammatical agreement consistent.

Missing quotations/citations
You have placeholders marked as . These must be completed. If you refer to Friedan and Farrell, include the actual quotations or remove the placeholders. Also, if this is an academic essay, make sure to cite sources in the required style.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong core idea and clear moral seriousness. For revision, focus on:
writing a sharper thesis,
improving paragraph structure,
adding more analysis,
reducing overgeneralization,
editing grammar and word choice carefully.

With these changes, your argument will become clearer, more academic, and more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear, energetic voice and a strong opinion. You argue that men and women are naturally different and that society should appreciate those differences rather than treat them as shameful. The essay is bold and confident, which is a strength in argumentative writing. However, to revise successfully, you need to improve focus, logic, organization, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear position: The reader understands your general claim.
Confidence and originality: You are not afraid to take a controversial stance.
Use of examples: You try to support your ideas with sports, advertising, restaurants, and entertainment.

Main problems with argument
Your biggest issue is that the essay often confuses “difference” with “sexual display” or “unequal treatment.” These are not the same thing.

For example, you say laws should treat everyone equally, but then you defend cases where people are treated differently because of sex. If you want to argue this, you must explain why this difference is fair and where the limits are.

Also, some examples do not strongly support your main claim:
The football/volleyball funding example becomes more about money and school priorities than about respecting differences between men and women.
Strip clubs and Hooters are used as examples of appreciating difference, but many readers will see them as examples of objectification, not respect. If you keep these examples, you must address that counterargument directly.
The comparison between Charles Barkley and Madonna is interesting, but it is oversimplified. You need stronger reasoning and evidence.

Organization and structure
The essay needs a more logical structure. Right now it moves from one example to another without enough explanation.

A stronger structure could be:

Introduction
   - Define your key idea: What does it mean to “enjoy differences”?
   - State your thesis clearly.

Body Paragraph 1: Equality under the law
   - Explain that equal rights do not necessarily mean men and women are identical.

Body Paragraph 2: Physical differences in sports
   - Use sports as your strongest example because it directly relates to biological differences.
   - Be careful not to mix this with weak financial arguments.

Body Paragraph 3: Cultural attitudes toward attraction
   - Discuss media, beauty, and attraction.
   - Acknowledge concerns about sexism and objectification.

Counterargument paragraph
   - Recognize that some people worry that emphasizing difference can justify discrimination.
   - Explain how appreciation of difference can exist without inequality.

Conclusion
   - Restate your claim in a more thoughtful, balanced way.

Tone and audience awareness
Your tone is sometimes too casual or provocative for an academic essay. Phrases like:
“let us lust after the opposite sex”
“open one called buttocks for women”
make the essay sound more like a speech or opinion rant than a serious academic argument.

Try to use a more formal tone. You can still make the same point, but with language that sounds more thoughtful and credible.

Need for evidence
The essay makes many broad claims without evidence. For example:
“Women and men are, of course, different physically and emotionally.”
“Most guys do not want to have men in small outfits serving them food.”
“The main point of wearing cologne or perfume is to attract the opposite sex.”

These are large generalizations. In academic writing, you should support such claims with:
statistics
research
expert opinion
more precise examples

Language issues
There are many grammar, word choice, and spelling errors. Some common ones:

there / their / they’re
  - “their differences” not “there differences”
woman / women
  - singular = woman, plural = women
resent → recent
underlining → underlying
were → where
vise versa → vice versa
scene → seen
sexes → sexist (in context)
sells job → sales job
retumed → returned
trask → trash
since of love → sense of love

Also, many sentences are awkward or unclear. Example:
“These differences are the underlining driving force of opposite sex interaction.”
This could become:
“These differences often shape how men and women relate to one another.”

Thesis revision
Your thesis should be clearer and more precise. For example:

Possible revision:
“Although men and women should have equal legal rights and social respect, society should not assume that equality requires sameness; in many areas of life, natural differences between the sexes can be acknowledged and appreciated without justifying discrimination.”

This version is more balanced and academic.

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Clarify your thesis
Use fewer examples, but explain them better
Address opposing views
Adopt a more formal tone
Correct grammar and spelling carefully

You have a strong sense of purpose, but to make this an effective argumentative essay, you need to turn strong opinions into a clear, logical, and well-supported argument.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that welfare reform should combine Democratic and Republican ideas. This is a strong argumentative approach because it presents a compromise rather than only one side. You also use sources throughout the essay, which helps give your argument support. For revision, focus on strengthening your thesis, improving organization, and making your reasoning more careful and precise.

Main strengths
Clear topic and stance: You identify the debate and state your own solution.
Use of sources: You include evidence from several texts, which shows research.
Consistent focus: Most body paragraphs relate to your main claim about reform.

Thesis and argument
Your thesis appears at the end of the introduction:

> “The welfare program would be successful if it changed to include a time limit, training programs that are required, less government funding for certain welfare programs, and abolishing the law that states that women should get more money for having more children.”

This is a solid start, but it can be made more precise and academic. Right now, it is a list of ideas, but it does not explain why these changes would work or address possible objections.

Revision suggestion
Try a thesis like this:

“The U.S. welfare system should be reformed through mandatory job training, reasonable time limits, and reduced incentives for long-term dependency because these changes would encourage employment, reduce government spending, and make welfare a temporary support rather than a permanent way of life.”

This version is more direct and shows the logic of your argument.

Organization
Your essay generally follows the points in your thesis, which is good. However, some paragraphs feel repetitive or underdeveloped.

Suggestions
Give each body paragraph one clear main idea.
Start each paragraph with a topic sentence that connects to your thesis.
After each quotation, explain how it supports your argument.

For example, in the paragraph about training programs, you include examples, but sometimes the paragraph becomes a series of quotations. Add more of your own explanation between them.

A possible structure:
Introduction with background + thesis  
Training programs  
Time limits  
Spending cuts / AFDC reform  
Counterargument and response  
Conclusion

Adding a counterargument would make the essay stronger. For example, you could acknowledge that some people need long-term assistance and then explain why reform is still necessary.

Use of evidence
You use many quotations, but sometimes they are not smoothly integrated. Also, some evidence is old, emotional, or too general. In an argumentative essay, avoid relying too much on statements that sound like opinion rather than fact.

Improve by:
Introducing the source clearly
Explaining the quotation afterward
Using fewer direct quotations and more summary/paraphrase

Example:

Instead of:
> “Welfare has become a way of life for some people...”

Try:
Silverstein argues that long-term welfare use can create dependency, which supports the need for time limits.

This sounds more academic and keeps your voice in control.

Reasoning and tone
Some claims in the essay are too absolute, such as:
“The welfare problem could easily be solved...”
“If these time limits were enforced there would no chance for recipients to become dependent...”

These are too strong and may weaken your credibility. Social issues are complex, so use more careful language:
“could help reduce dependency”
“would likely encourage recipients to seek employment”
“may reduce long-term use of welfare”

Also, some statements may sound biased, especially when discussing mothers and children. In academic writing, it is important to sound fair and objective.

Grammar and sentence-level issues
There are many language errors, but they are fixable. The main patterns are:

a. Capitalization
“fourteen” at the beginning of a quotation should be capitalized if it begins a sentence.
“whitman” should be Whitman
“highschool” should be high school

b. Word forms / spelling
“heatedly argued” → heatedly debated
“Sliverstein” → Silverstein
“life time” → lifetime
“tax payers” → taxpayers
“governments expense” → government’s expense
“get on welfare” → gets on welfare
“systems” → system

c. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or incorrect:
“If these time limits were enforced there would no chance...”  
  → If these time limits were enforced, there would be less chance...
“cut off recipients, benefits”  
  → cut off recipients’ benefits

d. Punctuation and quotation format
There are many quotation mark and comma problems. For example:
commas and periods should usually go inside quotation marks in American English.
make sure page citations are formatted consistently.
avoid double punctuation like lifetime,, or 199911

Conclusion
Your conclusion restates your point, but it could be stronger if it summarized the main reasons for your argument rather than criticizing the political parties. End by reinforcing your claim:

“By combining training requirements, time limits, and selective spending cuts, welfare reform can better support people in crisis while encouraging long-term independence.”

Final advice
For your next draft, focus on:
Revising the thesis
Adding more explanation after evidence
Using a more balanced academic tone
Editing carefully for grammar, spelling, and citation format

You have a clear argument and good material to work with. With stronger analysis and cleaner language, this essay can become much more persuasive.

Because college students are adults, because curfews create unnecessary inconvenience and safety risks, and because current dorm rules are inconsistent, single-sex dormitories should allow overnight visitation or at least remove the 2 a.m. curfew on weekends.

This gives your reader a roadmap.

State your thesis

Some dorms are co-ed or allow more freedom

Cousin visiting from Germany/military example

Leaving at 2 a.m. can be dangerous
Intoxicated students are more vulnerable

Restate the argument
End with a practical solution: no curfew, or weekend overnight visitation

This structure will make your essay more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay raises an important topic and shows that you have done some research by citing sources. You also try to present more than one side of the debate, which is a good start for an argumentative essay. To improve the paper, focus on having a clear thesis, stronger organization, and more precise explanation of your argument.

Main issue: your position is unclear
An argumentative essay needs a clear claim. In your introduction, you say violence on TV is “a serious concern,” but your conclusion says violence is used “to add texture to the show and not to poison children’s minds.” These ideas seem to conflict.

Ask yourself:
Do you argue that TV violence harms children?
Or do you argue that censorship and tools like the V-chip are not effective?
Or do you argue that parents, not government, should be responsible?

Choose one main position and make it clear in the introduction. For example:

Option 1: “Although violence on television is widespread, government censorship such as the V-chip is not the best solution; parental guidance is more effective.”
Option 2: “Because television violence can negatively influence children, stronger controls on violent programming are necessary.”

Once you choose, every paragraph should support that position.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay moves from TV violence, to the V-chip, to Congress, to adult viewers, to cartoons, and then to your conclusion. The reader may feel the ideas are disconnected.

A clearer structure could be:

Introduction
   - Background on TV violence
   - Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Extent of TV violence
   - Use evidence about how common violent programming is

Body Paragraph 2: Possible effects on children
   - Explain how or why children may be influenced

Body Paragraph 3: Responses to the problem
   - V-chip, congressional proposals, ratings, warnings

Body Paragraph 4: Your evaluation
   - Explain whether these solutions work or not, and why

Conclusion
   - Restate your argument clearly

Develop paragraphs more fully
Some paragraphs are too short or end without analysis. For example, you mention the Senate Commerce Committee and several proposals, but you do not explain how they support your argument. In an argumentative essay, evidence should always be followed by explanation.

A useful pattern is:
Topic sentence
Evidence
Analysis
Connection to thesis

For example, instead of only listing proposals, explain:
What each proposal is meant to do
Why supporters think it will help
Why critics oppose it
What you think

Use sources more carefully
You include citations, which is good, but some quotations or facts are not well integrated. Also, one sentence seems confusing: “1995-1995 season” is probably an error in the source or in copying.

Be careful to:
Introduce sources clearly
Explain the significance of the evidence
Use correct quotation marks and punctuation

For example:
Instead of: “An exhaustive examination … has concluded…”
Try: “According to Jensen, a study of network programming found that violent conflict was common in Saturday-morning cartoons.”

Then explain why this matters.

Make your reasoning more logical
Some claims need stronger support. For instance:
“Many children watch violent tv shows because the action thrills them and can sometimes cause the child to become violent.”
  - This is a strong claim. You need evidence or explanation.
“If a certain plot involves a murder, then the murder must happen.”
  - This is too absolute. Consider softer wording: “In some crime dramas, violence is presented as part of the plot.”
“One day that child will see violence and they will have to deal with it.”
  - This idea needs explanation. Seeing real-world violence is not the same as repeated exposure to entertainment violence.

Try to avoid unsupported generalizations and explain your logic step by step.

Language and style
Your writing is understandable, but there are grammar and style problems that weaken the essay.

Common issues:
Capitalization: “tv” should be TV
Word choice: “Some of the proposals to antiviolence” → “Some anti-violence proposals”
Repetition: You use “violence” many times in close sentences. Try synonyms like “violent content,” “aggressive imagery,” or “harmful programming.”
Awkward phrasing: “whomever is writing the television show” → “whoever writes the show”
Sentence clarity: “Some television shows show violence” is repetitive

Example revision:
Original:  
“Some television shows show violence because it appeals to adult viewers.”

Better:  
“Some television programs include violent scenes because producers believe such content appeals to adult audiences.”

Conclusion
Your conclusion should not introduce a new argument. It should clearly summarize your position and the main reasons supporting it. Right now, the ending feels abrupt.

Final advice
Before revising, write one sentence answering: What exactly am I trying to prove? Then revise each paragraph so it directly supports that sentence. You have a strong topic and useful research; now your goal is to turn the paper into a focused, persuasive argument.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: government should continue, and possibly increase, support for the arts. This is a strong and worthwhile topic, and your writing shows genuine engagement with it. You offer several kinds of support—historical, social, economic, educational, and therapeutic—which gives your argument breadth. For revision, focus on making your thesis sharper, organizing your ideas more logically, and improving sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths

Clear stance:  
From the introduction, your opinion is clear: arts funding should be protected.

Range of arguments:  
You do not rely on only one reason. You discuss:
art as cultural memory
art as communication
art as community-building
economic benefits
creativity and mental health
rehabilitation and prevention

This variety makes the essay thoughtful and ambitious.

Serious tone and vocabulary:  
Your language is often formal and appropriate for argumentative writing. You aim for an academic style, which is good.

Main areas to improve

A. Make the thesis more specific
Your current thesis is understandable, but it is somewhat broad:

> “there are some areas, such as government support for the arts, which should keep, if not receive more, support…”

Try to state *why* in one sentence. For example:

Possible revision:  
*Government funding for the arts should be maintained or increased because the arts preserve culture, strengthen communities, promote mental and intellectual development, and contribute to the economy.*

This gives the reader a clear roadmap.

B. Improve organization
Several body paragraphs have useful ideas, but the essay sometimes feels like a list rather than a progression. Group similar ideas together.

Suggested structure:
Introduction + thesis
Arts preserve culture and history
Arts communicate values and connect people
Arts benefit communities socially and economically
Arts improve education, creativity, and mental health
Counterargument / limits of funding
Conclusion

The paragraph about pornography feels disconnected from the main argument. If you keep it, connect it clearly to public funding and censorship. Otherwise, consider removing it, since it shifts attention away from your central claim.

C. Use stronger evidence
Many claims are interesting, but they need more specific support. For example:
“psychologists and psychoanalysts have told us for years”
“researchers have found”
“new research is being conducted”

These phrases are too vague. In academic argument, it is better to mention a specific study, field, or example. Even if this is a draft, try adding concrete evidence such as:
arts programs in schools improving student engagement
museum tourism revenue
prison arts programs and rehabilitation outcomes

D. Avoid overgeneralization
Some statements are too absolute, which weakens your credibility.

For example:
“art is something which produces a positive, constructive analysis of aspects of human existence”
“some works are not meant be valued as art”

These claims are debatable and may distract from your main point. In argumentative writing, avoid defining complex concepts too narrowly unless that definition is essential and well defended.

Language and grammar issues

Your meaning is usually clear, but there are recurring grammar and style problems.

A. Articles and word form
“which should keep” → which should be kept
“in a ever-more complex” → in an ever more complex
“meant be valued” → meant to be valued

B. Awkward phrasing
Some sentences sound unnatural in English. For example:

> “it leaves many to think which area of government spending will be cut next.”

Better:
“it leads many people to wonder which area of government spending will be cut next.”

> “These are very basic, but it is interpretable…”

Better:
“These drawings are very basic, but they can be understood by nearly everyone.”

C. Word choice
“pschosanalysts” → psychoanalysts
“oftentiines” → oftentimes or simply often
“diffuse some violent situations” → defuse some violent situations
“inadvertently encourage” may not be the right meaning; perhaps indirectly encourage

D. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long or abstract. Try to make them more direct. For example:

> “Summarily, our human needs may dictate that government…”

Better:
“In conclusion, because the arts meet important human and social needs, governments should continue to support them.”

Style suggestions

Avoid outdated generic nouns like “man” when you mean humanity. Use “human beings,” “people,” or “humanity.”
Be careful with rhetorical questions. One is fine, but too many can sound less academic.
Use transitions more precisely: furthermore, for example, in addition, however, therefore.

Revision priorities

Rewrite the thesis to include your main reasons.
Reorganize paragraphs so related ideas stay together.
Remove or revise the paragraph about pornography unless you connect it clearly to public funding.
Replace vague claims with specific examples or evidence.
Edit for grammar, word choice, and sentence clarity.

Final comment

This essay has strong potential because it shows original thought and a sincere belief in the value of the arts. Your next step is to turn that passion into a more focused academic argument. If you clarify your structure and support your claims more concretely, your essay will become much more persuasive.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0008.2.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You clearly take a strong position, and that is a good starting point for an argumentative essay. Your thesis is easy to identify: you believe professional football players’ salaries should be capped. You also organize your essay around several reasons, which gives the paper a recognizable structure. To improve the essay, focus on logic, evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: The reader always knows what side you are on.
Basic organization: You introduce reasons and develop them in separate paragraphs.
Engagement: Your writing shows passion, which can be persuasive if controlled carefully.

Main areas to revise

A. Make the argument more logical and focused
Right now, some claims do not directly prove that a salary cap is necessary. For example:
doctors are more important than athletes
some stadiums are in poor condition
players are greedy
money causes hate

These points may be related, but you need to explain how each point proves that a salary cap is the best solution. Ask yourself:
Does this point show a real problem?
Does it show that salary caps would solve that problem?
Do I have evidence?

A stronger essay would use 2–3 main arguments and develop them well, such as:
A salary cap promotes competitive balance between teams.
A salary cap controls excessive spending and protects team finances.
A salary cap can help ensure more money is available for fan experience and long-term investment.

B. Avoid weak or unfair generalizations
Several statements sound too extreme or unfair, for example:
“football players don't do anything that is considered rewarding”
“all you have to be able to do is run and catch balls”
“a person could not be smart enough to pass the second grade”
“greedy football players”

These statements weaken your credibility because they sound emotional rather than reasoned. In academic argument, it is better to be fair to the opposing side. You can still argue for salary caps without attacking players personally.

C. Use stronger evidence
Many claims need facts, examples, or statistics. For instance:
Do football players really make more in one year than doctors make in a lifetime?
Do salary caps improve competition?
How much money goes to players versus stadium maintenance?

Use evidence such as:
average NFL salaries
average doctor salaries
examples from leagues with salary caps
revenue-sharing or team spending data

Without evidence, the essay sounds based mostly on opinion.

D. Be careful with examples
The OJ Simpson example is problematic because it is emotionally charged and not clearly connected to salary caps. Also, it makes a broad legal claim that is difficult to prove. If you use examples, choose ones that directly support your point, such as player contracts, free agency, or team payroll differences.

Organization and development
Your introduction gives three reasons, which is good, but later the essay expands into many additional ideas. Try this structure:

Introduction
Hook
Background
Clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1
Topic sentence: salary caps reduce excessive player salaries and help teams manage finances
Evidence
Explanation

Body Paragraph 2
Topic sentence: salary caps create fairer competition between rich and poor teams
Evidence
Explanation

Body Paragraph 3
Topic sentence: salary caps may allow teams to invest more in fans and facilities
Evidence
Explanation

Conclusion
Restate thesis
Summarize reasons
End with a strong final thought

Style and tone
Try to sound more formal and less conversational. For example:
“So, why don't we stop giving them so much money” → “Therefore, limiting salaries may reduce this problem.”
“do wee really need professional football players?” → “This raises the question of whether entertainers should be compensated more than essential professionals.”

Avoid rhetorical questions too often. A few are fine, but too many make the essay sound informal.

Language corrections
Here are some recurring issues:

Word choice
“very affective” → very effective
“use to be” → used to be
“other peoples lives” → other people’s lives
“ant other team” → any other team

Grammar
“A doctor, or a nurse work” → A doctor or a nurse works / Doctors and nurses work
“the players go on strike is because of the money” → players go on strike because of money
“there are many ways which this could be done” → there are many ways this could be done

Sentence clarity
Some sentences are long and repetitive. For example:
“The one reason why they go on strike is because of the money.”
This repeats an idea you already stated. Remove repetition and combine similar sentences.

Revision priorities
Rewrite the thesis and choose 3 strong reasons.
Remove insulting or exaggerated statements.
Add specific evidence and facts.
Improve formal tone.
Edit grammar, spelling, and word choice.

Final advice
You have a clear viewpoint and strong energy as a writer. Now your goal is to make the essay sound more credible, fair, and evidence-based. If you revise with a calmer tone and stronger proof, your argument will become much more convincing.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0008.3.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that stronger gun control is constitutional and necessary to reduce violence. You also use sources and statistics, which is a strong feature in an argumentative essay. The main revision goal is to improve organization, accuracy, style, and language control so that your argument becomes more convincing and academic.

Strengths
Clear thesis and purpose: You consistently support gun control.
Use of evidence: You include historical background, court interpretation, and statistics.
Awareness of counterarguments: You mention pro-gun activists and the NRA, which helps make the essay argumentative rather than one-sided.

Main issues to revise

A. Organization and focus
At the moment, the essay feels somewhat repetitive. Several paragraphs make similar points about the Constitution, violence statistics, and the need for regulation. Try to organize the paper into clear sections:

Introduction: background + thesis  
Constitutional argument  
Evidence that gun availability increases harm  
Response to counterarguments  
Policy solutions  
Conclusion

Also, some paragraphs begin with one topic and shift to another. For example, the paragraph about the Second Amendment should stay focused on constitutional interpretation only.

B. Thesis
Your first paragraph contains the thesis, but it is too long and includes many supporting points. Make the thesis shorter and more direct.

For example:  
“Although some Americans argue that gun ownership is a constitutional right, stronger federal gun control measures such as background checks, waiting periods, licensing, and registration are constitutional and would help reduce violent crime and prevent avoidable deaths.”

C. Use of evidence
You include many statistics, but some are old and some claims are too absolute. For example:
“Law-abiding citizens do not need handguns” is too broad.
“The courts agree” is also too broad.

In academic writing, avoid overstating. Use more cautious language:
“Many law-abiding citizens rarely use handguns for self-defense.”
“Many court decisions have allowed significant firearm regulation.”

Also, explain the evidence more. Do not only present statistics; show how they support your claim.

D. Counterargument
You mention the pro-gun position, but you should engage with it more fairly. A stronger paragraph would:
State the opposing view accurately.
Acknowledge why some people believe it.
Refute it with evidence.

For example:  
“Supporters of broad gun rights argue that firearms protect citizens from crime. However, this claim is difficult to verify statistically, while evidence of firearm deaths, accidents, and suicides is well documented.”

Language and grammar issues

A. Capitalization
Do not capitalize common nouns in the middle of sentences:
“Gun Control” → gun control
“Constitutional Rights” → constitutional rights
“Federal Legislation” → federal legislation
“Guns” → guns

B. Word form / spelling
There are many spelling and word-choice errors. Examples:
“buyers backgrounds” → buyers’ backgrounds
“sawed of shotguns” → sawed-off shotguns
“hand gun” → handgun
“machineguns” → machine guns
“fire arms” → firearms
“ant-gun” → anti-gun
“corpses” is too emotional; better deaths or victims
“definatly” → definitely
“can not” → cannot
“all together” → altogether
“prevent identification” → present identification
“assault riffles” → assault rifles
“licencing” → licensing

C. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are difficult to read or have punctuation problems. Example:
“lnl981,JimBrady,W'hiteHousePresssecretarywasshot...”  
This needs correction and spacing:  
“In 1981, Jim Brady, the White House press secretary, was shot during the attempted assassination of President Ronald Reagan.”

D. Quotations and citation style
Be consistent with source names and formatting:
You use Cozic, Cole, CUF, Cosec, and The Close Up Foundation inconsistently.
Choose one citation format and use it throughout.
Integrate quotations smoothly into your own sentence.

For example:
According to Cozic, the assassination attempt on Reagan “revived the debate about private gun ownership” (84).

Style improvements
Aim for a more formal academic tone. Avoid emotionally loaded or absolute phrases such as:
“foolish to believe”
“tools for social violence”
“Clearly, our citizens agree”

Instead, use neutral academic language:
“It is unrealistic to assume...”
“These weapons are often misused in violent crimes.”
“Public opinion has often supported certain gun regulations.”

Suggested revision priorities
Rewrite the introduction with one strong thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs by topic.
Remove repetition and combine similar points.
Correct grammar, spelling, and formatting errors.
Use a more formal, balanced tone.
Check all citations for consistency and accuracy.

Final comment
This essay has a solid argumentative foundation and good supporting material. Your main challenge is not ideas, but presentation: clearer structure, more careful wording, and cleaner grammar will make your argument much stronger. After revision, this could become a persuasive and effective academic essay.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0008.4.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear position on abortion, and your essay shows strong personal engagement with the topic. You also try to consider exceptions, such as rape and danger to the mother’s life, which makes your argument more complex than a simple “for or against” essay. In addition, your personal example about your cousin gives the essay emotional force. These are all strengths.

Main issue: clarify and narrow your argument
At the moment, your thesis is not fully precise. In the introduction, you say there should be “strict rules regarding abortions,” but later you argue for punishment, exceptions, government regulation, and personal morality. These are related, but not the same claim.

To revise: decide exactly what you want to argue:
Should abortion be illegal except in certain cases?
Should government regulate abortion more strictly?
Should abortion be morally discouraged but still legal in some cases?

Write one thesis statement that clearly states your position and the exceptions you accept.

Organization and paragraph structure
Your essay has several good ideas, but the structure is loose. Some paragraphs are too long and contain multiple points. This makes it harder for the reader to follow your argument.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Body Paragraph 1: consensual sex and responsibility  
Body Paragraph 2: alternatives to abortion (contraception, adoption, aid)  
Body Paragraph 3: exceptions (rape, danger to mother’s life)  
Body Paragraph 4: personal example of your cousin  
Conclusion: restate your main claim in a more formal way

Use a topic sentence at the start of each paragraph so the reader knows the main point immediately.

Tone and academic style
Your essay expresses strong feelings, but some word choices sound emotional, judgmental, or informal. In academic writing, this can weaken your argument because it may sound biased instead of reasoned.

For example, phrases like:
“play the game”
“horseplay”
“kill it”
“wild maniac”
“vicious sperm”
“fiend”

These expressions are too informal or emotionally loaded for an academic essay.

To revise: use neutral and respectful language. For example:
“engage in consensual sex”
“accept responsibility for the consequences”
“terminate the pregnancy”
“a woman who becomes pregnant as a result of rape”

A more formal tone will make your argument stronger and more persuasive.

Development of evidence
Some of your claims need stronger support. You mention contraception effectiveness, welfare, and medical consequences of abortion, but you do not cite any sources. In academic argument, facts and statistics should be supported by reliable evidence.

Also, the personal story about your cousin is powerful, but one example does not prove that abortion usually causes infertility. Be careful not to generalize from one case.

To revise:
Add reliable sources for statistics about contraception and pregnancy.
If you discuss medical risks of abortion, use medical or scholarly evidence.
Keep the cousin example as a personal illustration, not as proof of a universal medical outcome.

Logic and fairness
You make some strong claims that may seem unfair or too absolute, such as saying women who have abortions after consensual sex “should be put into jail for murder.” This is a very serious claim, but the essay does not fully explain or justify it. Readers may reject your argument if they feel it is too extreme.

Also, in one part you say abortion is wrong because the fetus is a life, but later you allow abortion in cases of rape. This creates a logical tension: if abortion is always murder, why would rape change that? You need to explain your moral reasoning more carefully.

To revise: address possible counterarguments and explain your exceptions clearly. This will make your essay more balanced and intellectually convincing.

Grammar and sentence-level issues
There are many grammar and style errors, but most can be fixed through careful editing. Main patterns include:

Subject-verb agreement:  
  “a sensible, well-minded women consents” → “a sensible woman consents”
Word form:  
  “prevent pregnancies” / “prevention of pregnancy” — be consistent
Missing words:  
  “The birth control pill is said to ninety-five percent effective” → “is said to be ninety-five percent effective”
Pronouns:  
  be careful switching between “person,” “woman,” “they,” “he or she”
Run-on sentences/comma splices:  
  several sentences are too long and should be divided
Spelling errors:  
  “govermnent,” “prevent,” “how are financially unable” (should be “who are”)
Awkward phrasing:  
  “case and point” → “case in point”

Final advice
Your essay has passion, a clear sense of personal conviction, and a willingness to discuss difficult cases. For revision, focus on:
writing a precise thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one main idea,
using formal, neutral language,
supporting claims with evidence,
checking grammar and sentence structure carefully.

If you revise in this way, your essay will become much more persuasive and academically effective.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0009.2.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear opinion and a strong sense of purpose. Your essay argues consistently that sex is overused in the media and that this can influence teenagers’ attitudes and behavior. This gives your paper a clear direction, which is a good foundation for revision.

Strengths
Clear position: Your main argument is easy to identify from the beginning.
Relevant examples: You use examples from television, radio, and magazines, which helps show that the issue is widespread.
Strong concern for consequences: The connection you make between media messages and teenage behavior gives the essay importance and seriousness.

Main Areas to Improve

Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your main idea is repeated many times (“sex is overrated in the media”), but the argument would be stronger if you stated exactly why this is a problem.

For example, your thesis could be revised to something like:

“Sex is overused in television, music, and advertising, and this constant exposure can give teenagers unrealistic and irresponsible ideas about relationships and safe sex.”

This version is more specific and prepares the reader for the points in your body paragraphs.

Improve organization
Your essay has several good ideas, but they are sometimes repeated instead of being developed. Try organizing it like this:

Introduction: Present the issue and your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: Sex in television.
Body Paragraph 2: Sex in music/radio.
Body Paragraph 3: Sex in magazines/advertising.
Body Paragraph 4: Effects on teenagers and why this matters.
Conclusion: Summarize the argument and suggest what media should do differently.

This structure will make the essay easier to follow.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some expressions sound conversational, such as:
“Now radio is almost as bad as television.”
“Now if I’m not mistaken…”
“That’s not reality.”

In academic writing, try to use more neutral language. For example:
“Radio also contributes to this problem.”
“This image does not relate clearly to the product being advertised.”
“Such portrayals do not reflect the realities of responsible sexual behavior.”

Also, avoid speaking directly to the reader too often with “you.” Use “viewers,” “audiences,” or “teenagers” instead.

Support claims more carefully
Your argument would be more persuasive if you explained your examples in greater depth. For instance, instead of only listing TV shows or song titles, explain:
what message they send,
how often these messages appear,
why they may affect teenagers.

Also, some claims are very broad, such as “everyone sleeps with everyone.” In academic argument, it is better to avoid exaggeration unless you can prove it. You could say:
“Many popular programs portray casual sexual relationships as normal and consequence-free.”

Work on sentence clarity and grammar
There are frequent grammar, punctuation, and word-choice problems. Here are some important examples:

“Sex Is used to do everything.”  
  → “Sex is used to sell almost everything.”

“R. Kellye”  
  → likely “R. Kelly”

“bluntly saids”  
  → “bluntly says”

“soak and wet”  
  → “soaked and wet”

“your selling”  
  → “you’re selling”

“Its funny”  
  → “It’s funny”

“taking precautions measures”  
  → “taking precautionary measures” or “taking precautions”

You also often place commas where they are not needed:
“I have ever read, has ads…”  
  Remove the comma: “I have ever read has ads…”

Strengthen paragraph development
Some paragraphs begin well but do not fully develop one main point. For example, your magazine paragraph has interesting examples, but it could connect more clearly to your overall argument. After each example, add 1–2 sentences explaining why it matters.

For instance:
“These advertisements rely on sexual imagery rather than on the quality of the product itself. As a result, audiences may begin to associate attractiveness and desirability with consumer goods, reinforcing the idea that sexuality is the best way to gain attention.”

Revise the conclusion
Your final sentence restates your point, but the conclusion could be stronger if it briefly summarizes your main reasons and ends with a thoughtful final comment.

For example:
“Overall, the media’s overuse of sexual content in entertainment and advertising promotes unrealistic ideas about intimacy while rarely showing responsibility or consequences. A more balanced portrayal of relationships would better serve young audiences.”

Final Advice
When revising, focus on:
writing a more specific thesis,
organizing each paragraph around one main point,
reducing repetition,
using more formal language,
correcting grammar and punctuation.

Your essay has a strong opinion and many usable ideas. With clearer organization and more formal, accurate language, it can become a much more convincing argumentative essay.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0009.3.txt =====
Feedback Report

Your essay addresses an interesting topic—the psychological and social influence of clothing—and you use research to support your ideas. You clearly understand that fashion is connected to identity, peer pressure, modesty, and self-image. The essay has strong potential, but it needs revision to improve its focus, organization, style, and grammar.

Main strength: strong topic and use of sources
A major strength of your essay is that you bring in many different perspectives: adolescents, older adults, consumer behavior, and modesty. This shows wide reading. You also include citations, which helps give your essay academic credibility.

However, at the moment the paper reads more like a collection of notes from sources than a fully developed argument. Your revision should aim to make your own central claim clearer.

Clarify your thesis
Your introduction suggests that clothing influences how people judge others and how people see themselves. That is a strong argument, but it is too broad in the current version.

You could revise your thesis into something more focused, for example:

“Clothing influences both social judgment and self-perception, and its effects are especially strong during adolescence, consumer decision-making, and ideas about modesty.”

A clearer thesis will help the reader understand the purpose of each paragraph.

Improve organization
Right now, the essay moves from judgments, to designers, to poetry, to self-concept, to adolescents, to consumers, to the elderly, and then to modesty. These sections are related, but the connections are not always clear.

A better structure might be:

Introduction – explain the topic and present the thesis  
Clothing and first impressions  
Clothing and self-identity  
Peer pressure and adolescence  
Consumer behavior and fashion trends  
Modesty and cultural values  
Conclusion

Also, each paragraph should begin with a topic sentence that connects directly to the thesis.

Use a more academic tone
Some word choices weaken the essay because they sound informal, judgmental, or too absolute. For example:

“slut” and “pimp” are too harsh and inappropriate for formal academic writing.
“If she wears a mini-skirt, she probably feel sexy” is too simplistic and assumes too much.

You could replace these with more neutral language, such as:

“People may form positive or negative judgments based on clothing.”
“Certain styles may be associated with confidence, professionalism, or sexuality.”

Also avoid generalizations like “one can normally gather any of these characteristics from the way a person dresses.” Clothing may influence perception, but it does not always reveal true personality.

Develop analysis, not just summary
Many paragraphs quote or paraphrase sources but do not explain enough why the evidence matters. After presenting a source, add 1–2 sentences of analysis.

For example, after discussing adolescent peer pressure, you could explain:
why teenagers are especially vulnerable to clothing-based judgment,
how dress affects belonging and self-esteem,
whether this supports your overall argument.

Try to make sure every source answers this question: How does this evidence support my thesis?

Grammar and sentence-level issues
Your essay contains frequent grammar errors. The most common are:

a. Subject-verb agreement
“that person feel” → “that person feels”
“she probably feel” → “she probably feels”
“one doesn’t know what style he/she wants” is grammatically possible, but awkward

b. Capitalization
“one doesn't know…” → “One doesn’t know…”
“older men and women…” → “Older men and women…”

c. Word form
“strong affect on adolescents” → “strong effect on adolescents”
“The needs for clothing” → “The needs for clothing” or better “Clothing needs”
“fell self-pity” → “feel self-pity”

d. Pronoun consistency
You switch between one, he, she, they, I. Choose one approach and stay consistent. In formal writing, plural forms are often easiest:
“People choose clothing styles that reflect how they want to be seen.”

e. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are too long or awkward. For example:
“Designers make outfits that infer the instabilities people have…”  
This is unclear. You may mean:
“Designers create clothing that appeals to people’s insecurities and desires for self-expression.”

Integrate quotations more smoothly
The poem and some long source material are inserted without enough explanation. Introduce quotations clearly and explain their relevance afterward. Also check formatting for poetry and citations.

For instance, before the poem, explain why it matters:
“Herrick’s poem suggests that slightly disordered dress can appear more attractive than perfect neatness.”

Strengthen the conclusion
Your conclusion repeats earlier ideas but becomes too broad and informal at the end. Instead of ending with “the excitement of walking down the street,” return to your argument. A stronger conclusion should restate the thesis and emphasize why the topic matters socially and psychologically.

Revision priorities
When revising, focus on these steps:

Write a clear, arguable thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs by theme.
Use more formal and precise language.
Add analysis after each source.
Correct grammar, pronouns, and sentence structure.
Revise the conclusion to match the academic tone of the essay.

Overall, you have a thoughtful topic and useful source material. With better organization and clearer academic writing, this essay can become much stronger.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-009.4.txt =====
Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you argue that the decision about abortion should be left to the woman. This is a strong thesis for an argumentative essay, and you do try to support it by addressing opposing arguments. That is a good foundation. For revision, focus on three main areas: organization, clarity of argument, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear stance: Your opinion is consistent throughout the essay.
Relevant topic sentences: Several paragraphs begin with a clear idea, such as discussing anti-abortion arguments or the final claim that the decision should belong to the woman.
Use of counterargument: You attempt to mention the opposing side and explain why you disagree. This is important in argumentative writing.

Main issues with argument and organization

A. Make your thesis more precise
Your current thesis appears in paragraph 2:  
“the decision to have an abortion or not should be left entirely up to the woman”  
This is clear, but you should briefly state why. For example:

because pregnancy directly affects her body,
because circumstances such as rape or health risks vary,
and because forcing birth can cause harm to both woman and child.

This will give your essay a stronger structure.

B. Improve paragraph development
Some paragraphs contain unclear or incomplete reasoning. For example, the paragraph beginning “Although if you analyze many of these arguments…” is difficult to follow because the example is not fully explained. You mention rape, but the paragraph does not clearly introduce the argument you are responding to.

A stronger structure for each body paragraph is:
state the opposing argument,
explain it fairly,
respond with your criticism,
connect back to your thesis.

C. Avoid overgeneralizations
Some claims are too absolute, which weakens your argument. For example:
“she is the only one who knows what consequences will be taken on”
“If a child is born unwanted, he is automatically psychologically damaged for life”

Words like only, automatically, and for life are very strong and may not be true in every case. In academic argument, it is better to qualify your claims:
*she is usually in the best position to understand her circumstances*
*an unwanted pregnancy may increase the risk of emotional and social difficulties for the child*

This makes your writing sound more reasonable and credible.

Use stronger evidence
At the moment, most of your points are based on opinion. Academic argumentative essays are stronger when they include:
facts,
statistics,
expert opinions,
legal or ethical reasoning.

For example, instead of saying self-induced abortions would increase, you could refer to research about unsafe abortions in places where abortion is restricted. Try to support at least 2–3 major claims with evidence.

Language and grammar issues

There are many grammar and sentence-structure problems that make some parts hard to understand. Below are common patterns to revise:

A. Sentence boundaries / comma splices
Example:
“These debates began over two decades ago, they have done nothing, but enlarge throughout the years.”

This joins two sentences incorrectly. You can revise:
*These debates began over two decades ago, and they have only grown over the years.*
or
*These debates began over two decades ago. They have only grown over the years.*

B. Word form and word choice
Examples:
“The two major different views” → *The two major views*
“obtains enough knowledge” → *has enough knowledge*
“proffers” → probably *pro-lifers*
“substantial” → *convincing* or *well supported*
“woman women” → typo; should be *women*

C. Articles, plurals, and possessives
Examples:
“this categories” → *these categories*
“its hard” → *it’s hard*
“anti-abortionist's” → *anti-abortionists’* if plural
“there tendencies” → *their tendencies*
“woman's” → *woman’s*

D. Spacing and punctuation
Examples:
“pro-choiceor” → *pro-choice or*
“true,there” → *true, there*
Remove extra spaces before punctuation.

Style suggestions
Try to use a more formal academic tone. For example:
“whole argument seem very unrealistic about the whole issue”  
  → *their argument seems unrealistic and fails to address the complexity of the issue*
“Who is to say what a woman should do with her own body?”  
  This rhetorical question is acceptable, but in formal writing, a direct statement is often stronger:
  → *No one else is in a better position than the woman herself to decide what happens to her body.*

Suggested revision plan
Rewrite the introduction with a clearer thesis and 2–3 reasons.
Reorganize body paragraphs so each one covers one clear point.
Explain opposing arguments more clearly and respond to them logically.
Replace extreme claims with more careful language.
Edit grammar, punctuation, and word choice sentence by sentence.

Example of a stronger thesis
*Although abortion is morally controversial, the decision should remain with the pregnant woman because pregnancy directly affects her body, individual circumstances such as rape or health risks cannot be judged equally by outsiders, and restricting abortion may lead to greater harm.*

You have a good central idea. With clearer organization, stronger support, and careful editing, this essay can become much more persuasive and academically effective.

===== ICLE-US-SCU-0010.2.txt =====
Feedback Report

You have a clear position and a strong sense of concern about children’s exposure to adult television content. Your essay also uses concrete examples and personal experience, which helps readers understand why the issue matters to you. For revision, focus on three main areas: thesis and organization, evidence and reasoning, and sentence-level accuracy.

Strengths
Clear topic: The essay clearly argues that children are exposed to too much sexual and adult content on television.
Strong voice: Your opinion is direct and consistent.
Relevant examples: You use specific TV programs and a personal story about your child, which makes the essay feel real and engaged.

Main revision priorities

A. Write a sharper thesis
Right now, your main point is repeated in different ways, but it needs to be stated more precisely in one sentence near the beginning.

For example, you could revise the thesis to something like:

“Television stations should schedule more child-appropriate programs after school and in the early evening because many children watch TV without supervision and are not emotionally mature enough to understand adult content.”

This thesis is stronger because it:
states your argument clearly,
identifies the cause of the problem,
suggests a solution.

B. Improve organization
The essay has many ideas, but they are sometimes repeated and not grouped clearly. Try using a structure like this:

Introduction
Introduce the issue
Present your thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Why children are exposed
Parents are busy
Many children watch TV alone
TVs in bedrooms increase access

Body Paragraph 2: Why adult content is harmful
Children imitate behavior
Children misunderstand sexuality
Children are not developmentally ready

Body Paragraph 3: Examples
Specific shows
Cartoon example (*The Simpsons*)
Personal anecdote about your daughter

Body Paragraph 4: Solution
More child-friendly programming after school
Better scheduling
More parental awareness

Conclusion
Restate your argument
Emphasize the importance of protecting children

This will help the essay sound more logical and persuasive.

C. Use stronger evidence
Some claims are too broad or unsupported. For example:
“Approximately ninety eight percent of television programs are not for children.”
“That only leaves a small two percent...”

These numbers are very specific, so readers will expect a source. If you do not have evidence, avoid exact statistics. You could write:

“A large proportion of daytime and evening television programming is aimed at adults rather than children.”

Also, some cause-and-effect claims are too certain. For example, saying that children who watch certain shows “will think it’s all right to have sex and get pregnant” is too absolute. Instead, use more careful language:

“Children may begin to misunderstand relationships or view risky behavior as normal.”

This sounds more academic and credible.

Tone and academic style
Your essay is passionate, but some parts sound too informal or emotional for academic argument. Try to:
avoid rhetorical questions such as “How is a parent suppose to explain that...?”
replace phrases like “big people shows” with more formal wording such as “adult-oriented programs”
avoid speaking in absolutes such as “every day,” “all the time,” or “all children”

Academic writing is usually stronger when it sounds measured and precise.

Grammar and language corrections
Because you are a non-native speaker, sentence-level editing will make a big difference. Watch for these issues:

A. Word choice and form
“make the children to want” → “make children want”
“for an example” → “for example”
“suppose to explain” → “supposed to explain”
“its up to” → “it is up to”

B. Articles and plurals
“a man and a women” → “a man and a woman”
“televisions stations” → “television stations”

C. Sentence clarity
Some sentences are very long and should be divided. For example:

Original:  
“With these television programs on every day the child starts to watch his or her favorite show and once interested then wants to experiment.”

Possible revision:  
“When children watch these programs every day, they may become attached to certain shows. Once they become interested, they may want to imitate what they see.”

D. Punctuation and spelling
Be careful with:
quotation marks
commas after introductory phrases
spelling: nighttime, woman, somewhere, every day
typing errors: “Opra” should be “Oprah”

Final advice
For your next draft, concentrate on:
writing one clear thesis,
organizing the essay into distinct paragraphs,
replacing unsupported statistics with careful claims,
using more formal academic language,
editing grammar and punctuation closely.

You have a strong topic and clear concern for children’s well-being. With better organization and more precise language, this essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay shows strong effort and a clear concern for an important topic. You include many concrete examples of recyclable materials, and your conclusion tries to persuade readers to act. This gives your paper energy and purpose. For revision, the main goals are to strengthen the argument, improve organization, and correct grammar/style issues so your ideas are clearer and more convincing.

Main strength
A major strength of your essay is that you provide specific examples: plastic bags, glass, refrigerators, batteries, motor oil, printer cartridges, and household toxics. This shows research and helps readers understand that recycling involves many different materials.

Main issue: argument and focus
Right now, the essay sometimes reads more like an informational list than a focused argumentative essay. You argue that recycling is important, but the central claim should be clearer and more consistent.

How to improve:
Create a stronger thesis in the introduction. For example:

Possible revised thesis:  
*Recycling should become a normal part of everyday life because it reduces landfill waste, saves energy and natural resources, and prevents harmful pollution.*

This thesis gives you three clear body points:
Recycling reduces landfill use.
Recycling saves energy/resources.
Recycling protects human health and the environment.

Then organize the body around these points instead of discussing one material after another.

Organization
Your essay has many good details, but some paragraphs feel disconnected. For example, the discussion of plastic vs. paper bags is long, while other sections are very short. Also, some ideas appear suddenly, without transitions.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: environmental problem + thesis
Body Paragraph 1: landfill space
Body Paragraph 2: energy/resource savings
Body Paragraph 3: dangerous waste and health risks
Body Paragraph 4: practical solutions / what consumers can do
Conclusion: restate argument + call to action

Use topic sentences at the start of each paragraph. Example:

*One important reason to recycle is that it reduces pressure on already crowded landfills.*

Use of evidence
You include many quotations and facts, which is good, but several are dropped into the essay without explanation. After each quote, explain why it matters and how it supports your claim.

For example, after the statistic about glass bottles or landfill space, add a sentence like:
*This statistic shows that recycling has a measurable impact on reducing waste and conserving space.*

Also, some sources seem old or incomplete. In revision, make sure your sources are reliable, current, and cited consistently.

Tone and style
Your tone is persuasive, but sometimes it becomes too emotional or informal. Academic argument is stronger when it sounds confident and precise.

For example:
“please make the effort” → too conversational
“the world would not be in danger” → too broad

Try more formal wording:
*Consumers should make a greater effort to recycle paper products when reusable alternatives are not available.*

Grammar and sentence issues
There are frequent grammar, punctuation, and word-choice problems. Focus especially on these areas:

A. Subject-verb agreement
“the human population needs to educate themselves”  
  → needs to educate itself

B. Word choice
“maximize the room that is left in landfills for recyclable materials”  
  → better: reduce the amount of waste sent to landfills
“fight a one hundred watt fight bulb”  
  → light a one-hundred-watt light bulb
“fun”  
  → full

C. Sentence structure
Some sentences are awkward or unclear:
“There are many possible ways for this to be achieved, one of which being recycling.”  
  → There are many ways to address this problem, and recycling is one of the most effective.

D. Punctuation
Avoid semicolon misuse:
“This includes but is not limited to; plastic bags...”  
  → This includes, but is not limited to, plastic bags...

E. Articles and prepositions
“categorized in rechargeable and non-rechargeable”  
  → categorized as rechargeable and non-rechargeable

Clarity and accuracy
Some claims need clearer wording or more careful accuracy. For example:
“Plastic bags are much better for the environment than paper bags” is a very broad claim and may be debated.
Instead, write:
*Some sources argue that plastic bags may require fewer natural resources to produce than virgin paper bags; however, both materials create environmental problems if not reused or recycled.*

This makes your argument more balanced and credible.

Conclusion
Your conclusion has a strong call to action, but it should avoid exaggeration and focus on your main reasons. A stronger conclusion would briefly restate the three key benefits of recycling and end with a realistic appeal.

Final revision priorities
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize body paragraphs by main argument, not by item type alone.
Add topic sentences and transitions.
Explain how each quote supports your point.
Correct grammar and awkward sentences.
Use a more formal academic tone.

You have a solid foundation and many useful examples. With clearer structure and more polished language, this can become a much stronger argumentative essay.
