Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear topic and a consistent focus: you argue that colors, especially pink and blue, are used in society to reinforce gender stereotypes. You also use several everyday examples—commercials, baby clothing, and products—which helps make your point easy to understand. This is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay.

What is working well

Clear central idea  
   Your main claim is easy to identify: pink is socially linked to femininity and blue to masculinity.

Relevant examples  
   You use specific examples such as Skintimate, Secret, Huggies, and Gerber. These details help support your argument.

Logical overall pattern  
   The essay moves from general ideas about pink, to advertising, to babies, and then to boys and blue. This gives the paper a basic structure.

Main areas to revise

Make your thesis stronger
Right now, the essay begins with questions, but it does not clearly state a strong argumentative thesis. Try to say exactly what you are arguing in one sentence.

Possible revised thesis:  
*Advertising and consumer products reinforce the stereotype that pink belongs to girls and blue belongs to boys, which shapes how people identify gender from infancy.*

This is stronger because it is specific and arguable.

Improve organization
Some ideas repeat, especially the points about baby girls wearing pink and people assuming gender from color. Try organizing the essay into clear body paragraphs, each with one main point:

Paragraph 1: Introduction + thesis  
Paragraph 2: Advertising aimed at women uses pink and soft colors  
Paragraph 3: Baby girls and baby products are coded pink  
Paragraph 4: Baby boys and male identity are coded blue  
Paragraph 5: Conclusion about how these stereotypes affect society

This structure will make your argument more focused.

Develop analysis, not just description
Often you describe examples, but you should also explain why they matter. For example, after mentioning Huggies or shaving gel commercials, explain how these images teach viewers to connect products and colors with gender roles.

For instance:  
*These advertisements do more than sell products; they teach consumers to see softness, beauty, and femininity as naturally connected to women.*

That kind of sentence adds analysis and makes the essay more argumentative.

Use a more academic tone
Some phrases are too conversational, such as:
“you would not catch a baby boy with pink on”
“get away with it”

Try replacing them with more formal wording:
*Baby boys are rarely dressed in pink.*
*Girls are more socially permitted to wear traditionally masculine colors than boys are to wear traditionally feminine colors.*

Also, avoid directly addressing the reader with “you” unless necessary.

Correct grammar and sentence structure
There are several grammar, punctuation, and capitalization problems. Here are some important ones:

Question punctuation:  
  “When you see pink what do you think of?”  
  → *When you see pink, what do you think of?*

Capitalization:  
  “on the Secret deodorant commercial”  
  → *In the Secret deodorant commercial*

Sentence fragment:  
  “Why? because it is a very soft color...”  
  → *This is because pink is seen as a soft color...*

Agreement and pronouns:  
  “once a baby has developed its looks” sounds unnatural.  
  Better: *Once a baby’s sex is visually clear, parents may feel less pressure to use color to signal gender.*

Spelling/typing error:  
  “jam referring to babies”  
  → probably *I am referring to babies.*

Word choice:  
  “pictured as being soft and tender”  
  → *portrayed as soft and tender*

Strengthen the conclusion
Your last paragraph repeats earlier ideas but does not leave the reader with a strong final thought. A better conclusion should summarize the argument and explain its significance.

Possible direction:  
*In conclusion, the repeated use of pink for girls and blue for boys in advertising and baby products shows how early gender stereotypes are taught. These color codes may seem harmless, but they influence how society expects males and females to behave.*

Example of a revised sentence
Original:  
*Men do not usually wear pink because it is stereotypically considered a feminine color.*

Revised:  
*Many men avoid wearing pink because society has stereotypically labeled it as a feminine color.*

The revised version sounds more formal and precise.

Final advice
As you revise, focus on three goals:
Write a clear thesis.
Group similar ideas into organized paragraphs.
Add analysis explaining how your examples support your argument.

You already have a solid topic and useful examples. With clearer structure, more formal language, and careful proofreading, this essay can become much stronger.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you strongly support drug legalization and try to explain its social, economic, and public-health benefits. This is a good foundation for an argumentative essay because your stance is clear from the introduction, and you include several reasons to support it. You also show engagement with the topic and attempt to address large social consequences, which gives your essay ambition.

What is working well
Clear thesis and opinion  
   Your introduction makes your position easy to understand. The reader knows immediately what you believe.

Relevant main points  
   You identify several arguments in favor of legalization: lower prices, less crime, reduced prison overcrowding, economic benefits, and public health. These are appropriate points for this topic.

Paragraph organization  
   Most body paragraphs begin with a topic sentence such as “Another benefit…” This helps the reader follow your structure.

Main areas to revise

Complete and develop your ideas
Several places in the essay contain `(*)`, which means key parts of your reasoning are missing. These gaps make the argument hard to follow. Before editing language, complete every paragraph with:
a clear claim
an explanation of why it is true
a specific example, fact, or source
a sentence linking the point back to your thesis

For example, in the paragraph about lower drug prices, explain why legalization lowers prices and how that would reduce street dealing.

Improve logical support
Some claims are too broad or are not fully explained. For instance:
“drug legalization has many beneficial outcomes” → Which outcomes specifically?
“legalizing heroin, cocaine, and marijuana would produce a reduction in the use of tobacco and alcohol” → This is a strong claim and needs strong evidence.
“The drug users would be responsible for their own debts” → This idea is unclear. Do you mean taxpayers would no longer pay for enforcement and imprisonment?

Try to avoid unsupported generalizations. Each paragraph should answer: How do I know this?

Strengthen the thesis
Your thesis is currently understandable but informal and somewhat repetitive. A stronger version would clearly list your main reasons. For example:

Possible revision:  
*Drug legalization should be considered in the United States because it could reduce black-market crime, lower incarceration rates, save public money, and improve public health through regulation.*

This is more academic and previews your body paragraphs.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too conversational:
“personally I think”
“it’s time”
“big money”
“hooked up in the drug business”

In academic writing, choose more formal wording:
“This essay argues that…”
“Drug legalization would reduce incentives to enter the illegal drug trade.”

Also be careful with emotionally charged statements such as “come to reality.” A more neutral phrase would be “have recognized.”

Fix grammar and sentence structure
There are frequent problems with punctuation, spacing, agreement, and sentence clarity. Some common issues:

Spacing/apostrophes: “it' s” → “it’s”
Comma errors: “they have come to reality and decided that ,drug prohibition…”  
Article/preposition use: “effects drug laws are having on the society of America” → “effects drug laws are having on American society”
Word choice: “beneficiary to America” → “beneficial to America”
Incomplete sentence: “Similarly, when heroin, they become alcohol abusers.” This sentence is not grammatical and must be rewritten.

Read each sentence aloud and check whether it is complete and clear.

Paragraph-level advice

Introduction
Good topic and position, but revise for clarity and formality. Avoid repeating “work” too many times.

Body paragraphs
Right now, many paragraphs list benefits but do not fully explain them. Use this structure:
Topic sentence  
Explanation  
Evidence/example  
Analysis  
Concluding sentence

Public health paragraph
This is your most ambitious paragraph, but also the least clear. Some claims seem contradictory. For example, “If drugs were to increase by a factor of two or three…” probably does not express what you mean. Do you mean availability or price? Also, be careful when discussing substitution between drugs, alcohol, and tobacco; this requires reliable evidence.

Conclusion
The conclusion summarizes your position, which is good. However, avoid introducing unclear new ideas such as “use legal drugs to our advantage.” Be precise: regulation, taxation, treatment, and reduced incarceration are clearer benefits.

Suggested revision priorities
Fill in all missing sections `(*)`.
Rewrite the thesis in a clear academic style.
Add evidence and explanation to each paragraph.
Correct grammar and awkward sentences.
Make your tone more formal and objective.

Example of a stronger sentence
Original:  
*America needs to use that money on more important things such as helping the homeless or promoting education.*

Revised:  
*Instead of spending large sums on drug enforcement, the government could redirect resources toward education, addiction treatment, and social services.*

Overall, your essay has a strong opinion and a useful structure, but it needs more complete reasoning, stronger evidence, and careful editing to become persuasive.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position: you believe stronger gun control is needed in South Carolina because it can reduce crime, save lives, and ease public fear. That strong opinion gives your paper energy and purpose. You also use local examples and personal experience, which make the topic feel real and important. For revision, focus on improving organization, evidence, style, and grammar so your argument becomes more convincing.

Main strengths
Clear argumentative stance: Your thesis shows exactly what you believe.
Strong sense of urgency: The essay communicates concern about violence effectively.
Use of examples: School shootings, robberies, and personal/family experiences give emotional power.
Local focus: Referring to South Carolina and Orangeburg helps ground the issue in a specific context.

Thesis and argument
Your thesis is:

> “Gun Control needs to implemented because it would mainly reduce crime, save lives and lessen the fears of innocent ones.”

This is a good start, but it needs clearer wording and more formal phrasing. For example:

Possible revision:  
*Gun control should be strengthened in South Carolina because it can reduce violent crime, protect innocent lives, and improve public safety.*

Also, your essay sometimes assumes that every gun crime happened because gun control was weak, but you do not always explain how stronger laws would have prevented each event. In revision, make the connection more logical:
What specific law is missing?
How would that law have stopped the shooter or robber?
Would background checks, waiting periods, safe storage laws, or age restrictions help?

This will make your argument stronger and more analytical.

Organization
Right now, the essay reads more like a series of examples than a carefully structured argument. Try this structure:

Introduction
Define gun control briefly
Give context about violence
End with a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Gun access and youth violence
Use the school shooting example
Explain how stronger laws or safe storage rules might help

Body Paragraph 2: Guns and public crime
Use the bank robbery example
Explain how regulation might reduce armed robbery

Body Paragraph 3: Community and personal impact
Use the Glover case and your uncle’s case
Show emotional and social consequences

Body Paragraph 4: Counterargument
Address groups that oppose gun control
Explain why you disagree using evidence

Conclusion
Restate thesis
Summarize key reasons
End with a thoughtful final statement

Make sure each paragraph begins with a topic sentence and focuses on one main idea.

Evidence and sources
You include quotations and incidents, but some problems weaken your credibility:
Some sources are old or incomplete.
“Personal Recollection” and “Interview With Mom” are meaningful, but they are not strong academic evidence by themselves.
The statistic about “hate crimes” seems unrelated to your argument about general gun violence, robbery, and murder.

For revision:
Use more reliable sources such as government crime reports, public health studies, or major news organizations.
Make sure statistics directly support your claim.
Introduce and explain each quotation more smoothly.

For example, instead of dropping in a quote, write:
*According to* The Times and Democrat, *a 15-year-old student shot two teachers before killing himself. This case suggests that minors can gain access to firearms too easily, which supports the need for stricter controls.*

Style and tone
Your voice is passionate, which is good, but some wording is too informal or emotional for an academic essay. For example:
“people killing others over nonsense”
“create trouble”
“use their heads instead of the trigger”

Try using more formal language:
“violent disputes”
“cause harm”
“act responsibly”

Also, avoid overgeneralizations such as “anybody can just walk in with a gun.” Be precise and careful in your claims.

Grammar and language issues
You have many good ideas, but sentence-level errors sometimes make them harder to follow. Focus on:

Common issues
Spelling: *government, teachers, members, misuse, acquaintance, resources*
Capitalization: “gun control” should not usually be capitalized in the middle of a sentence.
Grammar: “needs to implemented” → “needs to be implemented”
Word choice: “lessen the fears of innocent ones” → “reduce the fear felt by innocent people”
Sentence clarity: some sentences are long and awkward

Example corrections
Original: *Gun control is goverment limitation of the purchase and ownership of firearms.*  
Better: *Gun control refers to government regulation of the purchase and ownership of firearms.*

Original: *This crime was an act of nonsense.*  
Better: *This crime was a senseless act of violence.*

Final advice
Your essay has a strong foundation because you care deeply about the topic and you already have a clear claim. To improve it, revise for:
clearer thesis
better paragraph structure
stronger and more relevant evidence
more formal academic style
careful grammar and proofreading

With revision, your essay can become much more persuasive and effective.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay has a clear position, and that is a strong starting point. You argue consistently against capital punishment and organize the essay around three main counterarguments: cost, retribution, and deterrence. This structure is effective for an argumentative essay. You also show confidence and engagement with the topic. For revision, focus on making your tone more academic, improving accuracy and grammar, and developing evidence more carefully.

Main strengths

Clear thesis and position: Your introduction makes your opinion obvious.
Logical organization: Each body paragraph addresses one argument from supporters of capital punishment.
Relevant argumentative strategy: Refuting opposing points is a good method for persuasion.
Strong conclusion: You restate your main points clearly.

Most important areas to revise

A. Use a more academic tone
At times, the essay sounds emotional or conversational rather than formal. In academic writing, avoid attacking opposing views with insulting language.

Examples:
“obscene form of punishment”
“these reasons are as ridiculous”
“Let me throw a couple of statistics at you”
“Correct me if I’m wrong”

These phrases weaken your credibility. Replace them with neutral, precise wording.

For example:
Instead of “these reasons are as ridiculous”, write “these arguments are unconvincing when examined closely.”
Instead of “Let me throw a couple of statistics at you”, write “Several statistics illustrate this point.”

B. Strengthen your thesis
Your introduction states your opinion strongly, but the thesis could be clearer and more concise. Try to directly name your three main reasons.

Possible revision:
“Capital punishment should be abolished because it is more expensive than life imprisonment, it promotes revenge rather than justice, and it does not effectively deter violent crime.”

This gives the reader a clear roadmap.

C. Improve evidence and explanation
You mention “statistics” and “studies,” but you do not identify their sources. In academic writing, evidence is stronger when you name where it comes from.

For example:
Who conducted the study on costs?
Which states were studied for murder rates?
When were these studies done?

Also, explain the evidence more carefully. Some claims are too general, such as “It has also been proven...” In academic writing, avoid absolute statements unless you can fully support them.

Better wording:
“Research suggests that the death penalty does not significantly reduce murder rates.”

D. Avoid logical overstatement
Some sentences are too extreme:
“justice and capital punishment can’t coexist”
“With no reasons left to rationalize capital punishment...”

These claims sound absolute and may seem unsupported. A more careful style is more persuasive:
“These problems suggest that capital punishment is difficult to justify within a justice system that values fairness and human life.”

Language and grammar issues

There are many spelling and word-choice errors. These do not hide your meaning, but they reduce the essay’s professionalism. Here are some important corrections:

untill → until
it’s existence → its existence
Ounce society → Once society
inprison → imprison
recieves → receives
midevil → medieval
deterent → deterrent
murdeers / murdeer → murders / murder
prols and con’s → pros and cons
deference → deterrence
detramental → detrimental

Also check sentence structure. Some sentences are long and unclear. Shorter sentences will improve readability.

Example:
Original:
“How this statement can be valid is due to the reasons that when someone recieves the death penalty they are not killed the next day.”

Improved:
“This is because a person sentenced to death is usually not executed immediately.”

Paragraph-level advice

Introduction
Reduce emotional language.
End with a concise thesis naming your three main points.

Body paragraph 1: Cost
Good topic: cost is relevant.
Add a source for the claim that execution costs more than life imprisonment.
Replace informal phrases with formal ones.

Body paragraph 2: Retribution
This paragraph raises an important moral issue.
However, some sentences are unclear or too conversational.
Focus on the distinction between justice and revenge in a calm, reasoned way.

Body paragraph 3: Deterrence
The main idea is good.
You need clearer evidence and more precise language.
Be careful with informal examples like “drawing up pros and cons.” Use more formal phrasing.

Conclusion
The conclusion summarizes effectively.
Avoid saying the issue is completely settled; instead, reinforce why your argument is convincing.

Suggested revision priorities

Correct spelling, grammar, and word forms.
Replace emotional or insulting language with academic language.
Rewrite the thesis to clearly list your three arguments.
Add sources or more specific references for statistics and studies.
Shorten and clarify long sentences.

Final comment

You have a solid argumentative structure and a clear sense of purpose. Your biggest challenge is not your ideas, but how you present them. If you revise for tone, accuracy, and clarity, this essay will become much more persuasive and academic.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay addresses an important and difficult topic, and you show clear interest in the ethical and legal debate around euthanasia. You include definitions, different types of euthanasia, and references to public cases such as Dr. Kevorkian. This gives your essay a serious academic direction. To improve the paper, focus mainly on clarity of argument, organization, source use, and sentence accuracy.

Main argument and thesis

Your essay raises the central question well: should physician-assisted suicide be legalized? However, your own position is not fully clear until the very end, and even there it is somewhat mixed. For example, you say doctors should not decide a patient’s fate, but also that they may “cooperate with a patient’s decision.” This sounds undecided.

How to revise:
State your position clearly in the introduction.
Make sure each body paragraph supports that position.
End with a conclusion that clearly restates your argument.

Example of a stronger thesis:
> Although terminally ill patients deserve compassion and pain relief, physician-assisted suicide should not be legalized because it conflicts with medical ethics, gives doctors too much moral authority, and creates serious legal and social risks.

Or, if you support legalization:
> Physician-assisted suicide should be legalized for terminally ill patients because it respects patient autonomy, reduces suffering, and allows a dignified death under strict medical regulation.

Choose one side and organize the essay around it.

Organization and paragraph structure

Your essay has useful material, but the structure is uneven. Some paragraphs define euthanasia, others discuss social forces, and others focus on Kevorkian or the Hippocratic Oath. These sections do not always connect smoothly.

Suggested structure:
Introduction: background + clear thesis  
Definition and distinctions: explain euthanasia and physician-assisted suicide carefully  
Arguments for or against legalization  
Ethical issue: Hippocratic Oath / role of doctors  
Legal and social examples: Kevorkian case  
Conclusion

Also, some paragraphs are too long and include multiple ideas. Try to use one main idea per paragraph.

Use of evidence and sources

You refer to William F. May and Joseph Fletcher, which is good, but the essay contains several missing quotations marked as “.” These gaps seriously weaken your paper because the reader cannot evaluate the evidence.

What to do:
Fill in all missing quotations or paraphrase the ideas instead.
Introduce sources clearly.
Explain how each source supports your argument.

For example:
> William F. May argues that religious belief, legal tradition, medical progress, and the authority of the medical profession all work against acceptance of the “right to die.” This helps explain why euthanasia remains controversial today.

Do not just insert a quotation; always analyze it.

Accuracy and style

Some word choices are unclear or too informal for academic writing.

Examples:
“easy way out” sounds judgmental and informal.
“vegetable” is offensive and inappropriate in academic writing. Use “persistent vegetative state” or “permanently unconscious state.”
“right to death by choice” is awkward; say “the right to choose death” or “the right to die.”

Also be careful with factual distinctions:
Euthanasia and physician-assisted suicide are not always the same thing.
Refusing treatment is usually considered legally and ethically different from active euthanasia.

You should define these terms more precisely.

Grammar and sentence-level issues

There are frequent grammar problems with possessives, subject-verb agreement, articles, and word forms.

Corrections from your essay:
“Those patient’s” → Those patients
“Christains” → Christians
“modern societies outlook” → modern society’s outlook
“end their own life” → end their own lives
“break throughs” → breakthroughs
“leave in their will expressed wishes” → express their wishes in their wills
“should it be legalized” → should it be legal
“than to continue” → than continue

Some sentences are also wordy or repetitive. Try to simplify them.

Example:
Original:
> When the pain becomes unbearable and the medication stops working some people would rather die than to continue to live in pain.

Revised:
> When pain becomes unbearable and medication no longer works, some patients would rather die than continue living in suffering.

Tone and consistency

Try to keep a neutral academic tone. At times, the essay sounds emotional or judgmental. Academic argument is stronger when it is precise and balanced.

Also, be consistent with pronouns. You shift between he, one, they, and the patient. Choose one clear form, such as the patient or patients.

Conclusion

Your final paragraph introduces a strong issue—the moral limits of physicians—but it feels abrupt because there is no full conclusion. Add a final paragraph that:
restates your thesis,
summarizes your main reasons,
ends with a thoughtful final comment.

Final advice

Your essay has a solid topic and some strong content, especially your attention to medical ethics and legal controversy. For revision, focus on:
making your thesis clear,
improving paragraph organization,
filling in missing source material,
using precise academic language,
correcting grammar and style.

With these changes, your argument will become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear position and a strong overall purpose: you argue that the wild card is good for baseball because it increases playoff participation, creates excitement, and improves competition. The paper shows good knowledge of baseball and uses specific examples from the 1995 season, which makes the argument more concrete and persuasive. To revise effectively, focus on three main areas: organization, clarity/style, and sentence-level correctness.

Strengths

Clear thesis: In the introduction, you state your main claim and preview your three reasons.
Relevant support: You use examples such as the Yankees, Mariners, Rockies, and Angels to show excitement and competitiveness.
Good use of evidence: The essay includes facts, quotations, and playoff scenarios, which show research and help support your argument.
Strong conclusion: The final paragraph effectively restates the importance of the wild card.

Main revision priorities

A. Improve organization and focus
Right now, the essay spends too much time explaining the playoff structure and rules before fully developing the argument. Some background is necessary, but several paragraphs give detailed procedural information that distracts from your main claim.

What to do:
Keep the background brief: explain the old system and the new system in 1 short paragraph.
Move more quickly to your argument.
Use one body paragraph for each main reason:
  1. more teams/stars/cities involved
  2. more excitement at the end of the season
  3. more competition in the playoffs

This will make your essay easier to follow.

B. Strengthen paragraph unity
Some paragraphs mix different purposes. For example, the discussion of home-field advantage is detailed, but it does not directly support your thesis very much. It interrupts the flow.

Suggestion: Cut or greatly shorten the home-field advantage section unless you clearly explain why it matters to your argument.

C. Make the argument more analytical
Often, you list facts, names, or playoff situations, but you do not always explain enough why they prove your point.

For example, after naming many star players, add analysis such as:
why star players attract viewers
how broader fan interest helps baseball recover after the strike
why more competitive rounds improve the legitimacy of the championship

Try to balance evidence and explanation.

Style and clarity

A. Avoid very long sentences
Several sentences are difficult to read because they contain too many ideas. Break them into shorter sentences.

Example:
> “The wild card, this fourth team, is decided by taking the team from each league with the best record the three Division winners.”

This is grammatically incorrect and unclear. A clearer version:
> “The wild card is awarded to the non-division winner in each league with the best record.”

B. Keep tone academic
Some phrases sound informal or opinionated in a conversational way.

Example:
> “Baseball, unfortunately, feels the need to be different…”

This weakens the academic tone. Instead, write:
> “Unlike many other sports, baseball did not award home-field advantage based strictly on regular-season record.”

C. Integrate quotations more smoothly
You include quotations, but sometimes they appear suddenly or are too long. Introduce them clearly and explain their significance afterward.

Grammar and language issues to correct

Here are some recurring problems:

A. Missing words / wrong word forms
“with the best record of the teams other than the three Division winners”
“National Leagued Championship Series” → “National League Championship Series”
“in the second round”
“The only two wild car spots” → “wild card spots”

B. Sentence structure and punctuation
Avoid comma splices and run-on sentences.
Use commas after introductory phrases.
Remove unnecessary capitalization: “division winners” does not always need capitals.

C. Plurals and articles
“More teams in the playoffs let people watch more of their favorite players”
“the strike of the previous season” is okay, but “because of the previous season’s strike” is more natural.

D. Word choice
“even up the playoff matches” → “balance the playoff bracket”
“big name stars” → “well-known players”
“post season” → “postseason”

Content concerns

The long playoff-scenario section is interesting, but it is too detailed. Summarize it instead of listing every possibility.
Be careful with the final sentence:
  > “after losing popularity to football and baseball”
  
This should be:
> “after losing popularity to football and basketball.”

Suggested revision plan

Rewrite the introduction so the thesis is concise and grammatically correct.
Condense background information about playoff format.
Delete or shorten the home-field advantage discussion.
Create 3 clear body paragraphs based on your three reasons.
After every example, explain its significance.
Edit for grammar and sentence clarity.

Final comment

This essay has a solid argumentative foundation and strong subject knowledge. Your main task in revision is not to find more evidence, but to organize the material more effectively and express it more clearly. If you reduce unnecessary detail and focus each paragraph on one clear point, your argument will become much stronger and more persuasive.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and passionate position: you argue that the media has abused the public’s “right to know” and has prioritized profit over responsibility. This gives your paper strong energy and a recognizable voice. You also use several concrete examples, which is a good strategy in argumentative writing. To revise effectively, focus on organization, clarity of argument, evidence, and tone.

Strengths
Clear opinion: The reader always knows what you believe.
Engaged voice: Your concern about privacy, fairness, and public responsibility is sincere and persuasive at moments.
Use of examples: Grenada, Desert Storm, the O.J. Simpson trial, and public figures give you material to build an argument.

Main issue: your thesis needs to be sharper
Right now, the essay discusses several related complaints: declining newspaper quality, war reporting, invasions of privacy, sensationalism, and political smear campaigns. These ideas connect, but the central claim is not stated clearly enough in one sentence.

Try writing a thesis such as:

“Although the media has a duty to inform the public, it often abuses that power by invading privacy, sensationalizing events, and placing profit above ethical responsibility.”

This kind of thesis helps unify the whole essay.

Improve organization
At the moment, the essay feels like a series of reactions rather than a carefully structured argument. Consider organizing it into clear body paragraphs, each with one main point.

A possible structure:
Introduction – background + thesis  
Media and national security – war reporting example  
Media and character attacks – Surgeon General candidate / university professor  
Media sensationalism for profit – O.J. Simpson case  
Privacy and public figures – Colin Powell and broader privacy concerns  
Conclusion – restate argument and call for responsible journalism

Begin each body paragraph with a topic sentence. Example:

“One way the media abuses its power is by reporting information that can endanger lives during wartime.”

Use evidence more carefully
You have many examples, but some need more explanation or support. In academic argument, readers need more than personal reaction; they need evidence and analysis.

For instance:
In the Grenada paragraph, explain specifically how reporting harmed military operations, and if possible cite a source.
In the Surgeon General example, identify the candidate and explain why the media coverage was unfair.
In the O.J. Simpson section, separate what the media did from what the legal system did.

Be careful with claims that are too broad, such as:
“We don’t see any good news anymore.”
“The media’s freedom of speech will be the downfall of personal liberty.”

These statements sound powerful, but without evidence they may seem exaggerated. A stronger argument is often a more precise one.

Tone: reduce emotional and absolute language
Your anger is understandable, but some phrases may weaken your credibility in academic writing. For example:
“How dare they…”
“circus”
“crucifixion”
“smear him”
“good news makes no money”

You can still be critical, but use more measured language. For example:

Instead of “The media took a perfectly good candidate and dug up as much dirt on him as possible,” try:  
“The media focused heavily on damaging aspects of the candidate’s past rather than evaluating his professional qualifications.”

A more balanced tone makes your argument sound more reasoned and persuasive.

Address the other side
A strong argumentative essay usually acknowledges a counterargument. For example, you might admit that:
the public does need information about political candidates,
the media can expose corruption,
free press is essential in a democracy.

Then explain that freedom of the press should be balanced by ethics and restraint. This will make your essay more sophisticated.

Sentence-level revision
There are many grammar, spelling, and punctuation errors. These do not hide your meaning completely, but they make the essay harder to read. Please proofread carefully. Some common problems:

Word choice/spelling: “successfiil” → “successful”; “fives” → “lives”; “inunune” → “immune”; “hwnburgers” → “hamburgers”
Quotation marks: be consistent with “right to know”
Comma use: avoid comma splices and unnecessary commas
Pronouns: be careful with “them,” “they,” and unclear references
Possessives: “media’s business,” not “medi&s business”
Formal style: avoid “I guess,” “Watch out,” and conversational expressions unless used very carefully

Example revision:

Original:  
“There seemed to be very little about justice in this circus, and a lot about making money.”

More academic:  
“The coverage appeared to emphasize entertainment and profit more than the pursuit of justice.”

Final advice
For revision, focus on these priorities:
Write a clear thesis
Group ideas into organized paragraphs
Support each claim with specific evidence and explanation
Use a more formal, balanced tone
Proofread for grammar and spelling

You have the basis for a strong argumentative essay because your position is clear and your concern is real. The next step is to turn that strong opinion into a more structured, evidence-based academic argument.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have a clear position: you believe marijuana should be legalized. Your essay also has a strong personal voice, which makes it engaging. The reader can see your perspective and your experiences. For revision, the main goal is to make your argument more academic, better organized, and more convincing with evidence.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: From the beginning, the reader knows your position.
Personal engagement: Your personal experiences give the essay energy and authenticity.
Relevant topic: Legalization is a serious social and political issue, so it works well for an argumentative essay.

Main areas to improve

A. Use a stronger thesis
Right now, your introduction begins with questions and personal opinion, but it does not clearly present a focused academic thesis.

For example, instead of:
> “What is marijuana? It is a plant, and I think it should be legalized.”

You could write:
> “Marijuana should be legalized because it has medical uses, criminalization has failed to stop its use, and legalization would allow better government regulation.”

This gives your essay a roadmap.

B. Improve organization
Your essay moves from childhood memories to politics to medical uses to celebrities. These ideas are interesting, but they need a clearer structure.

A possible structure:
Introduction – present the issue and thesis
Body paragraph 1 – marijuana as a plant / medical uses
Body paragraph 2 – problems with current laws and enforcement
Body paragraph 3 – social attitudes and why legalization may be more effective
Counterargument – acknowledge risks, then respond
Conclusion – restate thesis and final thought

Each paragraph should focus on one main idea.

C. Use evidence, not only personal observation
A major weakness is that many claims are based on anecdote or opinion. In academic writing, you need facts, statistics, studies, or reliable sources.

Examples of claims that need evidence:
marijuana is “highly non habit forming”
there are “nearly no long lasting side effects”
government officials keep it illegal for money
people can study better after using it

These are strong claims, but without evidence they sound unsupported. Be careful especially with conspiracy-style arguments about the government, because readers may reject them unless you provide very solid proof.

D. Make the tone more formal
Some phrases are too conversational or informal for an academic essay:
“weed”
“talking out of his head”
“mega bucks”
“little man”
“guys who keep weed illegal”

In revision, use more formal vocabulary:
marijuana instead of *weed*
law enforcement
government officials
low-level dealers
profits or financial gain

You can still keep your voice, but make it sound more academic.

Language and grammar corrections

Your writing has good ideas, but grammar errors sometimes make the meaning unclear. Focus on these issues:

A. Sentence boundaries and punctuation
Some sentences are too long or joined incorrectly. Example:
> “Although, that is the most political stance we all know that most politicians who set these laws up use drugs themselves.”

This could be revised as:
> “Although this may be the most politically acceptable stance, many people believe that some politicians who support these laws have used drugs themselves.”

B. Word choice and form
their → there  
  “their are nearly no long lasting side effects” → “there are nearly no long-lasting side effects”
it’s vs its  
  “used in it’s leafy form” → “used in its leafy form”
critisize → criticize
ession should be session
Columbian is usually Colombian
european should be European

C. Capitalization
Capitalize proper nouns and the beginning of quotations correctly:
Bob Marley
President of the U.S.
European
“Just Say No”

D. Clarity
Some sentences need clearer wording. Example:
> “The American government officials who see and don't see make mega bucks...”

This is unclear. If you mean corruption, say exactly what you mean and support it with evidence.

Argument quality
To make the essay stronger, avoid weak support such as:
celebrities who used marijuana
friends who seem relaxed after using it
classmates who studied after smoking

These examples do not prove legalization is a good policy. Stronger support would include:
medical research on pain relief or glaucoma
statistics on arrests and incarceration
economic data from places where marijuana has been legalized
comparisons with alcohol or tobacco regulation

Also, consider a counterargument:
Some people oppose legalization because they worry about addiction, driving impairment, or youth access. If you mention these concerns and respond to them reasonably, your essay will seem more balanced and mature.

Conclusion advice
Your ending uses a quote, which is memorable, but it would be stronger if you first summarized your main reasons. For example:
> “In conclusion, marijuana should be legalized because it has recognized medical benefits, prohibition has not stopped its use, and regulation would be more effective than criminalization.”

Then you may add the Bob Marley quote if you want.

Final advice
Your essay has passion and a clear point of view. Now work on turning it from a personal opinion piece into a persuasive academic argument. Focus on:
a clear thesis
organized paragraphs
reliable evidence
formal language
grammar and proofreading

You have a strong foundation. With revision, your essay can become much more convincing.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

Your essay shows a clear position: you strongly support shield laws and believe reporters should protect confidential sources. You also use several examples and sources, which is a real strength. The essay has good energy and a strong sense of purpose. For revision, focus on making your argument more precise, better organized, and more formal.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: Your position is consistent throughout the essay.
Use of evidence: You include legal history, state examples, and famous cases such as Watergate.
Good awareness of the issue: You understand that this topic involves journalism, law, ethics, and public trust.

Improve the thesis
Right now, your main claim is spread across the introduction instead of being stated clearly in one sentence. A stronger thesis will guide the whole essay.

For example, you could write:

“Journalists should be legally protected from revealing confidential sources because source confidentiality supports press freedom, encourages whistleblowers to speak, and helps the public receive important information.”

This is stronger because it clearly gives your position and the main reasons.

Organization and structure
Your essay contains many examples, but the order sometimes feels like a list rather than a fully developed argument. Try this structure:

Introduction
Introduce the issue
Explain why source confidentiality matters
End with a clear thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Trust between reporter and source
Explain why anonymity is necessary
Show how fear of exposure reduces truthful reporting

Body Paragraph 2: Legal protection and shield laws
Define shield laws
Briefly mention Maryland as the first example
Explain why state protection matters

Body Paragraph 3: Limits and problems
Discuss South Carolina and Twila Decker
Show that shield laws are often weak or inconsistent

Body Paragraph 4: Additional examples
Use Ohio, California, Watergate, or Charles Stuart
Explain how each example supports your claim

Conclusion
Restate the thesis in new words
Emphasize why society benefits from source protection

Develop analysis, not just summary
Often, you present a case but do not fully explain how it proves your argument. After each example, add 2–3 sentences of analysis.

For instance, after the Twila Decker case, explain:
why this case shows the limits of state shield laws
how legal pressure can discourage investigative journalism
why this harms the public interest

Always ask: “What does this example prove?”

Tone and academic style
Some sentences sound emotional or informal, such as:
“If the justice system were better…”
“Don’t make people scared to talk.”
“Journalists do what they do for the people.”

These ideas are understandable, but in academic writing, use a more formal tone. For example:

Instead of “Don’t make people scared to talk,” write:
“Without strong legal protections, potential sources may be unwilling to share important information.”

Also, avoid overgeneralizations such as:
“A journalist’s job is to get the story no matter what.”

This is too absolute and may sound unethical. A better version:
“A journalist’s responsibility is to gather important information while maintaining ethical obligations to sources and the public.”

Grammar and style issues
There are several language problems to correct:

a. Comma and spacing errors
Example:
“especially in a courtroom setting has always been a problem”
Better:
“especially in a courtroom setting, has always been a problem”

But even better stylistically:
“Keeping sources secret in courtroom settings has long been a problem for journalists.”

b. Repetition
You repeat phrases like “reveal his/her sources” many times. Try varying:
disclose confidential sources
identify sources
reveal the source’s identity

c. Word choice
“is varied greatly” → “varies greatly”
“can or cannot be beneficial” → “may or may not protect reporters”
“bring on feelings of guilt” → “create guilt” or “damage professional integrity”

d. Quotation formatting
Some quotations have punctuation and typing problems:
double commas
missing quotation marks
strange symbols
inconsistent capitalization

Check all quotations carefully and format them consistently.

Citation and source use
You cite sources such as Henry, Gordon, Montgomery, and Zeigler, which is good. However:
Make sure every quotation is accurately copied.
Use a consistent citation style.
Avoid very long block quotations unless necessary. For example, the full Maryland law is probably too long. You can summarize most of it and quote only a key phrase.

Most important revision priorities
Write a clear one-sentence thesis.
Reorganize the essay by reasons, not just by examples.
Add analysis after each example.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Edit grammar, punctuation, and quotation formatting.

Final comment
You have a strong foundation and a topic that clearly interests you. The essay already shows commitment and research. Your next step is to make the argument more focused, more analytical, and more polished in academic English. With revision, this could become a persuasive and well-structured argumentative essay.

Feedback Report

You take a clear position: women should be allowed to attend The Citadel. Your essay has a strong sense of purpose, and you use several relevant arguments, including equality, public funding, educational opportunity, and preparation for real life. These are good foundations for an argumentative essay.

What is working well

Clear opinion
   - Your position is easy to understand from the beginning.
   - You stay focused on the same argument throughout the essay.

Relevant points
   - You address common objections such as tradition, physical ability, and distraction.
   - You also make a strong legal and ethical point about public funding and equal access.

Personal voice
   - The sentence “as a woman I especially know that is not true” adds sincerity and shows engagement with the topic.

Main areas to revise

Strengthen the thesis
Your introduction gives background, but the main claim is not stated strongly enough. End the introduction with a direct thesis that lists your main reasons.

For example:
“Women should be allowed to attend The Citadel because excluding them is unfair, inconsistent with public education, and based on weak assumptions about women’s abilities.”

This will give the essay a clearer structure.

Improve organization
Right now, your ideas are good, but some paragraphs move too quickly from one point to another. Try this structure:

Introduction: background + thesis
Body paragraph 1: tradition is not a good reason
Body paragraph 2: women are capable / individuals should be judged individually
Body paragraph 3: public funding means equal access
Body paragraph 4: coeducation does not create harmful distraction
Conclusion: restate your argument and explain why it matters

Also, begin each body paragraph with a clear topic sentence.

Example:
“Tradition alone is not a valid reason to exclude women.”
“Arguments about women’s physical weakness unfairly generalize about all women.”
“Because The Citadel is publicly funded, it should provide equal opportunity.”

Develop your evidence
Many of your claims are reasonable, but they need more support. Try to add:
specific examples
facts or court/legal ideas
comparisons with other schools
explanation of why the evidence proves your point

For example, when you say many colleges are coeducational, explain how this shows that mixed-gender education works successfully.

Use a more formal academic tone
Some phrases are too informal for an argumentative essay.

Less formal:
“traditions are meant to be broken”
“and so on”

More academic:
“tradition alone should not justify exclusion”
“throughout history, many traditions have changed as society became more equal”

Also avoid overgeneralizations, such as “you would still have black slaves.” The point is understandable, but this example is too broad and abrupt. Use more careful wording.

Work on grammar and word choice
There are frequent grammar, spelling, and word form errors. These make your argument less persuasive. Below are some important corrections:

appearred → appeared
That questions is should women be allowed → That question is whether women should be allowed
There has been many objections → There have been many objections
alao → also
as a women → as a woman
publically funded → publicly funded
desciniinate → discriminate
pubfically → publicly
poliish → Polish
shouln't → shouldn’t
provides excellence discipline → provides excellent discipline
eqaul → equal
Every deserves → Everyone deserves
there may a few cases → there may be a few cases
learening → learning
work place → workplace
less superior → This is incorrect. Use inferior or say “that women are less capable or less important.”

Fix sentence structure and punctuation
Some sentences are run-on sentences or need clearer punctuation.

For example:
“That may be true in some cases, but others might be able to handle the challenges, Everyone does deserve the chance to try.”

Final advice

writing a stronger thesis,

correcting grammar and spelling,

This gives your essay good argumentative potential.

What is working well

Strong sense of purpose
Your tone shows that you care about the issue. This helps the essay feel engaged and persuasive.

Variety of support
You use examples from a TV program, your own family, and social attitudes. These examples show effort to develop the topic from different angles.

Main areas to revise

Improve organization
Right now, the essay is long and repetitive. Several paragraphs make similar points: that housework has value, that the husband should respect the wife, and that outsiders should not judge. Try reorganizing around 3–4 main reasons.

For example:

Body Paragraph 1: household labor is valuable work
Body Paragraph 2: marriage is a partnership based on mutual agreement
Body Paragraph 3: legal and financial equality should reflect that partnership
Body Paragraph 4: social stereotypes about stay-at-home wives are unfair
Conclusion: restate argument and significance

Each paragraph should begin with a clear topic sentence and focus on one main idea.

Use stronger, more formal evidence
Some examples are weak or incomplete. The TV-program example includes missing quotations (“”), so it cannot fully support your point. Also, phrases such as “watch the soaps” may sound stereotypical and may weaken your argument.

childcare saves money

emotional labor and home management support the whole family
equal ownership protects both spouses legally

You can still include your family example, but make sure it directly supports your argument and is explained clearly.

You often repeat the same claim in slightly different words. For instance, you say many times that the husband should not think he is superior and that the wife should be treated equally. State the point once clearly, then move on to explanation and evidence.

Some wording is too informal or imprecise for an academic essay.

Examples:
“stay home wife” → stay-at-home wife or stay-at-home spouse
“the guy” → the husband or the future husband
“watch the soaps” → avoid this stereotype unless you are criticizing it
“worship the ground he walks upon” works as a hook, but the essay should quickly move into a formal tone

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You have chosen a relevant and interesting topic, and your essay shows clear engagement with the debate about marijuana legalization. You also try to consider several angles—history, health effects, crime, and economics—which is a strong starting point for an argumentative essay. To improve the paper, focus on clarity of purpose, organization, evidence, and language accuracy.

Main issue: your essay’s purpose is unclear
At the beginning, you say:  
> “The purpose of this passage is not to convince you to legalize marijuana… Rather, it's purpose is to inform you…”

However, the conclusion clearly argues for legalization:  
> “there can only be one logical answer.”

This creates a contradiction. Decide whether your essay is:
informative (explaining both sides), or
argumentative (persuading the reader that marijuana should be legalized).

Because most of your body paragraphs support legalization, this should probably be an argumentative essay. If so, your introduction should state a clear thesis, such as:

Possible thesis:  
*Marijuana should be legalized in the United States because its medical benefits are recognized, its risks are often less severe than those of alcohol and tobacco, and legalization could reduce crime and increase tax revenue.*

Organization and paragraph structure
Your essay has useful ideas, but the structure is not fully controlled. Some paragraphs are lists of facts instead of developed arguments.

Try this structure:
Introduction – background + clear thesis
Body paragraph 1 – medical/history evidence
Body paragraph 2 – physical and psychological effects
Body paragraph 3 – comparison with alcohol and tobacco
Body paragraph 4 – crime and economic benefits
Conclusion – restate thesis and explain why it matters

Each body paragraph should begin with a topic sentence and then include:
one main claim
evidence
explanation of how the evidence supports your argument

For example, your paragraph about crime could begin:
*One important reason to legalize marijuana is that prohibition contributes to illegal trade and violent crime.*

Use of evidence and logic
You include many claims, but several need better support, more precise wording, or updated sources.

Examples:
“any doctor will inform you…” → this is too broad and not academic.
“NO ONE has ever died from marijuana.” → this is an absolute claim and may be challenged. Academic writing should avoid unsupported absolutes.
“most of what people smoke nowadays is not just pure Marijuana…” → this needs evidence and may not be accurate.
The quote from the DEA and the statements about FDA prescriptions need proper citation and verification.

Suggestions:
Use reliable sources: government reports, medical journals, academic books.
Avoid emotional or exaggerated wording.
Explain the evidence instead of just presenting it.

For example, instead of:
*Alcohol has proven time and time again to be more detrimental…*

Write:
*Some studies suggest that alcohol and tobacco cause more deaths and long-term health damage than marijuana, which raises questions about the consistency of current drug policy.*

Tone and academic style
Your tone is sometimes conversational:
“ask yourself”
“look at what generation is office now”
“there can only be one logical answer”

In academic argument, it is better to sound balanced and formal. Avoid directly telling the reader what to think.

Instead of:
*you will see there can only be one logical answer*

Write:
*These factors suggest that legalization deserves serious consideration.*

Also, capitalize marijuana normally unless it begins a sentence. Writing “Marijuana” throughout is unnecessary.

Grammar and sentence-level issues
There are many grammar, punctuation, and spelling errors. These do not hide your meaning completely, but they reduce the essay’s credibility.

Common problems:
A. Comma splices / sentence fragments
“The first thing to look at when deciding about a stance on Marijuana, the history of Marijuana in the United States.”
  - Better: *The first thing to consider when forming a position on marijuana is its history in the United States.*

B. Wrong word choice
“it's purpose” → *its purpose*
“govermnent” → *government*
“suffers of glaucoma” → *sufferers of glaucoma*
“elevate much of the crime” → probably *eliminate* or *reduce*
“is office now” → *is in office now*

C. Parallel structure
“Some short term effects… include, the ‘high’, and increased heart rate, and some loss of coordination.”
  - Better: *Some short-term effects of marijuana include a feeling of euphoria, an increased heart rate, and reduced coordination.*

D. Article/preposition problems
“the physical effects it has on the body” is acceptable, but often more natural is *the physical effects of marijuana on the body*.

What to revise first
Make your thesis clear and argumentative
Reorganize paragraphs around one main point each
Check facts and add stronger sources
Replace informal language with academic phrasing
Edit grammar, spelling, and punctuation carefully

Final comment
You already have a strong base because you clearly care about the topic and have several arguments to work with. Your next step is to turn this from a general discussion into a focused, well-supported academic argument. If you revise the thesis, strengthen your evidence, and improve sentence accuracy, this essay can become much more persuasive and effective.

What to do

Body Paragraph 1: Historical background

Show how those roles led to double standards.

Body Paragraph 2: Socialization and childhood
Discuss how boys and girls are raised differently.
Use your examples of dating rules, dolls, and fairy tales.

Body Paragraph 3: Media and beauty standards
Explain how television and magazines create unrealistic expectations for women.

Body Paragraph 4: Feminism and change
Show how feminism challenged these standards and gave women more rights.

Conclusion

End with a strong final thought about equality.

Use a more academic tone
Sometimes your wording sounds sarcastic or conversational, which weakens the essay’s academic credibility.

Examples
“Things were fine and dandy”
“getting a little ‘uppity’”
“now what kind of idea was that?”
“How fair was that?”

These phrases sound informal. Instead, explain the idea directly and analytically.

Better approach
Instead of:
> “Women actually thought that they were equal to men, now what kind of idea was that?”

Write:
> “Many men at the time rejected the idea that women were equal to them.”

This keeps your point but makes it stronger and more formal.

Develop evidence more carefully
You make many interesting claims, but some need explanation or support.

For example:
When you mention the Bible, explain how some people interpreted religion to justify male authority.
When you mention fairy tales, connect them more directly to gender roles.
When you mention media, give one or two specific examples of beauty standards.

Also, there is a missing quotation:
> “Another thing about women in present society are their attitudes and what is appropriate to society. .”

You must either insert the quotation properly or delete this reference.

Sentence-level grammar and wording
There are frequent grammar errors that make some sentences hard to understand. Focus especially on:

a. Missing words
“when were strictly in the home” → “when women were strictly in the home”
“why wanted to break this custom” → “why women wanted to break this custom”

b. Wrong word forms or spelling
“warm” → “women”
“particulary” → “particularly”
“appropiate” → “appropriate”
“horn” → “home”
“nuturing” → “nurturing”
“intellingence” → “intelligence”
“priorties” → “priorities”

c. Subject-verb agreement
“Another thing about double standards were” → “Another thing about double standards was”
“Things… makes life miserable” → “Things… make life miserable”

d. Run-on sentences and comma problems
Example:
> “Women were getting restless, they no longer wanted to be only in the home…”

This should be separated:
> “Women were getting restless. They no longer wanted to remain only in the home…”

Avoid overgeneralizations
Some claims are too broad:
“Women are equal partners with men and can do any job a man can do.”
“People of an older generation are the main ones…”

These may be partly true, but they sound absolute. Use more careful language:
“Many women today have greater access to professional opportunities…”
“Some older generations were raised with more traditional gender expectations…”

This makes your argument more accurate and persuasive.

Citations and sources
You include one citation, (Bloom, 24), which is good, but the essay would be stronger with more sources. If this is a research-based essay, add support from books, articles, or statistics. Also, make sure quotation formatting is correct.

Revision priorities
Write a clear thesis.
Reorganize the essay into focused body paragraphs.
Remove informal/sarcastic phrases.
Fix grammar, spelling, and sentence boundaries.
Add or clarify evidence, especially the missing quotation.

Final comment
This essay has a strong core argument and genuine energy. Your next step is to shape that passion into a more organized, formal, and polished academic essay. If you revise for structure and clarity, your ideas will become much more powerful.

Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear and passionate position: you argue that banning books harms education and freedom of expression. You also use examples and legal cases to support your claim, which is a strong foundation for an argumentative essay. To improve the paper, focus on clarity, organization, grammar, and stronger argument development.

Main strengths
Clear opinion: Your position against censorship is obvious throughout the essay.
Relevant examples: You mention real organizations, court cases, and authors, which gives the essay credibility.
Engaged voice: Your tone shows conviction, which is useful in persuasive writing.

Main revision priorities

A. Create a stronger thesis
Your introduction asks several questions, but it does not clearly present one focused thesis statement. End the introduction with a sentence that directly states your argument.

Possible revision:
> Although schools and parents may worry about inappropriate material, banning books in schools and libraries violates intellectual freedom and weakens students’ education.

This gives the reader a clear roadmap.

B. Improve organization
Right now, the essay moves from one example to another without enough explanation of how each example supports the main claim. Try this structure:

Introduction
   - Introduce the issue
   - Give thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Why censorship is harmful
   - Explain how it limits education and freedom of thought

Body Paragraph 2: School censorship
   - Use *Salvail v. Nashua Board of Education*
   - Explain why this case matters

Body Paragraph 3: Library censorship
   - Use the Madonna example
   - Show that public pressure can restrict access to ideas

Body Paragraph 4: Response to the opposing side
   - Acknowledge that parents/schools want to protect children
   - Argue why banning is not the best solution

Conclusion
   - Restate thesis
   - End with a strong final point

C. Develop analysis, not just examples
You include evidence, but sometimes you do not explain it enough. After each example, answer:
What happened?
Why is it important?
How does it prove your argument?

For example, after discussing *Ms.* magazine, explain that the court’s decision suggests that personal discomfort is not enough reason to remove reading materials.

Language and grammar issues

Your essay has frequent grammar, spelling, and word-choice errors. These do not hide your meaning completely, but they make the essay harder to read. Below are common patterns to fix.

A. Sentence errors
Some sentences are incomplete, run-on, or awkwardly punctuated.

Original:

This is a question, so it needs a question mark:
> Is it school boards of education, parents within various school districts, or censorship groups such as the Citizens for Excellence in Education (CEE)?

B. Word form and grammar
Examples:
fireedom → *freedom*
provided → *provides* (if subject is singular and present tense)
range → *ranges*
openingly → *openly*
provided the court → *provide the court*
an previous agreement → *a previous agreement*
attempt → *attempts*
baimed → *banned*
restrain → *restraint* or better *restriction*

C. Articles and plurals
Examples:
high students → *high school students*
their text books → *their textbooks*
student's education → *students’ education*

D. Capitalization and titles
Book and magazine titles should be formatted consistently:
*Ms.*
*I Know Why the Caged Bird Sings*
*Ninety-Five Theses*

E. Quotations and punctuation
Be careful with quotation marks and commas. Remove extra punctuation such as:
However,'
text")
In New Hampshire,,
the court case,,

Tone and style
Some phrases are effective, but others sound too emotional or too general for academic writing.

Also avoid overgeneralizations such as:

A more precise statement would sound stronger.

Evidence and accuracy

Madonna’s *Sex* is not a novel.
Make sure names of court cases, organizations, and statistics are correct.
If possible, cite sources more clearly.

Accuracy is especially important in argumentative writing because it increases trust.

Suggested revision goals

Write a clear thesis at the end of the introduction.
Organize body paragraphs by idea, not just by example.
Add analysis after each example.
Correct grammar, spelling, and punctuation.
Use a more formal academic tone.
Check factual details and titles carefully.

Final comment
This essay shows strong commitment to the topic and good potential for persuasive writing. You already have the passion and the material; revision will help your voice come through much more powerfully.

Feedback Report on Your Argumentative Essay

You chose an important and emotionally complex topic, and you included a wide range of sources and viewpoints. That shows effort in research and a desire to engage with controversy fairly. For revision, your main goals should be: (1) make your argument clearer, (2) organize your evidence more logically, and (3) correct grammar, punctuation, and citation problems.

Clarify your main argument
Right now, your position is only fully clear at the end, where you suggest that removing the Confederate flag would create more controversy. Earlier paragraphs present many opinions, but the essay does not clearly state your own claim.

What to revise
Add a clear thesis at the end of the introduction. For example:

Possible thesis: “Although some people view the Confederate flag as heritage, its historical connection to slavery and racial inequality makes it inappropriate for display on government property.”

Or, if you want to defend keeping it:

Possible thesis: “While many Americans see the Confederate flag as racist, others view it as a historical symbol of ancestry and regional identity, so decisions about its display should respect both history and public concern.”

Choose one clear side and make sure every body paragraph supports that position.

Improve organization
The essay currently reads like a list of quotations and poll results. A stronger argumentative essay needs a structure in which each paragraph has one purpose.

Suggested structure
Introduction
   - Brief background
   - Explain the modern controversy
   - State your thesis

Body Paragraph 1: Heritage argument
   - Explain why some people defend the flag
   - Use 1–2 quotations only
   - Analyze them

Body Paragraph 2: Racism and historical meaning
   - Explain why many see the flag as a symbol of slavery
   - Use the Alexander Stephens quotation here because it is powerful historical evidence
   - Analyze how this affects the flag’s meaning today

Body Paragraph 3: Public opinion and political debate
   - Use poll data and Senate discussion
   - Explain what the numbers suggest

Counterargument and response
   - Acknowledge the strongest opposing view
   - Respond to it

Conclusion
   - Restate your position
   - End with a thoughtful final point

Use evidence more effectively
You have many quotations, but often they are inserted without explanation. In academic writing, quotations should be introduced, cited, and then analyzed.

Example
Instead of:
> Stephens said, “Our government is founded upon exactly the opposite ideas…”

Try:
> One of the strongest arguments against the flag as a harmless heritage symbol comes from Confederate Vice President Alexander Stephens. In his 1861 “Cornerstone Speech,” he claimed that the Confederacy was founded on the belief that Black people were inferior to white people. This statement suggests that the Confederacy was explicitly tied to slavery and white supremacy, which makes the flag deeply offensive to many Americans.

This pattern is stronger: introduce → quote/paraphrase → explain significance.

Strengthen logic and avoid weak conclusions
Some claims need more explanation. For example:
“There is no reason to fight over something that was established so many years ago” is weak because many historical symbols still affect people today.
“If we let these people have their way…” sounds emotional and vague. Who are “these people”? What exactly will happen?

Try to be more precise and reasoned. Academic argument should sound thoughtful, not defensive.

Grammar, style, and mechanics
There are many sentence-level errors. Focus especially on:

Capitalization
Confederate flag, United Daughters of the Confederacy, United States Senate, Civil War
Do not capitalize common nouns unnecessarily, but capitalize official names.

Spelling
Examples:
fory → forty
siad → said
todl → told
unacceptabe → unacceptable
grand father → grandfather
an the Capital → on the Capitol
united States → United States

Punctuation and sentence structure
Some sentences are run-ons or have missing punctuation. Example:
“However the controversy is not…” → “However, the controversy is not…”
“The flag was, in a sense, a part of our ancestors lives.;” → incorrect punctuation

Word choice
“They fought under those colors to what they thought were their rights” should be revised to:
  - “They fought under that flag for what they believed were their rights.”

Citation and formatting
Your citations are incomplete and inconsistent. Some quotation marks are incorrect or broken, and one parenthesis is missing at the end of a citation. Make sure you follow the citation style your teacher requires (likely MLA or APA).

Also, be careful with source credibility. Polls and newspaper quotations can be useful, but historical interpretation should rely on strong academic or primary sources.

Final advice
Your essay has a strong topic and useful research, but it needs focus and control. For your next draft:
Write a clear thesis.
Organize paragraphs by idea, not by source.
Use fewer quotations, but explain them more.
Revise grammar and citation carefully.

With these changes, your essay will become much more persuasive and academically effective.

Feedback Report

You have a clear position and strong energy in this essay. Your main argument—that frivolous lawsuits waste public money and delay justice—is easy to identify, and you include several examples to support it. This gives your essay a strong argumentative purpose. For revision, focus on improving organization, evidence, tone, and language accuracy.

Strengths
Clear thesis and opinion: You clearly argue that lawmakers should reduce unnecessary lawsuits.
Relevant topic sentences: Many paragraphs stay focused on one aspect of the issue, such as inmate lawsuits, insurance fraud, and court delays.
Concrete examples: You use examples that make the essay vivid and interesting for readers.

Main areas to revise

A. Make the thesis more precise
Right now, your central idea is understandable, but it is broad and emotional. Try to state exactly what you are arguing.

For example:
> Lawmakers should reform the civil court system by discouraging frivolous lawsuits, increasing penalties for false claims, and allowing judges to dismiss meritless cases more efficiently.

This version is clearer and previews your main points.

B. Improve organization
Your essay has many ideas, but they are not always arranged in the most logical order. A clearer structure would help.

Possible structure:
Introduction: explain the problem and state your thesis.
Body paragraph 1: costs of frivolous lawsuits to taxpayers and courts.
Body paragraph 2: false insurance and accident claims.
Body paragraph 3: effects on workers, doctors, schools, and public institutions.
Body paragraph 4: delays in legitimate civil and criminal cases.
Conclusion: propose reforms.

Also, use transitions such as:
*In addition,*
*For example,*
*As a result,*
*Furthermore,*
*Therefore,*

C. Use stronger evidence
Many of your claims are persuasive in tone, but they need more factual support. For example, statements like “everybody is suing everybody” or “hundreds of lawsuits are filed by inmates” sound exaggerated unless you provide evidence.

Try to include:
statistics,
specific laws,
court data,
credible news sources,
expert opinions.

This will make your argument more convincing and more academic.

D. Use a more formal and balanced tone
Some phrases are too informal or emotionally charged for an academic essay, such as:
“everybody is suing everybody”
“ridiculous cases”
“begging for people to call them”
“bilk insurance money”

These expressions show strong feeling, but they may weaken your credibility. In argumentative writing, it is better to sound reasoned and fair.

For example:
“ridiculous cases” → meritless or frivolous cases
“begging for people to call them” → aggressively advertising legal services
“bilk insurance money” → commit insurance fraud

Also, consider acknowledging the other side. Not all lawsuits are unnecessary. Some civil suits protect injured people’s rights. If you briefly recognize this, your essay will sound more thoughtful:
> Although civil lawsuits can provide necessary protection for people who have been genuinely harmed, the system should discourage claims that lack merit.

Language and grammar issues

There are frequent spelling, word choice, and grammar errors. These make some sentences hard to read. Carefully proofread your draft.

Common issues:
Spelling/apostrophes
  - “1990's” → 1990s
  - “taxpayers dollars” → taxpayers’ dollars or taxpayer dollars
  - “worker's compensation” → workers’ compensation
Word choice
  - “corporation of lawyers” → cooperation of lawyers
  - “payoff” (verb) → pay off
  - “then to challenge it” → than to challenge it
Sentence structure
  - “Are all of these lawsuits necessary? Seems like…”  
    → Are all of these lawsuits necessary? It seems like…
Agreement and clarity
  - “It cost you and I millions” → It costs you and me millions
  - “The Hundreds of frivolous lawsuits filed bog down the system”  
    → The hundreds of frivolous lawsuits filed each year bog down the system

Example of sentence revision
Original:
> People are encouraged by the large number of lawyers on television, radio, and billboards begging for people to call them if they happen to hurt their back.

Improved:
> The heavy advertising of legal services on television, radio, and billboards may encourage some people to file questionable injury claims.

This revision is more formal, clearer, and less emotional.

Be careful with unsupported examples
Some examples about inmates and lawsuits may sound extreme. If you include unusual cases, explain whether they are isolated incidents or part of a larger pattern. Otherwise, readers may think you are relying on anecdotal evidence rather than a solid argument.

Final revision advice
When you revise, focus on these priorities:
Write a more precise thesis.
Reorganize paragraphs logically.
Replace emotional language with formal academic language.
Add evidence and examples from reliable sources.
Proofread carefully for spelling, grammar, and punctuation.

Overall
This essay has a strong argument and clear purpose, but it needs a more academic style and stronger evidence to be fully persuasive. Your ideas are interesting and relevant; with better organization and language control, this could become a much stronger argumentative essay.
