Feedback Report

Your essay has a clear opinion and a strong sense of urgency. You argue that partisan conflict harms the government and ordinary Americans, and you support this claim with a real historical event: the 1995 government shutdown. This gives your essay a serious topic and a strong argumentative direction. To revise successfully, focus on clarity, organization, accuracy, and sentence-level grammar.

Main strengths
Clear position: You strongly argue that political parties and refusal to compromise can damage government.
Relevant example: The 1995 shutdown is a good case study for your argument.
Concern for public impact: Your point that ordinary workers suffer from political conflict is persuasive and emotionally effective.

Thesis and argument
Your introduction begins with an interesting quotation, but your main thesis should be clearer and stated in your own words. Right now, the essay sometimes sounds like it is only reporting events, not always proving your argument.

A stronger thesis could be something like:
“The 1995 government shutdown shows that extreme partisanship weakens the U.S. government because it prevents compromise, disrupts public services, and harms ordinary workers.”

This thesis is effective because it clearly states:
the topic (1995 shutdown),
the claim (partisanship weakens government),
the reasons (prevents compromise, disrupts services, harms workers).

Organization
The essay would be stronger if each paragraph had a single clear purpose. Right now, some paragraphs mix background, opinion, and examples.

Try this structure:
Introduction – Jefferson quote, context, thesis
Background – explain fiscal year, budget bills, and why shutdowns happen
Causes of the 1995 shutdown – conflict between Congress and Clinton
Effects of the shutdown – workers, services, economy
Counterpoint and response – why divided power can be useful, but only if leaders compromise
Conclusion – restate argument and explain why it matters

Also, use transitions more clearly:
First,
In addition,
However,
As a result,
Therefore,

Development and evidence
You include many details, but some need clearer explanation or stronger connection to your argument.

For example, when you list places and services affected, explain why this matters:
How does closing national parks affect citizens?
How do delayed benefits hurt families?
Why does unpaid labor damage the economy?

Also, be careful with historical accuracy. Some statements seem oversimplified or possibly incorrect, such as:
the exact number and nature of the “thirteen essential bills”
“the president and congress can veto each other’s bill” (Congress does not “veto” in the same way the president does)
“October 1, 1962 was the first time the United States government shutdown” (this should be checked)

When revising, verify dates and facts.

Tone and style
Your tone is passionate, which is good for an argumentative essay, but sometimes the language becomes too informal:
“like two kids fighting on a playground”
“So who’s right? Neither side is right.”

These phrases are vivid, but for academic writing, use a more formal style:
“Both sides acted stubbornly and refused to compromise.”

Also avoid first-person repetition such as “I feel”. Academic writing is stronger when claims are stated directly:
Instead of: “I feel the American people have been unfairly made to pay…”
Write: “The American people were unfairly forced to bear the consequences…”

Grammar and sentence issues
There are many grammar and punctuation errors that make some sentences difficult to follow. Focus especially on:

A. Sentence fragments
“This statement that was made by Thomas Jefferson…”
  - Better: “This statement was made by Thomas Jefferson, the third president of the United States.”

“Starting with the release of all nonessential personnel and ending with the president of the United States.”
  - This is a fragment and needs to be connected to a complete sentence.

B. Run-on sentences and punctuation
“Neither side is right.Both sides were stubborn…”
  - Add a space and punctuation: “Neither side is right. Both sides were stubborn…”

C. Capitalization
American government
Congress
Democrats / Republicans
United States
August
Constitution

Do not capitalize words in the middle of a sentence unless they are proper nouns:
“Should” should not be capitalized in “The power of the United States Should be equally divided.”

D. Word form and spelling
Some examples:
partisan not partisan as a count noun in “there should be no partisan”
government
points of view
fighting
affect vs. effect
  - affect = verb
  - effect = noun

Example:
“How does this effect the ordinary person…?”
  - Correct: “How does this affect the ordinary person…?”

Example of revision
Original:
“Both sides were stubborn like two kids figthing on a playground.”

Revised:
“Both parties acted stubbornly, refusing to compromise even though their disagreement harmed federal workers and the public.”

This version is more formal and directly supports your argument.

Final advice
Your essay has a solid argument and a meaningful topic. The next step is to make it more academic by:
writing a clearer thesis,
organizing paragraphs more logically,
checking historical facts,
using formal language,
correcting grammar and punctuation.

With revision, this essay can become much more persuasive and effective.

Feedback Report

Your essay takes a clear position: abortion should remain a legal personal choice. You also try to support your argument with several reasons, including rape, poverty, women’s rights, and the dangers of illegal abortion. This gives your paper a strong sense of purpose. For revision, focus on three main areas: clarity of thesis and organization, strength of evidence, and language accuracy/tone.

Thesis and overall argument
Your thesis appears in the introduction: “Abortion is the right of the people and it should remain so.” This is clear, but the sentence before it is confusing: “Hapeo povertyg personal choice and illegal abortions are examples why people should maintain their right to decide.” This seems to contain typing errors. You likely mean something like:

“Cases of rape, poverty, personal choice, and the dangers of illegal abortions show why people should maintain the right to decide.”

This revised sentence would also help preview your body paragraphs.

Organization
Your essay has relevant points, but the structure is not fully controlled. Some paragraphs are only two or three sentences, and some ideas appear suddenly without enough explanation.

A clearer structure could be:

Introduction: explain the issue and present your thesis.
Body Paragraph 1: abortion in cases of rape or sexual violence.
Body Paragraph 2: personal autonomy and legal rights.
Body Paragraph 3: dangers of banning abortion, including unsafe illegal abortions.
Body Paragraph 4: economic and social consequences.
Conclusion: restate your position and summarize the strongest reasons.

Also, make sure each body paragraph begins with a topic sentence. For example:
“One reason abortion should remain legal is that survivors of rape should not be forced to continue a pregnancy caused by violence.”

Evidence and logic
This is the area that needs the most revision. Several claims are strong emotionally, but they need reliable evidence and more careful reasoning.

Good direction:
The paragraph about rape is emotionally powerful and relevant.
The argument about illegal abortions is one of your strongest points because it addresses the consequences of making abortion illegal.

What to improve:
Statistics need sources. For example, “One in four women experience rape or attempted rape” and “Lower income families also tend to have five children on average” should be supported by credible research.
Some claims are too broad or may sound biased. For example:
  - “Generally children born into impoverished environments remain in that socioeconomic level their entire life.”
  - “By offering abortion to the lower income people you are helping to maintain a balance…”
  
  These ideas can sound insensitive and risk weakening your argument. Instead of suggesting abortion should “balance” social classes, focus on access to healthcare, economic hardship, and the challenges of raising a child without resources.

The statement about adoption and taxpayers also needs support and clearer reasoning. Adoption does not automatically mean “adoption homes,” and the cost figure should be explained and cited.

Tone and academic style
Try to use a more formal academic tone. Some sentences are too conversational:
“Would be able to love him or her?”
“who is to say they will not try to remove other rights eventually.”

You can still be persuasive, but in academic writing it is better to present arguments calmly and precisely. For example:
“Forcing a survivor of rape to continue a pregnancy may intensify psychological trauma.”

Also avoid emotionally loaded or judgmental wording such as “ignorant and uncaring parents.” Instead, say:
“some parents may be unprepared emotionally or financially to care for a child.”

Grammar and sentence-level issues
There are many grammar, spelling, and capitalization errors. These make the essay harder to follow. Examples:

“today�s” → “today’s”
“life time” → “lifetime”
“being force” → “being forced”
“that they would already be receiving” → better: “than they would otherwise need”
“they won this right in the case of Rowe versus Way” → “women gained this legal protection in Roe v. Wade”
“if the remove this right” → “if they remove this right”
“sky racket” → “skyrocket”
“alsop” → “also”
“eightteen” → “eighteen”
“Abortion is the right of the people” → more precise: “Access to abortion is a right that should be protected.”
“they” after “Women deserve the right…” should be capitalized if it starts a sentence: “They”
“No many women…” → “No. Many women…” or better: “No; many women…”

Suggested priorities for revision
Rewrite the introduction and thesis clearly.
Organize each paragraph around one main reason.
Replace weak or offensive claims with more thoughtful analysis.
Add evidence from trustworthy sources.
Edit carefully for grammar, spelling, capitalization, and punctuation.

Final comment
You have chosen a difficult and emotional topic, and your position is clear and consistent. Your next step is to make your essay more academic, better organized, and better supported by evidence. If you revise carefully, this paper can become much more persuasive.
